\begin{center}
\parbox{0.9\linewidth}{\small\centering This is an expanded version of an earlier manuscript posted to
\url{https://github.com/mrubinst/L-function_zero_free_strips} on October~9, 2026.}
\end{center}

\begin{abstract}
For a nonzero integer $d$ let $E_d$ be the elliptic curve
$y^2=x^3+d$ over $\mathbb Q$. Part~I adapts OpenAI's $11/12$
argument~\cite{OAI1112} to prove that $L(E_d,s)\ne0$ for $\Re s>17/12$.
Part~II adapts OpenAI's $7/8$ argument~\cite{OAI78} to prove
$L(s,\overline\alpha\eta)\ne0$ for $\Re s>7/8$, where $\eta$ is any finite-order Hecke
character of $\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-3})$ and $\alpha(n)=n/|n|$ on primary generators.
Consequently $L(E_d,s)\ne0$ for $\Re s>11/8$.
The analytic normalization is $L(E_d,s+\frac12)$, so the two boundaries
are $11/12$ and $7/8$ within $0<\Re s<1$.
The curve characters have infinite order~\cite{Deuring}, and require new
transformation and moment arguments. These are proved here; steps identical to
the original arguments are cited. Part~II also uses OpenAI's finite-order $7/8$
theorem as an external input. Its CM adaptations have been checked internally
and numerically; external review is pending (Section~\ref{sec:78status}).
Higher-weight and symmetric-power extensions are discussed as expectations in
Section~\ref{sec:higherweight}; more general CM forms are the subject of a second paper, now being written.
\end{abstract}

\paragraph{The method in six sentences.}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{The sum to bound:} a bound
 $S_d(D)=\sum_mq_d(m)W(m/D)\ll D^{11/12+\varepsilon}$ for smooth sums of the
 coefficients of $1/L(E_d,s+\frac12)$, after deleting finitely many Euler factors,
 excludes zeros of $L(E_d,s)$ in $\Re s>17/12$, just as inverse-coefficient sums do
 in the zeta argument~\cite[Theorem~1.1]{OAI1112}.
\item \emph{The curve's row:} point counts rewrite the sum as
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:d001}
  S_d(D)=\sum_n\muk(n)\,\frac n{|n|}\,\chi_n\bigl((4d)^5\bigr)W(\N(n)/D)
 \end{equation}
 over primary Eisenstein integers $n$ coprime to $6$, where $\chi_n(u)=(u/n)_6$
 is the sextic residue symbol (the zeta row has $u=1$ and omits $n/|n|$).
\item \emph{The average:} varying $u$ gives
 $A_u(D)=\sum_n\muk(n)\frac n{|n|}\chi_n(u)W(\N(n)/D)$, and we prove
 $\sum_{0<\N(u)\le H}|A_u(D)|^2\ll D^{1+\varepsilon}H$ for
 $H=D^{1+\vartheta}$ and every sufficiently small $\vartheta>0$, the size of
 square-root cancellation on average.
\item \emph{Copies of the curve:} for fixed $d$, about $H^{1/6}/\log H$ prime
 rows $u=(4d)^5\pi^6$ repeat $S_d(D)$ up to $O(D/\N(\pi))$, so positivity of
 the average yields the exponent $11/12$ as $\vartheta\to0$
 \cite[Sections~2--3]{OAI1112}.
\item \emph{The transformation:} Poisson summation in $u$ converts the paired
 M\"obius coefficients into cubic Gauss sums times $(\bar n/|n|)^2$
 \cite[Lemma~4.1]{OAI1112}, which occur in the second derivative of Kubota's
 cubic theta function~\cite{Kub69,Pat77} (one derivative for zeta), whose
 reflection turns the twists into quadratic symbols controlled by the quadratic
 large sieve~\cite{HB95,GL}.
\item \emph{Closing the argument:} M\"obius inversion removes the theta function's
 extra cube indices $nb^3$, and two further Poisson summations shrink the number
 of rows by a fixed power of $D$, giving a finite induction that proves the
 mean-square bound and hence the zero-free boundary $17/12$
 \cite[Lemma~5.3, Section~7 and Section~5.5]{OAI1112}.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{What is proved here, and what is quoted.}
These notes follow the zeta guide~\cite{ZG}. A proof step identical to
\cite{OAI1112} or~\cite{OAI78} is cited by number or source label; every CM change
is written out. Background on $\OO$, residue symbols, Gauss sums, Poisson summation
and the cubic theta function is in~\cite{QF}. Part~I treats the $j=0$ curves over
$\mathbb Q$, with $d$ fixed and constants allowed to depend on it; Part~II treats
all weight-two CM characters of $K=\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-3})$.

The external inputs are the Kubota--Patterson theta theorem~\cite{Kub69,Pat77},
in the form~\cite[Theorem~23]{QF}; the quadratic large sieve~\cite{HB95,GL}; prime
counting in fixed ray classes~\cite{Lan03,Hec17,Hec20}; and Deuring--Hecke
continuation~\cite{Deuring,Hec20}. Standard local Euler factors~\cite{SilAEC}
give the finite-factor comparison in Lemma~\ref{lem:b1-euler}.
Part~II additionally uses OpenAI's finite-order $7/8$ theorem~\cite{OAI78}.
Section~\ref{sec:78status} records its proof dependencies, and
Section~\ref{sec:numerics} distinguishes identity checks from analytic estimates.

\paragraph{Lean formalization.} We are working toward a complete Lean certification of both results for these
elliptic-curve $L$-functions: the boundary $17/12$ of Part~I (the $11/12$ method) and the boundary $11/8$ of
Part~II (the $7/8$ method). OpenAI's $7/8$ theorem for finite-order characters is already formalized
in Lean~\cite{OAIlean}.

\paragraph{Higher weight.} Analytically, the angular factor $\alpha$ enters the theta step
through the number of derivatives. A weight-$k+1$ CM newform corresponds~\cite{Shimura71,Ribet77}
to $\overline\alpha^{\,k}\eta$ and needs $k+1$ derivatives; the Gamma factors keep
the same length exponents for fixed $k$. Its angular powers must also be checked
through the paired identities, cube inversion and reflection.
Section~\ref{sec:higherweight} explains the expected extension, including the
symmetric-power factorization~\cite[\S3.5]{MW}; constants may depend on $k$. We are writing up the case of
more general CM forms in a second paper; here these extensions are expectations, not claims.

\section{Why start with \texorpdfstring{$11/12$}{11/12} rather than \texorpdfstring{$7/8$}{7/8}?}

For this family, the $11/12$ method is substantially simpler to present.
Its main analytic task is one positive mean square, and the passage from
that bound to the zero-free region takes only a few inequalities and a
Mellin integral. Relative to the zeta argument, the main new ingredient is
the angular factor $\alpha(n)=n/|n|$: one theta derivative becomes two.

The $7/8$ argument~\cite{OAI78,G78} has additional layers: a supremum of
zero locations over a whole family, an average evaluated in two ways,
short prime sums arranged to cancel an unwanted term, and two different
moment estimates to count exceptional rows. These are substantial additions
to the machinery used below.

There is also a scope issue. The stated $7/8$ theorem in~\cite{OAI78}
concerns \emph{finite-order} Hecke characters over $\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-3})$.
The characters for our curves include $n/|n|$ and are not of finite order.
Thus $7/8+1/2=11/8$ is the corresponding arithmetic boundary only
after the required CM adaptation; it is not a consequence of
shifting the stated finite-order theorem. Part~I (Sections~\ref{sec:integer}--\ref{sec:descent})
explains the CM $11/12$ argument. Part~II
(Sections~\ref{sec:78}--\ref{sec:78status}) carries out the $7/8$
adaptation.

\section{Start with ordinary integers and point counts}\label{sec:integer}

At a prime $p\nmid6d$, put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d002}
 a_p(d)=p+1-\#E_d(\mathbb F_p)
       =-\sum_{x=0}^{p-1}\left(\frac{x^3+d}{p}\right),
 \qquad \lambda_d(p)=\frac{a_p(d)}{\sqrt p}.
\end{equation}
The symbol on the right is the ordinary Legendre symbol, with value zero
on multiples of $p$. Indeed, the number of solutions in $y$ to
$y^2=x^3+d$ is $1+((x^3+d)/p)$, and there is one point at infinity.
The good Euler factor is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d003}
 L_p(E_d,s+\tfrac12)^{-1}
 =1-\lambda_d(p)p^{-s}+p^{-2s}.
\end{equation}
The shift is chosen so that $L(E_d,s+1/2)$ has critical line
$\Re s=1/2$; the arithmetic function $L(E_d,s)$ has critical line
$\Re s=1$.
Consequently the reciprocal coefficients away from $6d$ are the
multiplicative function $q_d$ specified by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qd}
 q_d(1)=1,\qquad q_d(p)=-\lambda_d(p),\qquad
 q_d(p^2)=1,\qquad q_d(p^j)=0\quad(j\ge3).
\end{equation}
Set $q_d(m)=0$ when $(m,6d)>1$. Thus all these coefficients can be
computed using ordinary prime factorization and finite sums of Legendre
symbols. In particular, $q_d$ is not the ordinary M\"obius function.

Here is an explicit formula involving that function. Define
$\Lambda_d(r)=\prod_{p\mid r}\lambda_d(p)$ for squarefree $r$ coprime
to $6d$, with $\Lambda_d(1)=1$. For $(m,6d)=1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:integer-convolution}
 \boxed{\quad
 q_d(m)=\sum_{\substack{rt^2=m\\r\ \mathrm{squarefree}\\(r,t)=1}}
       \mu(r)\mu(t)^2\Lambda_d(r).
 \quad}
\end{equation}
At each prime, one chooses the constant, linear, or quadratic term of
the Euler polynomial. The restrictions in this formula make those choices
mutually exclusive.

Write $L(E_d,s)$ using the local factors of a minimal model, including at
primes dividing $6d$, and put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d004}
 B_d(s)=\prod_{p\mid6d}L_p(E_d,s+\tfrac12).
\end{equation}
Then, for $\Re s>1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reciprocal}
 \sum_{m\ge1}\frac{q_d(m)}{m^s}
 =\prod_{p\nmid6d}(1-\lambda_d(p)p^{-s}+p^{-2s})
 =\frac{B_d(s)}{L(E_d,s+\tfrac12)}.
\end{equation}
The finite factor $B_d(s)$ is holomorphic and nonzero for $\Re s>0$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:b1-euler} proves this, and~\eqref{eq:reciprocal} with absolute convergence for $\Re s>1$).
Deleting these primes therefore does not affect the zeros in question.
This remains true when the displayed equation for $E_d$ is not minimal.

Choose a complex-valued $W\in C_c^\infty((0,\infty))$, supported in a fixed
interval $[a,b]$ with $0<a<b$. The target sum is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:target}
 S_d(D)=\sum_{m\ge1}q_d(m)W(m/D).
\end{equation}
The weight restricts $m$ to size $D$. We want, for every $\varepsilon>0$,
$S_d(D)\ll_{d,W,\varepsilon}D^{11/12+\varepsilon}$.
Here $\ll$ allows a constant independent of $D$.

\subsection{The comparison with the zeta coefficients}
For zeta, the classical coefficients in the companion guide are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d005}
 c(m)=\sum_{rt=m}\mu(r)\mu(t)\chi_{-3}(t),\qquad
 \chi_{-3}(t)=
 \begin{cases}0&3\mid t,\\1&t\equiv1\pmod3,\\-1&t\equiv2\pmod3.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
For $p\nmid6d$, the comparison is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d006}
\begin{array}{c|cc|cc}
 &c(p)&c(p^2)&q_d(p)&q_d(p^2)\\\hline
 p\equiv1\pmod3&-2&1&-a_p(d)/\sqrt p&1\\
 p\equiv2\pmod3&0&-1&0&1
\end{array}
\end{equation}
The inert-prime sign really changes. For instance, for $d=1$,
$a_5(1)=0$, $a_7(1)=-4$, so
$q_1(5)=0$, $q_1(25)=1$, and $q_1(7)=4/\sqrt7$.
The character $\chi_{-3}$ records whether a prime splits; it does not
encode the point count of a particular elliptic curve.

\section{The triangular lattice and the curve's row}\label{sec:lattice}

Put $\omega=e^{2\pi i/3}$. An Eisenstein integer is an ordinary pair of
integers written as $n=A+B\omega$, with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d007}
 \N(n)=|n|^2=A^2-AB+B^2,\qquad
 \alpha(n)=\frac{A+B\omega}{\sqrt{A^2-AB+B^2}}.
\end{equation}
The ring $\OO$ has unique factorization and six units
$\pm1,\pm\omega,\pm\omega^2$. Choose the unique \emph{primary} associate
of each element coprime to $3$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d008}
 n\equiv1\pmod3,\qquad\text{that is, }A\equiv1\pmod3,
 \quad B\equiv0\pmod3.
\end{equation}
Unless otherwise stated, column variables and their divisors are primary
and coprime to $6$; row variables run over all of $\OO$. Coprimality to
$2$ means that $A,B$ are not both even. A star on a column sum means
squarefree in $\OO$.

Define $\muk(n)$ by the usual factorization rule: it is zero if a prime
square divides $n$, and otherwise is $(-1)^r$ for a product of $r$ distinct
primary primes. For example,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d009}
 \muk(-5)=-1\quad(\N(-5)=25),\qquad
 \muk(7)=1\quad(7=(1+3\omega)(-2-3\omega)).
\end{equation}
Thus $\muk(n)$ need not equal $\mu(\N(n))$.

For a prime $\pi\nmid6$, define $\chi_\pi(u)=(u/\pi)_6$ to be the sixth
root of unity congruent to $u^{(\N(\pi)-1)/6}$ modulo $\pi$, or zero if
$\pi\mid u$. Extend multiplicatively in the denominator, and write
$\chi_n(u)=(u/n)_6$. It is also multiplicative in $u$.
Powers of these characters retain their zero values, even when the
exponent is reduced to zero modulo $6$; $\chi_1(u)=1$ for all $u$.

Define the family
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Au}
 A_u(D)=\sum_n\muk(n)\alpha(n)\chi_n(u)W(\N(n)/D).
\end{equation}
It is the zeta family with one extra, explicitly given factor $n/|n|$.
For the desired curve, choose
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ud}
 u_d=(4d)^5,\qquad
 \psi_d(n)=\alpha(n)\chi_n(u_d)
          =\alpha(n)\overline{\chi_n(4d)}.
\end{equation}
We claim that $A_{u_d}(D)=S_d(D)$.

\paragraph{Where $u_d$ comes from.} At a split prime $p=\pi\bar\pi$, the point count
\eqref{eq:point-identity} says that $E_d$ contributes $\pi$ times the sixth root of unity
$\overline{\chi_\pi(4d)}$. In its Jacobi-sum proof the factor $d$ enters when the summation variable is
rescaled by $d$, and the factor $4$ enters through the discriminant $1-4a$ of $t^2-t+a$, which gives
$J(\kappa,\rho)=\kappa(4)J(\kappa,\kappa)$ for the cubic and quadratic characters $\kappa,\rho$ modulo $\pi$. The
family, however, twists by $\chi_n(u)$, with the row $u$ in the \emph{top} of the residue symbol. So we
need an Eisenstein integer $u$ with $\chi_n(u)=\overline{\chi_n(4d)}$ for every column $n$. Since
$\chi_n$ takes sixth roots of unity,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d010}
 \overline{\chi_n(4d)}=\chi_n(4d)^5=\chi_n\bigl((4d)^5\bigr),
\end{equation}
and this is the choice $u_d=(4d)^5$. Multiplying a row by a sixth power changes it only at the
columns that share a prime with that sixth power: $\chi_n(uv^6)=\chi_n(u)\,\ind_{(n,v)=1}$. Such a change
alters finitely many Euler factors, so it does not move any zeros in $\Re s>0$. When $v$ is supported on
primes above $2$ and $3$, nothing changes at all, because the columns are coprime to $6$. For instance
$u=16d^5$ gives exactly the same row, since $(4d)^5=16d^5\cdot2^6$.
For $d=16$ this is $2^{24}=(2^4)^6$, a sixth power. So the curve $y^2=x^3+16$ (conductor $27$) is the row
$u=1$ itself, namely the plain sum $\sum_n\muk(n)\frac n{|n|}W(\N(n)/D)$, and $y^2=x^3+1$ is the row $u=16$.
More generally, every nonzero rational row $u$ is the row of a $j=0$ curve over $\mathbb Q$: since
$(4d)^5\equiv(4d)^{-1}$ modulo sixth powers, $u$ belongs to $E_d$ with $d=4^5u^5$. For rows that are not a
rational number times a sixth power, the coefficients $q_u(m)$ of~\eqref{eq:allrows} are in general not real.
Such a row is the $L$-function of a Hecke character of $\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-3})$, not of an elliptic curve over
$\mathbb Q$.

\subsection{Checking the claim prime by prime}
If $p\equiv1\pmod3$, then $p=\pi\bar\pi$, with both generators primary
and $\N(\pi)=p$. The classical CM point-count identity is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:point-identity}
 a_p(d)=\pi\overline{\chi_\pi(4d)}
       +\bar\pi\chi_\pi(4d)\qquad(p\nmid6d).
\end{equation}
This classical finite-field identity is proved with Jacobi sums~\cite[Chapter~8]{IR} (compare
\cite[Chapter~18]{IR}, where primary means $\equiv2\pmod3$, which changes the sign of $\pi$).
Proposition~\ref{prop:b1-points} gives the proof in our normalization. We also checked the identity
numerically for all $0<|d|\le60$ and $p<1500$, at both primes above each $p$. Since
$\chi_{\bar\pi}(4d)=\overline{\chi_\pi(4d)}$, the two reciprocal
factors multiply to
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d011}
 (1-\psi_d(\pi)p^{-s})(1-\psi_d(\bar\pi)p^{-s})
 =1-\lambda_d(p)p^{-s}+p^{-2s}.
\end{equation}
If $p\equiv2\pmod3$, the primary prime is $-p$, of norm $p^2$.
For rational $x$ prime to $p$, $\chi_{-p}(x)=1$, because
$(p^2-1)/6=(p-1)(p+1)/6$. Since $\alpha(-p)=-1$, its factor is
$1+p^{-2s}$, as required by $a_p(d)=0$. If $p\mid d$, the symbols
vanish at primes above $p$, so their factors are deleted.

Comparing Dirichlet coefficients (Lemma~\ref{lem:b1-euler}(b)) gives the exact identities
\begin{equation}\label{eq:normgroup}
 q_d(m)=\sum_{\N(n)=m}\muk(n)\alpha(n)\chi_n(u_d),
 \qquad S_d(D)=A_{u_d}(D).
\end{equation}
The columns still run over primary $n$ coprime to $6$; the sum is empty
when $(m,6)>1$. The angular factor and sextic symbol are what distinguish
this identity from the zeta identity involving $\mu$ and $\chi_{-3}$.

\paragraph{The curve's coefficients are sextic residue symbols.} The identity~\eqref{eq:normgroup} for
the reciprocal has a companion for $L$ itself. Up to the finitely many Euler factors at $p\mid6d$,
$L(E_d,s+\frac12)=\prod_{\mathfrak p\nmid6}(1-\psi_d(\mathfrak p)\N(\mathfrak p)^{-s})^{-1}=\sum_n\psi_d(n)\N(n)^{-s}$,
so for $m$ coprime to $6d$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d012}
 \frac{a_m(E_d)}{\sqrt m}=\sum_{\N(n)=m}\alpha(n)\Bigl(\frac{u_d}n\Bigr)_6,\qquad
 q_d(m)=\sum_{\N(n)=m}\muk(n)\,\alpha(n)\Bigl(\frac{u_d}n\Bigr)_6 ,
\end{equation}
with $n$ primary and coprime to $6$. Here $a_m(E_d)$ are the Dirichlet coefficients of $L(E_d,s)$. The only
difference between the two is the M\"obius factor. At a prime the first identity is
$a_p(d)=\Tr\bigl(\pi\,(u_d/\pi)_6\bigr)$: the Frobenius eigenvalues are $\pi(u_d/\pi)_6$ and its conjugate, so the
sextic symbol selects which of the six rotations $\zeta\pi$ ($\zeta^6=1$) occurs. We checked the first identity
against the coefficients computed by PARI/GP~\cite{PARI} for $d\in\{1,2,-3,7\}$ and all $m\le400$ coprime to $6d$.

Thus the coefficients of $L(E_d,s+\frac12)$ are exactly the sextic residue symbols of the zeta argument,
evaluated at the fixed row $u_d$ and rotated by the angle $n/|n|$. In the zeta argument the row $u$ is the
M\"obius sum $\sum_n\muk(n)(u/n)_6W(\N(n)/D)$ of the finite-order Hecke $L$-function attached to
$n\mapsto(u/n)_6$. This is the Artin $L$-function~\cite[Ch.~VII, \S10]{Neukirch} of a character (by Kummer theory~\cite[Ch.~IV, \S3]{Neukirch}) of the Kummer extension
$\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-3},u^{1/6})/\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-3})$, one factor of the Dedekind zeta function of that
extension. The CM argument averages the same rows with every
coefficient multiplied by $n/|n|$, and its row $u_d$ is $L(s,\alpha\cdot(u_d/\cdot)_6)=L(E_d,s+\frac12)$, up to
finitely many Euler factors.


\subsection{Proofs of the point count and of the Euler products}\label{sec:b1-pointproofs}
OpenAI's paper has nothing corresponding to this subsection: for $\zeta$ the coefficients $c(m)$ are classical,
and~\cite[(2.3)]{ZG} only groups ideals by their norms. From~\cite[Sections~2--3]{QF} we use: unique
factorization of primary elements into primary primes, and the fact that $-p$ is the primary generator of an
inert prime $p$~\cite[Proposition~11]{QF}; Euler's criterion and the conjugation rule
$\overline{(x/\pi)_6}=(\bar x/\bar\pi)_6$~\cite[Section~3.1]{QF}; the values of the symbols at rational
integers~\cite[(3.5)--(3.6)]{QF}; and the cubic Jacobi sum~\cite[Theorem~15]{QF}.

The curves $E_d$ are all the $j=0$ curves over $\mathbb Q$. Indeed, every elliptic curve over $\mathbb Q$ has a model
$y^2=x^3+Ax+B$, whose $j$-invariant is $1728\cdot4A^3/(4A^3+27B^2)$~\cite[Chapter~III]{SilAEC}. So $j=0$ exactly when $A=0$, and the change of
variables $(x,y)\mapsto(t^2x,t^3y)$ makes $B$ a nonzero integer.

\begin{proposition}[Point counts]\label{prop:b1-points}
Let $d\ne0$ be an integer, let $p\ge5$ be a prime with $p\nmid d$, and put $a_p(d)=p+1-\#E_d(\mathbb F_p)$, where the
count includes the point at infinity.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] If $p\equiv2\pmod3$, then $a_p(d)=0$.
\item[(b)] If $p\equiv1\pmod3$ and $\pi$ is either primary prime of norm $p$, then
 $a_p(d)=\Tr\bigl(\pi\,\overline{\chi_\pi(4d)}\bigr)$; this is~\eqref{eq:point-identity}. In particular
 $|a_p(d)|\le2\sqrt p$, that is, $|\lambda_d(p)|\le2$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
(a) Since $3\nmid p-1$, cubing is a bijection of $\mathbb F_p$. So for each $y\in\mathbb F_p$ there is exactly one $x$
with $x^3=y^2-d$. There are $p$ affine points, and $\#E_d(\mathbb F_p)=p+1$.

(b) The residue field $\OO/\pi\OO$ has $p$ elements and contains the image of $\mathbb Z$, so
$\mathbb Z\to\OO/\pi\OO$ induces an isomorphism $\mathbb F_p\cong\OO/\pi\OO$. Through it, $\chi=\chi_\pi$ becomes a
character of $\mathbb F_p^\times$ with values in the sixth roots of unity, extended by $\chi(0)=0$. Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d013}
 \kappa=\chi^2=(\cdot/\pi)_3,\qquad \rho=\chi^3=(\cdot/\pi)_2 .
\end{equation}
By Euler's criterion the kernel of $\kappa$ on $\mathbb F_p^\times$ is the subgroup of cubes, and $\kappa$ takes
values in $\{1,\omega,\omega^2\}$. The character $\rho$ is the Legendre symbol modulo $p$~\cite[(3.6)]{QF}. Also
$\kappa(-1)=\kappa\bigl((-1)^3\bigr)=\kappa(-1)^3=1$. Since $\mathbb F_p^\times$ is cyclic of order divisible by $3$,
a nonzero cube has exactly three cube roots, while for a non-cube $V$ we have $\kappa(V)\in\{\omega,\omega^2\}$ and
$1+\omega+\omega^2=0$. Hence, for $U,V\in\mathbb F_p$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d014}
 \#\{y:y^2=U\}=1+\rho(U),\qquad \#\{x:x^3=V\}=1+\kappa(V)+\overline{\kappa(V)} .
\end{equation}
Counting the affine points $(x,y)$ according to the value $V=x^3$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b1-count}
 \#E_d(\mathbb F_p)-1=\sum_{V\in\mathbb F_p}\bigl(1+\rho(V+d)\bigr)\bigl(1+\kappa(V)+\overline{\kappa(V)}\bigr)
 =p+S+\overline S,\qquad S=\sum_{V\in\mathbb F_p}\rho(V+d)\kappa(V),
\end{equation}
because $\sum_V\rho(V+d)=\sum_V\kappa(V)=0$ and $\rho$ is real. Thus $a_p(d)=-S-\overline S$.

Since $p\nmid d$, the substitution $V=-dt$ is a bijection of $\mathbb F_p$. As
$\rho(d-dt)=\rho(d)\rho(1-t)$ and $\kappa(-dt)=\kappa(d)\kappa(t)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d015}
 S=\rho(d)\kappa(d)\,J(\kappa,\rho),\qquad J(\kappa,\rho)=\sum_{t\in\mathbb F_p}\kappa(t)\rho(1-t).
\end{equation}
To compare this with the cubic Jacobi sum $J(\kappa,\kappa)=\sum_t\kappa(t)\kappa(1-t)$, group the $t$ according
to $a=t(1-t)$. Since $p$ is odd, the equation $t^2-t+a=0$ has $1+\rho(1-4a)$ solutions, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d016}
 J(\kappa,\kappa)=\sum_{a\in\mathbb F_p}\kappa(a)\bigl(1+\rho(1-4a)\bigr)
 =\sum_a\kappa(a)\rho(1-4a)=\overline{\kappa(4)}\,J(\kappa,\rho),
\end{equation}
where the last step substitutes $a=b/4$. For primary $\pi$ we have $J(\kappa,\kappa)=-\pi$~\cite[Theorem~15]{QF}.
Hence $J(\kappa,\rho)=-\kappa(4)\pi$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b1-S}
 S=-\rho(d)\kappa(4d)\,\pi,\qquad a_p(d)=\Tr\bigl(\rho(d)\kappa(4d)\,\pi\bigr).
\end{equation}
Finally $\rho(d)\kappa(4d)=\chi(d)^3\chi(4d)^2=\chi(d)^5\chi(4)^2$. Since $\chi(4)^3=\chi(2)^6=1$, we have
$\chi(4)^2=\chi(4)^5$, and so $\rho(d)\kappa(4d)=\chi(4d)^5=\overline{\chi(4d)}$. This proves~\eqref{eq:point-identity}
for $\pi$. The two primes above $p$ give the same value, because $\chi_{\bar\pi}(4d)=\overline{\chi_\pi(4d)}$ for the
rational number $4d$. The bound follows from $|\chi_\pi(4d)|=1$ and $|\pi|=\sqrt p$.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Where the normalizations enter.} The sign comes from the Jacobi sum: $J(\kappa,\kappa)$ is the generator
of $\pi\OO$ that is $\equiv-1\pmod3$, which is $-\pi$ for our primary $\pi$. In the convention of~\cite[Chapter~9]{IR}
the primary generator is $\equiv2\pmod 3$, that is, $-\pi$, and $J(\kappa,\kappa)$ equals it~\cite[Remark~10]{QF}.
This is the sign change mentioned after~\eqref{eq:point-identity}. The factor $4$ comes only from the discriminant
$1-4a$, and the complex conjugation only from $\rho(d)\kappa(4d)=\chi(4d)^5$.

\begin{lemma}[Euler products]\label{lem:b1-euler}
Let $d\ne0$, and let $\psi_d(n)=\alpha(n)\chi_n(u_d)$ be as in~\eqref{eq:ud}.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Let $p\ge5$, and let $X$ be an indeterminate. If $p\nmid d$ and $p=\pi\bar\pi\equiv1\pmod3$ with
 $\pi$ primary, then $(1-\psi_d(\pi)X)(1-\psi_d(\bar\pi)X)=1-\lambda_d(p)X+X^2$. If $p\nmid d$ and $p\equiv2\pmod3$,
 then $1-\psi_d(-p)X^2=1+X^2=1-\lambda_d(p)X+X^2$. If $p\mid d$, then $\psi_d$ vanishes at every prime above $p$.
\item[(b)] For $\Re s>1$ the following series and products converge absolutely, and
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:b1-euler}
  \sum_n\frac{\muk(n)\psi_d(n)}{\N(n)^s}
  =\prod_\pi\Bigl(1-\frac{\psi_d(\pi)}{\N(\pi)^s}\Bigr)
  =\prod_{p\nmid6d}\bigl(1-\lambda_d(p)p^{-s}+p^{-2s}\bigr)
  =\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{q_d(m)}{m^s}
  =\frac{B_d(s)}{L(E_d,s+\frac12)} .
 \end{equation}
 Here $n$ runs over the primary elements coprime to $6$, and $\pi$ over the primary primes not dividing $6$.
 Consequently~\eqref{eq:normgroup} holds, and $a_m(E_d)/\sqrt m=\sum_{\N(n)=m}\psi_d(n)$ for $(m,6d)=1$.
\item[(c)] $B_d(s)$ is holomorphic and nonzero for $\Re s>0$, whether or not $y^2=x^3+d$ is a minimal model.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) Let $p=\pi\bar\pi$. Both $\pi$ and $\bar\pi$ are primary~\cite[Proposition~11]{QF}. We have
$\alpha(\bar\pi)=\overline{\alpha(\pi)}$, and $\chi_{\bar\pi}(4d)=\overline{\chi_\pi(4d)}$ because $4d$ is rational.
Hence $\psi_d(\bar\pi)=\overline{\psi_d(\pi)}$ and $|\psi_d(\pi)|=1$, so the product is
$1-\Tr(\psi_d(\pi))X+X^2$. By~\eqref{eq:ud} and Proposition~\ref{prop:b1-points},
$\Tr(\psi_d(\pi))=\Tr\bigl(\pi\overline{\chi_\pi(4d)}\bigr)/\sqrt p=\lambda_d(p)$. If $p\equiv2\pmod3$, the primary
generator of $p\OO$ is $-p$, of norm $p^2$, with $\alpha(-p)=-1$ and $\chi_{-p}(4d)=1$~\cite[(3.5)]{QF}. So
$\psi_d(-p)=-1$, while $a_p(d)=0$ by Proposition~\ref{prop:b1-points}(a). If $p\mid d$, every prime above $p$ divides
$u_d$, so the symbol vanishes there.

(b) The function $\psi_d$ is completely multiplicative on primary elements, because $\alpha$ is and
$\chi_{nn'}(u)=\chi_n(u)\chi_{n'}(u)$. Also $\muk\psi_d$ has modulus at most $1$ and is supported on squarefree $n$.
The series $\sum_n\N(n)^{-\sigma}$ over primary $n$ converges for $\sigma>1$~\cite[(2.8)]{QF}, so unique
factorization into primary primes~\cite[Proposition~11]{QF} gives the first equality, with absolutely convergent
series and product. Grouping the primes $\pi$ by the rational prime below them and using (a) gives the second
equality; the primes $2$ and $3$ do not occur. Expanding the factors gives the third, by the definition~\eqref{eq:qd}
of the multiplicative function $q_d$; the expansion converges absolutely because $|\lambda_d(p)|\le2$. For the last
equality, the equation $y^2=x^3+d$ has discriminant $-2^43^3d^2$. So it has good reduction at every $p\nmid6d$, and it
gives $L_p(E_d,s)^{-1}=1-a_p(d)p^{-s}+p^{1-2s}$ there, which becomes $1-\lambda_d(p)p^{-s}+p^{-2s}$ after the shift
$s\mapsto s+\frac12$. The factors at $p\mid6d$ make up $B_d(s)$.

The coefficient of $m^{-s}$ in the first series of~\eqref{eq:b1-euler} is $\sum_{\N(n)=m}\muk(n)\psi_d(n)$, and in
the fourth it is $q_d(m)$. Absolutely convergent Dirichlet series with the same sum have the same coefficients,
which gives~\eqref{eq:normgroup}. In the same way, (a) gives
$\sum_n\psi_d(n)\N(n)^{-s}=\prod_\pi(1-\psi_d(\pi)\N(\pi)^{-s})^{-1}=\prod_{p\nmid6d}(1-\lambda_d(p)p^{-s}+p^{-2s})^{-1}$.
For $(m,6d)=1$ the coefficient of $m^{-s}$ in $L(E_d,s+\frac12)=B_d(s)\prod_{p\nmid6d}(1-\lambda_d(p)p^{-s}+p^{-2s})^{-1}$
comes from the second factor alone, and it equals $a_m(E_d)/\sqrt m$.

(c) For $p\mid6d$ the factor $L_p(E_d,s)$ is computed on a minimal model. If the reduction at $p$ is bad, then
$L_p(E_d,s)^{-1}=1-a_pp^{-s}$ with $a_p\in\{0,\pm1\}$~\cite[Appendix~C, \S16]{SilAEC}, and $1-a_pp^{-s-1/2}$ does not
vanish for $\Re s>-\frac12$. If it is good, then $L_p(E_d,s+\frac12)^{-1}=1-(a_p/\sqrt p)X+X^2$ with $X=p^{-s}$, and
$|a_p|\le2\sqrt p$ by Hasse's theorem~\cite{Has36}, \cite[Theorem~V.1.1]{SilAEC}. The two roots of this real quadratic are then
complex conjugate (or equal to $\pm1$), and their product is $1$, so both have absolute value $1$; there is no zero
with $\Re s>0$. (One can also avoid Hasse's theorem here. For $p\in\{2,3\}$ the bounds
$1\le\#E(\mathbb F_p)\le2p+1$ give $|a_p|\le p\le2\sqrt p$. For $p\ge5$ write $d=p^{6k}d_1$ with $v_p(d_1)\le5$. Then
$E_d\cong E_{d_1}$ via $(x,y)\mapsto(p^{-2k}x,p^{-3k}y)$, and $y^2=x^3+d_1$ is minimal at $p$ because
$v_p(-432d_1^2)<12$~\cite[Remark~VII.1.1]{SilAEC}. So good reduction at $p$ means $p\nmid d_1$, and
Proposition~\ref{prop:b1-points} applies to $d_1$.) In every case $L_p(E_d,s+\frac12)$ is the
reciprocal of a polynomial in $p^{-s}$ with no zeros in $\Re s>0$. The model $y^2=x^3+d$ was used only at primes
$p\nmid6d$, where it is good, so nothing depends on its minimality.
\end{proof}

\section{The average, in coordinates and as a double sum}\label{sec:average}

The central average is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:M}
 M(D,H)=\sum_{0<\N(u)\le H}|A_u(D)|^2.
\end{equation}
There are about $H$ rows and about $D$ columns in each row.
Writing $Q(A,B)=A^2-AB+B^2$, this means exactly
\begin{equation}\label{eq:coordinates}
\begin{split}
 M(D,H)=\sum_{\substack{x,y\in\mathbb Z\\0<Q(x,y)\le H}}
 \Bigg|\sum_{\substack{A\equiv1\ (3),\ B\equiv0\ (3)\\
                          A,B\ \text{not both even}}}
 &\muk(A+B\omega)\frac{A+B\omega}{\sqrt{Q(A,B)}}\\[-1ex]
 &\times\left(\frac{x+y\omega}{A+B\omega}\right)_6
 W\left(\frac{Q(A,B)}D\right)\Bigg|^2.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Thus it is a sum over four ordinary integer coordinates, using
factorization and exponentiation in finite fields.

Expanding the square, with the first column conjugated, gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:double}
\begin{split}
 M(D,H)=\sum_{n_1,n_2}&\muk(n_1)\muk(n_2)
 \overline{\alpha(n_1)}\alpha(n_2)
 \overline{W(\N(n_1)/D)}W(\N(n_2)/D)\,S_H(n_1,n_2),\\
 S_H(n_1,n_2)&=\sum_{0<\N(u)\le H}
                 \overline{\chi_{n_1}(u)}\chi_{n_2}(u).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
The elliptic-curve factor in this double sum is simply
$\overline{n_1}n_2/(|n_1||n_2|)$. No abstract character notation is
needed to calculate it.

\subsection{An ordinary-integer description of every row}
If desired, put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d017}
 q_u(m)=\sum_{\N(n)=m}\muk(n)\alpha(n)\chi_n(u),\qquad
 A_u(D)=\sum_{m\ge1}q_u(m)W(m/D).
\end{equation}
For fixed $u\ne0$, $q_u$ is multiplicative, vanishes on integers divisible
by $2$ or $3$, and for $p\ge5$ has the following local values:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:allrows}
\begin{array}{c|cc}
 &q_u(p)&q_u(p^2)\\\hline
 p=\pi\bar\pi\equiv1\pmod3
 &-\dfrac{\pi\chi_\pi(u)+\bar\pi\chi_{\bar\pi}(u)}{\sqrt p}
 &\chi_\pi(u)\chi_{\bar\pi}(u)\\[2ex]
 p\equiv2\pmod3&0&\chi_{-p}(u)
\end{array}
\end{equation}
All $q_u(p^j)$ with $j\ge3$ vanish. These formulas include the case
when a symbol is zero. At $u=u_d$ they reduce to~\eqref{eq:qd}.
For a general row, the symbols distinguish $\pi$ from $\bar\pi$;
ordinary $\mu$ and $\chi_{-3}$ alone do not determine them.

\subsection{The estimate to be supplied by analysis}
The analytic input is the following mean-square estimate: for every fixed
$0<\vartheta\le1/10$ and every $\varepsilon>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:MS}
 \boxed{\quad M(D,D^{1+\vartheta})
       \ll_{W,\vartheta,\varepsilon}D^{2+\vartheta+\varepsilon}.
       \quad}
\end{equation}
The constant is controlled by finitely many derivatives of $W$. As in~\cite[Proposition~3.1]{OAI1112},
of which~\eqref{eq:MS} is the CM form, there is an integer $J'=J'(\vartheta,\varepsilon)$ such that the constant is
$\ll_{I,\vartheta,\varepsilon}\|W\|_{C^{J'}(I)}^2$ when $W$ is supported in a compact interval $I\subset(0,\infty)$
(the norm is as in Proposition~\ref{prop:b2-reduction}).
The curve parameter does not occur in this estimate: all the curves are
selected by different rows of the same family.

% (R1-11 of the r1 report: the logical structure of Part I, and why it is not circular.)
\paragraph{Logical structure of Part~I.} The zero-free half-plane is deduced
from~\eqref{eq:MS} in Sections~\ref{sec:sixth} and~\ref{sec:zeros}
(Lemmas~\ref{lem:b1-extract}--\ref{lem:b1-mellin}). That deduction needs~\eqref{eq:MS}
for arbitrarily small $\vartheta$: a single fixed $\vartheta$ gives
$S_d(D)\ll D^{11/12+5\vartheta/12+\varepsilon}$, hence only $\Re s>17/12+5\vartheta/12$. Its other
inputs are the identities of Section~\ref{sec:lattice}, the prime number theorem for primes
$\equiv1\pmod3$, and the continuation of $L(E_d,s)$. The estimate~\eqref{eq:MS} itself is
Proposition~\ref{prop:b2-reduction}, whose hypothesis~\eqref{eq:b2-hyp} follows from the
bound~\eqref{eq:finalenergy} of Section~\ref{sec:descent} with $\kappa=\vartheta/2$
(see~\eqref{eq:initialratio}), together with counting for bounded scales. That bound is proved
by an induction whose inputs are cube removal~\eqref{eq:remove} and the transfer estimate
(Proposition~\ref{prop:cm-transfer}); these rest on the completed estimate~\eqref{eq:completed},
the CM theta transformation (Proposition~\ref{prop:b3-reflection}) and the quadratic large
sieve~\cite{GL}. No step assumes anything about the zeros of $L(E_d,s)$ or of $\zeta(s)$. The
prime number theorem for primes $\equiv1\pmod3$ uses only the classical nonvanishing of
$\zeta(s)L(s,\chi_{-3})$ on $\Re s=1$~\cite{Had96,Lan03}, and the remaining inputs are identities, the theta
transformation and the large sieve.

The diagonal $n_1=n_2=n$ in~\eqref{eq:double} has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d018}
 S_H(n,n)=\#\{u:0<\N(u)\le H,\ (u,n)=1\}\ll H.
\end{equation}
More precisely, elementary lattice counting gives, exactly as for $\zeta$~\cite[(4.1)--(4.2)]{ZG},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d019}
 S_H(n,n)=\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt3}H
       \prod_{\pi\mid n}(1-\N(\pi)^{-1})
       +O\bigl(\tau(n)(\sqrt H+1)\bigr).
\end{equation}
Here $\tau(n)$ counts primary divisors. The factor $2\pi/\sqrt3$ is the
area of the disc divided by the lattice covolume. The diagonal is
$O_W(HD)$. Thus~\eqref{eq:MS} bounds the whole average on the diagonal
scale, up to a small power loss. It bounds the total off-diagonal
contribution, not the sum of its termwise absolute values.

\section{Sixth powers isolate the chosen elliptic curve}\label{sec:sixth}

Fix $u_0=u_d$ and $N_0=\N(u_0)=|4d|^{10}$. For any $v\ne0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d020}
 \chi_n(u_0v^6)=\chi_n(u_0)\ind_{(n,v)=1}.
\end{equation}
Consequently the rows we need are \emph{the fixed row $u_0$ times sixth
powers}; they need not themselves be sixth powers.

Take $H=D^{1+\vartheta}$, put $Y=(H/N_0)^{1/6}$, and let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d021}
 \mathcal P_Y=\{\pi\text{ primary prime}:\ \pi\nmid6d,
                                      \ Y/2<\N(\pi)\le Y\},
 \qquad J=|\mathcal P_Y|.
\end{equation}
For large $D$, the prime number theorem for $p\equiv1\pmod3$ (Landau's prime ideal theorem~\cite{Lan03} for $\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-3})$) gives
$J\asymp_d Y/\log Y$; for small $Y$ there may be no such primes at all, since no primary prime
coprime to $6$ has norm below $7$. The rows $u_0\pi^6$ are distinct and have norm
at most $H$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:b1-extract}). Their difference from the target is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d022}
 A_{u_0}(D)-A_{u_0\pi^6}(D)
 =\sum_{\pi\mid n}\muk(n)\alpha(n)\chi_n(u_0)W(\N(n)/D).
\end{equation}
There are $O_W(D/\N(\pi))$ possible multiples of $\pi$ in this sum,
because dividing by $\pi$ reduces the norm bound by $\N(\pi)$.
Every coefficient has absolute value at most one. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:approx}
 |A_{u_0}(D)-A_{u_0\pi^6}(D)|\ll_W D/Y.
\end{equation}
Using $|z|^2\le2|w|^2+2|z-w|^2$ for each selected row and the
nonnegativity of the mean square gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:extraction}
 \boxed{\quad J|S_d(D)|^2
 \le2M(D,H)+O_W(JD^2/Y^2).\quad}
\end{equation}
There is no assumption that different rows cancel against each other.
Positivity is precisely what lets us extract the special rows.

Inserting~\eqref{eq:MS}, absorbing logarithms into $D^\varepsilon$, and
allowing constants depending on $d$, we get
\begin{align}
 |S_d(D)|^2
 &\ll_{d,W,\vartheta,\varepsilon}
          DH^{5/6}D^\varepsilon+D^2H^{-1/3}\notag\\
 &=D^{11/6+5\vartheta/6+\varepsilon}
                  +D^{5/3-\vartheta/3}.\label{eq:exponents}
\end{align}
Take square roots, then choose $\vartheta>0$ and the power loss small
enough. For every $\eta>0$ this proves
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Sbound}
 S_d(D)\ll_{d,W,\eta}D^{11/12+\eta}.
\end{equation}
\paragraph{Dependence on $d$.} The mean-square estimate~\eqref{eq:MS} does not involve $d$; the curve
enters only through the position of its row. Keeping track of $N_0=\N(u_0)$ in the steps above gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d023}
 S_d(D)\ll_{W,\vartheta,\varepsilon}N_0^{1/12}D^{11/12+5\vartheta/12+\varepsilon}+N_0^{1/6}D^{5/6-\vartheta/6},
 \qquad N_0^{1/12}=|4d|^{5/6},
\end{equation}
valid for all $D\ge1$, with a constant independent of $d$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:b1-uniform}; when $Y$ is small,
for instance when $Y<7$ and so $J=0$, the right side exceeds the trivial bound $|S_d(D)|\ll_WD$). This is
nontrivial only for $D$ larger than about
$|d|^{10}$. That is a dependence on the chosen parameter $d$, not on the curve: $E_{dt^6}$ is isomorphic
to $E_d$ over $\mathbb Q$, so $|d|$ can be made arbitrarily large while the conductor stays fixed. If $d$ is
first replaced by its sixth-power-free part, then every prime $p\ge5$ dividing $d$ divides the
conductor $N(E_d)$ exactly twice. (The model is minimal at $p$ because $v_p(-432d^2)<12$, the reduction is
additive because $c_4=0$, and additive reduction at $p\ge5$ has conductor exponent~$2$;
see~\cite[Remark~VII.1.1 and Proposition~VII.5.1]{SilAEC} and~\cite[Chapter~IV, \S10]{SilATAEC}.)
So $|d|\le6^5N(E_d)^{5/2}$, and $|d|^{10}$ is at most a fixed power of the
conductor. For the zero-free half-plane this dependence is harmless, because each $d$ is treated
separately.

The exponent is easy to remember: the average has size about $D^2$,
and about $D^{1/6}$ useful copies of the target occur in it, so the
target squared has size about $D^{2-1/6}=D^{11/6}$.

\subsection{The extraction in detail}\label{sec:b1-extraction}
For $\zeta$ this is~\cite[Section~3, proof of Theorem~1.1, (3.2)--(3.3)]{OAI1112}; see also~\cite[(4.3)--(4.6) and
Section~5]{ZG}. The rows $\pi^6$ there become the rows $u_0\pi^6$ here. This changes the scale to
$Y=(H/N_0)^{1/6}$, excludes the primes dividing $d$, and makes constants depend on $d$. The angular factor enters only
through $|\alpha(n)|=1$. We write the argument out with the dependence on $d$ explicit. Throughout, $W$ is supported in
$[a,b]$, $\|W\|_\infty=\sup|W|$, $H=D^{1+\vartheta}$ with $0<\vartheta\le1/10$, $Y=(H/N_0)^{1/6}$, and
$\mathcal P_Y$, $J$ are as above.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:b1-extract}
Let $D\ge1$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $\#\{m\in\OO:\N(m)\le x\}\le1+8x$ for every $x\ge0$. Hence $|A_u(D)|\le(1+8bD)\|W\|_\infty$ for every
 row $u$, in particular for $S_d(D)=A_{u_0}(D)$.
\item[(ii)] The rows $u_0\pi^6$ with $\pi\in\mathcal P_Y$ are distinct, and $0<\N(u_0\pi^6)\le H$.
\item[(iii)] For $\pi\in\mathcal P_Y$, $|A_{u_0}(D)-A_{u_0\pi^6}(D)|\le(1+16b)\|W\|_\infty D/Y$.
\item[(iv)] $J|S_d(D)|^2\le2M(D,H)+2(1+16b)^2\|W\|_\infty^2\,JD^2/Y^2$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(i) This is an explicit form of~\cite[(2.4)]{QF}. The open discs of radius $\frac12$ about the points of $\OO$ are
disjoint, and those about the points of norm at most $x$ lie in the disc of radius $\sqrt x+\frac12$. Comparing areas
gives at most $4(\sqrt x+\frac12)^2\le1+8x$ points when $x\ge1$; when $x<1$ only $m=0$ is counted. The terms
of $A_u(D)$ have modulus at most $\|W\|_\infty$ and vanish unless $\N(n)\le bD$.

(ii) $\N(u_0\pi^6)=N_0\N(\pi)^6\le N_0Y^6=H$. If $u_0\pi^6=u_0\pi'^6$, then $(\pi/\pi')^6=1$. So $\pi/\pi'$ is a root of
unity in $K$, hence a unit of $\OO$, and the primary associates $\pi,\pi'$ are equal~\cite[Proposition~11]{QF}.

(iii) The symbol is multiplicative in the numerator, and $\chi_n(\pi)^6=\ind_{\pi\nmid n}$ by the convention on zero
values. So $\chi_n(u_0\pi^6)=\chi_n(u_0)\ind_{\pi\nmid n}$, and the difference is the sum over $\pi\mid n$ displayed
before~\eqref{eq:approx}. Its terms have modulus at most $\|W\|_\infty$, since $|\muk(n)\alpha(n)\chi_n(u_0)|\le1$, and
vanish unless $\N(n)\le bD$. Write $n=\pi m$. Then $m\in\OO$ and $\N(m)\le bD/\N(\pi)<2bD/Y$, so by (i) there are at
most $1+16bD/Y\le(1+16b)D/Y$ terms. Here $Y\le H^{1/6}=D^{(1+\vartheta)/6}\le D$, because $N_0\ge1$.

(iv) For each $\pi\in\mathcal P_Y$,
$|S_d(D)|^2\le2|A_{u_0\pi^6}(D)|^2+2|S_d(D)-A_{u_0\pi^6}(D)|^2$. Sum over $\pi\in\mathcal P_Y$ and insert (iii). By
(ii), the rows $u_0\pi^6$ are distinct terms of the sum $M(D,H)$, whose terms are nonnegative.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Proof of~\eqref{eq:Sbound}.} Fix $d$, $W$, $\vartheta$ and $\varepsilon$. Each prime $p\equiv1\pmod3$ with
$Y/2<p\le Y$ gives two primary primes of norm $p$, and at most $2\omega(d)$ primes of $\OO$ divide $d$. So the prime
number theorem for $p\equiv1\pmod 3$ gives $J\ge Y/(5\log Y)$ for $Y\ge Y_0(d)$, that is, for $D\ge D_0(d,\vartheta)$.
For such $D$, Lemma~\ref{lem:b1-extract}(iv) and~\eqref{eq:MS} give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d024}
 |S_d(D)|^2\le\frac{2M(D,H)}J+O_W\Bigl(\frac{D^2}{Y^2}\Bigr)
 \ll_{d,W,\vartheta,\varepsilon}\frac{D^{2+\vartheta+\varepsilon}\log D}{Y}+\frac{D^2}{Y^2},
\end{equation}
which is~\eqref{eq:exponents}, because $Y\asymp_dH^{1/6}$. For $1\le D<D_0$, Lemma~\ref{lem:b1-extract}(i) gives
$|S_d(D)|\ll_{d,W,\vartheta}1$. So~\eqref{eq:Sbound} holds for all $D\ge1$. Also $S_d(D)=0$ for $D<1/b$, and
$S_d(D)$ is bounded for $1/b\le D<1$.

\begin{lemma}[Uniformity in $d$]\label{lem:b1-uniform}
Let $0<\vartheta\le1/10$ and $\varepsilon>0$. For all $d\ne0$ and all $D\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d025}
 S_d(D)\ll_{W,\vartheta,\varepsilon}N_0^{1/12}D^{11/12+5\vartheta/12+\varepsilon}+N_0^{1/6}D^{5/6-\vartheta/6},
\end{equation}
with an implied constant that does not depend on $d$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $Y^6=D^{1+\vartheta}/N_0$, the two terms on the right are $D^{1+\vartheta/2+\varepsilon}Y^{-1/2}$ and $D/Y$. By the
prime number theorem for primes $\equiv1\pmod 3$, there are absolute constants $c_1>0$ and $Y_1\ge7$ such that for
$Y\ge Y_1$ at least $2c_1Y/\log Y$ primary primes $\pi\nmid6$ satisfy $Y/2<\N(\pi)\le Y$. Some threshold is
necessary: no primary prime coprime to $6$ has norm below $7$, so $J=0$ when $Y<7$.

Let $Y\ge Y_1$. The primes $\pi\mid d$ with $\N(\pi)>Y/2$ number at most $2\log|d|/\log(Y/2)$, because the product
of their norms divides $\N(d)=d^2$. Also $\log|d|<\frac1{10}\log N_0\le\frac1{10}\log H$, since $N_0=|4d|^{10}$ and
$N_0\le H$ when $Y\ge1$. So the condition $\pi\nmid d$ removes fewer than $\log H/(5\log(Y/2))$ primes from
$\mathcal P_Y$.

\emph{Case 1: $Y\ge Y_1$ and $\log H/(5\log(Y/2))\le c_1Y/\log Y$.} Then $J\ge c_1Y/\log Y$. For $D\ge2$,
Lemma~\ref{lem:b1-extract}(iv), \eqref{eq:MS} and $\log Y\le\log D$ give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d026}
 |S_d(D)|^2\ll_{W,\vartheta,\varepsilon}\frac{D^{2+\vartheta+\varepsilon}\log D}Y+\frac{D^2}{Y^2},
\end{equation}
and the claim follows after renaming $\varepsilon$.

\emph{Case 2: otherwise.} If $Y\ge Y_1$, then $\log(Y/2)\ge\frac12\log Y$, because $Y\ge4$. So the failure of Case~1
gives $c_1Y/\log Y<2\log H/(5\log Y)$, that is, $Y<2\log H/(5c_1)$. Hence in Case~2
$Y<Y_2:=\max\bigl(Y_1,\,2(1+\vartheta)\log(2D)/(5c_1)\bigr)$, and the first term on the right is at least
$D^{1+\vartheta/2}Y_2^{-1/2}\gg_\vartheta D$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:b1-extract}(i), $|S_d(D)|\le(1+8b)\|W\|_\infty D$.
Case~1 cannot occur for $D<2$, because then $Y\le D^{(1+\vartheta)/6}<7\le Y_1$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Other perfect powers and the rational slice}
The exact identities
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d027}
 \chi_n(v^3)=(v/n)_2,\qquad \chi_n(v^2)=(v/n)_3
\end{equation}
identify cube and square rows as quadratic and cubic twists.
If $v$ is a nonzero rational integer, then
$(v/n)_2=(v/\N(n))$, an ordinary Jacobi symbol. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:quadratic-twists}
 A_{u_dv^3}(D)=\sum_{m\ge1}q_d(m)
                       \left(\frac vm\right)W(m/D).
\end{equation}
The finite-field reason at an inert prime is that every nonzero element
of $\mathbb F_p$ is a square in $\mathbb F_{p^2}$; at a split prime it
is ordinary Euler's criterion.

The entire rational slice $u\in\mathbb Z$ has only $\asymp H^{1/2}$ rows,
since $\N(u)=u^2$, whereas the lattice family has $\asymp H$ rows.
Sixth powers of lattice elements number $\asymp H^{1/6}$; sixth powers
of rational integers number $\asymp H^{1/12}$. The primes used in
\eqref{eq:extraction} are lattice primes, not merely rational prime bases.
General rational numerators still require sextic residue symbols when
computing their coefficients at split primes.

\section{Why the bound excludes zeros}\label{sec:zeros}

\begin{proposition}
If~\eqref{eq:MS} holds for every smooth compactly supported complex
weight and every fixed $0<\vartheta\le1/10$, then
$L(E_d,s)\ne0$ for $\Re s>17/12$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
We have proved~\eqref{eq:Sbound} for every such weight (Section~\ref{sec:b1-extraction}). Let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d028}
 \widehat W(s)=\int_0^\infty W(y)y^s\,\frac{dy}{y},\qquad
 F_W(s)=\int_0^\infty S_d(D)D^{-s}\,\frac{dD}{D}.
\end{equation}
The second integral is holomorphic for $\Re s>11/12$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:b1-mellin}(i)). At the lower
endpoint there is no convergence problem: $S_d(D)=0$ for $D<1/b$.
For $\Re s>1$, absolute convergence (Lemma~\ref{lem:b1-mellin}(i)) and~\eqref{eq:reciprocal} give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mellin-zero}
 L(E_d,s+\tfrac12)F_W(s)=\widehat W(s)B_d(s).
\end{equation}
Since $L(E_d,s)$ is entire (by Deuring's theorem~\cite{Deuring}; see Section~\ref{sec:b1-analytic}), the identity
theorem extends this equality to $\Re s>11/12$.

Suppose $L(E_d,\rho)=0$ with $\Re\rho>17/12$, and put
$s_0=\rho-1/2$. Choose a nonzero nonnegative smooth function $\phi$
supported in $(1,2)$, and use $W(y)=y^{-s_0}\phi(y)$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d029}
 \widehat W(s_0)=\int_1^2\phi(y)\,\frac{dy}{y}>0.
\end{equation}
At $s=s_0$ the left side of~\eqref{eq:mellin-zero} is zero, because $F_W$ is
holomorphic at $s_0$. The right side $\widehat W(s_0)B_d(s_0)$ is nonzero, because
$B_d$ is holomorphic and nonzero for $\Re s>0$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:b1-euler}(c)). This
contradiction proves the assertion.
\end{proof}
This explains both the allowance of complex weights and the shift by
$1/2$. The zero-free boundary asserted is strict; no conclusion at
$\Re s=17/12$ is being drawn.

\subsection{The analytic steps in detail}\label{sec:b1-analytic}
The skeleton is that of~\cite[Section~3, proof of Theorem~1.1, after (3.3)]{OAI1112} and~\cite[Section~5]{ZG}.
Three things change in the CM case: the Dirichlet series is that of Lemma~\ref{lem:b1-euler}, the removed Euler factors
$B_d(s)$ are those of the curve, and the continuation of $L$ comes from Deuring's theorem rather than from Hecke's
theorem for finite-order characters. We record the three analytic facts used in the proof above. Parts~(ii)
and~(iii) show that the continuation of $L(E_d,s)$ is needed only to give its zeros a meaning.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:b1-mellin}
Suppose that $S_d(D)\ll_{d,W,\eta}D^{11/12+\eta}$ for $D\ge1$, for every $W\in C_c^\infty((0,\infty);\mathbb C)$ and
every $\eta>0$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $F_W$ is holomorphic on $\Re s>11/12$, and~\eqref{eq:mellin-zero} holds for $\Re s>1$.
\item[(ii)] There is a holomorphic function $G_d$ on $\Re s>11/12$ with $G_d(s)=1/L(E_d,s+\frac12)$ for $\Re s>1$.
 It is $G_d=F_W/(\widehat W B_d)$ for any $W$ with $\widehat W(s)\ne0$. In other words, $1/L(E_d,s)$, defined by its
 Euler product for $\Re s>3/2$, continues holomorphically to $\Re s>17/12$.
\item[(iii)] Any meromorphic continuation of $L(E_d,s)$ to $\Re s>17/12$ has no zeros there.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(i) The holomorphy of $F_W$ on $\Re s>11/12$ follows from the hypothesis exactly as
in~\cite[Section~3]{OAI1112}: the truncated integrals are entire and converge locally uniformly. Now let
$\sigma=\Re s>1$. By~\eqref{eq:normgroup},
$|q_d(m)|\le r(m)$, where $r(m)$ is the number of primary $n$ coprime to $6$ with $\N(n)=m$, and
$\sum_mr(m)m^{-\sigma}\le\sum_{n\ \mathrm{primary}}\N(n)^{-\sigma}<\infty$~\cite[(2.8)]{QF}. So, substituting $D=m/y$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d030}
 \int_0^\infty\sum_{m\ge1}\bigl|q_d(m)W(m/D)\bigr|D^{-\sigma}\frac{dD}D
 =\sum_{m\ge1}\frac{|q_d(m)|}{m^\sigma}\int_0^\infty|W(y)|y^\sigma\frac{dy}y<\infty .
\end{equation}
Fubini's theorem then gives $F_W(s)=\widehat W(s)\sum_mq_d(m)m^{-s}$, which is~\eqref{eq:mellin-zero}
by~\eqref{eq:reciprocal}.

(ii) Since $W$ has compact support in $(0,\infty)$, $\widehat W$ is entire. For two weights $W,W'$ and $\Re s>1$,
part (i) gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d031}
 F_W(s)\widehat{W'}(s)=\frac{\widehat W(s)\widehat{W'}(s)B_d(s)}{L(E_d,s+\frac12)}=F_{W'}(s)\widehat W(s).
\end{equation}
Both sides are holomorphic on the connected half-plane $\Re s>11/12$, so they agree there. Since $B_d$ is holomorphic
and nonzero on $\Re s>0$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:b1-euler}(c)), the functions $F_W/(\widehat WB_d)$ and
$F_{W'}/(\widehat{W'}B_d)$ coincide wherever $\widehat W$ and $\widehat{W'}$ are both nonzero. Given $s_0$ with
$\Re s_0>11/12$, the weight $W_{s_0}(y)=y^{-s_0}\phi(y)$ of the proof above has
$\widehat W_{s_0}(s_0)=\int_1^2\phi(y)\,dy/y>0$, so $\widehat W_{s_0}\ne0$ near $s_0$. These local quotients therefore
define one holomorphic function $G_d$ on $\Re s>11/12$, and $G_d(s)=1/L(E_d,s+\frac12)$ for $\Re s>1$ by (i).

(iii) If $L(E_d,s+\frac12)$ is meromorphic on $\Re s>11/12$, then $L(E_d,s+\frac12)G_d(s)=1$ on $\Re s>1$, hence on all
of $\Re s>11/12$ away from the poles of $L$. At a zero $s_0$ of $L(E_d,s+\frac12)$ with $\Re s_0>11/12$ both factors
would be holomorphic and the product would vanish at $s_0$, which is impossible.
\end{proof}

In fact $L(E_d,s)$ is entire. By Deuring's theorem~\cite{Deuring} it is the $L$-function of a Hecke character of
$K=\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-3})$~\cite[Chapter~II, \S10]{SilATAEC}, and Hecke~\cite{Hec20} continued such $L$-functions to entire
functions~\cite[Chapter~VII, \S8]{Neukirch}. It also follows from the modularity of $E_d$~\cite{Shimura71}. This is the fact used in
the proof of the proposition; part~(iii) shows that a meromorphic continuation would suffice. For $\zeta$ the
corresponding input is the meromorphic continuation of $\zeta(s)L(s,\chi_{-3})$~\cite[Section~5]{ZG}.

\section{Poisson summation reveals the cubic Gauss sums}\label{sec:poisson}

We now explain the analytic work behind~\eqref{eq:MS}. Additive characters, Gauss sums and
Poisson summation over $\OO$, including the identities used in this section, are explained
in~\cite[Section~4]{QF}. The CM statements of this section are proved in
Section~\ref{subsec:b2-cm}.
Choose a fixed smooth radial majorant $\Phi(\N(u)/H)$, nonnegative and
at least one on $\N(u)\le H$, rapidly decreasing at infinity, with
compactly supported planar Fourier transform. Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d032}
 e(z)=\exp(4\pi i\operatorname{Im}z/\sqrt3),\qquad
 \widehat\Phi(\N(w))=\frac2{\sqrt3}\int_{\mathbb C}
                         \Phi(|z|^2)e(-zw)\,dx\,dy.
\end{equation}
The smooth mean square over all $u\in\OO$, including zero, is an upper
bound for~\eqref{eq:M}.

Only squarefree columns occur. Write $n_i=gz_i$, where $g$ is their
primary greatest common divisor. Then $(z_1,z_2)=(g,z_1z_2)=1$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d033}
 \overline{\chi_{n_1}(u)}\chi_{n_2}(u)
 =\ind_{(u,g)=1}\psi(u),\qquad
 \psi=\overline{\chi_{z_1}}\chi_{z_2}.
\end{equation}
This is a primitive character modulo $z_1z_2$. For a primitive character
modulo a primary $q$ (here $q=z_1z_2$), define the normalized finite Gauss sum
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d034}
 \gamma(\psi)=\frac1{\sqrt{\N(q)}}\sum_{x\bmod q}\psi(x)e(x/q).
\end{equation}
Inclusion--exclusion for $(u,g)=1$, followed by planar Poisson summation~\cite[Chapter~4]{IK04},
gives the exact formula~\cite[Lemma~4.2]{OAI1112}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:poisson-exact}
\begin{split}
 &\sum_{u\in\OO}\Phi(\N(u)/H)\ind_{(u,g)=1}\psi(u)\\
 &\quad=\frac{H\gamma(\psi)}{\sqrt{\N(z_1z_2)}}
 \sum_{e_0\mid g}\frac{\muk(e_0)\psi(e_0)}{\N(e_0)}
 \sum_{h\in\OO}\overline{\psi(h)}
 \widehat\Phi\left(\frac{H\N(h)}{\N(e_0)\N(z_1z_2)}\right).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
The divisor $e_0$ must be kept. The zero frequency contributes only when
$z_1=z_2=1$; it gives the diagonal main term. Nonzero frequencies from these
diagonal pairs need $H\N(h)\ll\N(e_0)\le\N(g)\ll D$, because $\widehat\Phi$ has compact
support; for $H=D^{1+\vartheta}$ they therefore occur only for bounded $D$. They are
kept in the estimate anyway.
The other frequencies satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d035}
 \N(h)\ll\frac{\N(e_0)\N(z_1z_2)}H
          \ll\frac{D^2}{H\N(g)}.
\end{equation}
This is the first shortening: $H>D$ produces frequencies of norm less
than about $D$.

\subsection{The arithmetic identity and the extra angular factor}
For squarefree primary $n$ coprime to $6$ define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d036}
 \gamma_j(n)=\frac1{\sqrt{\N(n)}}
                 \sum_{x\bmod n}\chi_n(x)^j e(x/n),\qquad
 G(n)=\overline{\chi_n(4)}\gamma_3(n).
\end{equation}
In particular, $\gamma_2$ is a cubic Gauss sum. The finite-sum identity
used in both arguments is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gauss}
 \muk(n)\gamma_{-1}(n)
 =\chi_n(-1)G(n)^{-1}\overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n).
\end{equation}
It is~\cite[Lemma~4.1]{OAI1112}; see also~\cite[Proposition~18]{QF}. It is proved prime
by prime from Jacobi-sum relations~\cite[Chapter~8]{IR} (compare the Hasse--Davenport product formula~\cite{DH35}), which give $\gamma_2(\pi)^3=-\alpha(\pi)$ (compare~\cite[pp.~443--445]{Has50} and~\cite[(2)]{HB00}) for a primary
prime $\pi$, and extends to squarefree $n$ by the Chinese remainder theorem. Reciprocity is
not needed for~\eqref{eq:gauss} itself. It is needed for the next two facts: that $G$ is a
function of a residue class, and the paired identity below. The M\"obius sign is essential
here. The function $G$ has absolute value one and depends only on the primary invertible
residue class modulo $72$ (in fact only on $n\bmod4$).

Let $\mathfrak G$ be that finite group of classes:
$\mathfrak G=\{x\in(\OO/72\OO)^\times:x\equiv1\pmod3\}$. The prime $2$ is inert and $3$ is
ramified, so $|(\OO/72\OO)^\times|=\N(72)(1-\frac14)(1-\frac13)=2592$. Reduction modulo $3$
maps this group onto $(\OO/3\OO)^\times$, whose six classes are those of the
units~\cite[Proposition~11]{QF}, and $\mathfrak G$ is the kernel; so $|\mathfrak G|=432$.
Since every ideal prime to $6$ has exactly one primary generator, $\mathfrak G$ is the ray
class group modulo $72$, and its characters $\xi$ are finite-order Hecke characters; $\xi(n)$
means $\xi$ of the class of the primary element $n$. By the Chinese remainder theorem
($72=8\cdot9$), reduction maps $\mathfrak G$ onto $(\OO/4\OO)^\times$, a group of order $12$.
Define, quite explicitly,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d037}
 c_\xi=\frac1{|\mathfrak G|}\sum_{t\in\mathfrak G}
                         G(t^{-1})\overline{\xi(t)},\qquad
 a_\xi(n)=\overline{\alpha(n)}^{\,2}\gamma_2(n)\xi(n),
\end{equation}
where $\xi$ ranges over the characters of this fixed finite group.
For coprime squarefree $z_1,z_2$, the useful paired identity, the CM form of~\cite[(4.15)]{OAI1112}, is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:paired}
\begin{split}
 &\muk(z_1)\muk(z_2)\overline{\alpha(z_1)}\alpha(z_2)
       \gamma(\overline{\chi_{z_1}}\chi_{z_2})\\
 &\hspace{3em}=\sum_\xi c_\xi a_\xi(z_1)\overline{a_\xi(z_2)}.
\end{split}
\end{equation}
This preserves the structure of a squared sum. The zeta version produces
$\overline\alpha\gamma_2$; the present version produces
$\overline\alpha^{\,2}\gamma_2$. The first factor of $\overline\alpha$ comes
from~\eqref{eq:gauss}, as for zeta. The second is the angular factor of~\eqref{eq:Au}:
expanding $|A_u|^2$ gives $\overline{\alpha(n_1)}\alpha(n_2)=\overline{\alpha(z_1)}\,
\overline{\overline{\alpha(z_2)}}$, which has the same paired form. Unlike a finite-order
twist, it cannot be absorbed into the characters $\xi$. Lemma~\ref{lem:b2-paired} proves
\eqref{eq:paired}, together with the reverse identity used in Section~\ref{sec:descent}.

\subsection{The next average, with every variable displayed}
After resolving greatest common divisors, the averages have the form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:energy}
 \E(\mathcal H,X,F)=\frac1{XF}
 \ssum_{F\le\N(f)<2F}\ \sum_{0<\N(k)\le\mathcal H}
 \left|\ssum_n a_\xi(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4
                       W(\N(n)/X)\right|^2.
\end{equation}
Both $f,n$ are primary, squarefree, and coprime to $6$; $k$ runs over
the nonzero lattice points of norm at most $\mathcal H$. The weight and the finite character $\xi$ have been
suppressed on the left. Put $\Sigma=XF$.

There are two sources of the fourth power. The divisor $e_0$ in
Poisson contributes $\chi_n(he_0^5)$. The common divisor $v_0$ that removes the
condition $(z_1,z_2)=1$ uses the twisted multiplicativity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d038}
 a_\xi(v_0n)=a_\xi(v_0)a_\xi(n)\chi_n(v_0)^4
 \qquad((v_0,n)=1).
\end{equation}
With $f=e_0v_0$ and $k=e_0h$, these combine as
$he_0^5v_0^4=kf^4$. Neither source can be omitted; see Step~3 in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:b2-reduction}.

The initial scales are, for a further divisor-size parameter $B\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:initial}
 X=\frac D{BF},\qquad \Sigma=\frac DB,\qquad
 \mathcal H\ll\frac{D^2}{HB^2}.
\end{equation}
The first Poisson reduction, Proposition~\ref{prop:b2-reduction} below, shows that the
estimate $\E\ll D^\varepsilon\Sigma$ in these ranges implies
\eqref{eq:MS}. Smooth kernels in two column variables are separated
using Mellin integrals, with a controlled finite number of
derivatives~\cite[Lemma~B.2]{OAI1112}; they are not simply discarded.

\subsection{The CM changes in detail}\label{subsec:b2-cm}
This subsection proves the statements of this section for the CM coefficients. We first
list the inputs that are literally those of the zeta case. Their proofs involve the column
coefficients at most through~\eqref{eq:b2-twisted} and $|a_\xi|\le1$, so we only cite them.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Poisson summation.} Formula~\eqref{eq:poisson-exact} is~\cite[Lemma~4.2]{OAI1112}
 with modulus $z_1z_2$ and exclusion ideal $(g)$. It is proved in~\cite[Lemma~21]{QF}
 from Poisson summation over the self-dual lattice $\OO$~\cite[Proposition~16 and
 Theorem~19]{QF}. A majorant $\Phi$ with the stated properties exists~\cite[end of
 Section~4.3]{QF}. We fix it, together with a constant $C_\Phi$ such that
 $\widehat\Phi(t)=0$ for $t>C_\Phi$.
\item \emph{Gauss sums.} The character $\psi=\overline{\chi_{z_1}}\chi_{z_2}$ is primitive
 modulo $z_1z_2$, because its component at each prime $\pi\mid z_1z_2$ is
 $\chi_\pi^{\pm1}\ne1$, and therefore $|\gamma(\psi)|=1$~\cite[Lemma~17 and
 Section~4.5]{QF}. For squarefree primary $n$ coprime to $6$ and $6\nmid j$, the character
 $\chi_n^j$ is primitive modulo $n$, so $|\gamma_j(n)|=1$~\cite[Lemma~17]{QF}.
 \cite[Lemma~4.1]{OAI1112} (see also~\cite[Proposition~18]{QF}) gives the
 identity~\eqref{eq:gauss}, which is~\cite[(4.5)]{OAI1112}, and~\cite[(4.4)]{OAI1112}:
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:b2-g1g2}
  \overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n)\gamma_1(n)=\muk(n)G(n).
 \end{equation}
 The factor $\overline{\alpha(n)}$ in these identities comes from
 $\gamma_2(n)^3=\muk(n)\alpha(n)$. It has nothing to do with the curve and is present for
 zeta as well.
\item \emph{The finite group.} By~\cite[Appendix~A.1]{OAI1112}, $G(n)$ depends only on
 $n\bmod4$. There is a symmetric bicharacter $\mathcal R$ with values $\pm1$, depending
 only on classes modulo $4$, such that $\chi_b(a)=\mathcal R(a,b)\chi_a(b)$ for coprime
 primary $a,b$ coprime to $6$; this is sextic reciprocity~\cite[\S7.3]{Lemmermeyer}, in the form~\cite[(4.1)]{OAI1112}. That
 $\mathcal R$ depends only on classes modulo $4$ is shown in~\cite[Appendix~A.1]{OAI1112},
 where it is given explicitly on the square classes of $(\OO/4\OO)^\times$; also
 $\mathcal R(c,c)=\chi_c(-1)$ there. Reduction
 from $\mathfrak G$ to $(\OO/4\OO)^\times$ is a homomorphism, so $G$ and $\mathcal R$ are
 functions on $\mathfrak G$, and so is $\chi_x(-1)=(-1)^{(\N(x)-1)/6}$. On $\mathfrak G$,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:b2-quotient}
  \chi_x(-1)\overline{G(x)}G(y)\mathcal R(x,y)=G(yx^{-1})\qquad(x,y\in\mathfrak G),
 \end{equation}
 which is~\cite[(4.7)]{OAI1112}. In~\cite{OAI1112} the group is an unspecified fixed ray
 class group; classes modulo $4$ would suffice for this section, and the modulus $72$
 leaves room for the residue-class factors absorbed into $\xi$ later.
\item \emph{Exclusions.} Write $\E(\mathcal H,X,F;\xi,W)$ for~\eqref{eq:energy} with the
 twist and the weight shown, and $\E_{(r)}$ for the same average with the extra condition
 $(n,r)=1$ on the columns. For squarefree primary $r$ coprime to $6$, $F\ge1$ and $\mathcal H,X>0$,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:b2-remove}
  \E_{(r)}(\mathcal H,X,F;\xi,W)\le\tau(r)\sum_{j\mid r}
  \E\Bigl(\mathcal H,\frac X{\N(j)},F\N(j);\xi,W\Bigr).
 \end{equation}
 This is~\cite[Lemma~4.4]{OAI1112}. Its proof is the identity~\eqref{eq:exclusionid}
 followed by Cauchy--Schwarz over $j$ and the injectivity of $f\mapsto jf$. It uses only
 the twisted multiplicativity of $a_\xi$ and $|a_\xi|\le1$, which hold for the CM
 coefficients by~\eqref{eq:b2-twisted} below.
\item \emph{Smooth weights.} The Mellin separation of smooth weights, and its version for
 kernels that depend on the row, are~\cite[Lemmas~B.1 and~B.2]{OAI1112}.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Normalizations.} The identities above are sensitive to normalizations, and we use
those of~\cite{OAI1112}: primary means $\equiv1\pmod3$, $e(z)=\exp(4\pi i\operatorname{Im}z/\sqrt3)$,
$\chi_n^{-1}=\overline{\chi_n}$ with zero values kept, and every Gauss sum is formed with the
primary generator of its modulus. The last point matters: replacing $q$ by $\zeta q$, with
$\zeta$ a unit, multiplies $\gamma(\psi)$ by $\psi(\zeta)$. The first point matters too: if $\pi$ is a
primary prime, then $\pi'=-\pi\equiv2\pmod3$ is primary in the sense of~\cite{IR}, and
$\gamma_2(\pi')=\chi_\pi(-1)^2\gamma_2(\pi)=\gamma_2(\pi)$ but $\alpha(\pi')=-\alpha(\pi)$.
The sign $\muk(n)$ in $\gamma_2(n)^3=\muk(n)\alpha(n)$, which supplies the M\"obius factor
in~\eqref{eq:gauss}, is therefore tied to the normalization $\equiv1\pmod3$.

\paragraph{What changes.} Only the column coefficient changes. In~\cite{OAI1112} the column
coefficients are $\muk(n)\nu(n)$, with $\nu$ of finite order. In~\cite[(4.15)]{OAI1112} the
product $\overline{\nu(z_1)}\nu(z_2)$ is a function of the class of $z_1z_2^{-1}$, and it is
absorbed into the characters $\xi$. The angular factor $\alpha$ has infinite order, so
$\overline{\alpha(z_1)}\alpha(z_2)$ is not a function of a residue class. It does have the
paired form $\overline\alpha(z_1)\cdot\overline{\overline\alpha(z_2)}$, so it can be absorbed
into the coefficients instead.

\begin{lemma}[Paired identities, CM form]\label{lem:b2-paired}
For characters $\xi,\xi_1$ of $\mathfrak G$ define $c_\xi(\xi_1)$ and $\ell_{\xi_1}(\xi)$ by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d039}
 \xi_1(t)G(t^{-1})=\sum_\xi c_\xi(\xi_1)\xi(t),\qquad
 (\xi G)(t)=\sum_{\xi_1}\ell_{\xi_1}(\xi)\xi_1(t)\qquad(t\in\mathfrak G).
\end{equation}
Thus $c_\xi(\mathbf1)=c_\xi$, and all these coefficients have absolute value at most one.
Put $\beta_{\xi_1}(n)=\muk(n)\overline{\alpha(n)}\xi_1(n)$. Let $z_1,z_2$ be coprime,
squarefree, primary and coprime to $6$. Then
\begin{align}
 \muk(z_1)\muk(z_2)\overline{\alpha(z_1)}\alpha(z_2)\xi_1(z_1)\overline{\xi_1(z_2)}
 \gamma(\overline{\chi_{z_1}}\chi_{z_2})
 &=\sum_\xi c_\xi(\xi_1)a_\xi(z_1)\overline{a_\xi(z_2)},\label{eq:b2-forward}\\
 a_\xi(z_1)\overline{a_\xi(z_2)}\gamma(\chi_{z_1}\overline{\chi_{z_2}})
 &=\sum_{\xi_1}\ell_{\xi_1}(\xi)\beta_{\xi_1}(z_1)\overline{\beta_{\xi_1}(z_2)}.
 \label{eq:b2-reverse}
\end{align}
Moreover, for coprime squarefree primary $a,b$ coprime to $6$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b2-twisted}
 a_\xi(ab)=a_\xi(a)a_\xi(b)\chi_b(a)^4,\qquad |a_\xi(a)|=1.
\end{equation}
In particular,~\eqref{eq:paired} is~\eqref{eq:b2-forward} with $\xi_1=\mathbf1$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $a^\zeta(n)=\overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n)$ for the zeta coefficient, so that
$a_\xi=\overline\alpha a^\zeta\xi$. By the Chinese remainder theorem and sextic
reciprocity~\cite[(4.27)]{QF},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b2-crt}
 \gamma(\overline{\chi_{z_1}}\chi_{z_2})
 =\mathcal R(z_1,z_2)\gamma_{-1}(z_1)\gamma_1(z_2).
\end{equation}
In the same way
$\gamma(\chi_{z_1}\overline{\chi_{z_2}})=\mathcal R(z_1,z_2)\gamma_1(z_1)\gamma_{-1}(z_2)$.
Since $|\gamma_2|=|\alpha|=|G|=1$ and $\muk(n)^2=1$, the identities~\eqref{eq:gauss}
and~\eqref{eq:b2-g1g2} can be written as
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b2-two}
 \muk(n)\gamma_{-1}(n)=\chi_n(-1)\overline{G(n)}\,a^\zeta(n),\qquad
 \muk(n)\gamma_1(n)=G(n)\,\overline{a^\zeta(n)}.
\end{equation}

\emph{Proof of~\eqref{eq:b2-forward}.} Apply the first identity of~\eqref{eq:b2-two} to
$z_1$ and the second to $z_2$, and then~\eqref{eq:b2-quotient} with $x=z_1$, $y=z_2$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d040}
 \muk(z_1)\muk(z_2)\gamma(\overline{\chi_{z_1}}\chi_{z_2})
 =\chi_{z_1}(-1)\overline{G(z_1)}G(z_2)\mathcal R(z_1,z_2)\,
  a^\zeta(z_1)\overline{a^\zeta(z_2)}
 =G(z_2z_1^{-1})\,a^\zeta(z_1)\overline{a^\zeta(z_2)}.
\end{equation}
This is the zeta identity~\cite[(4.28)]{QF}; expanded in characters it
is~\cite[(6.8)]{ZG}, or~\cite[(4.15)]{OAI1112} with $\nu=\mathbf1$.
It supplies the first factor $\overline\alpha$ of $a_\xi$. The second factor comes from the
coefficients $\alpha(n)$ of~\eqref{eq:Au}: the conjugated column of $|A_u|^2$ gives
$\overline{\alpha(z_1)}$ and the other column gives
$\alpha(z_2)=\overline{\overline{\alpha(z_2)}}$, so multiplication by
$\overline{\alpha(z_1)}\alpha(z_2)$ turns $a^\zeta(z_1)\overline{a^\zeta(z_2)}$ into
$\overline{\alpha(z_1)}^{\,2}\gamma_2(z_1)\cdot\overline{\overline{\alpha(z_2)}^{\,2}\gamma_2(z_2)}$.
Finally multiply by $\xi_1(z_1)\overline{\xi_1(z_2)}=\xi_1(t)$, where
$t=z_1z_2^{-1}\in\mathfrak G$. Since $G(z_2z_1^{-1})=G(t^{-1})$, expanding
$\xi_1(t)G(t^{-1})$ and using $\xi(t)=\xi(z_1)\overline{\xi(z_2)}$
gives~\eqref{eq:b2-forward}.

\emph{Proof of~\eqref{eq:b2-reverse}.} Now apply the second identity of~\eqref{eq:b2-two}
to $z_1$ and the first to $z_2$. Since $\overline\alpha^{\,2}\gamma_2=\overline\alpha a^\zeta$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d041}
 \overline{\alpha(z_1)}^{\,2}\gamma_2(z_1)\gamma_1(z_1)=\overline{\alpha(z_1)}\muk(z_1)G(z_1),\qquad
 \overline{\overline{\alpha(z_2)}^{\,2}\gamma_2(z_2)}\,\gamma_{-1}(z_2)
 =\alpha(z_2)\muk(z_2)\chi_{z_2}(-1)\overline{G(z_2)}.
\end{equation}
Multiply these, include $\xi(z_1)\overline{\xi(z_2)}$ and $\mathcal R(z_1,z_2)$, and
use~\eqref{eq:b2-quotient} with $x=z_2$, $y=z_1$ and the symmetry of $\mathcal R$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d042}
 a_\xi(z_1)\overline{a_\xi(z_2)}\gamma(\chi_{z_1}\overline{\chi_{z_2}})
 =\muk(z_1)\muk(z_2)\overline{\alpha(z_1)}\alpha(z_2)\,(\xi G)(z_1z_2^{-1}).
\end{equation}
Expand $\xi G$, and note that
$\muk(z_1)\muk(z_2)\overline{\alpha(z_1)}\alpha(z_2)\xi_1(z_1)\overline{\xi_1(z_2)}
=\beta_{\xi_1}(z_1)\overline{\beta_{\xi_1}(z_2)}$. Without the factor
$\overline{\alpha(z_1)}\alpha(z_2)$ this is~\cite[(7.3)]{OAI1112}. In the CM case the
M\"obius coefficients returned by the reverse identity are $\muk\overline\alpha\xi_1$, as
used in Section~\ref{sec:descent}.

\emph{Proof of~\eqref{eq:b2-twisted}.} For zeta this is~\cite[(4.6)]{OAI1112}, where the
twisted multiplicativity comes from $\gamma_2$ alone (via~\cite[(4.14)]{QF} and cubic
reciprocity~\cite{Eis44}, \cite[Chapter~9]{IR}). Replacing $\overline\alpha$ by the completely multiplicative unimodular factor
$\overline\alpha^{\,2}$ changes nothing.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[First Poisson reduction, CM form]\label{prop:b2-reduction}
Fix $0<\vartheta\le1/10$ and put $H=D^{1+\vartheta}$. For a compact interval
$I\subset(0,\infty)$ write $\|W\|_{C^J(I)}=\max_{0\le i\le J}\sup_{x\in I}|W^{(i)}(x)|$.
Suppose that for every $C\ge1$ and every $\varepsilon>0$ there is an integer
$J=J(\vartheta,\varepsilon)$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b2-hyp}
 \E(\mathcal H,X,F;\xi,W)\ll_{I,C,\vartheta,\varepsilon}\|W\|_{C^J(I)}^2D^\varepsilon XF
\end{equation}
for every character $\xi$ of $\mathfrak G$, every compact interval $I\subset(0,\infty)$,
every $W\in C_c^\infty(I)$, and all real $B,F\ge1$ and $\mathcal H$ with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b2-ranges}
 X=\frac D{BF},\qquad 0<\mathcal H\le\frac{CD^2}{HB^2}.
\end{equation}
Then, for every compact $I\subset(0,\infty)$ and $W\in C_c^\infty(I)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d043}
 M(D,H)\ll_{I,\vartheta,\varepsilon}\|W\|_{C^{J'}(I)}^2D^{2+\vartheta+\varepsilon},
 \qquad J'=2J(\vartheta,\varepsilon/4)+4 .
\end{equation}
In particular~\eqref{eq:MS} holds.
\end{proposition}

The ranges~\eqref{eq:b2-ranges} are those of~\eqref{eq:initial}, with $\Sigma=XF=D/B$.
For zeta this is~\cite[Proposition~4.5]{OAI1112}; the hypothesis has the same form, with the
coefficients $a_\xi=\overline\alpha^{\,2}\gamma_2\xi$ in~\eqref{eq:energy}.

\begin{proof}
Write $I=[a_0,b_0]$. We follow the proof of~\cite[Proposition~4.5]{OAI1112} and say at each
step what the factor $\alpha$ does.

\emph{Step~1: expansion and Poisson summation in the row.} Since $\Phi\ge0$ and $\Phi\ge1$
on $[0,1]$, we have $M(D,H)\le D\mathcal M_D$, where
$\mathcal M_D=D^{-1}\sum_{u\in\OO}\Phi(\N(u)/H)|A_u(D)|^2$. Expand
$|A_u(D)|^2=\overline{A_u(D)}A_u(D)$, with $n_1$ the column of the conjugated factor, and
write $n_i=gz_i$ as before. The pair $(n_1,n_2)$ carries the coefficient
$\muk(n_1)\muk(n_2)\overline{\alpha(n_1)}\alpha(n_2)\overline{W(\N(n_1)/D)}W(\N(n_2)/D)$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d044}
 \muk(n_1)\muk(n_2)=\muk(z_1)\muk(z_2),\qquad
 \overline{\alpha(n_1)}\alpha(n_2)=|\alpha(g)|^2\,\overline{\alpha(z_1)}\alpha(z_2)
 =\overline{\alpha(z_1)}\alpha(z_2),
\end{equation}
because $\muk(g)^2=1$ and $\alpha$ is multiplicative of absolute value one. The coefficient
does not depend on $u$, so the sum over $u$ is given by~\eqref{eq:poisson-exact}, exactly
as for zeta. The frequency $h=0$ occurs only when $z_1=z_2=1$. There the coefficient is
$|\muk(g)\alpha(g)|^2|W(\N(g)/D)|^2=|W(\N(g)/D)|^2$, and these terms give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d045}
 Z=\frac HD\,\widehat\Phi(0)\ssum_g\Bigl|W\Bigl(\frac{\N(g)}D\Bigr)\Bigr|^2
 \prod_{\pi\mid g}\bigl(1-\N(\pi)^{-1}\bigr)\ll_IH\|W\|_\infty^2,
\end{equation}
the same as for zeta. All $h\ne0$ are kept, including those with $z_1=z_2=1$.

\emph{Step~2: the paired identity.} This is the only step in which the CM coefficients
change the algebra. By Step~1 and~\eqref{eq:paired},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d046}
 \muk(n_1)\muk(n_2)\overline{\alpha(n_1)}\alpha(n_2)\gamma(\psi)
 =\sum_\xi c_\xi a_\xi(z_1)\overline{a_\xi(z_2)};
\end{equation}
when $z_1=z_2=1$ this reads $1=\sum_\xi c_\xi=G(1)$. Moreover
$\psi(e_0)\overline{\psi(h)}=\chi_{z_1}(he_0^5)\overline{\chi_{z_2}(he_0^5)}$, because
$(e_0,z_1z_2)=1$ and $\overline{\chi_z(e_0)}=\chi_z(e_0)^5$. Put
$W_0(x)=x^{-1/2}\overline{W(x)}$, so that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d047}
 \frac{\overline{W(\N(gz_1)/D)}\,W(\N(gz_2)/D)}{\sqrt{\N(z_1z_2)}}
 =\frac{\N(g)}D\,W_0\Bigl(\frac{\N(gz_1)}D\Bigr)\overline{W_0\Bigl(\frac{\N(gz_2)}D\Bigr)}.
\end{equation}
Then $\mathcal M_D-Z=\sum_\xi c_\xi\mathcal S_\xi$, where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b2-Sxi}
\begin{split}
 \mathcal S_\xi=\frac H{D^2}\ssum_g\sum_{e_0\mid g}\frac{\muk(e_0)\N(g)}{\N(e_0)}
 \sum_{h\ne0}\quad
 &\ssum_{\substack{(z_1,z_2)=1\\(z_1z_2,g)=1}}a_\xi(z_1)\overline{a_\xi(z_2)}
  \chi_{z_1}(he_0^5)\overline{\chi_{z_2}(he_0^5)}\\
 &\times W_0\Bigl(\frac{\N(gz_1)}D\Bigr)\overline{W_0\Bigl(\frac{\N(gz_2)}D\Bigr)}
 \widehat\Phi\Bigl(\frac{H\N(h)}{\N(e_0)\N(z_1)\N(z_2)}\Bigr).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
There are $|\mathfrak G|=432$ characters and $|c_\xi|\le1$, so it suffices to bound each
$\mathcal S_\xi$. Apart from the coefficients $a_\xi$, \eqref{eq:b2-Sxi} is the display
following~\cite[(4.15)]{OAI1112}.

\emph{Step~3: the coprimality condition and the fourth power.} Insert
$\ind_{(z_1,z_2)=1}=\sum_{v_0\mid z_1,\,v_0\mid z_2}\muk(v_0)$ and write $z_i=v_0m_i$.
By~\eqref{eq:b2-twisted}, $a_\xi(v_0m_i)=a_\xi(v_0)a_\xi(m_i)\chi_{m_i}(v_0)^4$, and the
last factor vanishes unless $(m_i,v_0)=1$; so the condition that $z_i$ be squarefree may be
dropped. Since $|a_\xi(v_0)|=1$ and $(v_0,e_0)=1$, the two copies of $v_0$ leave
$|a_\xi(v_0)\chi_{v_0}(he_0^5)|^2=\ind_{(v_0,h)=1}$, and the twist becomes
$\chi_{m_i}(he_0^5v_0^4)$. The CM factor $\overline\alpha^{\,2}$ enters this step only
through~\eqref{eq:b2-twisted}: like the factor $\overline\alpha$ of the zeta coefficients,
it is completely multiplicative and unimodular. Now change variables:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d048}
 r_0=g/e_0,\quad f=e_0v_0,\quad k=e_0h;\qquad
 e_0=(f,k),\quad v_0=f/e_0,\quad g=r_0e_0,\quad h=k/e_0
\end{equation}
(in~\cite{OAI1112}, $r_0$ is called $b$). This is the bijection of the proof
of~\cite[Proposition~4.5]{OAI1112}, onto the triples $(r_0,f,k)$ with $r_0,f$ squarefree,
$(r_0,f)=1$ and $k\ne0$; it does not see the coefficients. It satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d049}
 gv_0=r_0f,\qquad he_0^5v_0^4=kf^4,\qquad \muk(e_0)\muk(v_0)=\muk(f),
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d050}
 \frac{\N(g)}{\N(e_0)}=\N(r_0),\qquad \frac{\N(h)}{\N(e_0)\N(v_0)^2}=\frac{\N(k)}{\N(f)^2}.
\end{equation}
Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b2-output}
\begin{split}
 \mathcal S_\xi=\frac H{D^2}\ssum_{(r_0,f)=1}\muk(f)\N(r_0)\sum_{k\ne0}\quad
 &\ssum_{(m_1m_2,r_0)=1}a_\xi(m_1)\overline{a_\xi(m_2)}
  \chi_{m_1}(kf^4)\overline{\chi_{m_2}(kf^4)}\\
 &\times W_0\Bigl(\frac{\N(r_0fm_1)}D\Bigr)\overline{W_0\Bigl(\frac{\N(r_0fm_2)}D\Bigr)}
 \widehat\Phi\Bigl(\frac{H\N(k)}{\N(f)^2\N(m_1)\N(m_2)}\Bigr).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
The factors $\chi_{m_i}(f)^4$ enforce $(m_i,f)=1$, and no condition $(m_1,m_2)=1$ remains.
This is~\cite[(4.16)]{OAI1112} with the CM coefficients. The inner sums are the column sums
of~\eqref{eq:energy}, with the exclusion $(n,r_0)=1$.

\emph{Step~4: the scales.} On the support, $\N(r_0fm_i)\le b_0D$. Hence
$\N(r_0)\N(f)\le b_0D$ and
$H\N(k)\le C_\Phi\N(f)^2\N(m_1)\N(m_2)\le C_\Phi b_0^2D^2/\N(r_0)^2$. Split $r_0$ and $f$
into dyadic ranges $B\le\N(r_0)<2B$ and $F\le\N(f)<2F$, write $\mathcal S_{\xi;B,F}$ for the
corresponding part of~\eqref{eq:b2-output}, and put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d051}
 X=\frac D{BF},\qquad \mathcal H=\frac{C_1D^2}{HB^2},\qquad C_1=\max(1,C_\Phi b_0^2).
\end{equation}
These satisfy~\eqref{eq:b2-ranges} with $C=C_1$; they are the scales~\eqref{eq:initial},
with $\Sigma=XF=D/B$. The angular factor does not affect any support, so this step is that
of~\cite[proof of Proposition~4.5]{OAI1112} verbatim.

\emph{Step~5: separation of the kernel.} The rest is the end of the proof
of~\cite[Proposition~4.5]{OAI1112}, from its kernel $\mathcal K_{b,f,k}$ to its
display~(4.17), word for word. In the variables $x_i=\N(m_i)/X$ the weight
in~\eqref{eq:b2-output} is
$W_0(\varrho x_1)\overline{W_0(\varrho x_2)}\widehat\Phi(\varkappa/(x_1x_2))$, with
$\varrho=\N(r_0)\N(f)/(BF)\in[1,4)$ and $0<\varkappa=H\N(k)/(\N(f)^2X^2)\le C_1$; it is supported in
$[a_0/4,b_0]^2$, with $C^q$ norms $\ll_{I,q}\|W\|_{C^q(I)}^2$. For fixed $r_0$, the
scales $(X/\N(j),F\N(j))$ with $j\mid r_0$ again satisfy~\eqref{eq:b2-ranges}, so~\eqref{eq:b2-remove}
and~\eqref{eq:b2-hyp} with $\varepsilon/4$ bound the mean square over $(f,k)$ of the column
sums in~\eqref{eq:b2-output}, with any weight $U\in C_c^\infty([a_0/8,2b_0])$ in place of
the kernel, by $D^{\varepsilon/2}(XF)^2\|U\|_{C^J}^2$, where $J=J(\vartheta,\varepsilon/4)$
and $\tau(r_0)^2\ll D^{\varepsilon/4}$. Then~\cite[Lemma~B.2]{OAI1112}, with rows $(f,k)$
and coefficients $\muk(f)\ind_{(f,r_0)=1}$, transfers this bound to the kernel at the cost
of $\|W\|_{C^{2J+4}(I)}^2$. The coefficients enter only through~\eqref{eq:b2-remove}, that
is, through~\eqref{eq:b2-twisted} and $|a_\xi|\le1$. Summing over the $O(B)$ elements $r_0$
of the dyadic range gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d052}
 |\mathcal S_{\xi;B,F}|\ll D^{\varepsilon/2}\|W\|_{C^{2J+4}(I)}^2
 \ssum_{B\le\N(r_0)<2B}\frac{H\N(r_0)}{D^2}\Bigl(\frac DB\Bigr)^2
 \ll D^{\varepsilon/2}\|W\|_{C^{2J+4}(I)}^2H .
\end{equation}
There are $O((\log D)^2)$ nonempty dyadic ranges. Summing over them and over $\xi$, and adding
$Z$, gives $\mathcal M_D\ll\|W\|_{C^{2J+4}(I)}^2HD^\varepsilon$. Hence
$M(D,H)\le D\mathcal M_D\ll\|W\|_{C^{2J+4}(I)}^2D^{2+\vartheta+\varepsilon}$.
\end{proof}

Thus the angular factor enters the first Poisson reduction at one place, the paired identity
of Step~2, where it turns the zeta coefficient $\overline\alpha\gamma_2$ into
$\overline\alpha^{\,2}\gamma_2$. Steps~1, 3, 4 and~5 use only that $\alpha$ is completely
multiplicative with $|\alpha|=1$: it does not interact with Poisson summation in $u$, it
drops out of the greatest common divisor, it passes through the divisors $e_0$ and $v_0$
by~\eqref{eq:b2-twisted}, and it leaves the scales~\eqref{eq:initial} unchanged.
We checked the exact identity $\mathcal M_D=Z+\sum_\xi c_\xi\mathcal S_\xi$, with
$\mathcal S_\xi$ in the form~\eqref{eq:b2-output}, numerically for $D=120$ and $H=40,170$,
with a Gaussian $\Phi$ (for which the identity also holds). The relative error is at most
$3\cdot10^{-15}$.

\section{The completed sums and the second theta derivative}\label{sec:theta}

For fixed $k,f$, put $\Psi(n)=\xi(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4$.
The completed sum is the following explicit double sum:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:T}
\begin{split}
 T(X;k,f)=\frac1{\sqrt X}\ssum_n\sum_b
 &\overline{\alpha(n)}^{\,2}\gamma_2(n)\Psi(n)
 \overline{\alpha(b)}^{\,6}\Psi(b)^3\N(b)^{1/2}\\
 &\hspace{3em}\times W\left(\frac{\N(n)\N(b)^3}X\right).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Here $b$ is primary and coprime to $6$ but need not be squarefree.
All sums are finite. The term $b=1$ is exactly $X^{-1/2}$ times the
column sum in~\eqref{eq:energy}. The other terms are added because they
occur naturally in the theta expansion.

\subsection{Where those coefficients come from}
Put $\lambda=1+2\omega=i\sqrt3$ and
$\ce(z)=\exp(2\pi i(z+\bar z))$. Kubota's cubic theta function~\cite{Kub69,Pat77}, a residue of a cubic metaplectic Eisenstein series (for general covers see~\cite{KP84}), is a
Fourier series in a planar variable $z$ and a positive height $v$,
with terms $d_0(\ell)vK_{1/3}(4\pi|\ell|v)\ce(\ell z)$ in its
conjugate. Here $K_{1/3}$ is the usual modified Bessel function~\cite[\S10.25]{DLMF}.
For $n$ squarefree, primary and coprime to $6$ and $b$ primary, Patterson's coefficient formula~\cite[Theorem~8.1]{Pat77}, which Heath-Brown and Patterson~\cite{HBP79} used to study Kummer sums, gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:theta-coeff}
 d_0(\lambda^{-3}nb^3)
  =3^{5/2}|b|\,\overline{(\lambda/n)_3}\,\gamma_2(n).
\end{equation}
Restricting $\lambda^3\ell$ to be primary and coprime to $6$ selects
exactly these indices. Other units and powers of $\lambda$ are excluded
from the twisted series by this restriction. In the reflected series, at
the three cusps, the indices lie in the larger set of
$\ell=u\lambda^mnb^3$ with $u$ a unit and $m\ge-4$. The tables
of~\cite[Theorem~23]{QF} restrict them further; for example
$d_0(\lambda^{-4})=0$. The factor $(\lambda/n)_3$ depends only
on $n\bmod9$ (a supplement to cubic reciprocity~\cite{Eis44}; see~\cite[Chapter~7]{Lemmermeyer}) and is canceled by inserting $(\lambda/\lambda^3\ell)_3$
in the twist. It is finite residue-class data, not a missing Gauss factor.

\paragraph{What is imported.} Precisely, $\theta$ is the function of
\cite[Theorem~23]{QF}, where $\sqrt{-3}$ denotes our $\lambda$, so that
$g(-3,c)=g(\lambda^2,c)$. The action of $\mathrm{SL}_2(\OO)$ on
$\mathbb H^3=\mathbb C\times\mathbb R_{>0}$ is~\cite[(5.1)]{QF}, and the
Kubota character~\cite{Kub69} $\kappa$ of
$\Gamma(3)=\{\gamma\in\mathrm{SL}_2(\OO):\gamma\equiv I\pmod3\}$ is
\cite[(5.4) and Theorem~22]{QF}. The theorem gives the automorphy (T1)
of $\theta$ under $\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb Z)$ and, with multiplier $\kappa$,
under $\Gamma(3)$. It also gives the Fourier expansions (T2), (T3) at the
cusps $\gamma_\sigma\infty$, where $\gamma_0=I$,
$\gamma_+=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\\omega&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$
and
$\gamma_-=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\\omega^2&1\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$.
For $\sigma\in\{0,+,-\}$ let $d_\sigma(\ell)$ be the coefficient of
$vK_{1/3}(4\pi|\ell|v)\ce(\ell z)$ in $\overline{\theta(\gamma_\sigma(z,v))}$;
