it vanishes unless $\ell\in\lambda^{-4}\OO$. Only the expansion at the
identity cusp has a constant term, namely $\frac{3^{5/2}}2v^{2/3}$.
Formula~\eqref{eq:theta-coeff}, the selection statement after it, and
the support and size bounds for all three sequences recorded in
Appendix~\ref{app:reflection} are~\cite[Corollary~24]{QF}; the bounds are
also~\cite[Lemma~6.4]{OAI1112}. None of these facts involves the angular
factor, so they are used exactly as in the zeta case.

Differentiating $\ce(\ell z)$ twice with respect to $\bar z$ multiplies
it by $(2\pi i\bar\ell)^2=-4\pi^2\N(\ell)\overline{\alpha(\ell)}^{\,2}$. The Mellin integral over the
height absorbs the radial factor $\N(\ell)$ by shifting its variable by $\frac12$ (Step~3 of
Section~\ref{sec:b3-proof}); what remains is the angular factor $\overline{\alpha(\ell)}^{\,2}$.
Since $\ell=\lambda^{-3}nb^3$, its factors in $n,b$ are exactly
$\overline{\alpha(n)}^{\,2}\overline{\alpha(b)}^{\,6}$, up to the
fixed factor $\overline{\alpha(\lambda^{-3})}^{\,2}=-1$.
This explains every angular power in~\eqref{eq:T}.

\subsection{Why differentiating twice is legitimate}
The theta symmetry relates a rational translate $a/c+z$ to coordinates
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d053}
 z'=-\frac\delta c-\frac{\bar z}{c^2(v^2+z\bar z)},\qquad
 v'=\frac v{\N(c)(v^2+z\bar z)}.
\end{equation}
At $z=\bar z=0$, pure $\bar z$ derivatives of $\bar z'$ and $v'$
vanish, as do pure $\bar z$ derivatives of $z'$ of order at least two, while
$\partial_{\bar z}z'=-(cv)^{-2}$. Therefore the ordinary chain rule gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:chain}
 \left.\partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}F(z',\bar z',v')\right|_{z=\bar z=0}
   =(cv)^{-4}(\partial_{z'}^{\,2}F)
                  \left(-\delta/c,1/(\N(c)v)\right).
\end{equation}
No unwanted lower derivative survives. The derivative also kills any
constant Fourier term. Of the three expansions used, only the one at the
identity cusp has a nonzero constant term, and the derivative removes it.
This permits Mellin reflection without constant-term contributions.

For $V(y)=\sqrt y\,W(y)$, with Mellin transform
$\widehat V(s)=\int_0^\infty V(y)y^{s-1}\,dy$, the reflected weight is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:weight}
 V^\sharp(x)=\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{\Re t=0}\widehat V(-t)
 \frac{\Gamma(4/3+t)\Gamma(5/3+t)}{\Gamma(4/3-t)\Gamma(5/3-t)}
 \left(\frac{(2\pi)^4x}{27}\right)^{-t}dt.
\end{equation}
This is the CM version of the gamma quotient in the zeta argument.
The reflected coefficients have angular factor $\alpha(\ell)^2$;
the fixed cusp prefactor is $-\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}/81$, before
the finite Fourier coefficients and local phases are included.
The full reflected series is Proposition~\ref{prop:b3-reflection} in
Appendix~\ref{app:reflection}. The appendix also proves it
(Section~\ref{sec:b3-proof}) and gives the decay of $V^\sharp$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:b3-decay}). The main discussion can therefore focus on
the lengths and the averaging.

\subsection{The local step from sextic to quadratic}
By sextic reciprocity~\cite[\S7.3]{Lemmermeyer}, $\Psi$ is of the form
$\xi_0(n)\prod_{\pi\in\mathcal P}\chi_\pi(n)^{j_\pi}$. Here $\xi_0$ is a
character of $\mathfrak G$ (it collects $\xi$, the unit and reciprocity
factors, and the factors at $\lambda$ and $2$), $\mathcal P$ is the set
of primes dividing $kf$ away from $6$, and
$j_\pi\equiv v_\pi(k)+4v_\pi(f)\pmod6$~\cite[(6.17)]{OAI1112}.
The local reflected factors, apart from constant phases,
are~\cite[(6.5) and (A.12)]{OAI1112}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:local}
 B_{\pi,j}(x)=
 \begin{cases}
  \chi_\pi(x)^{-j-2},&j\notin\{0,4\},\\
  \N(\pi)^{-1/2}\chi_\pi(x)^{-2},&j=0,\\
  \N(\pi)^{-1/2}(-1+\N(\pi)\ind_{\pi\mid x}),&j=4.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d054}
 B_{\pi,1}(x)=\chi_\pi(x)^3=(x/\pi)_2.
\end{equation}
Away from the fixed factors of $6f$, squarefree row primes have exponent
$j=1$. These are the varying primes that become quadratic. The other
local factors are still present and have to be estimated.

If $Q=\N(c)$ is the norm of the reduced cusp denominator, the weight
in the reflected sum is $V^\sharp(\N(\ell)X/Q^2)$. Its effective norm
range is about $Q^2/X$, shorter than $X$ when $Q<X$.
By rapid decay (Lemma~\ref{lem:b3-decay}), the terms with $\N(\ell)X/Q^2\ge D^\varepsilon$ are
negligible, and since $\N(\ell)\ge3^{-4}$ the whole reflected sum is negligible once
$X\ge81D^\varepsilon Q^2$ (end of Section~\ref{sec:b3-lemmas}); the inequality $Q^2<X$ alone does not
specify a quantitative saving.

\section{The quadratic average and the completed bound}\label{sec:sieve}

The quadratic large sieve used here is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qls}
 \ssum_{\substack{\N(k)\le K\\(k,6)=1}}
 \left|\ssum_{\N(n)\le U}\beta(n)\left(\frac nk\right)_2\right|^2
 \ll_\varepsilon(KU)^\varepsilon(K+U)\ssum_n|\beta(n)|^2.
\end{equation}
Both $k,n$ are primary and squarefree, the coefficients $\beta(n)$ are
arbitrary, and the column $n$ may be even. The squarefree restrictions
matter: square columns would give repeated values $1$ rather than
cancellation. This is~\cite[Lemma~6.5]{OAI1112}, a consequence of the
number-field quadratic large sieve~\cite[Theorem~1.1]{GL}, which generalizes Heath-Brown's~\cite{HB95}; the deduction
is recorded in Section~\ref{subsec:b4-completed}.

Applying~\eqref{eq:qls} to the reflected sums, and retaining the factors
in~\eqref{eq:local}, gives the completed mean-square estimate
\begin{equation}\label{eq:completed}
 \boxed{\quad
 \sum_{0<\N(k)\le\mathcal H}|T(X;k,f)|^2
 \ll D^\varepsilon\left(\mathcal H+
                   \frac{\mathcal H^2\N(f)}X\right).
 \quad}
\end{equation}
All parameters are at least one and bounded by a fixed power of $D$;
a fixed finite smoothness norm of $W$ is understood. This is the CM form
of~\cite[Proposition~5.2]{OAI1112}. It is stated precisely as
Proposition~\ref{prop:r2-completed} at the end of this section and proved in
Section~\ref{subsec:b4-completed}.

For clarity, passing from squarefree rows to all rows uses
$k=u s v^2$, with $u$ a unit and $s$ squarefree. The identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d055}
 \chi_n(v^2)\chi_n(f)^4=\chi_n(fv^2)^4
\end{equation}
absorbs the square into the auxiliary twist. The squarefree-row bound
allows that auxiliary argument to be nonsquarefree. Its contributions
are summed using $\sum_{v}\N(v)^{-2}<\infty$.

This is the main benefit of the theta transformation: the hard sextic
average has been reduced to an average of quadratic symbols. However,
\eqref{eq:completed} bounds the completed sum, whose cube terms still
have to be removed.


% (Section 9 promises a precise statement of (9.2), "stated precisely and proved in Section 9.1", but no
%  statement with quantifiers is given; Lemma 6 (cube removal) uses (9.2) at scale X/N(h)^3 with C_0+1 in
%  place of C_0, which needs exactly this form.  Goes with edits E1 and E2.)
We record~\eqref{eq:completed} with its quantifiers. As in~\cite[Proposition~5.2]{OAI1112}, the row $k$
runs over all nonzero elements of $\OO$ in the range, and $T(X;k,f)$ is~\eqref{eq:T} with
$\Psi(n)=\xi(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4$.

\begin{proposition}[Completed estimate; CM form of {\cite[Proposition~5.2]{OAI1112}}]\label{prop:r2-completed}
Fix $\varepsilon>0$, $C_0\ge1$ and a character $\xi$ of $\mathfrak G$. There is an integer
$J=J(\varepsilon,C_0)\ge1$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d056}
 \sum_{0<\N(k)\le\mathcal H}|T(X;k,f)|^2
 \ll_{I,\xi,\varepsilon,C_0}D^\varepsilon\|W\|_{C^J(I)}^2
 \Bigl(\mathcal H+\frac{\mathcal H^2\N(f)}X\Bigr)
\end{equation}
whenever $1\le\mathcal H,X,\N(f)\le D^{C_0}$ and $f$ is primary, squarefree and coprime to $6$, for every
compact interval $I\subset(0,\infty)$ and every $W\in C_c^\infty(I)$. This is~\eqref{eq:completed}.
\end{proposition}

\subsection{Proof of the completed estimate}\label{subsec:b4-completed}

Proposition~\ref{prop:r2-completed} is the CM form of~\cite[Proposition~5.2]{OAI1112}. Its proof
in~\cite[Section~6]{OAI1112} has three steps: the quadratic large sieve~\cite[Lemma~6.5]{OAI1112},
the bound for squarefree rows~\cite[Lemma~6.6]{OAI1112}, and the passage to all rows described
after~\eqref{eq:completed}. All three apply verbatim in the CM case once the zeta transformation
formula, \cite[Proposition~6.2 and Lemmas~6.3 and~6.4]{OAI1112}, is replaced by its CM form,
Proposition~\ref{prop:b3-reflection} with Lemmas~\ref{lem:b3-uniform} and~\ref{lem:b3-decay}. The
reason is that the proof of~\cite[Lemma~6.6]{OAI1112} uses the transformation formula only through
the following properties, each of which holds in the CM case (only the implied constant in the weight
bound changes).
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Shape.} $T(X;\Psi)$ is a sum of $O(2^{|\mathcal P|})$ cusp terms. Each is a constant $C$
 with $|C|\ll1$ times a sum over $0\ne\ell\in\lambda^{-4}\OO$ of the theta coefficient $d_\sigma(\ell)$,
 a unimodular angular factor, $\N(\ell)^{-1/2}$, an additive character, the local factors and
 $V^\sharp(\N(\ell)X/\N(c)^2)$. For $\zeta$ the angular factor is $\alpha(\ell)$; in the CM case it is
 $\alpha(\ell)^2$, and $C=-\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}C'/81$ with $|C'|\le1$. The local factors and the
 additive characters are literally those of the zeta case. In the proof of~\cite[Lemma~6.6]{OAI1112}
 the row is $u_0tk_0$, with $k_0$ squarefree and prime to $6g$, and $c=c_*k_0$. Once $k_0$ is restricted
 to a residue class modulo $M^2$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:b3-uniform}), $k_0$ enters a cusp term only through the
 scalar $C$, through the quadratic factors $B_{\pi,1}(\lambda^4\ell)=\chi_\pi(\lambda^4\ell)^3$ at the
 primes $\pi\mid k_0$, and through $\N(c)=\N(c_*)\N(k_0)$ in the weight. The angular factor is a function
 of $\ell$ alone, so it goes into the coefficient $A_{u,m}(n,b)$ of that proof, which does not depend on
 $k_0$; only $|A_{u,m}(n,b)|\le27$ is used. The scalar depends on $k_0$, in the CM case also through
 $\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}$, but only $|C|\le1/81$ is used.
\item \emph{Uniformity in the twist.} Lemma~\ref{lem:b3-uniform}, which
 is~\cite[Lemma~6.3]{OAI1112}.
\item \emph{The coefficients.} $d_\sigma(\ell)=0$ unless $\ell=u\lambda^mnb^3$ with $u$ a unit, $m\ge-4$,
 $n$ squarefree and $n,b$ primary, and then $|d_\sigma(\ell)|\le27\cdot3^{m/6}|b|$. These bounds
 of~\cite[Lemma~6.4]{OAI1112} concern the theta coefficients alone, which are the same in both cases.
\item \emph{The weight.} $|(x\partial_x)^jV^\sharp(x)|\ll\|W\|_{C^J(I)}\min(x^{1/4},x^{-A})$ with
 $J=\lceil4A\rceil+j+2$. For $\zeta$ this is~\cite[Lemma~6.4]{OAI1112}, stated there with
 $\|V\|_{C^J(I)}\ll_{J,I}\|W\|_{C^J(I)}$; in the CM case it is Lemma~\ref{lem:b3-decay}. The different
 gamma factors change only the implied constant; the derivative order is that of~\cite[(A.20)]{OAI1112}.
\end{itemize}
The twists $\Psi_{k,g}(n)=\xi(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(g)^4$ are those of~\cite[Section~6.4]{OAI1112}, put in the
form~\cite[(6.17)]{OAI1112} by sextic reciprocity. In the passage
to all rows, write $k=u_0sv^2$ with $u_0$ a unit and $s$ squarefree. Then
$T(X;k,f)=T(X;u_0s,fv^2)$, and the bound for squarefree rows is applied with row bound
$\mathcal H/\N(v)^2$ and auxiliary argument $g=fv^2$. Since $\N(g)\le\mathcal H\N(f)\le D^{2C_0}$, it is
applied with $2C_0$ in place of $C_0$.

\paragraph{The large sieve.} Inequality~\eqref{eq:qls} is~\cite[Lemma~6.5]{OAI1112}, deduced there
from~\cite[Theorem~1.1]{GL}; dyadic decomposition passes from $\N(n)\asymp U$ to $\N(n)\le U$. Three
points are implicit there or only sketched. First,~\cite{GL} indexes the character by the inner
variable, so it is applied to the transposed matrix, which has the same operator norm; the bound
$(KU)^\varepsilon(K+U)$ is symmetric. Second, the reflected columns may be even (the proof
of~\cite[Lemma~6.5]{OAI1112} fixes the factors of $n$ in $S$): a squarefree primary $n$ divisible by $2$
is $n=-2n'$ with $n'$ odd and primary, and $(n/k)_2=(-2/k)_2(n'/k)_2$, where $|(-2/k)_2|=1$ depends only
on the row. Third, the family. For squarefree primary $k$ coprime to $6$ put
$\chi_{(k)}(x)=(x/k)_2\kappa_\lambda(x)^{e_k}$, with $\kappa_\lambda$ the nontrivial character modulo
$\lambda$ and $\N(k)\equiv(-1)^{e_k}\pmod4$. It is trivial on the units, so it is a primitive Hecke
character of trivial infinite type, of conductor $k\lambda^{e_k}$, and $\chi_{(k)}(n')=(n'/k)_2$ for
primary $n'$. These characters form a quadratic Hecke family in the sense of~\cite[Definition~1]{GL},
with $\mathfrak c=6\OO$. Their order divides $2$. By quadratic reciprocity in $\OO$~\cite[\S8.5]{Lemmermeyer},
$\chi_{(k)}(k')\chi_{(k')}(k)$ depends only on the classes of the primary generators $k,k'$ modulo $4$, so
the reciprocity law of~\cite[Definition~1]{GL} holds with $G=(\OO/4\OO)^\times$ and $[(k)]=k\bmod4$.
Finally, if coprime $k_1,k_2$ satisfy $k_1\equiv k_2\pmod4$, then $e_{k_1}=e_{k_2}$, so
$\chi_{(k_1)}\overline{\chi_{(k_2)}}$ is induced by $(\cdot/k_1k_2)_2$, which is primitive modulo the
squarefree $k_1k_2$.

\section{Remove the cubes, then reduce the number of rows}\label{sec:descent}

The purpose of descent is to bound the average $\E(\mathcal H,X,F)$
in~\eqref{eq:energy}. In plain terms, the completed theta estimate
handles small cube indices. The remaining large cube indices lead to
averages of exactly the same kind with fewer rows. The point requiring
care is that all the other conditions, including the smooth weights,
must survive this reduction.

One cannot discard the $b\ne1$ terms inside~\eqref{eq:T}: they could
cancel the term we want. Instead use the exact cube inversion of~\cite[Section~5.3]{OAI1112} (compare~\cite[Theorem~6.1]{Pat77} and~\cite[Section~5]{DR})
\begin{equation}\label{eq:inversion}
 \frac1{\sqrt X}\ssum_n a_\xi(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4W(\N(n)/X)
 =\sum_h\frac{\muk(h)\overline{\alpha(h)}^{\,6}\Psi(h)^3}{\N(h)}
                  T(X/\N(h)^3;k,f).
\end{equation}
Here $h$ is primary and coprime to $6$. To check the formula, substitute
\eqref{eq:T}, collect the total cube index $b'=hb$, and use
$\sum_{h\mid b'}\muk(h)=\ind_{b'=1}$. The angular and character
factors are completely multiplicative, so this cancellation is exact.

\subsection{Small cubes are controlled by the completed estimate}
Choose
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Hc}
 H_c^3=\min\left(X,\frac{X^2}{\mathcal H^2}\right),\qquad
 L_b=\frac X{\N(b)^3}.
\end{equation}
For $\N(h)\le H_c$, the completed estimate~\eqref{eq:completed} at
scale $X/\N(h)^3$ gives terms bounded by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:smallcubes}
 \mathcal H+\frac{\mathcal H^2F\N(h)^3}X\ll\Sigma,
 \qquad\Sigma=XF,
\end{equation}
provided $\mathcal H\le\Sigma$. Cauchy--Schwarz costs only divisor sums
and logarithms, which fit into $D^\varepsilon$.
Expanding the remaining completed sums in~\eqref{eq:inversion} leaves
the same average at smaller column scales. The resulting cube-removal
estimate, the CM form of~\cite[Lemma~5.3]{OAI1112} (Lemma~\ref{lem:b4-cube}), is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:remove}
 \E(\mathcal H,X,F)\ll D^\varepsilon
 \left(\Sigma+
  \sup_{\substack{\N(b)>H_c\\L_b>1}}\E(\mathcal H,L_b,F)\right).
\end{equation}
An empty supremum is zero. Scales $L_b\le1$ are bounded by counting.
If $\mathcal H^2\le X$, the supremum is empty and the desired estimate
already follows. Here and below, the suppressed dependence on $W$ is
through a fixed finite smoothness norm. More precisely, the $\Sigma$
term in~\eqref{eq:remove} is $\Sigma\|W\|_{C^{J_c}(I)}^2$ for a finite
integer $J_c$, with the same $W$ in each average in the supremum. Here
$I\subset(0,\infty)$ is a compact interval containing the support of $W$,
and $\|W\|_{C^J(I)}=\max_{0\le i\le J}\sup_{x\in I}|W^{(i)}(x)|$.

\subsection{Large cubes force a smaller row range}
Keep $\Sigma=XF$ from the original average fixed while replacing the
column length $X$ by $L=L_b$. In particular, do not reset $\Sigma$
to $LF$. The extra room $LF\le\Sigma$ is what permits the enlargement
below.
For the remaining terms, two more Poisson summations~\cite[Section~7]{OAI1112} exchange cubic
Gauss coefficients for M\"obius coefficients and then back again.
The paired identity~\eqref{eq:paired} is used in both directions, with
twists (Lemma~\ref{lem:b2-paired}).
Smooth the row sum in the explicit average
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d057}
 \frac1{LF}\ssum_{F\le\N(f)<2F}\sum_{k\in\OO}
 \Phi(\N(k)/\mathcal H)
 \left|\ssum_n a_\xi(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4W(\N(n)/L)\right|^2.
\end{equation}
The reverse paired Gauss identity converts its coefficients back to
M\"obius coefficients $\muk(n)\overline{\alpha(n)}\xi_1(n)$.
For precision, the intermediate column sums, with their exclusions, are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:P}
 P_{C,e_0,t}(y;U)=\ssum_n\muk(n)\overline{\alpha(n)}\xi_1(n)
 \ind_{(n,t)=1}\overline{\chi_n(y)}\chi_n(C)^4\chi_n(e_0)
 U(\N(n)/\ell),\quad \ell=\frac L{\N(C)\N(t)}.
\end{equation}
Here $C,t$ are coprime squarefree primary elements and $e_0\mid C$.
After separation of smooth weights, the nonnegative average used is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Q}
 \sum_{\substack{C,t\ \mathrm{squarefree}\\(C,t)=1}}
 \sum_{e_0\mid C}\frac{\mathcal H\N(C)}{\N(e_0)L^2F}
 \sum_{y\in\OO}\Phi(\N(y)/Y_{C,e_0})
                       |P_{C,e_0,t}(y;U)|^2,
\end{equation}
where the outer sum is restricted to $\N(Ct)\ll_W L$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d058}
 Y_{C,e_0}=c_*\frac{\Sigma LF\N(e_0)}{\mathcal H\N(C)^2}.
\end{equation}
The constant $c_*$ is fixed and chosen to cover the support of the weights.

Here is the crucial enlargement (compare~\cite[Lemma~9]{HB95} and~\cite[Lemma~4.4]{GL}). The first Poisson frequency $h$ has
norm about $L^2/\mathcal H$, with additional divisor factors. Combining
it with $f$ as $y=hf^2$ gives a natural M\"obius-row range of size
$L^2F^2/\mathcal H$. Suppressing the common divisor factor
$\N(e_0)/\N(C)^2$ and fixed support constants, the two scales are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:enlarge}
 Y_{\rm natural}=\frac{L^2F^2}{\mathcal H},\qquad
 Y_{\rm enlarged}=\frac{\Sigma LF}{\mathcal H},\qquad
 \frac{Y_{\rm enlarged}}{Y_{\rm natural}}=\frac{\Sigma}{LF}\ge1.
\end{equation}
After separating the smooth weights, the row sums are nonnegative mean
squares, so the larger smooth majorant gives a valid upper bound.
The enlarged range makes the second Poisson frequency range smaller.
The second Poisson summation, in $y$, uses~\eqref{eq:paired}, twisted by
$\xi_1$ as in~\eqref{eq:b2-forward}, and returns
to the cubic coefficients $a_\xi$. Its output is indexed by the common
divisor $g$ of the two M\"obius columns in $|P_{C,e_0,t}|^2$, a divisor
$e\mid g$ coming from the row exclusion, a squarefree $w$ that removes the
remaining coprimality condition, and a nonzero frequency $h_2$. These
combine into a new exclusion divisor, auxiliary divisor and row:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d059}
 r=tg/e,\qquad f'=Cew,\qquad k'=e_0eh_2,
\end{equation}
as in~\cite[proof of Lemma~7.3]{OAI1112}. For $r$ in a dyadic range
$R\le\N(r)<2R$, the new row scale is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Hprime}
 \mathcal H'=\frac{\mathcal HL}{\Sigma FR^2}.
\end{equation}
Without enlargement the corresponding scale would be
$\mathcal H/(F^2R^2)$, which need not decrease when $F=R=1$.

Regrouping the signed divisor contributions \emph{before} taking
absolute values forces a divisibility condition $f'\mid k'$.
At each prime $\pi\mid f'$, the choices $\pi\mid C$, $\pi\mid e$ and
$\pi\mid w$ contribute the three terms on the left of
\begin{equation}\label{eq:localcancel}
 (1+\ind_{\pi\mid k'})-\ind_{\pi\mid k'}
                         -\ind_{\pi\nmid k'}=\ind_{\pi\mid k'}.
\end{equation}
This divisibility keeps the next average in the ranges needed for
induction. The kernel after the second summation vanishes unless
$0<\N(k')\le\mathcal HL/(\Sigma F\N(r)^2)$. Retain only those $r$ for
which a nonzero term occurs. If $F'\le\N(f')<2F'$, then $f'\mid k'$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:retained}
 F'\le\N(f')\le\N(k')\le\frac{\mathcal HL}{\Sigma F\N(r)^2}\le\mathcal H',
\end{equation}
so $\mathcal H'\ge F'\ge1$.

The new columns are restricted by $(n,r)=1$. Put $X'=L/(RF')$, and let
$\mathcal C_r(X';k',f')$ be the column sum of~\eqref{eq:energy} with this
restriction, and $\mathcal C_1$ the unrestricted one. Inclusion--exclusion over
$j\mid r$, together with the twisted multiplicativity of $a_\xi$, gives
the exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:exclusionid}
 \mathcal C_r(X';k',f')=\sum_{\substack{j\mid r\\(j,f')=1}}\muk(j)a_\xi(j)
 \chi_j(k')\chi_j(f')^4\,\mathcal C_1\bigl(X'/\N(j);k',jf'\bigr).
\end{equation}
Indeed, writing $n=jn'$, the factor $\chi_{n'}(j)^4$ from
$a_\xi(jn')=a_\xi(j)a_\xi(n')\chi_{n'}(j)^4$ enforces $(n',j)=1$ and
combines with $\chi_{n'}(f')^4$. The factor $\overline{\alpha}^{\,2}$ in $a_\xi$ is
completely multiplicative, so this is the identity in the proof
of~\cite[Lemma~4.4]{OAI1112}, with one more factor $\overline{\alpha}$.
Cauchy--Schwarz in $j$ then bounds the restricted average by
$\tau(r)\sum_{j\mid r}\E(\mathcal H',X'/\N(j),F'\N(j))$.
Thus removing an exclusion divisor $j\mid r$ produces the replacement
\begin{equation}\label{eq:exclusionswap}
 (X',F')\longmapsto\bigl(X'/\N(j),\ F'\N(j)\bigr).
\end{equation}
It preserves both $\mathcal H'$ and the product $\Sigma'=X'F'=L/R$,
and hence the ratio $\mathcal H'/\Sigma'$. Since $\N(j)\le\N(r)$,
$R\le\N(r)$, $F\ge1$ and $\mathcal H\le\Sigma$, \eqref{eq:retained} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:childcolumn}
 \frac{X'}{\N(j)}\ge\frac{\Sigma F\N(r)^2}{\mathcal HR\N(j)}
 \ge\frac{\Sigma F}{\mathcal H}\ge1.
\end{equation}

\subsection{The precise transfer estimate}
We now state the analytic input in the form needed here. As in
Section~\ref{subsec:b2-cm}, we write $\E(\mathcal H,X,F;\xi,W)$ when the twist and weight
in~\eqref{eq:energy} need to be shown.

\begin{proposition}[Transfer estimate; CM form of {\cite[Proposition~5.4]{OAI1112}}]\label{prop:cm-transfer}
Fix $C_0\ge1$, a compact interval $I\subset(0,\infty)$,
and $W\in C_c^\infty(I)$, and let $D\ge2$. Suppose
$1\le\mathcal H,L,F,\Sigma\le D^{C_0}$, with
$\max\{\mathcal H,LF\}\le\Sigma$. Here $\Sigma$ is an independent
target scale. For every integer $m\ge0$ and $\delta>0$, with the
derivative order $J_t(m)=4m+12$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:transferprop}
 \E(\mathcal H,L,F;\xi,W)
 \ll D^\delta\Sigma\|W\|_{C^{J_t(m)}(I)}^2
 \left(1+\sup\frac{\E(\mathcal H',X',F';\xi',U)}{\Sigma'}\right).
\end{equation}
The supremum runs over averages of the same form~\eqref{eq:energy}, with
$\mathcal H',X',F'\ge1$, $\Sigma'=X'F'$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:transfer}
 \mathcal H'\le\frac{\mathcal HL}{\Sigma F},\qquad
 \frac{\mathcal H'}{\Sigma'}\le\frac{\mathcal H}{\Sigma},\qquad
 \Sigma'\le L.
\end{equation}
For $I=[u,v]\subset(0,\infty)$, the new weights satisfy
$U\in C_c^\infty([u/16,4v])$ and $\|U\|_{C^m}\le1$.
The characters $\xi'$ belong to the same fixed finite family of
residue-class twists. An empty supremum is zero. The implied constant
may depend on $m,C_0,\delta,I$ and the fixed arithmetic data, but not
on $D$ or the moving scales.
\end{proposition}

For $\zeta$ this is~\cite[Proposition~5.4]{OAI1112}, with the same
$J_t(m)=4m+12$. The source proves the stronger version with a smooth
majorant of the row sum on the left, and so do we. Its proof,
in~\cite[Section~7]{OAI1112}, is the two-Poisson argument just
outlined, including the bounds for the separated smooth kernels and
zero frequencies. In the CM case the two Poisson summations change
through the angular factors and the rest of the argument does not; the
proof is given in Section~\ref{subsec:b4-transfer}. The content of the
proposition is the uniform mean-square bound: the three scale
inequalities~\eqref{eq:transfer}, by themselves, do not establish it.

\subsection{Why the induction closes}
This subsection follows the proof of~\cite[Proposition~5.1]{OAI1112}, which follows Heath-Brown's recursive method~\cite[Lemma~8 and \S8]{HB95} (see also~\cite[Theorem~4.1]{GL}),
given in~\cite[Section~5.5]{OAI1112}; it uses only~\eqref{eq:remove},
Proposition~\ref{prop:cm-transfer} and counting, so it carries over
unchanged. Fix $\kappa>0$, $C_0\ge1$, and $\varepsilon>0$, and assume
\begin{equation}\label{eq:standing}
 1\le\mathcal H,X,F,\qquad \Sigma=XF\le D^{C_0},\qquad
 \mathcal H\le\Sigma D^{-\kappa}.
\end{equation}
The inductive assertion, for each integer $\nu\ge0$, is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:inductive}
 \E(\mathcal H,X,F;\xi,W)
 \ll D^\varepsilon\Sigma\|W\|_{C^{J_\nu}(I)}^2
 \qquad\text{whenever }\mathcal H\le D^{\nu\kappa}.
\end{equation}
This assertion is made for every $\varepsilon>0$ and every compact
interval $I\subset(0,\infty)$. The finite derivative order $J_\nu$ may
depend on $\nu,\kappa,C_0,\varepsilon$, but not on $D$ or $I$; the implied
constant may also depend on $I$. This formulation allows the support
interval to enlarge at a transfer step.

\paragraph{Base case.}
If $\nu=0$, then $\mathcal H\le1$, so there are at most six nonzero
rows, the units. There are $O(F)$ choices of $f$ and $O_I(X)$ column
terms, each of absolute value at most $\|W\|_\infty$, since
$|a_\xi(n)|\le1$. The normalization in~\eqref{eq:energy} therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:basecase}
 \E(\mathcal H,X,F;\xi,W)
 \ll_I\frac{F}{XF}(X\|W\|_\infty)^2
 =X\|W\|_\infty^2\le\Sigma\|W\|_\infty^2.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Induction step.}
Assume the assertion at level $\nu$, and let
$\mathcal H\le D^{(\nu+1)\kappa}$. Use that assertion with loss
$\varepsilon/3$, and let $m$ be its required derivative order.
Apply cube removal~\eqref{eq:remove} and
Proposition~\ref{prop:cm-transfer}, each with loss $\varepsilon/3$.
If the cube-removal supremum is empty, its $\Sigma$ term already
suffices. Otherwise $\N(b)^3>X^2/\mathcal H^2$.
For each remaining $L=L_b$ we have $LF\le\Sigma$, so the transfer
estimate applies with the unchanged target $\Sigma=XF$. Every child
average satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:contract}
 \mathcal H'\le\frac{\mathcal H}{\N(b)^3F^2}
 <\mathcal H\left(\frac{\mathcal H}{XF}\right)^2
 \le\mathcal H D^{-2\kappa}.
\end{equation}
In particular, $\mathcal H'\le D^{\nu\kappa}$. The other hypotheses survive
as well:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:survive}
 \frac{\mathcal H'}{\Sigma'}\le\frac{\mathcal H}{\Sigma}
 \le D^{-\kappa},\qquad
 \Sigma'\le L\le\Sigma\le D^{C_0},\qquad
 \mathcal H',X',F'\ge1.
\end{equation}
The induction hypothesis applies to the child weight $U$ on
$[u/16,4v]$. Since $\|U\|_{C^m}\le1$, it gives the uniform bound
\begin{equation}\label{eq:childbound}
 \sup\frac{\E(\mathcal H',X',F';\xi',U)}{\Sigma'}
 \ll D^{\varepsilon/3}.
\end{equation}
The transfer contributes a second factor $D^{\varepsilon/3}$ and
cube removal a third. Taking $J_{\nu+1}\ge\max\{J_t(m),J_c\}$ proves the
assertion at level $\nu+1$. Thus both the derivative requirement and
the total $D^\varepsilon$ loss are controlled.

Finally take $\nu=\lceil C_0/\kappa\rceil$; this covers every allowed
$\mathcal H$. The number of induction levels depends on $C_0,\kappa$,
not on $D$. Suppressing the resulting fixed smoothness norm again,
we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:finalenergy}
 \E(\mathcal H,X,F)\ll D^\varepsilon\Sigma
 \qquad(\mathcal H\le\Sigma D^{-\kappa}).
\end{equation}
No estimate for zeros of $L(E_d,s)$ or of $\zeta$ is assumed in this
induction: its inputs are cube removal and the transfer estimate, and
its starting case is counting. In the CM case these inputs, and the
completed estimate~\eqref{eq:completed} on which cube removal rests, are
proved in Sections~\ref{subsec:b4-completed}, \ref{subsec:b4-cube}
and~\ref{subsec:b4-transfer}. Their analytic inputs are the CM theta
transformation (Proposition~\ref{prop:b3-reflection}, with
Lemmas~\ref{lem:b3-uniform} and~\ref{lem:b3-decay}) and the quadratic large
sieve~\cite{GL}.

It remains to check the initial scales~\eqref{eq:initial}. With $H=D^{1+\vartheta}$, they
satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:initialratio}
 \frac{\mathcal H}{\Sigma}\ll\frac{D^{-\vartheta}}B.
\end{equation}
For sufficiently large $D$ this is at most $D^{-\vartheta/2}$.
Thus~\eqref{eq:finalenergy} applies with $\kappa=\vartheta/2$.
Empty ranges and bounded column scales are handled by counting.
The first Poisson reduction, Proposition~\ref{prop:b2-reduction}, gives~\eqref{eq:MS},
with the constant controlled by a finite smoothness norm of $W$, and
Section~\ref{sec:zeros} then gives the zero-free half-plane.

\paragraph{What changes from the zeta guide.}
The architecture is the same, but its angular factors change coherently:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d060}
\begin{array}{c|cc}
 &\text{zeta guide}&\text{present CM guide}\\\hline
 \text{original coefficients}&\muk(n)&\muk(n)\alpha(n)\\
 \text{cubic coefficients}&\overline\alpha\gamma_2&\overline\alpha^{\,2}\gamma_2\\
 \text{theta derivative}&\partial_{\bar z}&\partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}\\
 \text{angular factor on cubes}&\overline{\alpha(b)}^3&\overline{\alpha(b)}^6\\
 \text{dual angular factor}&\alpha(\ell)&\alpha(\ell)^2
\end{array}
\end{equation}
These are genuine identities to check, not consequences of the factors
having absolute value one. Once checked, the same large-sieve and descent
estimates apply.


\subsection{Proof of cube removal}\label{subsec:b4-cube}

\begin{lemma}[Cube removal; CM form of {\cite[Lemma~5.3]{OAI1112}}]\label{lem:b4-cube}
Fix $C_0\ge1$ and $\varepsilon>0$, and let $1\le\mathcal H,X,F\le D^{C_0}$ with
$\mathcal H\le\Sigma=XF$. There is $J_c=J_c(\varepsilon,C_0)$ such that, for every character $\xi$
of $\mathfrak G$, compact $I=[u,v]\subset(0,\infty)$ and $W\in C_c^\infty(I)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d061}
 \E(\mathcal H,X,F;\xi,W)\ll_{I,\varepsilon,C_0}D^\varepsilon\Bigl(\Sigma\|W\|_{C^{J_c}(I)}^2+
 \sup_{\substack{\N(b)>H_c\\L_b>1}}\E(\mathcal H,L_b,F;\xi,W)\Bigr),
\end{equation}
with $H_c$ and $L_b$ as in~\eqref{eq:Hc} and $b$ primary and coprime to $6$. An empty supremum is
zero, and the supremum is empty if $\mathcal H^2\le X$. This is~\eqref{eq:remove}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The proof of~\cite[Lemma~5.3]{OAI1112} applies verbatim, with~\eqref{eq:completed} in place
of~\cite[Proposition~5.2]{OAI1112} (used at scale $X/\N(h)^3$ with $C_0+1$ in place of $C_0$,
since $\N(f)<2D^{C_0}$). The angular factor on the cube indices, $\overline\alpha^{\,6}$ instead of
$\overline\alpha^{\,3}$ (see~\eqref{eq:inversion} and~\eqref{eq:T}), enters that proof only in two
ways. It is completely multiplicative, so that
$\overline{\alpha(h)}^{\,6}\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,6}=\overline{\alpha(hc)}^{\,6}$ when the cube
indices $h$ of~\eqref{eq:inversion} and $c$ of~\eqref{eq:T} are collected into $b=hc$. And it has
absolute value one, which is all that the Cauchy--Schwarz steps use.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of the transfer estimate}\label{subsec:b4-transfer}

Throughout, $\mathcal H,L,F,\Sigma$, $I=[u,v]$ and $\xi$ are as in Proposition~\ref{prop:cm-transfer},
and $I_*=[u/2,2v]$, $I'=[u/16,4v]$. Let $\Phi$ be the row weight of Section~\ref{sec:poisson},
normalized so that $\Phi\ge1$ on $[0,1]$, and let $C_\Phi$ be the constant of
Section~\ref{subsec:b2-cm}, so that $\widehat\Phi(t)=0$ for $t>C_\Phi$. Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b4-A}
 \mathcal A(W)=\frac1{LF}\ssum_{F\le\N(f)<2F}\sum_{k\in\OO}\Phi\Bigl(\frac{\N(k)}{\mathcal H}\Bigr)
 \Bigl|\ssum_na_\xi(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4W\Bigl(\frac{\N(n)}L\Bigr)\Bigr|^2,
\end{equation}
the smoothed average displayed before~\eqref{eq:P}; thus $\E(\mathcal H,L,F;\xi,W)\le\mathcal A(W)$.
For coprime squarefree $C,t$ and $e_0\mid C$ put $\ell=L/(\N(C)\N(t))$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d062}
 w_{C,e_0}=\frac{\mathcal H\N(C)}{\N(e_0)L^2F},\qquad
 Y_{C,e_0}=c_*\frac{\Sigma LF\N(e_0)}{\mathcal H\N(C)^2},\qquad c_*=\max(1,4C_\Phi v^2).
\end{equation}
For $U\in C_c^\infty(I_*)$ and a character $\xi_1$ of $\mathfrak G$, let $P_{C,e_0,t}(y;U)$ be as
in~\eqref{eq:P}, and let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b4-Q}
 \mathcal Q_{\xi_1}(U)=\sum_{\substack{C,t\ \mathrm{squarefree},\ (C,t)=1\\\N(C)\N(t)\le2vL}}
 \sum_{e_0\mid C}w_{C,e_0}\sum_{y\in\OO}\Phi\Bigl(\frac{\N(y)}{Y_{C,e_0}}\Bigr)|P_{C,e_0,t}(y;U)|^2,
\end{equation}
which is~\eqref{eq:Q} written out. The proof follows \cite[Section~7]{OAI1112}. The angular factors
enter only through the two Poisson summations (Lemmas~\ref{lem:b4-first}
and~\ref{lem:b4-second-id}), which we give in full. They use the paired identities with twists of
Lemma~\ref{lem:b2-paired}: \eqref{eq:b2-reverse} in the first and~\eqref{eq:b2-forward} in the
second. The CM changes are the factor $\overline{\alpha(z_1)}\alpha(z_2)$ there and the
coefficients $a_\xi=\overline\alpha^{\,2}\gamma_2\xi$.

\begin{lemma}[First Poisson summation; CM form of {\cite[Lemma~7.1]{OAI1112}}]\label{lem:b4-first}
Fix an integer $J_1\ge0$ and $\varepsilon>0$. If $M\ge0$ satisfies
$\mathcal Q_{\xi_1}(U)\le M\|U\|_{C^{J_1}(I_*)}^2$ for every $U\in C_c^\infty(I_*)$ and every character
$\xi_1$, then $\mathcal A(W)\ll D^\varepsilon(\Sigma+M)\|W\|_{C^{2J_1+4}(I)}^2$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Expand the square in~\eqref{eq:b4-A} with $n_1$ unconjugated and $n_2$ conjugated, and write
$C=(n_1,n_2)$ and $n_i=Cu_i$, so that $(u_1,u_2)=(u_1u_2,C)=1$. By~\eqref{eq:b2-twisted} and $|a_\xi(C)|=1$,
$a_\xi(n_1)\overline{a_\xi(n_2)}=a_\xi(u_1)\overline{a_\xi(u_2)}\chi_{u_1}(C)^4\overline{\chi_{u_2}(C)^4}$.
Also $\chi_{n_1}(k)\overline{\chi_{n_2}(k)}=\ind_{(k,C)=1}\chi_{u_1}(k)\overline{\chi_{u_2}(k)}$ and
$\chi_{n_1}(f)^4\overline{\chi_{n_2}(f)^4}=\ind_{(C,f)=1}\chi_{u_1}(f)^4\overline{\chi_{u_2}(f)^4}$.
The row character $\chi_{u_1}\overline{\chi_{u_2}}$ is primitive modulo $u_1u_2$, so the sum over
$k$ with weight $\Phi(\N(k)/\mathcal H)\ind_{(k,C)=1}$ is given by~\eqref{eq:poisson-exact} with
$\psi=\chi_{u_1}\overline{\chi_{u_2}}$, $(z_1,z_2,g)=(u_1,u_2,C)$ and $\mathcal H$ in place of $H$.
The frequency $h=0$ contributes only when $u_1=u_2=1$, and these terms give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d063}
 Z=\frac{\mathcal H\widehat\Phi(0)}{LF}\ssum_{F\le\N(f)<2F}\ssum_{(C,f)=1}
 \Bigl|W\Bigl(\frac{\N(C)}L\Bigr)\Bigr|^2\prod_{\pi\mid C}\Bigl(1-\frac1{\N(\pi)}\Bigr)
 \ll_I\mathcal H\|W\|_\infty^2\le\Sigma\|W\|_\infty^2 .
\end{equation}
For $h\ne0$ apply~\eqref{eq:b2-reverse} at $(u_1,u_2)$; here the CM coefficients
$\muk(u)\overline{\alpha(u)}\xi_1(u)$ appear. The characters at $u_1$ coming from $h$, $e_0$, $C$
and $f$ combine as
$\overline{\chi_{u_1}(h)}\chi_{u_1}(e_0)\chi_{u_1}(C)^4\chi_{u_1}(f)^4
=\overline{\chi_{u_1}(hf^2)}\chi_{u_1}(C)^4\chi_{u_1}(e_0)$, since $\chi^4=\overline\chi^{\,2}$
with the zero-value convention; those at $u_2$ are the complex conjugates of the same expression
at $u_2$. Insert $\ind_{(u_1,u_2)=1}=\sum_{t\mid u_1,\,t\mid u_2}\muk(t)$ and write $u_i=tx_i$.
Squarefreeness gives $(x_i,t)=1$, and no condition $(x_1,x_2)=1$ remains. The factors at $t$ have
product $|\overline{\alpha(t)}\xi_1(t)\chi_t(hf^2)\chi_t(C)^4\chi_t(e_0)|^2=\ind_{(t,Chf)=1}$, and
$\muk(u_1)\muk(u_2)=\muk(x_1)\muk(x_2)$. With $\N(u_1u_2)=\N(t)^2\N(x_1)\N(x_2)$, $\N(t)\ell=L/\N(C)$
and the factor $1/(LF)$, this gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b4-first-id}
\begin{split}
 \mathcal A(W)-Z=\sum_{\xi_1}\ell_{\xi_1}(\xi)\sum_{\substack{C,t\\(C,t)=1}}\sum_{e_0\mid C}
 &\muk(e_0)\muk(t)w_{C,e_0}\ssum_{\substack{F\le\N(f)<2F\\(f,Ct)=1}}\ \sum_{\substack{h\ne0\\(h,t)=1}}\\
 &\times\ssum_{x_1,x_2}p_{hf^2}(x_1)\overline{p_{hf^2}(x_2)}\,
 \mathcal K_h\Bigl(\frac{\N(x_1)}\ell,\frac{\N(x_2)}\ell\Bigr),
\end{split}
\end{equation}
where $C,t$ are squarefree with $\N(C)\N(t)\le vL$,
$p_y(x)=\muk(x)\overline{\alpha(x)}\xi_1(x)\ind_{(x,t)=1}\overline{\chi_x(y)}\chi_x(C)^4\chi_x(e_0)$
are the coefficients of~\eqref{eq:P}, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d064}
 \mathcal K_h(z_1,z_2)=(z_1z_2)^{-1/2}W(z_1)\overline{W(z_2)}
 \widehat\Phi\Bigl(\frac{\mathcal H\N(h)\N(C)^2}{L^2\N(e_0)z_1z_2}\Bigr),\qquad
 \sup_{C,e_0,h}\|\mathcal K_h\|_{C^q(I^2)}\ll_{I,q}\|W\|_{C^q(I)}^2.
\end{equation}
The factor $\overline{\alpha(x)}$ in $p_y$ is the only CM change; in \cite[(7.1)]{OAI1112} the
coefficient is $\muk(x)\xi_1(x)$. The support of $\widehat\Phi$ forces
$\N(h)\le C_\Phi v^2L^2\N(e_0)/(\mathcal H\N(C)^2)$, so $y=hf^2$ satisfies
$\N(y)\le4C_\Phi v^2L^2F^2\N(e_0)/(\mathcal H\N(C)^2)\le Y_{C,e_0}$, because $LF\le\Sigma$. This is
the enlargement~\eqref{eq:enlarge}. For fixed $C,e_0,t$ the map $(f,h)\mapsto hf^2$ has
multiplicity at most $\tau(y)\ll D^\varepsilon$, and $\Phi\ge1$ on $[0,1]$, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d065}
 \sum_{C,t}\sum_{e_0\mid C}w_{C,e_0}\sum_{f,h}|P_{C,e_0,t}(hf^2;U)|^2
 \ll D^\varepsilon\mathcal Q_{\xi_1}(U)\le D^\varepsilon M\|U\|_{C^{J_1}(I_*)}^2 .
\end{equation}
Now \cite[Lemma~B.2]{OAI1112}, with rows $(C,t,e_0,f,h)$, weights $w_{C,e_0}$, coefficients
$\muk(e_0)\muk(t)w_{C,e_0}$, both column sums equal to $P_{C,e_0,t}(hf^2;\cdot)$, and the kernels
$\mathcal K_h$ supported in $I^2\subset\operatorname{int}I_*^{\,2}$, gives
$|\mathcal A(W)-Z|\ll D^\varepsilon M\|W\|_{C^{2J_1+4}(I)}^2$, since there are finitely many $\xi_1$
and $|\ell_{\xi_1}(\xi)|\le1$.
\end{proof}

For the second Poisson summation, fix coprime squarefree $C,t$, $e_0\mid C$, a character $\xi_1$,
scales $\ell,Y>0$, and $U\in C_c^\infty(I_*)$ with $I_*=[a_*,b_*]$. Put $P(y)=P_{C,e_0,t}(y;U)$
with this $\ell$, $\mathcal M=\sum_{y\in\OO}\Phi(\N(y)/Y)|P(y)|^2$, and $U_0(x)=x^{-1/2}U(x)$.

\begin{lemma}[Second Poisson identity; CM form of {\cite[Lemma~7.2]{OAI1112}}]\label{lem:b4-second-id}
With $c_{\xi'}(\xi_1)$ as in Lemma~\ref{lem:b2-paired},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d066}
 \mathcal M=Z'+\sum_{g,e,w,h_2,\xi'}\frac{Y\muk(e)\muk(w)c_{\xi'}(\xi_1)\N(g)}{\N(e)\ell}
 \ssum_{n_1,n_2}a^\sharp(n_1)\overline{a^\sharp(n_2)}\,
 \mathcal K\Bigl(\frac{\N(n_1)}{X_0'},\frac{\N(n_2)}{X_0'}\Bigr).
\end{equation}
Here $g,w$ are squarefree, $e\mid g$ and $0\ne h_2\in\OO$, subject to $(g,Ct)=(w,gCth_2)=1$,
$\N(gw)\le b_*\ell$ and $\N(h_2)\le C_\Phi b_*^2\ell^2\N(e)/(Y\N(g)^2)$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d201}\begin{gathered}
 X_0'=\frac\ell{\N(gw)},\qquad
 a^\sharp(n)=a_{\xi'}(n)\ind_{(n,tg/e)=1}\chi_n(e_0eh_2)\chi_n(Cew)^4,\\
 \mathcal K(x_1,x_2)=U_0(x_1)\overline{U_0(x_2)}
 \widehat\Phi\Bigl(\frac{Y\N(h_2)\N(g)^2}{\N(e)\ell^2x_1x_2}\Bigr).
\end{gathered}\end{equation}
The zero-frequency term is
$Z'=Y\widehat\Phi(0)\ssum_{(g,Ct)=1}|U(\N(g)/\ell)|^2\prod_{\pi\mid g}(1-\N(\pi)^{-1})\ll_{I_*}Y\ell\|U\|_\infty^2$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Expand $|P(y)|^2$ with $n_1$ from $P$ and $n_2$ from $\overline P$, put $g=(n_1,n_2)$ and
$n_i=gz_i$. Nonzero terms have $(z_1,z_2)=(z_1z_2,gCt)=(g,Ct)=1$, where $(g,C)=1$ comes from
$|\chi_g(C^4e_0)|^2$. Moreover
$\overline{\chi_{n_1}(y)}\chi_{n_2}(y)=\ind_{(y,g)=1}\overline{\chi_{z_1}(y)}\chi_{z_2}(y)$. Apply
\cite[Lemma~21]{QF} with $\psi=\overline{\chi_{z_1}}\chi_{z_2}$, primitive modulo $z_1z_2$, and
exclusion $g$. It introduces $e\mid g$ and a frequency $h_2$, and its zero frequency occurs only
for $z_1=z_2=1$, giving $Z'$. The coefficient of $n_1$ is $\muk(n_1)\overline{\alpha(n_1)}\xi_1(n_1)$
and that of $n_2$ is its complex conjugate. Since $\muk(g)^2=|\alpha(g)|^2=|\xi_1(g)|^2=1$, their
product is $\muk(z_1)\muk(z_2)\overline{\alpha(z_1)}\alpha(z_2)\xi_1(z_1)\overline{\xi_1(z_2)}$.
So for $h_2\ne0$,~\eqref{eq:b2-forward} replaces it, together with $\gamma(\psi)$, by
$\sum_{\xi'}c_{\xi'}(\xi_1)a_{\xi'}(z_1)\overline{a_{\xi'}(z_2)}$. This is the only CM change: in
\cite[proof of Lemma~7.2]{OAI1112} the M\"obius coefficient is $\muk\xi_1$ and the cubic
coefficient is $\overline\alpha\gamma_2\xi'$. The characters combine as
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d067}
 \psi(e)\overline{\psi(h_2)}\chi_{z_1}(C^4e_0)\overline{\chi_{z_2}(C^4e_0)}
 =\chi_{z_1}(e_0eh_2)\chi_{z_1}(Ce)^4\,\overline{\chi_{z_2}(e_0eh_2)\chi_{z_2}(Ce)^4},
\end{equation}
since $\overline{\chi_z(e)}=\chi_z(e)^5$. Insert $\ind_{(z_1,z_2)=1}=\sum_{w\mid z_1,\,w\mid z_2}\muk(w)$
and write $z_i=wn_i$; then $(w,gCt)=1$. By~\eqref{eq:b2-twisted},
$a_{\xi'}(wn_i)=a_{\xi'}(w)a_{\xi'}(n_i)\chi_{n_i}(w)^4$. The factors at $w$ have product
$|a_{\xi'}(w)\chi_w(e_0eh_2)\chi_w(Ce)^4|^2=\ind_{(w,h_2)=1}$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d068}
 \ind_{(n,gCtw)=1}\chi_n(e_0eh_2)\chi_n(Cew)^4=\ind_{(n,tg/e)=1}\chi_n(e_0eh_2)\chi_n(Cew)^4.
\end{equation}
Finally
$U(\N(gz_1)/\ell)\overline{U(\N(gz_2)/\ell)}/\sqrt{\N(z_1z_2)}
=\frac{\N(g)}\ell U_0(x_1)\overline{U_0(x_2)}$ with $x_i=\N(gz_i)/\ell=\N(n_i)/X_0'$, the Poisson
factor is $Y\muk(e)/\N(e)$, and the argument of $\widehat\Phi$ is
$Y\N(h_2)/(\N(e)\N(z_1)\N(z_2))=Y\N(h_2)\N(g)^2/(\N(e)\ell^2x_1x_2)$. The support conditions follow
from those of $U$ and $\widehat\Phi$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Second transfer; CM form of {\cite[Lemma~7.3]{OAI1112}}]\label{lem:b4-second}
For an integer $m\ge0$, let $S_m$ be the supremum in~\eqref{eq:transferprop}: over characters
$\xi'$, parameters $\mathcal H',X',F'\ge1$ satisfying~\eqref{eq:transfer} with $\Sigma'=X'F'$, and
$U\in C_c^\infty(I')$ with $\|U\|_{C^m(I')}\le1$. Then, for every $\varepsilon>0$ and $U\in
C_c^\infty(I_*)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d069}
 \mathcal Q_{\xi_1}(U)\ll D^\varepsilon\Sigma(1+S_m)\|U\|_{C^{2m+4}(I_*)}^2 .
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The proof of \cite[Lemma~7.3]{OAI1112} applies verbatim, with \cite[Lemma~7.2]{OAI1112} replaced by
Lemma~\ref{lem:b4-second-id} and \cite[Lemma~4.4]{OAI1112} replaced by its CM form~\eqref{eq:b2-remove}.
The coefficients $a_{\xi'}$ enter only through these two inputs, and the steps are the ones described
after~\eqref{eq:enlarge}: the preimage regrouping~\eqref{eq:localcancel}, which gives the factor
$\tau(r)\ind_{f'\mid k'}$; the retained support~\eqref{eq:retained} and~\eqref{eq:childcolumn}; exclusion
removal~\eqref{eq:exclusionswap}; and~\cite[Lemma~B.2]{OAI1112} for the kernels.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:cm-transfer}.} Lemma~\ref{lem:b4-second} supplies the
hypothesis of Lemma~\ref{lem:b4-first} with $J_1=2m+4$ and $M\ll D^{\delta/2}\Sigma(1+S_m)$. Then
Lemma~\ref{lem:b4-first} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d070}
 \E(\mathcal H,L,F;\xi,W)\le\mathcal A(W)\ll D^{\delta}\Sigma(1+S_m)\|W\|_{C^{4m+12}(I)}^2,
\end{equation}
which is~\eqref{eq:transferprop} with $J_t(m)=4m+12$, as for $\zeta$, and in the stronger form with
the smooth majorant $\mathcal A(W)$ on the left. \qed

%======================== PART II: THE 7/8 BOUNDARY ========================
\clearpage
\section{Part II: the \texorpdfstring{$7/8$}{7/8} boundary for the same curves}\label{sec:78}

The remaining sections adapt OpenAI's $7/8$ argument~\cite{OAI78}, explained
for $\zeta$ in~\cite{G78}, to the curves $E_d$. The result is a wider
zero-free strip. Equation and line references to~\cite{OAI78} are to its
\TeX\ source. Throughout Part~II, $\mu=\muk$ is the M\"obius function on primary
elements, and $\chi_\bullet(u)$ denotes the character $n\mapsto\chi_n(u)$.

\begin{theorem}[see Section~\ref{sec:78status} for its status]\label{thm:78}
Let $\eta$ be a finite-order Hecke character of $K=\mathbb Q(\sqrt{-3})$, and put
$\psi=\overline\alpha\,\eta$, that is, $\psi(n)=\overline{\alpha(n)}\,\eta(n)$ on
primary $n$ prime to the conductor of $\eta$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d071}
 L(s,\psi)\ne0\qquad(\Re s>7/8).
\end{equation}
In particular, for every nonzero integer $d$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d072}
 L(E_d,s)\ne0\qquad(\Re s>11/8),
\end{equation}
a zero-free strip $11/8<\Re s<3/2$ along the right edge of the critical strip.
\end{theorem}

\paragraph{How the curves enter.} Put $\psi_d^*(n)=\overline{\alpha(n)}\chi_n(4d)$ for primary $n$.
If $p\nmid6d$ splits as $p=\pi\bar\pi$ with $\pi$ primary, then
$\sqrt p\,\bigl(\psi_d^*(\pi)+\psi_d^*(\bar\pi)\bigr)=a_p(d)$; if $p\nmid6d$ and $p\equiv2\pmod3$,
then $\psi_d^*(-p)=-1$, matching $a_p(d)=0$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:b1-points}). Since $\psi_d^*$ and the
character $\psi_d$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:b1-euler} give the same Euler factors (see below), that lemma gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d073}
 L(E_d,s+\tfrac12)=B_d(s)\sum_n\frac{\psi_d^*(n)}{\N(n)^s},
\end{equation}
with $B_d$ holomorphic and nonzero in $\Re s>0$. (We also checked both statements numerically for
$2352$ split pairs $(d,p)$, testing both primes above each $p$, and $854$ inert pairs.)
The character $\psi_d^*$ is of the type in Theorem~\ref{thm:78}. Indeed $n\mapsto\overline{\alpha(n)}$, on primary
$n$, is a Hecke character modulo $3$ with infinity type $\bar z/|z|$, and $n\mapsto\chi_n(4d)$ is a finite-order
ray character whose conductor is supported on the primes dividing $6d$~\cite[Lemma~4.1]{OAI78}.
So $\psi_d^*=\overline\alpha\eta_d$ with $\eta_d$ of finite order, and its primitive inducing character $\psi_d'$
lies in the family $\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$ defined below. Moreover
$\sum_n\psi_d^*(n)\N(n)^{-s}=L(s,\psi_d')\prod_{\mathfrak p\mid6d}\bigl(1-\psi_d'(\mathfrak p)\N\mathfrak p^{-s}\bigr)$,
and each factor is nonzero in $\Re s>0$ because $|\psi_d'(\mathfrak p)|\le1$. Hence $L(s,\psi_d')\ne0$ for
$\Re s>7/8$ implies $L(E_d,s)\ne0$ for $\Re s>11/8$.
The character $\psi_d^*$ is the complex conjugate of the character
$\psi_d=\alpha\,\overline{\chi_\bullet(4d)}=\alpha\,\chi_\bullet\bigl((4d)^5\bigr)$ used in
Sections~\ref{sec:lattice}--\ref{sec:descent}. The two have the same
$L$-function: since $4d$ is rational, $\psi_d^*(\bar\pi)=\overline{\psi_d^*(\pi)}$, so both
characters give the same Euler factor at each split prime, and $\psi_d^*(-p)=-1$ is real.
More generally, every unitary Hecke character of $K$ whose infinity type is
$(\bar z/|z|)^{\pm1}$ is of the form $\overline\alpha\eta$ or its conjugate
$\alpha\bar\eta$. So the theorem covers every weight-two newform with complex
multiplication by $K$~\cite{Ribet77}. In particular it covers every elliptic curve over
$\mathbb Q$ with complex multiplication by an order of $K$ (the $j$-invariants
$0$, $54000$ and $-12288000$; see~\cite{Cox89}): by Deuring's theorem~\cite{Deuring} the $L$-function of such a curve is $L(s,\psi)$ for a Hecke
character $\psi$ of $K$ of this infinity type, also when the order is not maximal~\cite[Chapter~II, \S10, and
Appendix~A, \S3]{SilATAEC}.

\paragraph{What ``proof'' means in Part II.} We do not rewrite the
$16{,}700$ lines of~\cite{OAI78}. Instead we do three things:
\begin{enumerate}
\item we prove the identities in which the angular factor changes a
 computation, and test them numerically;
\item we state each ingredient of~\cite{OAI78} in the form it takes for a CM
 target;
\item we checked, section by section, that each proof in~\cite{OAI78} uses only
 properties that CM characters share with finite-order characters, or we record
 the change.
\end{enumerate}
Section~\ref{sec:78status} lists the statements that needed new proofs; they are proved, at the
generality of~\cite{OAI78}, in Appendices~\ref{app:s1-general}--\ref{app:s6}.

\subsection{The family and the number \texorpdfstring{$\beta_*$}{beta*}}
Let $\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$ be the set of primitive Hecke characters of $K$ with infinity type
$\bar z/|z|$, that is, the primitive characters inducing $\overline\alpha\eta$ with $\eta$ of finite
order. Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d074}
 \beta_*=\sup\bigl(\{1/2\}\cup\{\Re\rho:\ L(\rho,\psi)=0,\ 1/2\le\Re\rho\le1,\
 \psi\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}\}\bigr).
\end{equation}
Three properties make this family suitable.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Closure under finite-order twists.} If $\psi\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$ and $\theta$
 has finite order, then the primitive character inducing $\psi\theta$ lies in
 $\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$. Every denominator row below is such a twist.
\item \emph{Conjugation.} $\overline\psi=\alpha\bar\eta$ and
 $L(s,\overline\psi)=\overline{L(\bar s,\psi)}$, so conjugates contribute no new real parts
 of zeros.
\item \emph{No poles.} Every $L(s,\psi)$ with $\psi\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$ is entire. No
 member is principal, so the principal-character clauses of~\cite{OAI78} disappear for the target and
 the denominators. They remain for the finite-order numerators: the row $u=1$, and the rows supported
 on $S$, which the detector removes as in~\cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78}.
\end{itemize}
The rows of the argument also contain finite-order $L$-functions. For them we do
not use $\beta_*$. Instead we use OpenAI's theorem~\cite[Theorem~1.1]{OAI78}:
they have no zeros in $\Re s>7/8$. So, under the contradiction hypothesis
$\beta_*>7/8$, every target, numerator and row $L$-function whose zeros enter the
detector or logarithmic control has its zeros in $\Re s\le\beta_*$.
In~\cite{OAI78} this was automatic from the definition of $\beta_*$. Here the finite-order theorem is an
input in two ways: wherever~\cite{OAI78} uses that a finite-order row or witness character has no zeros to
the right of $\beta_*$ (the bin ceiling of the detector, Lemma~\ref{lem:r3-ceiling}, and the global part of the
logarithmic control for the numerators and for the finite-order members of the label sets), and in the zero-free
hypothesis for the slot characters in the plain moment (Section~\ref{sec:78moments}).
There is no circularity: the zero-location bounds used to prove OpenAI's theorem
concern finite-order $L$-functions, and do not assume a CM zero-free strip.
Its theta completion also contains angular auxiliary series, for example
$L_0(s,\Psi)=\sum_b\overline{\alpha(b)}^{\,3}\Psi(b)^3\N(b)^{-3s+1/2}$
\cite[Proposition~5.1]{OAI78}; their zeros are not used in that proof.

The continuation criterion~\cite[Prop.~2.1]{OAI78}
(see~\cite[Proposition~1]{G78}) holds verbatim with $\eta$ replaced by $\psi$ and the family of primitive
finite-order characters replaced by $\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$. Besides its hypotheses on $H_\psi$ (holomorphic on
$\Re s>\sigma_0$ with $|H_\psi-1|\le1/2$; here $H_\psi$ comes from the principal row,
Proposition~\ref{prop:cm-rows}), its proof uses only:
\begin{itemize}
\item the definition of $\beta_*$ as a supremum over $\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$;
\item absolute convergence of the Euler product of $L^S(s,\psi)$ in $\Re s>1$, so that $1/L^S(s,\psi)$ is
 holomorphic there, and the bound $|1/L^S(s,\psi)|\le\zeta_K(2)$ on $\Re s\ge2$;
\item the continuation of $L(s,\psi)$ to $\Re s>\sigma_0$ (it is entire), so that a zero of $L(s,\psi)$ is a
 pole of $1/L^S(s,\psi)$;
\item the nonvanishing of finitely many Euler factors in $\Re s>0$.
\end{itemize}
It therefore suffices, for each $\psi\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$, to construct a function
$J_\psi(Z)$ with the low and high estimates of the criterion, for $\sigma_0=7/8$ and
$C(s)=s-11/16$, with margins independent of $\psi$.

The construction is that of~\cite{OAI78}, with three main changes:
\begin{itemize}
\item the weight of the target (Section~\ref{sec:78probe});
\item the bookkeeping of the characters attached to rows
 (Section~\ref{sec:78rows});
\item an angular factor in the moment estimates
 (Section~\ref{sec:78moments}).
\end{itemize}
There are also three smaller ones: OpenAI's theorem for finite-order characters~\cite[Theorem~1.1]{OAI78}
is an input for the finite-order rows and slots; the capacity parameter is fixed at $\kappa=3/4$, with the bin
ceiling $\delta\le2\beta_*-1$ kept separate from it; and the slots are measured against the finite-order part of
the row character (Section~\ref{sec:78rows}). Section~\ref{sec:78final} checks that the final inequality holds
with these changes.

\paragraph{The arithmetic datum.} \cite{OAI78} fixes for each target a datum (\cite[Section~4.1]{OAI78}): a finite set
$S$ of primes containing those above $6$, and complete zero-extended presentations of finitely many
\emph{finite-order} ray characters with moduli supported on $S$, together with the finite ray group presenting
them. Every ``fixed character'' of~\cite{OAI78} belongs to this datum, and constants may depend on it. A CM target
$\psi=\overline\alpha\eta$ is not of this kind. We put the finite part $\eta$ into the datum. The excluded set $S$,
and with it $b_*,\xi,\tau,\Xi$, is chosen as in~\cite[Section~6.1]{OAI78} for $\eta$; it contains the primes above
$6$, hence the conductor of $\overline\alpha$, so the same $S$ serves for $\psi$. The ray group $T$ is chosen
independently of the target~\cite[Section~6.1]{OAI78}, and $\Theta_0=\langle\eta,\widehat T\rangle$ is finite. The angular
factor is carried as an explicit coefficient $\overline{\alpha(n)}$ of primary generators, as~\cite{OAI78} already
does with $\overline\alpha\gamma_2$ and in~\cite[eq.~(4.7)]{OAI78}. Whenever~\cite{OAI78} applies a ray-class
argument to a fixed character (a finite Fourier expansion on the fixed ray group, \cite[Section~4.2]{OAI78}; membership in the
finite group generated by the target and $\widehat T$; ``the same fixed finite-ray twist'' in a product of plain
factors, \cite[Section~4.1]{OAI78}), the CM version applies it to the finite part only, and keeps $\alpha^{\pm1}$ outside, with the
same orientation on every variable that carries it. This is used in items~1, 3, 4 and~5 of
Section~\ref{sec:78status}.

\section{The probe for a CM target}\label{sec:78probe}

\subsection{The weight}
In~\cite{OAI78} the target $\eta$ enters the probe~\cite[eq.~(6.1)]{OAI78},
\cite[\S3.1]{G78}, only through the weight on the completed index $A=cn^3$:
$\gamma_2(c)\overline{\alpha(A)}\eta(A)$. For the target $\psi=\overline\alpha\eta$ the weight is
therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cm-weight}
 \gamma_2(c)\overline{\alpha(cn^3)}\,\psi(cn^3)
 =\gamma_2(c)\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,2}\,\overline{\alpha(n)}^{\,6}\,\eta(c)\eta(n)^3 .
\end{equation}
Everything else in the probe is unchanged:
\begin{itemize}
\item the additive polynomial with its sextic Gauss sums;
\item the averaging variable $m$;
\item the fixed phases;
\item the scales $X,Y,Z$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Why $\overline\alpha\eta$ and not $\alpha\bar\eta$.} The two targets have conjugate
zeros, so either could be used. With $\alpha\bar\eta$ the weight would be
$\gamma_2(c)\bar\eta(cn^3)$, with no angular factor. The completed sums would then be
sums of coefficients of the cubic theta function itself. Its expansion at the identity cusp has a
nonzero constant term; the expansions at the other two cusps have none~\cite[Theorem~23]{QF}. In~\cite{OAI78} a
$\bar z$-derivative removes that term (\cite[proof of Proposition~5.1, \emph{Mellin normalization and continuation}]{OAI78}). Without a derivative it enters the reflection of the
translates attached to the identity cusp, and the proof in~\cite{OAI78} has no provision for the resulting term.
With the target $\overline\alpha\eta$ the weight carries $\overline\alpha^{\,2}$. The completed sums are then those of
$\partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}\theta$, which is the input of Section~\ref{sec:theta}, and the constant term is
removed.

\subsection{Second evaluation: the rows}
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:cm-rows}
With the weight~\eqref{eq:cm-weight}, Poisson summation in $m$ gives the
representation~\cite[eq.~(7.5)]{OAI78} of the probe, with row $u$
equal to
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d075}
 \frac{\zeta_K^S(6z)\,L^S\bigl(w,\chi_\bullet(u)\bigr)}
      {L^S\bigl(x,\psi\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)}\bigr)}\,\mathcal H_{\psi,u}(x,w,z),
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal H_{\psi,u}$ is the Euler product of~\cite[Lemma~7.1]{OAI78}
with $\eta(p)$ replaced by $\psi(p)$. It has the following properties:
\begin{itemize}
\item it converges in the same regions;
\item it satisfies $\mathcal H_{\psi,u}\ll\N(u)^\varepsilon$ there;
\item $\mathcal H_{\psi,1}=1+O(P_0^{-4/5})$ in the second region of~\cite{OAI78} (\cite[Lemma~7.1]{OAI78}).
\end{itemize}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
\cite{OAI78} evaluates the coefficient prime by prime. The target enters only through
the unimodular numbers $\eta(p)$ and complete multiplicativity, and no ray-class
condition on $p$ is used (\cite[Section~7.2]{OAI78}).

The angular factor of the weight is absorbed by the Gauss-sum identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:signal-cm}
 \overline{\alpha(c)}\,\gamma_2(c)\,\gamma_1(c)=\mu(c)\,G(c),
\end{equation}
a rearrangement of the second identity $\gamma_1\gamma_2=\mu\alpha G$ in~\cite[eq.~(4.7)]{OAI78}.
Here $c$ is squarefree, $\gamma_1$ is the normalized sextic Gauss sum, and $G(c)=\overline{\chi_c(4)}\gamma_3(c)$ is the
unimodular phase of~\cite[Lemma~4.4]{OAI78}. It factors through a fixed ray group supported
over $2$ and $3$ but is not multiplicative, $G(vw)=G(v)G(w)\mathcal R(v,w)$~\cite[eq.~(4.5)]{OAI78};
as in~\cite{OAI78} it is cancelled by the correction $\overline{G(cn^3)}$ of the probe, and no angular factor is
involved. For the
weight~\eqref{eq:cm-weight}, $\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,2}\gamma_2(c)\eta(c)\gamma_1(c)=\mu(c)G(c)\psi(c)$.
So every entry of the local table of~\cite{OAI78} holds with $\eta(p)$ replaced by
$\psi(p)$:
\begin{itemize}
\item The squarefree part gives $-\psi(p)\overline{\chi_p(u)}\N(p)^{-x}$, which is the
 Euler factor of $1/L(x,\psi\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)})$.
\item The cube part gives the phases $a_p=\overline{\alpha(p)}^{\,3}\psi(p)^3$ and
 $b_p=\overline{\alpha(p)}^{\,2}\psi(p)^2\overline{\chi_p(4)}$.
\item The relation $b_p^3=a_p^2$ used there still holds, since $\chi_p(4)^3=(4/p)_2=1$.
\end{itemize}
The convergence regions and the bounds for $\mathcal H$ use only $|\psi(p)|=1$.
\end{proof}
We checked~\eqref{eq:signal-cm} numerically at $60$ primes (error below
$4\cdot10^{-15}$). The proposition gives the following.
\begin{itemize}
\item The numerators $L(w,\chi_\bullet(u))$ have finite order. They are the same as for
 $\zeta$.
\item The denominators $L(x,\psi\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)})$ are induced by members of $\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$
 (Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(d)) and are never principal.
\item The principal row $u=1$ is $\zeta_K^S(w)\zeta_K^S(6z)/L^S(x,\psi)$. Its residues at
 $w=1$ and $z=1/6$ give the signal with $H_\psi(x)/L^S(x,\psi)$, and with the same
 positive constant $c_S$. The positivity $M(1/6)>0$ is a property of the averaging
 weight alone.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{First evaluation: completed rows with two derivatives}
Cauchy--Schwarz splits the probe \cite[\S3.2]{G78} into two mean squares. The first is
the additive one, which does not involve the target. The second is the mean square of
the completed rows
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d076}
 B_m=\sum_{\substack{c\ {\rm sf},\ n\\(cn,S)=1}}
 \frac{\gamma_2(c)\,\overline{\alpha(cn^3)}^{\,2}\,\nu(cn^3)\,\chi_{cn^3}(m)}{\sqrt{\N(c)}\,\N(n)}
 \,V\Bigl(\frac{\N(c)\N(n)^3}Z\Bigr),
\end{equation}
where $c$ and $n$ are primary and may share primes. Here $\nu$ is a finite-order character whose conductor is
supported on the fixed set $S$, extended by zero; it is the finite part $\eta$ times the fixed phases. The test $V$ is
the Gaussian $V_{\rm G}$ of~\cite[Section~5.5]{OAI78}, which does not have compact support. The angular factor must
stay in the weight, not in $\nu$: the theta step of~\cite{OAI78} expands $\nu$ over residue
classes, which is impossible for $\overline\alpha$.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:cm-completed}
For every $\varepsilon>0$ and every $Q=Z^M$ with $M$ in a fixed bounded range,
$\sum_{0<\N(m)\le Q}|B_m|^2\ll Z^{\varepsilon}(Q+Q^2/Z)$, uniformly over the fixed finite family of
finite-ray twists $\nu$ of~\cite{OAI78}. This is the case $N=1$
of~\cite[Lemma~5.8]{OAI78}, summed over the dyads of the powerful part of~$m$.
\end{proposition}
This is~\eqref{eq:s1-cm-completed}, proved in Appendix~\ref{app:s1-general} for every log-Schwartz test $V$, with a
finite seminorm of $V$ in the constant. \cite{OAI78} proves its version through its completed reflection
\cite[Prop.~5.1]{OAI78}, which is the transformation formula for
$\partial_{\bar z}\theta$. For~\eqref{eq:cm-weight} one uses instead $\partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}\theta$,
as in Section~\ref{sec:theta} and Appendix~\ref{app:reflection}; at the generality of~\cite{OAI78} this is
Proposition~\ref{prop:s1-reflection}. Appendix~\ref{app:reflection} alone does not suffice, because the probe
uses the Gaussian test and an excluded set $S$ that contains every prime of norm at most $P_0$ and the moduli
of~$\eta$~\cite[Section~6.1]{OAI78}. The changes are:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
 & $\zeta$ and finite order~\cite{OAI78} & CM target\\\midrule
derivative & $\partial_{\bar z}\overline\theta$ & $\partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}\overline\theta$\\
gamma factor of $T(s)$ & $\Gamma(s+\frac13)\Gamma(s+\frac23)$ & $\Gamma(s+\frac56)\Gamma(s+\frac76)$\\
reflected kernel & $\prod_\pm\frac{\Gamma(1+t\pm1/6)}{\Gamma(1-t\pm1/6)}$
 & $\frac{\Gamma(4/3+t)\Gamma(5/3+t)}{\Gamma(4/3-t)\Gamma(5/3-t)}$\\
cusp scalar & $-\frac{i}{81}\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,2}$ & $-\frac1{81}\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}$\\
angular factor, original side & $\overline{\alpha(n)}\,\overline{\alpha(b)}^{\,3}$ & $\overline{\alpha(n)}^{\,2}\overline{\alpha(b)}^{\,6}$\\
angular factor, dual side & $\alpha(\ell)$ & $\alpha(\ell)^2$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Here the $j$-th derivative ($j=1,2$) of the completed theta sum at $z=0$ is integrated against $v^{2s+j-2}\,dv$;
in both cases the result is $\frac{3^{5/2}}4\bigl(\frac{27}{(2\pi)^2}\bigr)^s$ times the gamma factor times $T(s)$. (With
$v^{2s-1}\,dv$, as in~\eqref{eq:b3-J}, the CM factor is $\Gamma(s+\frac13)\Gamma(s+\frac23)$ times $T(s-\frac12)$.)
Both kernels satisfy $|R(\sigma+iu)|\ll_A(1+|u|)^{4\sigma}$ on $-1/4\le\sigma\le A$. The CM kernel is
holomorphic for $\Re t>-4/3$; OAI78's is holomorphic for $\Re t>-5/6$. So the reflected weight obeys
the same bound $\ll\min(x^{1/4},x^{-A})$, with the same seminorms
(Proposition~\ref{prop:s1-reflection}(e)). The other ingredients
carry over as follows.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Dual lengths.} They are unchanged.
\item \emph{Quadratic large sieve.} The dual angular factor $\alpha(\ell)^2$ is a unimodular
 coefficient of the dual index, so it is absorbed into the arbitrary coefficients of the
 sieve.
\item \emph{Marks.} Slot primes in the completed index are handled in~\cite{OAI78} through
 $\ind_{q\mid A}=1-\chi_q(A)^0$, which never requires splitting $\overline\alpha$.
\item \emph{Phases.} The cusp scalar $\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}$, which replaces $-i\,\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,2}$, is completely
 multiplicative, so it splits into a fixed, a row and a tuple factor exactly as in~\cite[Section~14.2]{OAI78}. The
 cross-prime phases~\cite[proof of Proposition~5.1, \emph{The reflected additive phase}]{OAI78} do not involve the derivative.
\end{itemize}
The balanced low estimate $|I_\psi(Z^{1/2},Z^{1/2},Z)|\ll Z^{1/4+\varepsilon}$ follows from the
proposition as in~\cite{OAI78}; it is used only in the $11/12$ stage of~\cite{OAI78}.

The compensated probe needs more than Proposition~\ref{prop:cm-completed}. Its marked rows involve sums over tuples
of slot primes, divisibility conditions on the completed index, and a scale that depends on the tuple.
In~\cite{OAI78} their bound comes from Corollary~14.1 and Lemmas~14.2,
14.3 and~15.1. Their CM forms, with the same uniformity in the tuples, the masks and
the smooth weights, are Proposition~\ref{prop:s1-marked} and Lemmas~\ref{lem:s1-reflected-energy}
and~\ref{lem:s1-probe-row-norm} in Appendix~\ref{app:s1-general}; \cite[Lemma~14.2]{OAI78} does not involve the weight. The
compensated low estimate $|I_{\rm mod}(Z)|\ll Z^{3/16+\varepsilon}$ then follows as
in~\cite[Prop.~15.3]{OAI78}, since the additive Gram bound~\cite[Prop.~15.2]{OAI78}, a planar additive large sieve (compare~\cite{Hux68}), does not
involve the target.
We checked numerically:
\begin{itemize}
\item the Bessel--Mellin identities for one and two derivatives (relative error
 $3\cdot10^{-26}$);
\item the two reflection constants;
\item the Stirling exponents of the kernels.
\end{itemize}

\section{Rows, zeros and prime slots}\label{sec:78rows}

\subsection{Growth of the CM \texorpdfstring{$L$}{L}-functions}
\begin{lemma}\label{lem:cm-growth}
Let $\psi\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$ have conductor $\mathfrak f$, and put $Q=\N\mathfrak f$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d077}
 \Lambda(s,\psi)=(3Q)^{s/2}(2\pi)^{-s}\,\Gamma(s+\tfrac12)\,L(s,\psi)
\end{equation}
is entire and $\Lambda(s,\psi)=\epsilon(\psi)\Lambda(1-s,\overline\psi)$ with $|\epsilon(\psi)|=1$. Moreover
$|L(\sigma+it,\psi)|\ll Q^{3/5}(3+|t|)^2$ for $-1/10\le\sigma\le11/10$, with an absolute implied constant. This
is the bound of~\cite[Lemma~4.8]{OAI78} for finite-order characters. For every compact
$I\subset(-\frac12,\frac32)$ and $\sigma\in I$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d078}
 |L(\sigma+it,\psi)|\ll_I(3Q)^{1/2-\sigma}(3+|t|)^{1-2\sigma}\,|L(1-\sigma-it,\overline\psi)| .
\end{equation}
All of this holds for $\overline\psi$ as well.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
See Appendix~\ref{app:s2-hecke}, Section~\ref{sec:s2-strip}. The functional equation is proved there from a
theta series with the harmonic polynomial $\bar a$, by Hecke's method~\cite{Hec20} (see~\cite[Ch.~VII, \S\S7--8]{Neukirch}), with $\epsilon(\psi)$ an explicit normalized Gauss sum
(Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-fe}). The gamma quotient $\Gamma(\frac32-s)/\Gamma(\frac12+s)$ has the exponent
$1-2\sigma$ of $\Gamma(1-s)/\Gamma(s)$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-gamma}). The strip bound then follows by the damped
Phragm\'en--Lindel\"of argument~\cite{PL08} of~\cite[proof of Lemma~4.8]{OAI78}.
\end{proof}
The logarithmic control of~\cite[Lemma~4.9]{OAI78} holds for $\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$ and the
conjugate characters (Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-logcontrol}). It has the same conductor and height exponents, its
constants do not depend on the character, and it has no principal clauses. For the characters of the rows,
finite-order or CM, its global form holds on $\Re s\ge b+v$ for every fixed $b\ge\max(\beta_*,\frac78)$.
Section~\ref{sec:s2-uses} lists every use in~\cite{OAI78} of these growth statements, together with the CM
statement that replaces it.

\subsection{The characters attached to a row}
In~\cite{OAI78} each row $u$ carries a finite set of characters: the twists
$\theta\chi_\bullet(u)^{\pm1}$ by members $\theta$ of the finite group
$\Theta_0=\langle\eta,\widehat T\rangle$, where $\widehat T$ consists of fixed characters
supported over $2$ and $3$. A row is \emph{bad} if one of these has a zero of height at most a small
power $Z^\tau$ well to the right of $1/2$. For a CM target the group generated by $\psi$ is
infinite. We use instead the finite set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d079}
 \mathcal X_u=\bigl\{\theta\chi_\bullet(u)^{\pm1}\bigr\}\cup
 \bigl\{\overline\alpha\,\theta\,\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)},\ \alpha\,\theta\,\chi_\bullet(u)\bigr\},
 \qquad\theta\in\Theta_0=\langle\eta,\widehat T\rangle .
\end{equation}
It has three properties.
\begin{itemize}
\item It contains the numerator, the denominator $\psi\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)}$, and their
 conjugates.
\item It is finite, with $4|\Theta_0|$ labels for every $u$ (the label set is independent of $u$), and closed
 under conjugation. Each CM member is induced by a primitive character in $\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$ or in its
 conjugate, of conductor norm $\ll\N(u)$ (Lemmas~\ref{lem:s2-rowcond} and~\ref{lem:s3-labels}); so it is never
 principal and its $L$-function is entire. Together with Lemma~\ref{lem:cm-growth}, the logarithmic control
 (Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-logcontrol}), the deleted-Euler-factor bounds
 (\cite[Lemma~4.10]{OAI78}, which use only $|\psi^*(\mathfrak p)|\le1$), and the removal of the
 finitely many $S$-supported rows (needed only for the finite-order members), these are all that the detector
 of~\cite[Section~8]{OAI78} uses. Appendix~\ref{app:s3-detector} goes through it line by line
 (Proposition~\ref{prop:s3-detector}).
\item Its finite-order members have no zeros in $\Re s>7/8$, by~\cite[Theorem~1.1]{OAI78}, and its CM
 members and their conjugates none in $\Re s>\beta_*$. Under the contradiction hypothesis $\beta_*>7/8$ this gives
 the bin ceiling $a\le\beta_*$ of \cite[\S3.3]{G78} (Lemma~\ref{lem:r3-ceiling} below).
\end{itemize}
\begin{lemma}[Bin ceiling for $\mathcal X_u$]\label{lem:r3-ceiling}
Suppose $\beta_*>7/8$. Let $u\ne0$, let $\chi\in\mathcal X_u$, and let $\chi'$ be the primitive character inducing
$\chi$. Then $L(s,\chi')\ne0$ for $\Re s>\beta_*$. Consequently, in~\cite[Lemma~8.1]{OAI78} with the
label set $\mathcal X_u$, every maximum $M_i(u)$ is at most $\beta_*$, every bin has $a\le\beta_*$, and
$\delta=2a-1\le2\beta_*-1$.
\end{lemma}
This replaces~\cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78}, whose first sentence (every primitive character inducing a member of the
label set has finite order) is false for $\mathcal X_u$.
\begin{proof}
There are three cases.
\begin{itemize}
\item $\chi=\theta\chi_\bullet(u)^{\pm1}$ with $\theta\in\Theta_0$. Then $\chi$ has finite order, since $\theta$ has and
 $n\mapsto\chi_n(u)$ is a finite-order ray character~\cite[Lemma~4.1]{OAI78}.
 By~\cite[Theorem~1.1]{OAI78}, $L(s,\chi')$ has no zero in $\Re s>7/8$, and $7/8<\beta_*$.
\item $\chi=\overline\alpha\,\theta\,\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)}$. Then $\theta\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)}$ has finite order, so
 $\chi'\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$. By the definition of $\beta_*$, $L(s,\chi')$ has no zero with
 $\beta_*<\Re s\le1$, and the Euler product excludes zeros in $\Re s>1$.
\item $\chi=\alpha\,\theta\,\chi_\bullet(u)$. Then $\overline\chi$ is as in the second case, its primitive inducing
 character is $\overline{\chi'}$, and $L(s,\chi')=\overline{L(\bar s,\overline{\chi'})}$. So the zeros of $L(s,\chi')$ are
 the conjugates of those of $L(s,\overline{\chi'})$, with the same real parts.
\end{itemize}
A presentation in $\mathcal X_u$ differs from $\chi'$ by finitely many factors $1-\chi'(\mathfrak p)\N\mathfrak p^{-s}$, which
are nonzero in $\Re s>0$. Each $M_i(u)$ is the maximum of the floor $51/100$ and of real parts of zeros of members
of $\mathcal X_u$ in a bounded rectangle~\cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78}. Hence
$M_i(u)\le\max(51/100,7/8,\beta_*)=\beta_*$.
\end{proof}
If the $11/12$ stage of~\cite{OAI78} is also carried out for $\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$, it runs under $\beta_*>11/12$, and
in the first case~\cite[Theorem~3.1]{OAI78} already suffices.
That finite-order theorem does not assume a zero-free bound for the CM family,
so using it here is not circular.

A zero of a CM member produces the two witnesses of \cite[\S3.3]{G78}, now with the
angular factor:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d080}
 M_r=U^{-r/2}\sum_n\mu(n)\overline{\alpha(n)}\theta(n)\overline{\chi_n(u)}\,W\Bigl(\frac{\N(n)}{U^r}\Bigr),
 \qquad
 S_m=U^{-m/2}\sum_l\overline{\alpha(l)}\theta(l)\overline{\chi_l(u)}\,W'\Bigl(\frac{\N(l)}{U^m}\Bigr),
\end{equation}
or their conjugates. In each, $\overline\alpha$ appears together with $\overline\chi$, or $\alpha$ together
with $\chi$. We call such sums \emph{co-oriented}; this matters in
Section~\ref{sec:78moments}.

\paragraph{Witnesses and slots.} \cite{OAI78} conjugates both witness factors if necessary, so that their row
character is $\nu(n)\overline{\chi_n(u)}$, and then measures each physical slot prime against this row: its
coefficient is $\overline{\nu(p)}\ind_{p\in1_T}$, a fixed combination of members of the finite group
(\cite[Section~19.2]{OAI78}). For a CM zero the conjugated row character is
$\overline\alpha\theta'\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)}$ with $\theta'\in\Theta_0$; measured against it, the slot coefficient
would be $\alpha(p)\overline{\theta'(p)}\ind_{p\in1_T}$, which is not of that form. The slots are therefore measured
against the finite-order part $\theta'\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)}$: their coefficient is
$\overline{\theta'(p)}\ind_{p\in1_T}$, and $\overline\alpha$ stays on the M\"obius and plain variables only. This is how
the moments of Section~\ref{sec:78moments} are stated: the inverse moment is used with $\epsilon=-1$, and, after the
conjugation of~\cite[Section~19.2]{OAI78}, the plain moment with $e=+1$, $\psi_k=\overline{\theta'}\chi_\bullet(u)$ and
slot coefficients $\theta'(p)\ind_{p\in1_T}$. A retained large row still induces outside $\Theta_0$, now for its
finite-order part, because it ramifies at a prime outside $S$ (\cite[Sections~8.1 and~19.2]{OAI78}).

\subsection{The compensated probe and the slots}
The compensated probe \cite[\S4.2]{G78} is formed with $\overline{\psi(p_{J^c})}$ in place of
$\overline{\eta(p_{J^c})}$. In row $u$ a slot prime $p\nmid u$ contributes the local factor
$\psi(p)\overline{\chi_p(u)}\N(p)^{-x}$ of the denominator, and $\overline{\psi(p)}\psi(p)=1$. So the
marked-minus-rescaled difference is $-\overline{\chi_p(u)}$ plus small terms, exactly as
for~$\zeta$. The slot amplitudes
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d081}
 Q_i(u)=P_i^{-1/2}\sum_{p\in\text{slot }i}\Omega_i\Bigl(\frac{\N(p)}{P_i}\Bigr)
 \Bigl(\frac{\N(p)}{P_i}\Bigr)^{z-1}\overline{\Bigl(\frac up\Bigr)_6}
\end{equation}
are the same finite-order prime polynomials as in \cite[\S4.4]{G78}. Two consequences
follow.
\begin{enumerate}
\item The principal normalizer $A_T(Z)$ is unchanged.
\item The pointwise bound $|Q_i(u)|\ll U^{\epsilon_1}P_i^{\delta/2+O(e)}$ of~\cite[Lemma~19.1]{OAI78}
 is unchanged, since the slot characters $\theta\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)}$ lie in $\mathcal X_u$.
 Separately, the uniform slot bound inside the plain moment (\cite[eq.~(18.12)]{OAI78}) needs a
 zero-free region only for finite-order characters. OpenAI's theorem supplies it at $7/8$, so
 $\kappa=3/4$ may be used there and in the plain capacity, in place of $\kappa=2\beta_*-1$.
 In~\cite{OAI78} the same $\kappa$ also writes the bin ceiling as $\delta\le\kappa$
 (\cite[eq.~(12.2)]{OAI78}). With $\kappa=3/4$ the two must be kept apart: the bin ceiling is
 $\delta\le2\beta_*-1$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:r3-ceiling}), which may exceed $3/4$. \cite{OAI78} uses $\delta\le\kappa$ only
 through $\delta\le5/6$ (Section~\ref{sec:78final}).
\end{enumerate}

\section{Counting bad rows with an angular factor}\label{sec:78moments}
\cite{OAI78} counts bad rows with the sextic large sieve of \cite[\S3.3]{G78} (which builds on~\cite{GL,BGL,HB00}) and the three averages of \cite[\S5.1]{G78}. For rows whose zero comes from a
finite-order member they apply unchanged. For CM members they are needed with the angular
factor on the summation variable, but not on the slot primes.

\paragraph{1. The sextic large sieve}~\cite[Lemma~9.1]{OAI78}.
It allows arbitrary coefficients fixed independently of the row, so it is unchanged. For a CM zero the witness
coefficient is $D^{-1/2}\mu(n)\overline{\alpha(n)}\theta(n)\overline{\chi_n(v)}W(\N(n)/D)$ (or its conjugate), with $v$
the frozen non-squarefree part of the row. Apart from elementary counts that do not involve the target, this is the
only row count used in the $11/12$ stage of~\cite{OAI78} (its Part~I); with CM labels it is
Lemma~\ref{lem:s6-sextic}.

\paragraph{2. The inverse moment with prime factors.} Let $\nu$ be a fixed finite-order ray character whose
zero-extended defining modulus has prime support in $S$; the angular factor is not part of $\nu$. Fix a sign
$\epsilon=\pm1$, bounded ranges for the nonnegative parameters $m,r,z_i$, a bound for the number of slots, and
constants $c_1,c_2>0$, and suppose, as in~\cite[Lemma~17.1]{OAI78}, $r+2z\le m-c_1$ and $2r+8z\le3m-c_2$.
For smooth annular $W,W_i$ put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d199}\begin{aligned}
 M_u&=Z^{-r/2}\sum_n\mu(n)\,\alpha(n)^{\epsilon}\nu(n)\chi_n(u)^{\epsilon}
 W\Bigl(\frac{\N(n)}{Z^r}\Bigr),\\
 Q_u&=\prod_{i}Z^{-z_i/2}\sum_{p\in\mathcal P_i}a_i(p)\nu(p)\chi_p(u)^{\epsilon}W_i\Bigl(\frac{\N(p)}{Z^{z_i}}\Bigr),
\end{aligned}\end{equation}
with the same $\nu$ and $\epsilon$ in $M_u$ and in every slot, where $z=\sum_iz_i$, the slot sets $\mathcal P_i$ are
disjoint and avoid $S$, $\N(p)\asymp Z^{z_i}$ for $p\in\mathcal P_i$, and the coefficients $a_i(p)$ are independent
of $u$ with $|a_i(p)|\le1$. Then for every $\varepsilon>0$
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cm-inverse}
 \sum_{\substack{u\in\OO\\0<\N(u)\ll Z^m}}|M_uQ_u|^2\ll Z^{m+\varepsilon},
\end{equation}
with the uniformity of~\cite[Lemma~17.1]{OAI78}: the constant depends only on the fixed arithmetic data,
$c_1,c_2$, the parameter ranges and finitely many seminorms of the tests; it is uniform in the prime supports and
the coefficients $a_i$; the bound holds for every subcollection of the slots; norm twists of the tests cost a
fixed power of their heights; and there is no mesh or lower-length condition on the slots.
\emph{Why.} Appendix~\ref{sec:s4-inverse} proves~\eqref{eq:cm-inverse} (Proposition~\ref{prop:s4-marked}). The
proof in~\cite{OAI78} alternates between two coefficient classes: Möbius coefficients
$\mu\nu$, and cubic coefficients $\overline\alpha\gamma_2\nu$ attached to the theta function. Each
switch uses~\eqref{eq:signal-cm} or its companion
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d082}
 \mu(n)\gamma_{-1}(n)=\chi_n(-1)G(n)^{-1}\,\overline{\alpha(n)}\,\gamma_2(n),
\end{equation}
which holds for squarefree $n$~\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The second Poisson transformation}]{OAI78} and which we checked at $60$ primes and at squarefree
composites. \cite{OAI78} runs the orientation $\mu\nu$ with $\overline\chi$: for $\epsilon=+1$ it first conjugates
the whole residual polynomial (finite character, marks and tests;~\cite[proof of Lemma~17.1]{OAI78}), which leaves the
row norm unchanged. Conjugation sends $\alpha\nu\chi_\bullet(u)$ to $\overline\alpha\,\bar\nu\,\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)}$,
so a co-oriented sum stays co-oriented. With the co-oriented factor $\overline\alpha$ on the Möbius variable the
classes are therefore $\mu\overline\alpha\leftrightarrow\overline\alpha^{\,2}\gamma_2$ for both signs. The canonical
class of~\cite[Lemma~17.2]{OAI78} is $\overline\alpha\gamma_2\nu\rho$, and that lemma admits no
additional residual coefficient (\cite[Lemma~17.2]{OAI78}); so the extra factor $\overline\alpha$ must be built into
the class, which becomes $\overline\alpha^{\,2}\gamma_2\nu\rho$ in the CM canonical estimate
(Lemma~\ref{lem:s4-canonical}). These are the dual coefficient and the cube
factor $\overline\alpha^{\,6}$ of Sections~\ref{sec:poisson}--\ref{sec:descent}, and the theta steps use
$\partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}\theta$ as in Appendix~\ref{app:s1-general}; the terminal application
of~\cite[Lemma~14.3]{OAI78} becomes Lemma~\ref{lem:s1-reflected-energy}. In the CM case each switch
is~\eqref{eq:signal-cm} or its companion multiplied by $\overline{\alpha(n)}$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:s4-switch}, checked at
all $366$ squarefree $n$ with $\N(n)\le1300$). Poisson summation acts on the row variable for a fixed pair of
columns $n_1,n_2$, so the factor $\overline{\alpha(n_1)}\alpha(n_2)$ passes through each transformation. It splits
into a factor on the new columns and a factor of modulus one on outer variables, which~\cite{OAI78} bounds only in
absolute value (Lemma~\ref{lem:s4-transfer}).

In the overlap reduction of~\cite{OAI78} ($n=jn_0$, $P=jP_0$, $c=n_0P_0$) one has
$\alpha(n)^\epsilon=\alpha(j)^\epsilon\alpha(c)^\epsilon\overline{\alpha(P_0)^{\epsilon}}$. The assigned primes
contribute a $u$-independent unimodular scalar, and each surviving slot prime, which becomes part of the new
Möbius variable $c$, has its coefficient multiplied by $\mu(p)\overline{\alpha(p)^{\epsilon}}$. That coefficient is
still bounded, independent of $u$, and of product form, which is all~\cite{OAI78} uses. The masks, punctures,
scales and counts of the descent, and the propagation of seminorms, do not involve the coefficients.

\emph{Orientation.} The anti-oriented sum, with $\alpha$ paired with $\overline\chi$, would turn into
plain $\gamma_2$, since $\alpha\cdot\overline\alpha\gamma_2=\gamma_2$. That is the theta function without a
derivative, with its constant terms. So the argument does not cover that case: its terminal step would need a
reflected energy for $\theta$ itself, and the clause ``for either common sign'' of~\cite[Lemma~17.1]{OAI78}
is not claimed for anti-oriented sums. The case is never needed. Every CM member of $\mathcal X_u$ is co-oriented,
\cite{OAI78} conjugates witness factors only to give their row character the orientation
$\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)}$ of the physical slots (\cite[Section~19.2]{OAI78}), and conjugation preserves
co-orientation. The inverse witness of a zero of $\alpha\theta\chi_\bullet(u)$ thus becomes
$\sum\mu\overline\alpha\,\bar\theta\,\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)}$, the case $\epsilon=-1$; the slots carry no angular
factor.

\paragraph{3. Sixth-power amplification}~\cite[Lemma~17.6]{OAI78}.
Its proof is unchanged, except that the column identity~\cite[eq.~(17.90)]{OAI78} acquires
