the factor $\alpha(d)^{\epsilon}$, of modulus one, on its divisor $d$ (equation~\eqref{eq:s4-sixth}); the
coefficient mass is the same. The identity $\chi_n(ua^6)=\chi_n(u)$ for $(a,n)=1$ does not involve $\alpha$. Its
input is~\eqref{eq:cm-inverse} without slots~\cite[eq.~(17.87)]{OAI78}. For $\epsilon=1$, $S$ the primes above
$6$ and $\nu$ principal, the mean square~\eqref{eq:MS} of Part~I of these notes is that raw moment with
$H=D^{1+\vartheta}$, that is,~\eqref{eq:cm-inverse} with $Z=H$, $m=1$, $r=1/(1+\vartheta)$, no slots and
$c_1=\vartheta/(1+\vartheta)$, for a fixed $0<\vartheta\le1/10$.

\paragraph{4. The plain fourth moment with prime factors}~\cite[Lemma~18.1]{OAI78}.
The CM statement needed is the following. Fix $e=\pm1$ and $3/4\le\kappa\le1$. For rows $k$ with
$0<\N(k)\ll Z^m$, write $\psi_k=\tau\chi_\bullet(k)$ for the finite-order row character, and put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d083}
 S_k(n_j)=Z^{-n_j/2}\sum_l\alpha(l)^e\psi_k(l)W_j\Bigl(\frac{\N(l)}{Z^{n_j}}\Bigr)\qquad(j=1,2).
\end{equation}
Let $Q$ be a product of slot polynomials in $\psi_k$ with no angular factor, of total length $z$, whose
coefficients are fixed combinations of characters in the finite group $\Theta$
of~\cite[Section~18]{OAI78}, which contains $\Theta_0$ and the characters separating the fixed
reciprocity phases. Let $\mathcal R_z$ be the rows of
\cite{OAI78}, defined through the finite-order part $\psi_k$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d084}
 \sum_{k\in\mathcal R_z}|S_k(n_1)S_k(n_2)Q|^2\ll Z^{M+\varepsilon},\qquad M=m+q,
\end{equation}
with the same hypotheses and uniformity as in~\cite{OAI78}: no restriction when $z=0$;
$n_1+n_2+6\kappa z\le M$ and slots of length at most the mesh when $z>0$, with a mesh independent of $\psi$ and
$e$; uniformity over moving moduli, masks and frozen labels. Here $Z^q$ bounds the norm of the full displayed
moving zero support of $\tau$, counted before cancellation or reduction of exponents modulo six
(\cite[Sections~13.1 and~18]{OAI78}). For $\kappa<1$, \cite{OAI78} also assumes $\beta_*\le(1+\kappa)/2$ for
its own $\beta_*$, the supremum over primitive finite-order characters. That hypothesis enters only
through~\cite[eq.~(18.11)]{OAI78}, applied to the primitive characters inducing the slot characters
$\psi_k\vartheta$, $\vartheta\in\Theta$, which have finite order because the slots carry no angular factor. So the CM
hypothesis is that no primitive finite-order character has a zero in $\Re s>(1+\kappa)/2$;
by~\cite[Theorem~1.1]{OAI78} it holds for every $\kappa\in[3/4,1]$, and $\kappa=3/4$ is allowed. It does not
involve the $\beta_*$ of $\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$. This statement is proved in Appendix~\ref{app:s5-plain}.
The changes to the proof are four.
\begin{itemize}
\item[(a)] \emph{Hecke reflection}~\cite{Hec20}. This is Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-reflection}, proved there and again, in the form
 used here, by direct Poisson summation in Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-reflection}, with an explicit root number. The dual
 profile is
 $\mathcal MW^\sharp(s)=\mathcal MW(1-s)\,\Gamma(s+\frac12)/\Gamma(\frac32-s)$, in place of
 $\mathcal MW(1-s)\Gamma(s)/\Gamma(1-s)$. Both quotients grow like $|t|^{2\sigma-1}$ on vertical lines,
 and the conductor scale changes only at the prime above $3$, by a factor between $1/9$ and $9$
 (Lemmas~\ref{lem:s2-rowcond} and~\ref{lem:s5-rowchar}). So the reflected lengths and seminorm bounds are
 unchanged.
\item[(b)] \emph{Poisson summation in the rows.} The first transform is Poisson over the row $k$, the second over
 the dual Gauss row $h$ (\cite[Lemma~13.3]{OAI78}), and the amplifier maps rows $h\mapsto hp^6$.
 The plain variables stay inside the column moduli throughout: they are never Poisson-summed and never become a
 row variable, and every reciprocity step involving them concerns only the finite characters. So $\alpha(l)^e$ is
 a column coefficient. Each extraction of a plain prime power $p^i$ leaves a frozen unimodular scalar
 $\alpha(p)^{ie}$.
\item[(c)] \emph{Main terms.} The induction of~\cite{OAI78} meets volume-sized main terms on child rows whose
 finite-order part induces a character in $\Theta_0$ (\cite[Section~18.7]{OAI78}), and cancels them with an
 equal-scale centered difference. In the CM case the plain sums there are
 $\mathcal L(X,t)=\sum_l\alpha(l)^e\vartheta(l)\ind_{(l,\mathfrak R_*)=1}\N(l)^{it}W(\N(l)/X)$, sums over primary
 generators in finitely many residue classes of a fixed lattice; the mask is removed by inclusion--exclusion,
 using $\alpha(dl)=\alpha(d)\alpha(l)$. The proof of~\cite[Lemma~18.3]{OAI78} applies to
 $f_t(z)=\alpha(z)^e|z|^{2it}W(|z|^2)$, and its zero frequency is
 $\int_{\mathbb C}f_t(z)\,dz=\int_0^\infty r^{2it}W(r^2)\,r\,dr\int_0^{2\pi}e^{ie\phi}\,d\phi=0$. So that lemma holds
 with main-term constant $0$ and the same uniform error; the centered difference is kept verbatim, with both main
 terms zero. Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-lattice} gives the bounds in the form the induction uses.
\item[(d)] \emph{The zero-free hypothesis.} The slot amplitudes have finite order, so the hypothesis
 is OpenAI's theorem, with $\kappa=3/4$.
\end{itemize}
Appendix~\ref{app:s5-plain} carries this out and proves the CM statement
(Proposition~\ref{prop:s5-plain}). It redoes the induction of~\cite{OAI78} with the angular factor
$\alpha(l_1l_2)^e$ carried through every transform and kept off the slot primes. Besides (a)--(d), the only
change is this bookkeeping (Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-coefficients}).

\section{The final inequality}\label{sec:78final}
A bin of rows of norm $U=Z^d$ contributes at most $Z^{C(7/8)+E(d)+o(1)}$, with the exponent
$E(d)$ of~\cite[eq.~(20.4)]{OAI78} \cite[\S6]{G78} (\cite[Lemma~20.1]{OAI78}). Its terms come
from four sources:
\begin{itemize}
\item the scales and the Mellin weight on the central contour;
\item the number of bad rows, given by the moments above with the same exponents;
\item the prime amplitudes, which have finite order;
\item the size $U^{\delta/2}$ of the finite-order numerator on the reflected line.
\end{itemize}
The CM denominator enters only through its reciprocal on the contour $\Re s=a+16e$, which costs $U^{\varepsilon}$
by the CM logarithmic control on the buffered disks. The exponent $63/50$ of the small rows
(\cite[proof of Lemma~10.6]{OAI78}) uses the strip bound of the finite-order numerator, and the large rows use only absolute
convergence. So no term involves the gamma factor or a convexity bound of a CM $L$-function; that gamma factor
affects only fixed powers of the height.

So the endpoint certificate of~\cite[Lemma~20.2]{OAI78},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d085}
 -E(h)\ge\frac{49}{440640}\qquad(0\le\delta\le5/6,\ 0\le x\le1/2),
\end{equation}
with $q=x\delta$ and $R=R_*(\delta,x)$,
applies verbatim. \cite{OAI78} compares it with $\Delta=\beta_*-7/8$ in
\cite[eq.~(20.10)]{OAI78}:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d086}
 E_{\rm actual}(h)-\Delta\le-\frac{49}{440640}-\Bigl(1-\frac h4\Bigr)\Delta+\epsilon_{\rm total}<0 .
\end{equation}
The term $h\Delta/4$ is a capacity loss caused by the choice $\kappa=2\beta_*-1$. With $\kappa=3/4$ it
is exactly zero, which only helps; the bin ceiling is then written $\delta\le2\beta_*-1$, not $\delta\le\kappa$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:r3-ceiling}).
The bin ceiling $\delta\le5/6$ comes from the $11/12$ stage, that is, from $\beta_*\le11/12$ for
$\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:s6-1112}). This bound is a statement about each member of the family
separately; it carries no uniformity in $\psi$. Part~I of these notes, as written, gives it for the characters
$\psi_d^*$, one curve at a time. Appendix~\ref{app:s6} runs Part~I with a fixed finite-order twist in the
columns, as~\cite{OAI1112} does for finite-order characters, and so proves it for every member. The balanced
probe of Section~\ref{sec:78probe}, at $X=Y=Z^{1/2}$ and with the sextic row count, gives a second proof: this
is Part~I of~\cite{OAI78} with the changes above, and it uses neither prime slots nor the plain moment
(Section~\ref{subsec:s6-shared}). In~\cite{OAI78} the bin ceiling is used only in two forms: as $a\le\beta_*$
(\cite[proof of Lemma~10.3]{OAI78} and, through~\cite[eq.~(12.2)]{OAI78}, \cite[proof of Lemma~20.1]{OAI78}), which Lemma~\ref{lem:r3-ceiling} supplies for
$\mathcal X_u$; and as $\delta\le\alpha=5/6$ (\cite[proof of Proposition~19.2, \emph{Cases requiring no selected primes}; Lemma~20.2; Section~20.5; proof of Proposition~20.3]{OAI78}), which follows from
Theorem~\ref{thm:s6-1112}. The shared high-side lemmas of~\cite{OAI78}, used again at $\sigma_0=7/8$, hold for
$\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$ by Proposition~\ref{prop:s6-shared}. Every margin is chosen before the target, as
in~\cite{OAI78}, so all margins are uniform in $\psi$. The continuation criterion then gives the
contradiction. Hence $\beta_*\le7/8$, which is Theorem~\ref{thm:78}.

\section{What has been checked, and what remains}\label{sec:78status}
We read~\cite{OAI78} in eight consecutive parts. For each part we listed every use of a property of
the target or of a row character, and classified it as unchanged, routine, or needing a new argument.
None needed a new idea, but several needed new proofs; these are written out in
Appendices~\ref{app:s1-general}--\ref{app:s6}. The table summarizes.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{p{2.7cm}p{4.6cm}p{7.2cm}}
\toprule
Sections of~\cite{OAI78} & content & CM change\\\midrule
1--4 & criterion, Gauss sums, growth lemmas & growth with $\Gamma(s+\frac12)$, functional equation, logarithmic
 control (Lemma~\ref{lem:cm-growth}, Appendix~\ref{app:s2-hecke}); OpenAI's theorem for finite-order rows\\
5 & completed reflection, row energy & $\partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}\theta$; table in Section~\ref{sec:78probe};
 Appendix~\ref{app:s1-general}\\
6--8 & probe, Poisson rows, detector & weight~\eqref{eq:cm-weight}; local identity formal in $\psi(p)$;
 label set $\mathcal X_u$; bin ceiling \cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78} replaced (Lemma~\ref{lem:r3-ceiling},
 Appendix~\ref{app:s3-detector})\\
9--11 & sextic count, high side, $11/12$ & witness coefficients carry $\alpha^{\pm1}$
 (Lemma~\ref{lem:s6-sextic}); the $11/12$ stage and the shared high-side lemmas (Appendix~\ref{app:s6})\\
12--16 & compensated probe, low side & normalization $\overline{\psi(p)}$; slots measured against the
 finite-order part (Section~\ref{sec:78rows})\\
17 & inverse moment & co-oriented classes $\mu\overline\alpha\leftrightarrow\overline\alpha^{\,2}\gamma_2$
 (Appendix~\ref{sec:s4-inverse})\\
18 & plain fourth moment & reflection with $\Gamma(s+\frac12)$; lattice main terms vanish
 (Appendix~\ref{app:s5-plain})\\
19--20 & row counts, endpoint & $\kappa=3/4$; bin ceiling $\delta\le2\beta_*-1$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The six statements that an earlier version of these notes left open are now written out, at the
generality of~\cite{OAI78}. In each, a step identical to~\cite{OAI78} is cited by its number there, and a
step that changes in the CM case is proved.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{The transformation formula for $\partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}\theta$}, for a general modulus supported on $S$,
 all log-Schwartz tests and the cross-prime phases, with its marked form and the lemmas built on it:
 Appendix~\ref{app:s1-general} (Propositions~\ref{prop:s1-reflection} and~\ref{prop:s1-marked},
 Lemmas~\ref{lem:s1-unmarked}, \ref{lem:s1-reflected-energy} and~\ref{lem:s1-probe-row-norm}).
 Lemma~14.2 of~\cite{OAI78} does not involve the weight and is used unchanged.
\item \emph{Growth and logarithmic control}: Lemma~\ref{lem:cm-growth}, proved in Appendix~\ref{app:s2-hecke}, which
 also gives the functional equation with an explicit root number, the conductors of the row characters, and the
 logarithmic control (Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-logcontrol}).
\item \emph{The detector with the label set $\mathcal X_u$}: Appendix~\ref{app:s3-detector}
 (Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels} and Proposition~\ref{prop:s3-detector}). Part~(e) of that lemma bounds the conductor
 exponents of the row characters at the primes above $2$ and $3$; \cite{OAI78} uses such a bound without stating
 it.
\item \emph{The inverse moment}~\eqref{eq:cm-inverse} for co-oriented coefficients: Appendix~\ref{sec:s4-inverse}
 (Proposition~\ref{prop:s4-marked}). Its terminal step is Lemma~\ref{lem:s1-reflected-energy}.
\item \emph{The plain fourth moment} with $\alpha^e$ on the plain variables: Appendix~\ref{app:s5-plain}
 (Proposition~\ref{prop:s5-plain}).
\item \emph{The $11/12$ stage}, $\beta_*\le11/12$ for $\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$: Theorem~\ref{thm:s6-1112}, proved one
 member at a time by Part~I of these notes with a finite-order twist, and the shared high-side lemmas used again
 at $\sigma_0=7/8$ (Proposition~\ref{prop:s6-shared}).
\end{enumerate}
The precise form of Proposition~\ref{prop:cm-completed} is Lemma~\ref{lem:s1-unmarked}. The bin ceiling for the
label set $\mathcal X_u$, which~\cite{OAI78} derives from a statement that is false for CM members, is
Lemma~\ref{lem:r3-ceiling}.

\paragraph{What Theorem~\ref{thm:78} rests on.} Besides the results proved here, the proof uses the steps
of~\cite{OAI78} cited as identical (each checked against the source), OpenAI's theorem for finite-order
characters~\cite[Theorem~1.1]{OAI78}, the theorem of Kubota and Patterson~\cite{Kub69,Pat77} in the form~\cite[Theorem~23]{QF}, the
quadratic large sieve~\cite{HB95,GL}, and the prime number theorem for prime ideals in ray classes~\cite{Hec17,Hec20}. OpenAI's
$7/8$ theorem is formalized in Lean~\cite{OAIlean}. Of the CM argument, only the last step of Part~I is
formalized so far; a complete certification of Parts~I and~II
is in progress.

\paragraph{Numerical checks.} We checked the new identities numerically:
\begin{itemize}
\item the point counts against $\psi_d^*$;
\item \eqref{eq:signal-cm} and its companion, and the switching identities, the marked cube inversion, the overlap
 identity and the sixth-power column identity of Appendix~\ref{sec:s4-inverse};
\item the transformation formula of Appendix~\ref{app:s1-general} end to end, with an active prime of norm $7$ and
 tests without compact support, and its marked form;
\item the theta transformation, functional equation and root number of Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-fe} for four characters,
 and the functional equations of eight CM members of the label sets (PARI/GP~\cite{PARI}), with the conductor exponents of
 Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(e);
\item the reflection of Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-reflection} for twelve primitive characters, the dual profile, and the
 vanishing of $\int\alpha^ef$;
\item the twisted paired identities and the twisted first Poisson reduction of Appendix~\ref{app:s6};
\item the Bessel--Mellin formulas, the reflection constants, and the Stirling exponents of all the gamma quotients.
\end{itemize}
The proof supplied here has been checked by internal referee passes and these tests;
external review of the CM adaptations is pending. The external inputs are those
listed above. Numerical checks support the identities, but do not establish the
uniform analytic estimates.

\section{CM newforms of higher weight: why the method should extend}\label{sec:higherweight}

Write $\psi_k=\overline\alpha^{\,k}\eta$ with $k\ge1$ and $\eta$ of finite order. These are the unitary Hecke
characters of $K$ with infinity type $(\bar z/|z|)^k$. Up to finitely many Euler factors, their
$L$-functions are those of the CM newforms of weight $k+1$ with complex multiplication by $K$~\cite{Shimura71,Ribet77}, shifted
to the center $1/2$: if $f$ is such a form, then $L(f,s+\frac k2)=L(s,\psi)$ for $\psi=\psi_k$ or its
conjugate. This section explains why we expect the following. A second paper, now being written, treats
these more general CM forms.

\paragraph{Expected statement.} For every $k\ge1$ and every finite-order $\eta$, $L(s,\overline\alpha^{\,k}\eta)\ne0$ for
$\Re s>7/8$. Equivalently, every CM newform $f$ of weight $k+1$ with CM by $K$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d087}
 L(f,s)\ne0\qquad\Bigl(\Re s>\frac k2+\frac78\Bigr),
\end{equation}
a zero-free strip along the right edge of the critical strip $\frac k2<\Re s<\frac k2+1$. The $11/12$
method of Part~I should give the weaker boundary $\frac k2+\frac{11}{12}$ in the same way.

We do not claim this as a theorem. The rest of this section records the changes in the number
$j=k+1$ of theta derivatives and in the Hecke Gamma factor. For each fixed $j$, the formulas have
the same structure and give the same length exponents; constants may depend on $j$.
The uniform analytic estimates listed at the end still have to be proved for each fixed weight.

\subsection{Two examples}
We computed the $q$-expansions of two eta products (the newforms 9.4.a.a and 12.3.c.a of~\cite{LMFDB}, both multiplicative $\eta$-quotients in the sense of~\cite{Martin96}) to $q^{3000}$ and compared them with Hecke characters.
In the exact $L$-function identities below, a product of Hecke characters means its primitive
inducing character, with all its Euler factors restored. Write $L^{(6)}$ when the Euler factors
at primes above $2$ and $3$ are deleted, as in the earlier sums.
\begin{itemize}
\item $f=\eta(3z)^8$, of weight $4$ and level $9$ ($k=3$). For all $207$ split rational primes
 $p=\pi\bar\pi<3000$, with both primary generators $\pi,\bar\pi$ tested ($414$ checks),
 $a_p=2\Re(\pi^3)$. For $p\equiv2\pmod3$, $a_p=0$ and $a_{p^2}=-p^3$. Thus
 $L(f,s+\frac32)=L(s,\alpha^3)$, and the target is the conjugate $\overline\alpha^{\,3}$, with
 $\eta$ trivial. This is the CM analogue of the case $u=1$ of the zeta argument. The expected strip
 is $19/8<\Re s<5/2$ (center $2$).
\item $f=\eta(2z)^3\eta(6z)^3$, of weight $3$ and level $12$ ($k=2$). For all $207$ split rational primes below
 $3000$, again testing both primary generators ($414$ checks),
 $a_p=2\Re\bigl(\pi^2(2/\pi)_3\bigr)=2\Re\bigl(\pi^2\chi_\pi(4)\bigr)$. For $p\nmid12$ with $p\equiv2\pmod3$,
 $a_p=0$ and $a_{p^2}=p^2$, as required by the nebentypus $\chi_{-3}$. The exclusion matters:
 $a_4=0$, not $4$. We have $L(f,s+1)=L(s,\alpha^2\chi_\bullet(4))$, and
 the target is $\overline\alpha^{\,2}\,\overline{\chi_\bullet(4)}$. The expected strip is $15/8<\Re s<2$ (center $3/2$).
 Since $a_3=-3$, the normalized Euler factor at $3$ is $(1+3^{-s})^{-1}$; the factor at $2$ is $1$.
 Thus, in the convention of the earlier sums,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:d088}
  L(f,s+1)=(1+3^{-s})^{-1}L^{(6)}(s,\alpha^2\chi_\bullet(4)).
 \end{equation}
\end{itemize}

\subsection{What changes with \texorpdfstring{$k$}{k}}
\paragraph{1. The weight and the rows.} With target $\psi=\overline\alpha^{\,k}\eta$ the weight~\eqref{eq:cm-weight} becomes
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d089}
 \gamma_2(c)\overline{\alpha(cn^3)}\,\psi(cn^3)=\gamma_2(c)\,\overline{\alpha(cn^3)}^{\,k+1}\eta(cn^3).
\end{equation}
Proposition~\ref{prop:cm-rows} is formal in $\psi(p)$, so the rows are again
$\zeta_K^S(6z)L^S(w,\chi_\bullet(u))/L^S(x,\psi\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)})$. The numerators still have finite order,
the denominators have infinity type $(\bar z/|z|)^k$, and the slot amplitudes are unchanged.

\paragraph{2. The theta step with $j=k+1$ derivatives.} Every formula of Section~\ref{sec:theta} and of the
table in Section~\ref{sec:78probe} has a version for all $j\ge1$, as follows.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Chain rule.} At $z=0$ the coordinate $z'$ is affine in $\bar z$, while $\bar z'$ and $v'$ do not
 depend on $\bar z$. Hence, exactly,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:d090}
  \left.\partial_{\bar z}^{\,j}F(z',\bar z',v')\right|_{z=\bar z=0}
  =\bigl(-(cv)^{-2}\bigr)^{j}\,(\partial_{z'}^{\,j}F)\bigl(-\delta/c,\,1/(\N(c)v)\bigr).
 \end{equation}
 Every derivative of order $j\ge1$ kills the constant term at the identity cusp. We checked this
 identity symbolically for $j\le4$.
\item \emph{Direct Mellin transform.} For a nonzero Fourier mode and $\Re s>\frac16-\frac j2$,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:d091}
  \int_0^\infty(2\pi i\bar\ell)^j\,vK_{1/3}(4\pi|\ell|v)\,v^{2s+j-2}\,dv
  =\frac{i^j}4(2\pi)^{-2s}\,\Gamma\Bigl(s+\frac j2-\frac16\Bigr)\Gamma\Bigl(s+\frac j2+\frac16\Bigr)
   \overline{\alpha(\ell)}^{\,j}\N(\ell)^{-s}.
 \end{equation}
 The integral converges absolutely in this half-plane; elsewhere the right side gives its
 meromorphic continuation. For $j=1,2$ this gives the two entries of the table. We checked it numerically for $j\le5$ (relative
 error below $10^{-25}$).
\item \emph{Reflected side.} The same substitution gives $\prod_\pm\Gamma(\frac{j+2}2-s\pm\frac16)$, the cusp
 factor $\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,2j}$, and the dual angular factor $\alpha(\ell)^j$.
\item \emph{Kernel.} Write
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:d092}
  R_j(t)=\prod_\pm\frac{\Gamma(\frac{j+1}2+t\pm\frac16)}{\Gamma(\frac{j+1}2-t\pm\frac16)} .
 \end{equation}
 Then $R_1$ and $R_2$ are the two kernels of the table. $R_j$ is holomorphic for
 $\Re t>\frac16-\frac{j+1}2$, which is to the left of $-1/4$ for every $j\ge1$, and
 $|R_j(\sigma+iu)|\ll_{j,A}(1+|u|)^{4\sigma}$ on $-1/4\le\sigma\le A$ (checked numerically for $j\le5$). So the
 reflected weight obeys the same bound $\ll\min(x^{1/4},x^{-A})$, with constants depending on $j$.
 There is no uniform bound as $j$ grows: for example,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:d093}
  R_j(1)=\frac{(3j-4)(3j-2)(3j+2)(3j+4)}{1296}\sim\frac{j^4}{16}.
 \end{equation}
\end{itemize}
The dual lengths are unchanged, because the reflected argument $\N(\ell)X/\N(c)^2$ does not depend on
$j$. In the quadratic large sieve, $\alpha(\ell)^j$ is a unimodular coefficient of the dual index. Hence
Proposition~\ref{prop:cm-completed} and the low estimates $Z^{1/4+\varepsilon}$ and $Z^{3/16+\varepsilon}$ should hold
for every $k$.

\paragraph{3. Growth.} The functional equation has $\Gamma(s+\frac k2)$ in place of $\Gamma(s+\frac12)$. The
exponent of $|\Gamma(1-s+\frac k2)/\Gamma(s+\frac k2)|$ on $\Re s=\sigma$ is $1-2\sigma$, independent of $k$, so
Lemma~\ref{lem:cm-growth} keeps its exponents. The theta series now has the harmonic polynomial
$\bar a^{\,k}$. With the Fourier convention $e(-a\xi)$ of Appendix~\ref{app:s2-hecke},
its Poisson transform is a multiple of $\xi^ke^{-c'|\xi|^2/v}v^{-1-k}$, which vanishes at $\xi=0$,
so $L(s,\psi)$ is entire.

\paragraph{4. Moments.} In the inverse moment the co-oriented classes become
$\mu\overline\alpha^{\,k}\leftrightarrow\overline\alpha^{\,k+1}\gamma_2$. This follows from the same identity
$\mu\gamma_{-1}=\chi(-1)G^{-1}\overline\alpha\gamma_2$, and the cube factor becomes $\overline\alpha^{\,3(k+1)}$. The theta steps
use $\partial_{\bar z}^{\,k+1}\theta$. In the plain moment, Hecke reflection uses
$\Gamma(s+\frac k2)/\Gamma(1-s+\frac k2)$, a Hankel kernel of order $k$ with the same vertical growth. The
lattice main terms vanish because $\int\alpha(z)^{\pm k}f(|z|^2)\,dz=0$.

\paragraph{5. Unchanged.} The following carry over verbatim:
\begin{itemize}
\item the label sets, with $\overline\alpha^{\,k}\Theta_0\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)}$ and $\alpha^k\Theta_0\chi_\bullet(u)$;
\item the slot normalization $\overline{\psi(p)}$;
\item $\kappa=3/4$ from OpenAI's theorem;
\item every exponent in the final inequality, and hence the endpoint certificate.
\end{itemize}
For Part~I the targets are rows of the same kind. In the orientation $\muk(n)\alpha(n)^k\chi_n(u_0)$ of
Part~I, the row is $u_0=1$ for $\eta(3z)^8$ and $u_0=4$ for $\eta(2z)^3\eta(6z)^3$.

\subsection{Remarks}
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{$k=0$ is OpenAI's case.} There $j=1$ and the weight is $\gamma_2\overline\alpha\eta$. The recipe ``target
 $\overline\alpha^{\,k}\eta$, $k+1$ theta derivatives'' therefore covers unitary Hecke characters of $K$ with
 purely angular infinity type; negative $k$ reduces to positive $k$ by conjugation. A general unitary
 Hecke character may also have a continuous norm factor $\N^{it}$, with $t\in\mathbb R$. Since
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:d094}
  L(s,\psi\N^{it})=L(s-it,\psi),
 \end{equation}
 these vertical shifts leave the proposed zero-free half-plane unchanged.
\item \emph{Orientation.} The conjugate target $\alpha^k\bar\eta$ would leave $\alpha^{k-1}$ in the weight. For $k=1$
 that is no angular factor at all: the theta function without a derivative, whose constant terms are
 not controlled. That is why Part~II uses $\overline\alpha\eta$. For $k\ge2$ the factor $\alpha^{k-1}$ is produced by
 $\partial_z^{\,k-1}$. The chain rule is symmetric: at $z=\bar z=0$, $\partial_z^{\,j}$ becomes
 $(-(\bar cv)^{-2})^j\partial_{\bar z'}^{\,j}$, exactly; we checked this symbolically for $j\le3$. So for $k\ge2$
 either orientation should work; we use $\overline\alpha^{\,k}\eta$ for uniformity.
\item \emph{Symmetric powers.} For a CM curve $E_d$ and $m\ge1$, put
 $L_{\rm an}(\mathrm{Sym}^mE_d,s)=L(\mathrm{Sym}^mE_d,s+m/2)$.
 Up to finitely many Euler factors, the factorization is
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:d095}
  L_{\rm an}(\mathrm{Sym}^mE_d,s)
  =\prod_{0\le i<m/2}L(s,(\psi_d^*)^{m-2i})
   \begin{cases}
    1,&m\text{ odd},\\
    L(s,\chi_{-3}^{\,m/2}),&m\text{ even}.
   \end{cases}
 \end{equation}
 The last factor is $\zeta(s)$ when $m\equiv0\pmod4$ and $L(s,\chi_{-3})$ when
 $m\equiv2\pmod4$, with the trivial character interpreted primitively. This is the CM
 factorization of~\cite[\S3.5]{MW}. The positive-power factors are of the type above; the remaining
 factor is covered by OpenAI's finite-order result. Together these would give a zero-free
 half-plane $\Re s>7/8$ in analytic normalization. When $4\mid m$, the function has a pole at $s=1$:
 ``zero-free'' here is a statement about a meromorphic function, not a claim of holomorphy throughout
 the half-plane. These $L$-functions are not primitive for $m\ge2$.
\item \emph{What would still have to be written.} The six statements of Section~\ref{sec:78status}, proved here
 for $k=1$, are needed for each $k$, with $\partial_{\bar z}^{\,k+1}$ in place of $\partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}$. We see no step where $k$
 enters other than through $j=k+1$ and the Gamma factors. The constants depend on $k$, but $k$ is fixed.
\end{itemize}
The numerical checks of this section were made with short Python scripts.

\section{Numerical checks}\label{sec:numerics}

Every numbered display of these notes was catalogued and checked where a computation can check it: identities
and explicit inequalities were tested on finite samples, and estimates received size checks where feasible.
For twelve estimates not even a size check is possible. Testing every display is overkill, but we wanted to catch mistakes of every size: wrong normalizations,
sign errors, dropped factors and careless slips. Section~\ref{sec:numerics-displays} lists all $342$ numbered displays, and for each one says what was
done and what was found. Section~\ref{sec:numerics-limits} says what the checks do not establish.

The checks use Python and the conventions of these notes. Elements of $\OO$ are written as pairs $A+B\omega$.
Primary means $\equiv1\pmod3$, $\alpha(n)=n/|n|$, $u_d=(4d)^5$, and $\chi_n(x)=(x/n)_6$ is computed by Euler's
criterion at each prime. Gauss sums are computed by brute force over complete residue systems. Point counts and
Dirichlet coefficients of the curves are computed by brute force and compared with PARI/GP~\cite{PARI}.

Many comparisons are exact: in $\OO$ or $\mathbb Q$, or, as in
Sections~\ref{sec:integer}--\ref{sec:sixth}, in $\OO$ after clearing the factors $\sqrt m$ of $\alpha$. The
others, including most identities involving $\alpha$ after Section~\ref{sec:sixth}, are computed in double
precision, and so are many integrals. Some Mellin and Bessel integrals use mpmath~\cite{mpmath} at $30$ to $40$ digits, and
symbolic checks use SymPy~\cite{SymPy}. Some functional equations are checked only by comparing values from PARI's
continuation; these are consistency checks, and the table says so. Zero counts come from the argument principle on
finitely many boundary samples, and maxima are taken over sampled points; such results hold on the tested grid
only.

The scripts are in a directory \texttt{tests\_cm/}, which will be posted with the source. The display tests are in its subfolder
\texttt{displays\_2026-10-13}, in nine groups \texttt{t1}--\texttt{t9}. The file \texttt{rows.json} of each group
names the script behind every row of the table below, and \texttt{run\_all.sh} reruns all of them in about half an
hour, including the earlier suites in the other subfolders of \texttt{tests\_cm/} on which they rely. Every runner
exits with a nonzero status if any check fails, including failures in nested suites. The script
\texttt{check\_runner\_propagation.py} verifies this by making each script, in turn, fail.

\subsection{Every numbered display}\label{sec:numerics-displays}

Each numbered display is of one of six kinds, and the kind determines what a computation can establish.
\begin{itemize}
\item[E] \emph{Identity.} An identity or an exact evaluation. It was tested on finite samples, exactly in $\OO$ or
 $\mathbb Q$, numerically in double or multiprecision arithmetic, or symbolically.
\item[I] \emph{Explicit inequality.} An inequality between computable quantities or exponents, with explicit
 constants. It was tested on finite samples or symbolically, in the same way.
\item[A] \emph{Estimate.} A bound with unspecified constants, or an asymptotic statement. No finite computation can
 verify it. Where feasible it received a \emph{size check}: the ratio of the two sides was computed over a finite
 range and seen not to grow. Otherwise it is marked ``not testable by computation''. Each is proved in the text, at the display or where its
 row indicates, except~\eqref{eq:d087}, which is an expectation (Section~\ref{sec:higherweight}).
\item[D] \emph{Definition.} Nothing is asserted. The table says how the definition was implemented in the tests,
 and what consistency checks were made.
\item[C] \emph{Cited result.} A display quoted from another paper. It was compared with its source.
\item[R] \emph{Restatement.} A display that repeats another one, or a special case of it. It is covered by the
 row of the display it restates.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}llrl@{}}
\toprule
Kind & & Displays & Outcome\\\midrule
E & identity & 185 & all pass\\
I & explicit inequality & 22 & all pass\\
A & estimate & 44 & 32 size checks, all consistent; 12 not testable by computation\\
D & definition & 72 & implemented in the tests\\
C & cited result & 6 & agrees with its source\\
R & restatement & 13 & covered by the restated display\\\midrule
 & total & 342 & \\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

No display was found to be wrong. A test verifies only the instances it computes; the estimates of kind~A
rest on their proofs, and~\eqref{eq:d087} is not claimed.

In the table, \emph{max err} is the largest absolute error over the tested instances, unless it is marked relative;
\emph{exact} means equality in exact arithmetic, and is used for nothing else. For a size check, the result column gives the observed ratio to the
claimed bound, whose implied constant is unknown. Identifiers such as A2.07, B30, K9 or S4.6 name individual tests in
the suites listed above.

\subsection{What the checks do not establish}\label{sec:numerics-limits}

No computation verifies an estimate with unspecified constants. This applies to the $44$ displays of kind~A,
among them the mean square~\eqref{eq:MS}, the completed estimate~\eqref{eq:completed}, cube
removal~\eqref{eq:remove}, the transfer estimate~\eqref{eq:transferprop}, and their Part~II counterparts; $12$ of
them are not testable by computation even for size. The Part~I estimates are proved in Sections~\ref{sec:poisson},
\ref{sec:sieve} and~\ref{sec:descent} and Appendix~\ref{app:reflection}, using the large sieve~\eqref{eq:qls}, which
is cited from~\cite{GL}. The Part~II statements are proved in Appendices~\ref{app:s1-general}--\ref{app:s6}. In both
parts the exact identities used in the proofs were tested, as the table shows. In particular, the random descent
chains behind~\eqref{eq:contract} and~\eqref{eq:survive} check scale inequalities under the displayed transition
formulas, not the transfer estimate itself. The one display of kind~A that is
not proved is~\eqref{eq:d087}, the expectation of Section~\ref{sec:higherweight}; its size check, which finds no
zeros of six such $L$-functions in a box, is evidence only.
A few remarks on presentation are recorded with the scripts.

\appendix
\section{The CM transformation formula}\label{app:reflection}

This appendix states the CM transformation formula precisely and proves it for the twists of Part~I.
Appendix~\ref{app:s1-general} extends it to the generality used in Part~II.
The coefficient data are tabulated in~\cite[Theorem~23]{QF}, so every
series in the theta step is given in finite-sum terms. It can be skipped on a first reading. The
imported theta function is that of Kubota and Patterson~\cite{Kub69,Pat77} in the
form~\cite[Theorem~23]{QF}, which follows~\cite[Section~5.1 and
Appendix]{DR} and the conventions of~\cite{OAI1112}; see the paragraph
``What is imported'' in Section~\ref{sec:theta}.
Only these theta facts are used from~\cite{DR}; its GRH-conditional
prime Gauss-sum asymptotic is not an input here.

Let $\xi_0$ be a character of $\mathfrak G$, extended by zero to
elements that are not primary or not coprime to $6$. Let $\mathcal P$ be
a finite set of primary primes coprime to $6$, and let $0\le j_\pi\le5$
for $\pi\in\mathcal P$. Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d096}
 \Psi(n)=\xi_0(n)\prod_{\pi\in\mathcal P}\chi_\pi(n)^{j_\pi},
\end{equation}
with the convention $\chi_\pi(n)^0=\ind_{\pi\nmid n}$. Then $\Psi$ is
completely multiplicative on primary elements and vanishes unless
$(n,6)=1$. Write $T(X;\Psi)$ for the right side of~\eqref{eq:T} with this
$\Psi$. By Section~\ref{sec:theta}, $T(X;k,f)$ has this form. Fix a
compact interval $I\subset(0,\infty)$, and let $W\in C_c^\infty(I)$ and
$V(y)=\sqrt y\,W(y)$.

\begin{proposition}[CM transformation formula]\label{prop:b3-reflection}
For every $X>0$, $T(X;\Psi)$ is a sum of at most
$5184\cdot2^{|\mathcal P|}$ terms of the form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reflection}
 C\sum_{0\ne\ell\in\lambda^{-4}\OO}
 \frac{d_\sigma(\ell)\alpha(\ell)^2}{\sqrt{\N(\ell)}}
 \psi(\lambda^4\ell)\prod_{\pi\in\mathcal A}B_{\pi,j_\pi}(\lambda^4\ell)
 V^\sharp\left(\frac{\N(\ell)X}{\N(c)^2}\right).
\end{equation}
In each term, $\sigma\in\{0,+,-\}$ and $d_\sigma$ is the coefficient
sequence of $\overline\theta$ at the cusp $\gamma_\sigma\infty$;
$\mathcal A$ is a set with
$\{\pi\in\mathcal P:j_\pi\ne0\}\subseteq\mathcal A\subseteq\mathcal P$;
$c=c_0\prod_{\pi\in\mathcal A}\pi$, where $c_0$ divides $8\lambda^2$ up to
a unit; $\psi(x)=e(\beta x/(\lambda^3c_0))$ for some $\beta\in\OO$; and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d097}
 C=-\frac{\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}}{81}\,C',\qquad|C'|\le1.
\end{equation}
Each series converges absolutely. The weight $V^\sharp$ and the local
factors $B_{\pi,j}$ are those of~\eqref{eq:weight} and~\eqref{eq:local}.
\end{proposition}

The scalar $C'$ collects a finite Fourier coefficient of the
residue-class twist, the conjugated Kubota character and the local
phases; it is given explicitly in~\eqref{eq:b3-C}. This is the CM form
of~\cite[Proposition~6.2]{OAI1112}, a variant of the theta transformation of~\cite[Section~5]{DR}, which extends work of Patterson~\cite{Pat77} and Yoshimoto~\cite{Yos87}. Relative to the zeta case, $\alpha(\ell)$
becomes $\alpha(\ell)^2$, the prefactor
$-\frac i{81}\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,2}$ becomes
$-\frac1{81}\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}$, and the gamma factors of the
reflected weight are shifted by $\frac12$. The proof is in
Section~\ref{sec:b3-proof}. Lemma~\ref{lem:b3-uniform} shows how the
terms vary with the twist.

The coefficient sequences are explicit. The coefficients $\tau$ of
$\theta$ at $\infty$ and $\tau_1,\tau_2$ at the other two cusps (the notation
of~\cite{QF}; elsewhere $\tau$ is the divisor function) are
tabulated in~\cite[Theorem~23, (5.7), (5.9), (5.10)]{QF} in terms of the
cubic Gauss sums $g(r,c)$ of~\cite[(5.5)]{QF}; there $\sqrt{-3}$ is our
$\lambda$, so $g(-3,c)=g(\lambda^2,c)$, and $c$ is primary, squarefree and
possibly even. With them,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d098}
 t_0(\ell)=\tau(\ell),\qquad
 t_+(\ell)=\omega\tau_2(\omega\ell)\ce(\ell),\qquad
 t_-(\ell)=\omega^2\tau_1(\omega^2\ell)\ce(\ell),\qquad
 d_\sigma(\ell)=\overline{t_\sigma(-\ell)}.
\end{equation}
These are precisely the coefficients of the conjugated theta functions
at the three cusps. Their support is contained in the set of indices
$\ell=u\lambda^mnb^3$, with $u$ a unit, $m\ge-4$, $n$ squarefree,
and $n,b$ primary (the tables of~\cite{QF} impose further restrictions);
this representation is unique, and, by~\cite[Corollary~24]{QF}
and~\cite[Lemma~6.4]{OAI1112},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d099}
 |d_\sigma(u\lambda^mnb^3)|\le27\cdot3^{m/6}|b|.
\end{equation}
In the varying quadratic symbol in~\eqref{eq:reflection}, the factor
$b^3$ contributes $(b/k)_2$, since $(b^3/k)_2=(b/k)_2$.
Fixing $u,m,b$ leaves squarefree columns $n$, exactly as required for
\eqref{eq:qls}. This is why the explicit support of the theta
coefficients matters to the averaging argument.

\subsection{Uniformity in the twist and decay of the weight}\label{sec:b3-lemmas}

The sieve of Section~\ref{sec:sieve} averages
Proposition~\ref{prop:b3-reflection} over squarefree rows $k_0$. For this
the cusp data must be chosen consistently as $k_0$ varies, and the
reflected weight must decay.

\begin{lemma}[Uniformity in the twist]\label{lem:b3-uniform}
Fix $\xi_0$, a finite set $\mathcal P_{\rm fix}$ of primary primes
coprime to $6$, and exponents $0\le j_\pi\le5$ for
$\pi\in\mathcal P_{\rm fix}$. For squarefree primary $k_0$ coprime to
$6\prod_{\pi\in\mathcal P_{\rm fix}}\pi$ put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d100}
 \Psi_{k_0}(n)=\xi_0(n)\prod_{\pi\in\mathcal P_{\rm fix}}\chi_\pi(n)^{j_\pi}
 \prod_{\pi\mid k_0}\chi_\pi(n).
\end{equation}
The terms of Proposition~\ref{prop:b3-reflection} for $\Psi_{k_0}$ can
be indexed by pairs $(h_0,\mathcal A_{\rm fix})$, with $h_0\in\OO/72\OO$
and
$\{\pi\in\mathcal P_{\rm fix}:j_\pi\ne0\}\subseteq\mathcal A_{\rm fix}
\subseteq\mathcal P_{\rm fix}$, so that
$\mathcal A=\mathcal A_{\rm fix}\cup\{\pi:\pi\mid k_0\}$ and
$c=c_0k_0\prod_{\pi\in\mathcal A_{\rm fix}}\pi$. For each index, the
data $\sigma$, $\psi$ and $c_0$ depend on $k_0$ only through its residue
class modulo $M^2$, where $M=\lambda^{12}72^4$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
This is~\cite[Lemma~6.3]{OAI1112} for the present family, and its proof
at the end of~\cite[App.~A.2]{OAI1112} applies verbatim. Every prime
dividing $k_0$ has exponent $1$, so it is active, and
$r=k_0\prod_{\pi\in\mathcal A_{\rm fix}}\pi$ in Step~2 of
Section~\ref{sec:b3-proof}. The data $c_0$, $\sigma$, $\kappa_0$ and
$\psi$ of that step depend only on $h_0$, $\mathcal A_{\rm fix}$ and $r\bmod M^2$.
They are fixed before any derivative is taken, so the number of
derivatives plays no role. The scalar $C$ depends on $k_0$, also through
$\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}$, but only the bound $|C|\le1/81$ is used,
as in the zeta case.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Decay of the reflected weight]\label{lem:b3-decay}
Let $I\subset(0,\infty)$ be a compact interval, $W\in C_c^\infty(I)$ and
$V(y)=\sqrt y\,W(y)$. For every $A>0$ and every integer $j\ge0$ there is
$J=J(A,j)$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d101}
 \bigl|(x\partial_x)^jV^\sharp(x)\bigr|
 \ll_{A,j,I}\|W\|_{C^J(I)}\min(x^{1/4},x^{-A})\qquad(x>0),
\end{equation}
where $\|W\|_{C^J(I)}=\max_{0\le i\le J}\sup_I|W^{(i)}|$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $R(t)$ be the gamma quotient in~\eqref{eq:weight}. Its numerator has
poles only at $t=-\frac43-k$ and $t=-\frac53-k$ ($k\ge0$), and $1/\Gamma$
is entire, so $R$ is holomorphic in $\Re t>-\frac43$. Stirling's formula~\cite[(5.11.9)]{DLMF}
$|\Gamma(x+iy)|=\sqrt{2\pi}\,|y|^{x-1/2}e^{-\pi|y|/2}(1+O(1/|y|))$, uniform
for $x$ in compact sets and $|y|\ge1$, gives
$|\Gamma(a+\eta+iu)/\Gamma(a-\eta-iu)|\asymp|u|^{2\eta}$ for
$a\in\{\frac43,\frac53\}$ and $|u|\ge1$. With continuity for $|u|\le1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b3-stirling}
 |R(\eta+iu)|\ll_A(1+|u|)^{4\eta}\qquad(-\tfrac14\le\eta\le A,\ u\in\mathbb R).
\end{equation}
The zeta kernel $\Gamma(\frac76+t)\Gamma(\frac56+t)/\bigl(\Gamma(\frac76-t)\Gamma(\frac56-t)\bigr)$ has the same
exponent and is holomorphic in $\Re t>-\frac56$; both regions contain
$\Re t\ge-\frac14$, which is all that is used. Next,
$\widehat V(-t)=\int_IV(y)y^{-t-1}dy$ is entire. For $|\Im t|\ge1$,
$k$ integrations by parts give
$|\widehat V(-t)|\ll_{k,A,I}\|V\|_{C^k(I)}(1+|t|)^{-k}$ uniformly for
$-\frac14\le\Re t\le A$, and the same bound is trivial for $|\Im t|\le1$.
Also $\|V\|_{C^k(I)}\ll_{k,I}\|W\|_{C^k(I)}$, since $\sqrt y$ and its
derivatives are bounded on $I$.

The integrand of~\eqref{eq:weight} therefore decays faster than any
power of $|\Im t|$ in the strip $-\frac14\le\Re t\le A$. We move the
contour to $\Re t=-\frac14$ if $x\le1$ and to $\Re t=A$ if $x\ge1$,
crossing no poles. Each application of $x\partial_x$ multiplies the
integrand by $-t$. With $Y=(2\pi)^4x/27$ and $\eta=-\frac14$ or $A$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d102}
 \bigl|(x\partial_x)^jV^\sharp(x)\bigr|
 \le\frac{Y^{-\eta}}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb R}|\eta+iu|^j\,
 |\widehat V(-\eta-iu)|\,|R(\eta+iu)|\,du .
\end{equation}
By~\eqref{eq:b3-stirling} and the bound for $\widehat V$ with
$k=J:=\lceil4A\rceil+j+2$, the integral is
$\ll_{A,j,I}\|W\|_{C^J(I)}$. Since $Y\asymp x$, this proves the lemma.
The argument is that of~\cite[(A.20)]{OAI1112}, with the CM kernel.
\end{proof}

In particular, suppose $1\le X,\N(c)\le D^{C_0}$ and
$\|W\|_{C^J(I)}\ll1$ for $J$ large. Then, in~\eqref{eq:reflection}, the
terms with $\N(\ell)X/\N(c)^2\ge D^\varepsilon$ contribute $O(D^{-B})$ for
every $B>0$. This follows from Lemma~\ref{lem:b3-decay} with $A$ large,
the coefficient bound for $d_\sigma$ and
$\prod_{\pi\in\mathcal A}|B_{\pi,j_\pi}|\le\N(c)^{1/2}$, as at the end of
Section~\ref{sec:b3-proof}. Since $\N(\ell)\ge3^{-4}$, the whole series is
negligible when $X\ge81D^\varepsilon\N(c)^2$.

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:b3-reflection}}\label{sec:b3-proof}

We follow the proof of~\cite[Proposition~6.2]{OAI1112} given
in~\cite[App.~A.2]{OAI1112}. Steps~1 and~2 construct the twisted theta
series and choose the cusp data. They do not involve the derivative, so
we quote them and record what they produce. Steps~3--6 are the Mellin
transform, the chain rule at a cusp, the functional equation and the
assembly. This is where the second derivative enters, and we write
these steps out.

Throughout, $\partial_{\bar z}$ and $\partial_z$ are the Wirtinger
derivatives $\frac12(\partial_{\Re z}\pm i\partial_{\Im z})$, so that
$\partial_{\bar z}\ce(\ell z)=2\pi i\bar\ell\,\ce(\ell z)$ and
$\partial_z\ce(\ell z)=2\pi i\ell\,\ce(\ell z)$. The Mellin integral of
the Bessel function is~\cite[(5.21)]{QF} (see~\cite[(10.43.19)]{DLMF})
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b3-bessel}
 \int_0^\infty v^{2s}K_{1/3}(4\pi|\ell|v)\,dv
 =2^{2s-1}\Gamma(s+\tfrac13)\Gamma(s+\tfrac23)(4\pi|\ell|)^{-2s-1}
 \qquad(\Re s>-\tfrac13).
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Step 1: the twisted series and its translates.} Let $L=72$.
In~\cite[Appendix~A.2]{OAI1112} $L$ is any modulus divisible by sufficiently
high powers of $2$ and $3$; $L=72$ suffices here. In that proof $L$ enters only through the
periodicity of $\phi$ below, which holds modulo $72$, and through $M=\lambda^{12}L^4$, which for $L=72$
is the rational integer $729\cdot72^4$, with $v_\lambda(M)=28$ and $v_2(M)=12$. The congruences of
Step~2 are imposed modulo the powers of $\lambda$ and $2$ dividing $Mc_0$, with $c_0$ as in Step~2(a),
and modulo the active primes. We checked
Step~2(a)--(e) with $L=72$ and this $M$ in exact arithmetic, for groups covering every cusp type
(Section~\ref{sec:numerics}).
Define $\phi:\OO\to\mathbb C$ by $\phi(x)=(\lambda/x)_3\,\xi_0(x)$ if $x$
is primary and coprime to $6$, and $\phi(x)=0$ otherwise. Since
$(\lambda/x)_3$ depends only on $x\bmod9$, $\phi$ is periodic modulo $L$,
and $|\phi|\le1$. Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d103}
 \Theta_\Psi(z,v)=\sum_{0\ne\ell\in\lambda^{-3}\OO}d_0(\ell)\,
 \phi(\lambda^3\ell)\prod_{\pi\in\mathcal P}\chi_\pi(\lambda^3\ell)^{j_\pi}\,
 vK_{1/3}(4\pi|\ell|v)\,\ce(\ell z).
\end{equation}
By~\eqref{eq:theta-coeff} and the selection statement after it, the
coefficient of $\Theta_\Psi$ at $\ell$ vanishes unless $\lambda^3\ell=nb^3$
with $n$ squarefree and $n,b$ primary and coprime to $6$. This
representation is unique, and the coefficient is then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b3-coeff}
 3^{5/2}|b|\,\overline{(\lambda/n)_3}\,\gamma_2(n)\cdot
 (\lambda/nb^3)_3\,\Psi(nb^3)
 =3^{5/2}|b|\,\gamma_2(n)\Psi(n)\Psi(b)^3,
\end{equation}
because $(\lambda/b^3)_3=1$ and $\Psi$ is completely multiplicative. For
$h_0\in\OO/L\OO$ and $h\in\OO/\pi\OO$ put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d104}
 \widehat\phi(h_0)=\frac1{\N(L)}\sum_{x\bmod L}\phi(x)\,
 e\Bigl(-\frac{h_0x}L\Bigr),\qquad
 C_{\pi,j}(h)=\frac1{\N(\pi)}\sum_{y\bmod\pi}\chi_\pi(y)^j\,
 e\Bigl(-\frac{hy}\pi\Bigr).
\end{equation}
Finite Fourier expansion~\cite[(4.5)]{QF} of $\phi$ modulo $L$ and of
$\chi_\pi^{j_\pi}$ modulo $\pi$ gives, as
in~\cite[Lemma~6.1 and (A.2)--(A.5)]{OAI1112},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b3-translates}
 \Theta_\Psi(z,v)=\sum_{\mathbf h}c_{\rm F}(\mathbf h)\,
 \overline{\theta(z+z_{\mathbf h},v)},\qquad
 z_{\mathbf h}=\lambda^2\Bigl(\frac{h_0}L+\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal P}
 \frac{h_\pi}\pi\Bigr),\qquad
 c_{\rm F}(\mathbf h)=\widehat\phi(h_0)\prod_{\pi\in\mathcal P}
 C_{\pi,j_\pi}(h_\pi),
\end{equation}
where $\mathbf h=(h_0,(h_\pi)_{\pi\in\mathcal P})$ runs over
$\OO/L\OO\times\prod_\pi\OO/\pi\OO$. Indeed, multiplying the mode
$\ce(\ell z)$ by $e(\lambda^3\ell\eta)=\ce(\lambda^2\ell\eta)$ (recall $e(x)=\ce(x/\lambda)$) translates
$z$ by $\lambda^2\eta$. The constant terms $\frac{3^{5/2}}2v^{2/3}$ of
the translates cancel, because
$\sum_{\mathbf h}c_{\rm F}(\mathbf h)
=\phi(0)\prod_\pi\chi_\pi(0)^{j_\pi}=0$.

We have $|\widehat\phi(h_0)|\le1$, $C_{\pi,j}(0)=0$ for $j\ne0$, and
$C_{\pi,0}(0)=1-\N(\pi)^{-1}$~\cite[(A.5)]{OAI1112}. So
$c_{\rm F}(\mathbf h)\ne0$ forces the active set
$\mathcal A(\mathbf h)=\{\pi:h_\pi\ne0\}$ to contain every $\pi$ with
$j_\pi\ne0$. Group the $\mathbf h$ according to
$(h_0,\mathcal A(\mathbf h))$. There are at most
$\N(L)\,2^{|\mathcal P|}=5184\cdot2^{|\mathcal P|}$ groups. Changing
$h_\pi$ modulo $\pi$ moves $z_{\mathbf h}$ by an element of
$\lambda^2\OO=3\OO$, under which $\theta$ is invariant by (T1). So we may
take representatives with $h_\pi\equiv0\pmod{M^2}$, where
$M=\lambda^{12}L^4$.

\paragraph{Step 2: the cusp data.} Fix a group $(h_0,\mathcal A)$ and put
$r=\prod_{\pi\in\mathcal A}\pi$. The following facts are proved
in~\cite[App.~A.2]{OAI1112}, in the paragraphs on reduced denominators
and on the local character transformation (see also~\cite[(5.18)]{QF}).
They concern only the translates $\overline{\theta(z+z_{\mathbf h},v)}$,
not their derivatives, so they apply without change. For each
$\mathbf h$ in the group:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] $z_{\mathbf h}=a/c$ in lowest terms, normalized by a common unit so that
 $a\equiv1\pmod3$ if $\lambda\mid c$ and $c\equiv1\pmod3$ otherwise (this fixes the unit in
 $\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}$ in~\eqref{eq:b3-C}), where $c=c_0r$ is the same
 for the whole group and $c_0$ is an associate of the reduced denominator
 of $\lambda^2h_0/L=h_0/(8\lambda^2)$. In particular $c_0$ divides
 $8\lambda^2$ up to a unit. For $\pi\in\mathcal A$,
 $a\equiv\sigma_\pi h_\pi\pmod\pi$ with $\sigma_\pi=\lambda^2c/\pi$.
\item[(b)] There are $\delta\in\OO$ with $a\delta\equiv1\pmod c$, a matrix
 $H\in\mathrm{SL}_2(\OO)$ and $\sigma\in\{0,+,-\}$ such that
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:d105}
  g=\begin{pmatrix}a&(a\delta-1)/c\\c&\delta\end{pmatrix}
  \in\mathrm{SL}_2(\OO),\qquad
  g_1=gH^{-1}\in\Gamma(3),\qquad
  \theta(HP)=\theta(\gamma_\sigma P)\quad(P\in\mathbb H^3).
 \end{equation}
 The label $\sigma$ and the residue of $\delta$ modulo $\lambda^3c_0$ are
 the same for the whole group. Since $\theta(gP)=\kappa(g_1)\theta(HP)$
 by (T1),
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:b3-automorphy}
  \overline{\theta(a/c+z,v)}
  =\overline{\kappa(g_1)}\,F_\sigma\bigl(g^{-1}(a/c+z,v)\bigr),
  \qquad F_\sigma(P)=\overline{\theta(\gamma_\sigma P)}.
 \end{equation}
\item[(c)] $\kappa(g_1)=\kappa_0\prod_{\pi\in\mathcal A}\chi_\pi(a)^2$,
 where $|\kappa_0|=1$ and $\kappa_0$ is the same for the whole
 group~\cite[(A.10)]{OAI1112}.
\item[(d)] For $\ell\in\lambda^{-4}\OO$,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:b3-crt}
  \ce\Bigl(-\frac{\delta\ell}c\Bigr)=\psi(\lambda^4\ell)
  \prod_{\pi\in\mathcal A}e\Bigl(\frac{\epsilon_\pi h_\pi^{-1}\lambda^4\ell}
  \pi\Bigr),
 \end{equation}
 where $\psi(x)=e(\beta x/(\lambda^3c_0))$ with $\beta\in\OO$ the same for
 the whole group, and $\epsilon_\pi\in(\OO/\pi\OO)^\times$ depends only
 on $c$ and $\pi$~\cite[(A.11)]{OAI1112}.
\item[(e)] For $\pi\in\mathcal A$, $0\le j\le5$ and $x\in\OO$,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:b3-local}
  \sum_{h\not\equiv0\ (\pi)}C_{\pi,j}(h)\,\chi_\pi(\sigma_\pi h)^{-2}\,
  e\Bigl(\frac{\epsilon_\pi h^{-1}x}\pi\Bigr)
  =\chi_\pi(\sigma_\pi)^{-2}\varpi_{\pi,j}B_{\pi,j}(x),
  \qquad|\varpi_{\pi,j}|=1,
 \end{equation}
 with $B_{\pi,j}$ as in~\eqref{eq:local}. This
 is~\cite[(A.12)]{OAI1112}, where $\varpi_{\pi,j}$ is called
 $\omega_{p,j}$.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Step 3: the Mellin transform at the identity cusp.} For
$\Re w>1$ put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d106}
 \mathcal T(w)=\Bigl(\ssum_n\frac{\overline{\alpha(n)}^{\,2}\gamma_2(n)
 \Psi(n)}{\N(n)^w}\Bigr)
 \Bigl(\sum_b\frac{\overline{\alpha(b)}^{\,6}\Psi(b)^3}{\N(b)^{3w-1/2}}\Bigr),
\end{equation}
a product of absolutely convergent series. The summand
of~\eqref{eq:T} is the coefficient times
$V(\N(n)\N(b)^3/X)/(\sqrt{\N(n)}\,\N(b))$. Inserting
$V(y)=\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{(\sigma_1)}\widehat V(w-\frac12)y^{1/2-w}dw$,
where $\int_{(\sigma)}$ runs upward along $\Re w=\sigma$, gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b3-inversion}
 T(X;\Psi)=\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{(\sigma_1)}\widehat V(w-\tfrac12)\,
 \mathcal T(w)\,X^{w-1/2}\,dw\qquad(\sigma_1>1).
\end{equation}

The series for $\Theta_\Psi$ has polynomially bounded coefficients, and
$K_{1/3}(x)\ll e^{-x}$ for $x\ge1$~\cite[(10.25.3)]{DLMF}, so it may be differentiated termwise.
Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d107}
 f(v)=\partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}\Theta_\Psi(z,v)\big|_{z=0},\qquad
 \mathcal J(s)=\int_0^\infty f(v)\,v^{2s-1}dv.
\end{equation}
Since $(2\pi i\bar\ell)^2=-4\pi^2\N(\ell)\overline{\alpha(\ell)}^{\,2}$,
\eqref{eq:b3-bessel} shows that a mode $\ell$ contributes its coefficient
times
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b3-mode}
 -\frac\pi2(2\pi)^{-2s}\Gamma(s+\tfrac13)\Gamma(s+\tfrac23)\,
 \overline{\alpha(\ell)}^{\,2}\N(\ell)^{1/2-s}.
\end{equation}
For $\ell=\lambda^{-3}nb^3$ we have $\alpha(\lambda^{-3})=i$, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d108}
 \overline{\alpha(\ell)}^{\,2}
 =-\overline{\alpha(n)}^{\,2}\overline{\alpha(b)}^{\,6},\qquad
 \N(\ell)^{1/2-s}=27^{s-1/2}\N(n)^{1/2-s}\N(b)^{3/2-3s}.
\end{equation}
Inserting
\eqref{eq:b3-coeff} and using $3^{5/2}\cdot27^{-1/2}=3$ and
$|b|\,\N(b)^{3/2-3s}=\N(b)^{2-3s}$, we get
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b3-J}
 \mathcal J(s)=\frac{3\pi}2\Bigl(\frac{27}{(2\pi)^2}\Bigr)^s
 \Gamma(s+\tfrac13)\Gamma(s+\tfrac23)\,\mathcal T(s-\tfrac12)
 \qquad(\Re s>\tfrac32).
\end{equation}
Termwise integration is justified. Since $K_{1/3}>0$, the sum over the
modes of the absolute values of the integrals is a constant times
$\sum_n\N(n)^{1/2-\Re s}\sum_b\N(b)^{2-3\Re s}$, which is finite for
$\Re s>\frac32$. With one derivative the same computation gives
$\mathcal T$ at $s$ itself~\cite[(A.16)]{OAI1112}. The shift by
$\frac12$, caused by the extra factor $|\ell|$, is the source of the CM
gamma factors.

\paragraph{Step 4: the chain rule at a cusp.} Fix $\mathbf h$, with the
data of Step~2, and fix $v>0$. By~\cite[(5.1), (5.19)]{QF} and
$\det g=1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d109}
 (z',v'):=g^{-1}(a/c+z,v)=\Bigl(-\frac\delta c-\frac{\bar z}
 {c^2(v^2+z\bar z)},\ \frac v{\N(c)(v^2+z\bar z)}\Bigr),
\end{equation}
and $\bar z'=\overline{z'}$. Regard $z'$, $\bar z'$ and $v'$ as smooth
functions of $(z,\bar z)$ near $0$. Differentiation gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d110}
\begin{aligned}
 \partial_{\bar z}z'&=-\frac{v^2}{c^2(v^2+z\bar z)^2},&
 \partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}z'&=\frac{2zv^2}{c^2(v^2+z\bar z)^3},\\
 \partial_{\bar z}\bar z'&=\frac{z^2}{\bar c^2(v^2+z\bar z)^2},&
 \partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}\bar z'&=-\frac{2z^3}{\bar c^2(v^2+z\bar z)^3},\\
 \partial_{\bar z}v'&=-\frac{zv}{\N(c)(v^2+z\bar z)^2},&
 \partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}v'&=\frac{2z^2v}{\N(c)(v^2+z\bar z)^3}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
At $z=0$ all six vanish except $\partial_{\bar z}z'=-(cv)^{-2}$. For a
smooth function $F$ of $(z',v')$, the chain rule in the variables
$Y\in\{z',\bar z',v'\}$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d111}
 \partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}\bigl[F(z',v')\bigr]
 =\sum_{Y,Y'}(\partial_Y\partial_{Y'}F)\,(\partial_{\bar z}Y)
 (\partial_{\bar z}Y')+\sum_Y(\partial_YF)\,\partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}Y,
\end{equation}
and at $z=0$ only the term with $Y=Y'=z'$ survives. This
proves~\eqref{eq:chain}. Taking $F=F_\sigma$ and
using~\eqref{eq:b3-automorphy},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b3-cusp}
 \partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}\overline{\theta(a/c+z,v)}\big|_{z=0}
 =\overline{\kappa(g_1)}\,(cv)^{-4}\,G\Bigl(\frac1{\N(c)v}\Bigr),
 \qquad G(v'):=(\partial_{z'}^{\,2}F_\sigma)(-\delta/c,v').
\end{equation}
In the expansion
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d112}
 F_\sigma(z',v')=\frac{3^{5/2}}2\ind_{\sigma=0}v'^{\,2/3}
 +\sum_{0\ne\ell\in\lambda^{-4}\OO}d_\sigma(\ell)\,v'K_{1/3}(4\pi|\ell|v')
 \,\ce(\ell z'),
\end{equation}
the operator $\partial_{z'}^{\,2}$ removes the constant term and
multiplies the mode $\ell$ by $(2\pi i\ell)^2=-4\pi^2\N(\ell)\alpha(\ell)^2$.
Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b3-G}
 G(v')=-4\pi^2\sum_{0\ne\ell\in\lambda^{-4}\OO}d_\sigma(\ell)\N(\ell)
 \alpha(\ell)^2\,v'K_{1/3}(4\pi|\ell|v')\,\ce\Bigl(-\frac{\delta\ell}c\Bigr).
\end{equation}
On this side the derivative is in $z'$ rather than $\bar z'$, which is
why $\alpha(\ell)^2$ appears instead of $\overline{\alpha(\ell)}^{\,2}$. In
the zeta case the single factor $-(cv)^{-2}$ produces the sign and the
factor $\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,2}$ of~\cite[(A.18)]{OAI1112}. Here its
square $(cv)^{-4}$ produces no sign.

\paragraph{Step 5: continuation and the functional equation.} On
$\lambda^{-3}\OO\setminus\{0\}$ we have $|\ell|\ge3^{-3/2}$, so the
defining series shows that $f(v)$ decays exponentially as $v\to\infty$.
As $v\to0$, by~\eqref{eq:b3-translates} and~\eqref{eq:b3-cusp}, $f(v)$
is the finite sum of the terms
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d113}
 c_{\rm F}(\mathbf h)\,\overline{\kappa(g_{1,\mathbf h})}\,
 (c_{\mathbf h}v)^{-4}\,G_{\mathbf h}\bigl(1/(\N(c_{\mathbf h})v)\bigr),
\end{equation}
where the subscript $\mathbf h$ marks the data of $\mathbf h$.
By~\eqref{eq:b3-G}, and since $|\ell|\ge1/9$ on
$\lambda^{-4}\OO\setminus\{0\}$, each $G_{\mathbf h}(v')$ decays
exponentially as $v'\to\infty$. Hence $\mathcal J(s)$ converges
absolutely for every $s$, is entire, and is bounded on vertical strips
of bounded width.

For $\Re s'>\frac32$ the computation of Step~3, with $\ell$ in place of
$\bar\ell$, gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b3-dual}
 \mathcal J^\vee_{\mathbf h}(s'):=\int_0^\infty G_{\mathbf h}(v')\,
 v'^{\,2s'-1}dv'
 =-\frac\pi2(2\pi)^{-2s'}\Gamma(s'+\tfrac13)\Gamma(s'+\tfrac23)
 \sum_{0\ne\ell\in\lambda^{-4}\OO}\frac{d_\sigma(\ell)\alpha(\ell)^2}
 {\N(\ell)^{s'-1/2}}\,\ce\Bigl(-\frac{\delta\ell}c\Bigr).
\end{equation}
The series and the termwise integration converge absolutely. Indeed,
write $\ell=u\lambda^mnb^3$ uniquely as in Appendix~\ref{app:reflection}.
The bound $|d_\sigma(\ell)|\le27\cdot3^{m/6}|b|$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d114}
 \sum_{\ell}|d_\sigma(\ell)|\,\N(\ell)^{1/2-\Re s'}
 \le6\cdot27\sum_{m\ge-4}3^{m(2/3-\Re s')}\sum_n\N(n)^{1/2-\Re s'}
 \sum_b\N(b)^{2-3\Re s'}<\infty .
\end{equation}

Now let $\Re s<\frac12$. In each term of $f$ substitute
$v=1/(\N(c)v')$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d115}
 \int_0^\infty(cv)^{-4}G\Bigl(\frac1{\N(c)v}\Bigr)v^{2s-1}dv
 =c^{-4}\N(c)^{4-2s}\int_0^\infty G(v')\,v'^{\,3-2s}dv'
 =\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}\N(c)^{2-2s}\mathcal J^\vee(2-s).
\end{equation}
This integral converges absolutely, because $\Re(2-s)>\frac32$; here
$c^{-4}\N(c)^2=(\bar c/c)^2=\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}$. Summing over
the finitely many $\mathbf h$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b3-fe}
 \mathcal J(s)=\sum_{\mathbf h}c_{\rm F}(\mathbf h)\,
 \overline{\kappa(g_{1,\mathbf h})}\,\overline{\alpha(c_{\mathbf h})}^{\,4}
 \N(c_{\mathbf h})^{2-2s}\mathcal J^\vee_{\mathbf h}(2-s)
 \qquad(\Re s<\tfrac12).
\end{equation}

By~\eqref{eq:b3-J}, for $\Re w>1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d116}
 \mathcal T(w)=\mathcal J(w+\tfrac12)\Big/\Bigl(\frac{3\pi}2
 \Bigl(\frac{27}{(2\pi)^2}\Bigr)^{w+1/2}\Gamma(w+\tfrac56)
 \Gamma(w+\tfrac76)\Bigr).
\end{equation}
The right side is entire, so $\mathcal T$ continues to an entire
function. Fix $\sigma_1\in(1,2]$ and $\sigma_2\in[-1,0)$. On
$\Re w=\sigma_1$, $\mathcal T$ is bounded. On $\Re w=\sigma_2$, apply
\eqref{eq:b3-fe} and~\eqref{eq:b3-dual} at $s'=\frac32-w$. Stirling's
formula then gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d117}
 |\mathcal T(w)|\ll\Bigl|\frac{\Gamma(\frac{11}6-w)\Gamma(\frac{13}6-w)}
 {\Gamma(w+\frac56)\Gamma(w+\frac76)}\Bigr|
 \ll(1+|\Im w|)^{2-4\sigma_2}.
\end{equation}
In the strip $\sigma_2\le\Re w\le\sigma_1$, $\mathcal J(w+\frac12)$ is
bounded and $1/\Gamma$ is entire of order one, so $\mathcal T$ has finite
order there. The Phragm\'en--Lindel\"of principle~\cite{PL08}, \cite[\S5.65]{Tit39} gives
$|\mathcal T(w)|\ll(1+|\Im w|)^{2-4\sigma_2}$ throughout the strip. Since
$\widehat V(w-\frac12)$ decays faster than any power of $|\Im w|$,
uniformly in the strip, the contour in~\eqref{eq:b3-inversion} may be
moved to $\Re w=\sigma_2$. No poles are crossed.

\paragraph{Step 6: assembly.} On $\Re w=\sigma_2$, insert
\eqref{eq:b3-J} at $s=w+\frac12$, then~\eqref{eq:b3-fe}
and~\eqref{eq:b3-dual} at $s'=\frac32-w$, and set $t=\frac12-w$. Then
$\frac32-w=1+t$, $w+\frac56=\frac43-t$, $w+\frac76=\frac53-t$,
$\N(c)^{2-2s}=\N(c)^{2t}$ and $X^{w-1/2}=X^{-t}$. The constants combine
as
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d118}
 \frac{-\frac\pi2(2\pi)^{-2-2t}}{\frac{3\pi}2\bigl(\frac{27}{(2\pi)^2}
 \bigr)^{1-t}}=-\frac1{81}\Bigl(\frac{(2\pi)^4}{27}\Bigr)^{-t}.
\end{equation}
With $R(t)$ the gamma quotient of~\eqref{eq:weight}, this gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d119}
\begin{split}
 T(X;\Psi)=-\frac1{81}\sum_{\mathbf h}c_{\rm F}(\mathbf h)\,
 &\overline{\kappa(g_{1,\mathbf h})}\,\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}
 \sum_{0\ne\ell\in\lambda^{-4}\OO}\frac{d_\sigma(\ell)\alpha(\ell)^2}
 {\sqrt{\N(\ell)}}\,\ce\Bigl(-\frac{\delta\ell}c\Bigr)\\
 &\times\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{(\frac12-\sigma_2)}\widehat V(-t)R(t)
 \Bigl(\frac{(2\pi)^4\N(\ell)X}{27\N(c)^2}\Bigr)^{-t}dt,
\end{split}
\end{equation}
where $c,\sigma,\delta$ are the data of $\mathbf h$. The interchange of
the $\ell$-sum and the $t$-integral is justified by the absolute
convergence in~\eqref{eq:b3-dual} (note $\Re(1+t)>\frac32$), the rapid
decay of $\widehat V(-t)$ and the polynomial bound~\eqref{eq:b3-stirling}.
For each $\ell$ the $t$-integrand is holomorphic in $\Re t>-\frac43$ and
decays rapidly in vertical strips. So its contour may be moved to
$\Re t=0$, and by~\eqref{eq:weight} the integral becomes
$V^\sharp(\N(\ell)X/\N(c)^2)$.

Finally fix a group $(h_0,\mathcal A)$. On it $c$, $\sigma$, $\psi$,
$\kappa_0$, $\sigma_\pi$ and $\epsilon_\pi$ are fixed (Step~2). For
$\pi\in\mathcal A$, $h_\pi$ runs over the nonzero residues modulo $\pi$;
for $\pi\in\mathcal P\setminus\mathcal A$, $h_\pi=0$ and $j_\pi=0$. By
Step~2(a),(c),
$\overline{\kappa(g_1)}=\overline{\kappa_0}\prod_{\pi\in\mathcal A}
\chi_\pi(\sigma_\pi h_\pi)^{-2}$. Insert this,~\eqref{eq:b3-crt} and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d120}
 c_{\rm F}(\mathbf h)=\widehat\phi(h_0)\prod_{\pi\in\mathcal P\setminus
 \mathcal A}\bigl(1-\N(\pi)^{-1}\bigr)\prod_{\pi\in\mathcal A}
 C_{\pi,j_\pi}(h_\pi).
\end{equation}
The sum over the $h_\pi$ factors into the local sums~\eqref{eq:b3-local}
with $x=\lambda^4\ell$. The group therefore contributes~\eqref{eq:reflection}
with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:b3-C}
 C=-\frac{\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}}{81}\,\widehat\phi(h_0)\,
 \overline{\kappa_0}\prod_{\pi\in\mathcal P\setminus\mathcal A}
 \bigl(1-\N(\pi)^{-1}\bigr)\prod_{\pi\in\mathcal A}
 \chi_\pi(\sigma_\pi)^{-2}\varpi_{\pi,j_\pi},
\end{equation}
so $|C|\le1/81$. The zeta case has $-\frac i{81}\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,2}$
in place of $-\frac1{81}\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}$, with the same
remaining factors~\cite[App.~A.2]{OAI1112}. Each
series~\eqref{eq:reflection} converges absolutely. Indeed
$|B_{\pi,j}|\le\N(\pi)^{1/2}$, and Lemma~\ref{lem:b3-decay} with $A=2$
bounds the series by a constant times
$\sum_\ell|d_\sigma(\ell)|\N(\ell)^{-5/2}$, which is finite by the
estimate after~\eqref{eq:b3-dual}. This proves
Proposition~\ref{prop:b3-reflection}.


\section{The CM transformation formula at full generality}\label{app:s1-general}

This appendix writes out item~1 of Section~\ref{sec:78status}. Part~II uses the transformation formula for
$\partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}\theta$ in the generality of~\cite[Prop.~5.1]{OAI78}:
\begin{itemize}
\item a general modulus supported on $S$;
\item tests that need not have compact support, such as the Gaussian of the probe;
\item the exact dependence of every phase on the moving primes.
\end{itemize}
It also uses the marked form~\cite[Cor.~14.1]{OAI78} and three estimates built on it.
Appendix~\ref{app:reflection} proves the formula for the twists of Part~I. Here we extend that proof, reusing its
Steps~3--6, and then go through~\cite[Sections~5 and~14--15]{OAI78} statement by statement.

The rule is the one used throughout Part~II. A step of~\cite{OAI78} that involves neither the derivative of
$\theta$, nor the gamma factors, nor an angular factor is identical in the CM case, and we cite it by its number
there. A step that involves any of these is written out.

\paragraph{Notation.} We use the conventions of~\cite[Section~5.1]{OAI78}: $\ce(z)=\exp(2\pi i(z+\bar z))$,
$e(z)=\ce(z/\lambda)$, and every power of a residue symbol vanishes at nonunits, so that
$\chi_p(x)^0=\ind_{p\nmid x}$. The set $S$ is finite and contains the primes above $6$; a \emph{good} prime is a
primary prime outside $S$. To avoid clashes with Part~II we rename some objects of~\cite{OAI78}:
\begin{itemize}
\item its norm $q_n$ is $\N(n)$, and its dual index $\mu$ is $\ell$;
\item its scalar $\zeta$ is $C$, as in~\eqref{eq:reflection};
\item its Gauss sums $\tau_{p,m}^\pm$ are $\mathfrak g_{p,m}^\pm$, and its $\omega_{p,j}$ is $\varpi_{p,j}$, as in
 Appendix~\ref{app:reflection};
\item its constant $K$ is written out as $(2\pi)^4/27$.
\end{itemize}
The kernels with one and two derivatives are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d121}
 R_1(t)=\prod_\pm\frac{\Gamma(1+t\pm\frac16)}{\Gamma(1-t\pm\frac16)},\qquad
 R_2(t)=\frac{\Gamma(\frac43+t)\Gamma(\frac53+t)}{\Gamma(\frac43-t)\Gamma(\frac53-t)}.
\end{equation}
$R_1$ is the kernel of~\cite{OAI78}, and $R_2$ is that of~\eqref{eq:weight}.

\subsection{The statement}

\paragraph{Arithmetic data.} These are those of~\cite[Proposition~5.1]{OAI78}.
\begin{itemize}
\item $\mathcal X$ is a fixed finite family of finite-ray characters with conductors supported on $S$.
\item $\mathfrak E$ is an integral modulus with prime support exactly $S$, divisible by all these conductors.
 Each member of $\mathcal X$ is extended by zero to the primary elements that are not prime to $\mathfrak E$.
\item $L\in\OO\setminus\{0\}$ has prime support $S$, with $(18)\mid(L)$ and $\mathfrak E\mid(L)$, and for every
 $\Psi_0\in\mathcal X$ the function
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:d122}
  \phi(x)=\begin{cases}\chi_x(\lambda)^2\Psi_0(x),&x\equiv1\pmod3,\ (x,\mathfrak E)=1,\\0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}
 \end{equation}
 is $L$-periodic.
\item $\Psi_0\in\mathcal X$; $\mathcal P$ is a finite set of primary primes not dividing $L$; $j_p\in\{0,\dots,5\}$
 for $p\in\mathcal P$; and $\Psi(n)=\Psi_0(n)\prod_{p\in\mathcal P}\chi_p(n)^{j_p}$ on primary $n$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Tests.} A \emph{log-Schwartz test} is a function $V\in C^\infty(0,\infty)$ such that
$(x\partial_x)^jV(x)=O_{C,j}(x^C)$ as $x\to0$ and $O_{C,j}(x^{-C})$ as $x\to\infty$, for every $C>0$ and every
integer $j\ge0$~\cite[Proposition~5.1]{OAI78}. Its Mellin transform $\widehat V(t)=\int_0^\infty V(x)x^{t-1}dx$ is
entire. Integration by parts in $\log x$ gives, uniformly for $\eta$ in a compact interval $I$ (in this appendix
$\eta$ denotes a real number, as in~\cite{OAI78}, not the finite-order character of Part~II),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s1-mellin-tail}
 |\widehat V(\eta+iu)|\ll_{I,N}(1+|u|)^{-N}\sum_{j=0}^N\int_0^\infty x^\eta|(x\partial_x)^jV(x)|\,\frac{dx}x
\end{equation}
for every $N\ge0$; this is~\cite[eq.~(4.11)]{OAI78}, whose boundary terms vanish for these tests.
For $A\ge0$ and integers $B\ge0$ put, as in~\cite[Proposition~5.1]{OAI78},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d123}
 \mathfrak M_{A,B}(V)=\sup_{-A\le\eta\le1/4}\int_{\mathbb R}(1+|u|)^B|\widehat V(\eta+iu)|\,du .
\end{equation}
The Gaussian $V_{\rm G}(y)=(2\sqrt\pi)^{-1}\exp(-\frac14(\log y)^2)$, with $\widehat V_{\rm G}(t)=e^{t^2}$, is a
log-Schwartz test, and so is $\sqrt y\,W(y)$ for every $W\in C_c^\infty(0,\infty)$.

\paragraph{The CM objects.} For $\Re s>1$ put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d124}
 \mathcal D_2(s,\Psi)=\ssum_{\substack{n\equiv1\ (3)\\(n,\mathfrak E)=1}}
 \frac{\gamma_2(n)\overline{\alpha(n)}^{\,2}\Psi(n)}{\N(n)^s},\qquad
 \mathcal L_2(s,\Psi)=\sum_{\substack{b\equiv1\ (3)\\(b,\mathfrak E)=1}}
 \frac{\overline{\alpha(b)}^{\,6}\Psi(b)^3}{\N(b)^{3s-1/2}},
\end{equation}
and $\mathcal T_2=\mathcal D_2\mathcal L_2$. These are the series $D,L_0,T$ of~\cite[Proposition~5.1]{OAI78} with
$\overline\alpha$ replaced by $\overline\alpha^{\,2}$. For the data of Appendix~\ref{app:reflection},
$\mathcal T_2(w,\Psi)$ is the series $\mathcal T(w)$ of Step~3 there. The completed sum is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d125}
 T_V(X;\Psi)=\sum_{\substack{n\ {\rm sf},\ b\\n,b\equiv1\ (3),\ (nb,\mathfrak E)=1}}
 \frac{\gamma_2(n)\overline{\alpha(nb^3)}^{\,2}\Psi(nb^3)}{\sqrt{\N(n)}\,\N(b)}
 \,V\Bigl(\frac{\N(n)\N(b)^3}X\Bigr)\qquad(X>0).
\end{equation}
Here $n$ and $b$ may share primes, and $\Psi$ acts on the whole index $nb^3$, with its zeros. For $V(y)=\sqrt
y\,W(y)$ this is the sum $T(X;\Psi)$ of~\eqref{eq:T}. The reflected test is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d126}
 V^\sharp(x)=\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{(\sigma_0)}\widehat V(-t)\,R_2(t)\Bigl(\frac{(2\pi)^4x}{27}\Bigr)^{-t}dt
 \qquad(\sigma_0>-\tfrac43);
\end{equation}
for $\sigma_0=0$ it is~\eqref{eq:weight}.

\begin{proposition}[CM completed reflection; CM form of {\cite[Prop.~5.1]{OAI78}}]
\label{prop:s1-reflection}
Let $V$ be a log-Schwartz test and $X>0$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] The series defining $T_V(X;\Psi)$ converges absolutely, and for every $a>1$
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:d127}
  T_V(X;\Psi)=\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{(a)}\widehat V(s-\tfrac12)\,X^{s-1/2}\,\mathcal T_2(s,\Psi)\,ds .
 \end{equation}
\item[(b)] $\mathcal T_2(\cdot,\Psi)$ continues to an entire function with polynomial growth on every vertical
 strip.
\item[(c)] The integral defining $V^\sharp$ converges absolutely and does not depend on $\sigma_0>-\frac43$. Take the
 terms, the cusp coefficient functions $d_\sigma$, the additive characters $\vartheta$, the local factors $B_p$ and the
 data $c=c_Fr$, $\kappa_F$, $\sigma_p$, $\epsilon_p$, $\varpi_{p,j}$ of~\cite[Prop.~5.1, items~(1)--(3)]{OAI78} for the same arithmetic data. Then
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:s1-reflection}
  T_V(X;\Psi)=\sum_{\rm terms}C\sum_{0\ne\ell\in\lambda^{-4}\OO}
  \frac{d_\sigma(\ell)\,\alpha(\ell)^2\,\vartheta(\lambda^4\ell)}{\sqrt{\N(\ell)}}
  \prod_{p\ {\rm active}}B_p(\lambda^4\ell)\;V^\sharp\Bigl(\frac{\N(\ell)X}{\N(c)^2}\Bigr),
 \end{equation}
 where every inner series converges absolutely and
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:s1-scalar}
  C=-\frac{\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}}{81}\,\widehat\phi(h_0)\,\overline{\kappa_F}
  \prod_{p\ {\rm inactive}}\bigl(1-\N(p)^{-1}\bigr)\prod_{p\ {\rm active}}\chi_p(\sigma_p)^{-2}\varpi_{p,j_p}.
 \end{equation}
 Thus $C=-i\,\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,2}\zeta$, where $\zeta$ is the scalar of the same term
 in~\cite[eq.~(5.3)]{OAI78}, and $|C|\le\frac1{81}$.
\item[(d)] The assertions of~\cite[Proposition~5.1, item~(3)]{OAI78} hold for these terms, with the same factor
 $1-\N(p)^{-1}$ in the branch compatibility. The cross-prime identity~\cite[eq.~(5.17)]{OAI78},
 $\chi_p(\sigma_p)^{-2}\varpi_{p,j}=\xi_{p,j}\chi_p(c/p)^{2j+2}$ with $|\xi_{p,j}|=1$ depending only on $p$ and $j$,
 holds unchanged. Compared with the scalar of~\cite{OAI78}, the only new factor in~\eqref{eq:s1-scalar} is
 $-i\,\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,2}$, and
 $\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}=\overline{\alpha(c_F)}^{\,4}\prod_{p\ {\rm active}}\overline{\alpha(p)}^{\,4}$.
\item[(e)] For $A\ge0$ and integers $j,J\ge0$, the two bounds~\cite[eqs.~(5.4) and~(5.5)]{OAI78} hold for this $V^\sharp$, with the same seminorms. In particular
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:d128}
  \bigl|(x\partial_x)^jV^\sharp(x)\bigr|\ll_{A,j}\min\{x^{1/4},(1+x)^{-A}\}\,
  \mathfrak M_{A,\lceil4A\rceil+j+2}(V)\qquad(x>0).
 \end{equation}
 The bound~\cite[eq.~(5.6)]{OAI78} for $\mathfrak M_{A,B}$ and the twist bound after it
 concern $V$ alone and hold as stated there.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

So the formula of~\cite{OAI78} changes in five places: the derivative $\partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}$, the angular
factor $\overline{\alpha(nb^3)}^{\,2}$ of the completed index, the dual factor $\alpha(\ell)^2$, the kernel $R_2$, and
the prefactor $-\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}/81$ in place of $-i\,\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,2}/81$. For $L=72$,
$\Psi_0=\xi_0$ and $V(y)=\sqrt y\,W(y)$ with $W\in C_c^\infty(I)$, Proposition~\ref{prop:s1-reflection}(c) is
a formula of the form of Proposition~\ref{prop:b3-reflection}, with $c_F$, $\kappa_F$ and $\vartheta$ in the roles of
$c_0$, $\kappa_0$ and $\psi$, and $C$ of the form~\eqref{eq:b3-C}.

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:s1-reflection}}\label{sec:s1-proof}

\paragraph{The identical part.} The proof of~\cite[Prop.~5.1]{OAI78} has six paragraphs. The first four, from
\emph{The fixed theta input} to \emph{The reflected additive phase}, work with the translated theta values $\overline{\theta(z+z_{\mathbf h},v)}$ and
never differentiate them. We use them without change:
\begin{itemize}
\item the theta input, the three cusp functions, their coefficient functions $d_\sigma$ with the support and size
 bound~\cite[eq.~(5.2)]{OAI78}, the infinity coefficient~\cite[eqs.~(5.9) and~(5.10)]{OAI78}, and the choice of $L$ (\cite[proof of Proposition~5.1, \emph{The fixed theta input}]{OAI78});
\item the finite Fourier inversion with all zero extensions, giving
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:d129}
  \mathcal F(z,v)=\sum_{\mathbf h}C(\mathbf h)\,\overline{\theta(z+z_{\mathbf h},v)},\qquad
  z_{\mathbf h}=\lambda^2\Bigl(\frac{h_0}L+\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}\frac{h_p}p\Bigr),\qquad
  C(\mathbf h)=\widehat\phi(h_0)\prod_pC_{p,j_p}(h_p),
 \end{equation}
 with the whole-index mask $(nb,\mathfrak E)=1$ (\cite[proof of Proposition~5.1, \emph{Finite Fourier inversion with the zero extensions}]{OAI78});
\item the reduced fractions $z_{\mathbf h}=a/c$ with $c=c_Fr$ (\cite[proof of Proposition~5.1, \emph{Finite Fourier inversion with the zero extensions}]{OAI78}), the matrices
 $g=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}a&b'\\c&\delta'\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)=GH\in\mathrm{SL}_2(\OO)$ with
 $G\in\Gamma(3)$ (the group written $\Gamma_1(3)$ in~\cite{OAI78}), and the multiplier formula~\cite[eq.~(5.15)]{OAI78};
\item the additive phase~\cite[eq.~(5.16)]{OAI78}, the local Gauss sums that produce
 $\varpi_{p,j}B_p$, the counts of terms, the branch compatibility and the cross-prime phase (\cite[proof of Proposition~5.1, \emph{The reflected additive phase}]{OAI78}).
\end{itemize}
From this part we need two facts. For every $\mathbf h$, with $z_{\mathbf h}=a/c$ and $g,G,H$ as above,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s1-automorphy}
 \overline{\theta(a/c+z,v)}=\overline{\kappa(G)}\;\overline{\theta\bigl(Hg^{-1}(a/c+z,v)\bigr)}
\end{equation}
(\cite[proof of Proposition~5.1, \emph{Mellin normalization and continuation}]{OAI78}). And, by~\cite[eq.~(5.7)]{OAI78},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d130}
 \overline{\theta(H(z',v'))}=C_H(v')+\sum_{0\ne\ell\in\lambda^{-4}\OO}d_\sigma(\ell)\,v'K_{1/3}(4\pi|\ell|v')\,\ce(\ell z'),
\end{equation}
where $C_H$ does not depend on $z'$ and $d_\sigma$, $\sigma\in\{0,+,-\}$, is the one of the three fixed coefficient
functions that belongs to $H$; these are the functions $d_\sigma$ of Appendix~\ref{app:reflection}, and $d_0$ belongs to
$H=I$.

The last two paragraphs of that proof, \emph{Mellin normalization and continuation} and \emph{Uniform estimates for
the transformed test}, use the single derivative. We redo it with two. Steps~1--4 below are Steps~3--6 of
Section~\ref{sec:b3-proof}, which we reuse; we record only what changes at the present generality.

\paragraph{Step 1: the direct transform.} Put $f(v)=\partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}\mathcal F(z,v)\big|_{z=0}$ and
$\mathcal J_2(s)=\int_0^\infty f(v)v^{2s-1}dv$. By~\cite[proof of Proposition~5.1, \emph{Finite Fourier inversion with the zero extensions}]{OAI78}, the expansion of $\mathcal F$ at the
identity cusp has the constant term $\sum_{\mathbf h}C(\mathbf h)\,C_I(v)=\phi(0)\prod_p\chi_p(0)^{j_p}C_I(v)=0$, and its
other terms are supported on $\ell\in\lambda^{-3}\OO\setminus\{0\}$, where the coefficient is
$d_0(\ell)\phi(\lambda^3\ell)\prod_p\chi_p(\lambda^3\ell)^{j_p}$. This coefficient vanishes unless $\lambda^3\ell=nb^3$
with $n$ squarefree, $n,b\equiv1\pmod3$ and $(nb,\mathfrak E)=1$, and then it equals
$3^{5/2}|b|\,\gamma_2(n)\Psi(n)\Psi(b)^3$. This replaces~\eqref{eq:b3-coeff}; $n$ and $b$ may share primes. In every
case its absolute value is at most $3^{5/2}|b|$. Since $|\ell|\ge3^{-3/2}$ and $K_{1/3}(y)\ll e^{-y}$ for $y\ge1$, the
expansion may be differentiated termwise. Step~3 of Section~\ref{sec:b3-proof} uses only this coefficient formula and
this bound, so it gives, with absolutely convergent termwise integrals,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s1-direct}
 \mathcal J_2(s)=\frac{3\pi}2\Bigl(\frac{27}{(2\pi)^2}\Bigr)^s\Gamma(s+\tfrac13)\Gamma(s+\tfrac23)\,
 \mathcal T_2(s-\tfrac12,\Psi)\qquad(\Re s>\tfrac32).
\end{equation}
In~\cite[eq.~(5.18)]{OAI78} the same integral with one derivative is
$\frac{3^{5/2}}4(\frac{27}{(2\pi)^2})^s\Gamma(s+\frac13)\Gamma(s+\frac23)T(s,\Psi)$. The second derivative brings
the extra factor $2\pi i\bar\ell$; its modulus $2\pi|\ell|$ shifts the Dirichlet series by $\frac12$, and its
phase turns $\overline\alpha$ into $\overline\alpha^{\,2}$.

\paragraph{Step 2: the cusps.} Fix $\mathbf h$. Step~4 of Section~\ref{sec:b3-proof} uses only that
$g\in\mathrm{SL}_2(\OO)$ has lower row $(c,\delta')$ and the formula for $g^{-1}(a/c+z,v)$ (\cite[proof of Proposition~5.1, \emph{Mellin normalization and continuation}]{OAI78} and~\cite[(5.1),
(5.19)]{QF}). With~\eqref{eq:s1-automorphy} it gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s1-cusp}
 \partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}\overline{\theta(a/c+z,v)}\big|_{z=0}
 =\overline{\kappa(G)}\,(cv)^{-4}\,\mathcal G_{\mathbf h}\Bigl(\frac1{\N(c)v}\Bigr),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d131}
 \mathcal G_{\mathbf h}(v')=-4\pi^2\sum_{0\ne\ell\in\lambda^{-4}\OO}d_\sigma(\ell)\N(\ell)\alpha(\ell)^2\,
 v'K_{1/3}(4\pi|\ell|v')\,\ce\Bigl(-\frac{\delta'\ell}c\Bigr).
\end{equation}
The operator $\partial_{z'}^{\,2}$ removes $C_H(v')$. This replaces the formula of \cite[proof of Proposition~5.1, \emph{Mellin normalization and continuation}]{OAI78}, which has
$-\overline{\kappa(G)}(cv)^{-2}$ and one derivative $\partial_{z'}$.

\paragraph{Step 3: continuation and growth.} By Step~1, $f(v)$ decays exponentially as $v\to\infty$. As $v\to0$,
$f(v)=\sum_{\mathbf h}C(\mathbf h)\overline{\kappa(G_{\mathbf h})}(c_{\mathbf h}v)^{-4}
\mathcal G_{\mathbf h}(1/(\N(c_{\mathbf h})v))$ decays exponentially as well, because $|\ell|\ge\frac19$ on
$\lambda^{-4}\OO\setminus\{0\}$ and $d_\sigma$ is polynomially bounded. So $\mathcal J_2$ is entire and bounded on vertical
strips. For $\Re s'>\frac32$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s1-dual}
 \mathcal J^\vee_{\mathbf h}(s'):=\int_0^\infty\mathcal G_{\mathbf h}(v')\,v'^{\,2s'-1}dv'
 =-\frac\pi2(2\pi)^{-2s'}\Gamma(s'+\tfrac13)\Gamma(s'+\tfrac23)
 \sum_{0\ne\ell\in\lambda^{-4}\OO}\frac{d_\sigma(\ell)\alpha(\ell)^2}{\N(\ell)^{s'-1/2}}\,\ce\Bigl(-\frac{\delta'\ell}c\Bigr),
\end{equation}
with absolute convergence of the series and of the termwise integrals. Indeed, writing $\ell=u\lambda^knb^3$ as
in~\cite[eq.~(5.2)]{OAI78},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d132}
 \sum_\ell|d_\sigma(\ell)|\,\N(\ell)^{1/2-\Re s'}\le6\cdot27\sum_{k\ge-4}3^{k(2/3-\Re s')}
 \ssum_n\N(n)^{1/2-\Re s'}\sum_b\N(b)^{2-3\Re s'}<\infty .
\end{equation}
Here $n$ and $b$ run over all primary elements, $n$ squarefree, including those divisible by primes of $S$ other than
$\lambda$, and pairs that share primes; the size bound covers all of them (\cite[Proposition~5.1; proof of Proposition~5.1, \emph{The fixed theta input}]{OAI78}). This is the majorant
of~\cite[proof of Proposition~5.1, \emph{Mellin normalization and continuation}]{OAI78} in the variable $s'-\frac12$. For $\Re s<\frac12$ the substitution $v=1/(\N(c)v')$ of
Step~5 of the proof in Appendix~\ref{app:reflection} (Section~\ref{sec:b3-proof}) gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s1-fe}
 \mathcal J_2(s)=\sum_{\mathbf h}C(\mathbf h)\,\overline{\kappa(G_{\mathbf h})}\;
 \overline{\alpha(c_{\mathbf h})}^{\,4}\,\N(c_{\mathbf h})^{2-2s}\,\mathcal J^\vee_{\mathbf h}(2-s)
 \qquad(\Re s<\tfrac12),
\end{equation}
where $c^{-4}\N(c)^2=\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}$. By~\eqref{eq:s1-direct},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d133}
 \mathcal T_2(w,\Psi)=\mathcal J_2(w+\tfrac12)\Big/\Bigl(\frac{3\pi}2\Bigl(\frac{27}{(2\pi)^2}\Bigr)^{w+1/2}
 \Gamma(w+\tfrac56)\Gamma(w+\tfrac76)\Bigr)
\end{equation}
continues to an entire function. Let $\sigma_2<0<1<\sigma_1$. On $\Re w=\sigma_1$, $\mathcal T_2$ is bounded. On
$\Re w=\sigma_2$, \eqref{eq:s1-fe} and~\eqref{eq:s1-dual} at $s'=\frac32-w$ and Stirling's formula give
$|\mathcal T_2(w)|\ll(1+|\Im w|)^{2-4\sigma_2}$, as in Step~5 of the proof in Appendix~\ref{app:reflection}. In the strip
$\sigma_2\le\Re w\le\sigma_1$, $\mathcal J_2(w+\frac12)$ is bounded and $1/(\Gamma(w+\frac56)\Gamma(w+\frac76))\ll
e^{\pi|\Im w|}(1+|\Im w|)^{O(1)}$. The Phragm\'en--Lindel\"of principle therefore gives
$|\mathcal T_2(w)|\ll(1+|\Im w|)^{2-4\sigma_2}$ throughout the strip. Every vertical strip lies in such a strip,
which proves~(b). As in~\cite{OAI78}, the constants depend on $L,\Psi_0,\mathcal P,(j_p)$ and the strip.

\paragraph{Step 4: a log-Schwartz test.} This is the step where the generality of the test matters. Since
$(x\partial_x)^0V=V$ is $O(x^{-1})$ at infinity and bounded at zero, $|V(y)|\ll_V y^{-1}$ for all $y>0$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d134}
 \sum_{n,b}\frac{|V(\N(n)\N(b)^3/X)|}{\sqrt{\N(n)}\,\N(b)}\ll_V X\ssum_n\N(n)^{-3/2}\sum_b\N(b)^{-4}<\infty .
\end{equation}
By~\eqref{eq:s1-mellin-tail}, $\widehat V(w)$ is integrable on every vertical line. Mellin inversion~\cite[\S1.14(iv)]{DLMF}
$V(y)=\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{(a-1/2)}\widehat V(w)y^{-w}dw$ holds, because $u\mapsto e^{(a-1/2)u}V(e^u)$ is a
Schwartz function. Since $\mathcal T_2$ converges absolutely on $\Re s=a>1$, Fubini's theorem gives~(a).

Now move the contour of~(a) from $\Re s=a$ to $\Re s=\sigma_2$, with $-1\le\sigma_2<0$. By~(b)
and~\eqref{eq:s1-mellin-tail}, the integrand decays faster than any power of $|\Im s|$, uniformly in the strip, so
the horizontal segments contribute nothing in the limit; there are no poles. On $\Re s=\sigma_2$
insert~\eqref{eq:s1-fe} and~\eqref{eq:s1-dual} with $s'=\frac32-s$, and put $t=\frac12-s$. Then
$\Gamma(\frac{11}6-s)\Gamma(\frac{13}6-s)/(\Gamma(s+\frac56)\Gamma(s+\frac76))=R_2(t)$,
