$\N(c)^{1-2s}=\N(c)^{2t}$, $\N(\ell)^{s-1}=\N(\ell)^{-1/2-t}$, $X^{s-1/2}=X^{-t}$, and the constants combine as in
Step~6 of Section~\ref{sec:b3-proof}:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d135}
 \frac{-\frac\pi2(2\pi)^{-2-2t}}{\frac{3\pi}2\bigl(\frac{27}{(2\pi)^2}\bigr)^{1-t}}
 =-\frac1{81}\Bigl(\frac{(2\pi)^4}{27}\Bigr)^{-t}.
\end{equation}
This gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d136}
\begin{split}
 T_V(X;\Psi)=-\frac1{81}\sum_{\mathbf h}C(\mathbf h)\,\overline{\kappa(G_{\mathbf h})}\;
 &\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}\sum_{0\ne\ell\in\lambda^{-4}\OO}\frac{d_\sigma(\ell)\alpha(\ell)^2}{\sqrt{\N(\ell)}}\,
 \ce\Bigl(-\frac{\delta'\ell}c\Bigr)\\
 &\times\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{(\frac12-\sigma_2)}\widehat V(-t)R_2(t)
 \Bigl(\frac{(2\pi)^4\N(\ell)X}{27\N(c)^2}\Bigr)^{-t}dt,
\end{split}
\end{equation}
with $c,H,\sigma,\delta'$ the data of $\mathbf h$. The interchange of the $\ell$-sum and the $t$-integral is justified by the
majorant of Step~3 at $\Re s'=1+\Re t=\frac32-\sigma_2>\frac32$, by~\eqref{eq:s1-mellin-tail}, and by the bound
$|R_2(\eta+iu)|\ll(1+|u|)^{4\eta}$ of Step~5 below. By Step~5 below, each $t$-integral equals
$V^\sharp(\N(\ell)X/\N(c)^2)$.

Finally group the $\mathbf h$ by $h_0$ and the active set. On a group, $c$, $H$, $\sigma$, $\kappa_F$ and the residue
of $\delta'$ that determines $\vartheta$ are fixed, and so is $\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}$. The sum over the active
frequencies of $C(\mathbf h)\overline{\kappa(G_{\mathbf h})}\ce(-\delta'\ell/c)$ is computed
in~\cite[proof of Proposition~5.1, \emph{The reflected additive phase}]{OAI78}; it does not involve the derivative. It gives
$\widehat\phi(h_0)\overline{\kappa_F}\vartheta(\lambda^4\ell)\prod_{\rm inactive}(1-\N(p)^{-1})\prod_{\rm active}
\chi_p(\sigma_p)^{-2}\varpi_{p,j_p}B_p(\lambda^4\ell)$, which proves~\eqref{eq:s1-reflection}
and~\eqref{eq:s1-scalar}. Every factor of $C$ other than $-1/81$ has modulus at most one, and
$-\frac1{81}\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}=-i\,\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,2}\cdot(-\frac i{81}\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,2})$.
Each inner series in~\eqref{eq:s1-reflection} converges absolutely by~(e) with $A=2$, the bound $|B_p|\le\N(p)^{1/2}$
and the majorant of Step~3, as at the end of Section~\ref{sec:b3-proof}. Part~(d) holds because $d_\sigma,\vartheta$, the
local data and the inactive factors are those of~\cite{OAI78}, while $\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}$ and the kernel
depend on a branch only through $c$, which an inactive prime does not change. The factorization of
$\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}$ holds because $c=c_Fr$ is a product of elements and $\alpha$ is multiplicative.

\paragraph{Step 5: the reflected test.} The poles of the numerator of $R_2$ are at $-\frac43-k$ and $-\frac53-k$
($k\ge0$), and $1/\Gamma$ is entire, so $R_2$ is holomorphic in $\Re t>-\frac43$. For $\eta_0>-\frac43$ and $A\ge0$,
Stirling's formula for $|u|\ge1$ and continuity for $|u|\le1$ give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s1-stirling}
 |R_2(\eta+iu)|\ll_{\eta_0,A}(1+|u|)^{4\eta}\qquad(\eta_0\le\eta\le A),
\end{equation}
which for $\eta_0=-\frac14$ is~\eqref{eq:b3-stirling}. Together with~\eqref{eq:s1-mellin-tail}, this shows that the
integral defining $V^\sharp$ converges absolutely on every line $\Re t>-\frac43$, and rectangle shifts show that its
value does not depend on the line. This is~(c) for $V^\sharp$. The proof of~\cite[eqs.~(5.4) and~(5.5)]{OAI78} in \cite[proof of Proposition~5.1, \emph{Uniform estimates for the transformed test}]{OAI78} uses only two properties of its kernel $R_1$: holomorphy in
$\Re t>-\frac56$, a region containing the strip $-\frac14\le\Re t\le A$, and the bound~\eqref{eq:s1-stirling} with
the exponent $4\eta$ on that strip. $R_2$ has both, so the proof applies verbatim and gives~(e). The rest of \cite[proof of Proposition~5.1, \emph{Uniform estimates for the transformed test}]{OAI78}
concerns $V$ alone. This completes the proof.

The CM kernel is holomorphic further to the left than $R_1$, so $V^\sharp(x)=O(x^{4/3})$ as $x\to0$; we do not use
this, and the exponent $\frac14$ in~(e) is kept so that every later estimate of~\cite{OAI78} applies with its own
exponents.

\paragraph{What the later arguments use.} The proofs in~\cite[Sections~5.2--5.5, Sections~14--15]{OAI78} use the reflection
only through the following properties. Each holds for Proposition~\ref{prop:s1-reflection}, by the part indicated.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(R1)] \emph{Shape and branches:} a finite expansion over $h_0$, active sets and Ramanujan splittings, with
 conductor $c=c_Fr$, the canonical $d_\sigma,\vartheta,B_p$, and the branch compatibility at inactive primes. By~(c) and~(d)
 these are literally those of~\cite{OAI78}.
\item[(R2)] \emph{The scalar:} $C$ does not depend on $\ell$, and $|C|\le\frac1{81}$. Its dependence on the active
 primes is that of~\cite{OAI78} times $\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,2}$, which is completely multiplicative in the active
 primes (part~(d)).
\item[(R3)] \emph{The dual coefficient:} $d_\sigma(\ell)\alpha(\ell)^2\vartheta(\lambda^4\ell)\N(\ell)^{-1/2}$, where
 $|\alpha(\ell)^2|=1$ and $\alpha(\ell)^2$ is a function of $\ell$ alone. The support and the size bound of $d_\sigma$
 are those of~\cite{OAI78}.
\item[(R4)] \emph{The kernel argument} $\N(\ell)X/\N(c)^2$, which is that of~\cite{OAI78}.
\item[(R5)] \emph{The kernel bounds}~(e), with the same seminorm orders as in~\cite{OAI78}.
\item[(R6)] \emph{The Mellin representation}~(a) of the completed sum, for every log-Schwartz test.
\end{enumerate}
In the proofs of~\cite{OAI78}, the factor $\alpha(\ell)$ of (R3) is always absorbed into a coefficient of the dual
index, and the angular part of the scalar in (R2) is always split into a row factor and a tuple factor. The same
operations apply to $\alpha(\ell)^2$ and to $\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}$. No norm power changes, so no exponent changes.

\subsection{Unmarked completed rows}\label{sec:s1-unmarked}

For a finite-ray character $\nu$ with conductor supported on $S$, extended by zero away from the primary elements prime
to $S$, a nonzero $m\in\OO$, a log-Schwartz test $V$ and $X>0$, put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s1-central}
 \mathcal C^{(2)}_V(X;m,\nu)=\sum_{\substack{c\ {\rm sf},\ n\\c,n\equiv1\ (3),\ (cn,S)=1}}
 \frac{\gamma_2(c)\,\overline{\alpha(cn^3)}^{\,2}\,\nu(cn^3)\,\chi_{cn^3}(m)}{\sqrt{\N(c)}\,\N(n)}
 \,V\Bigl(\frac{\N(c)\N(n)^3}X\Bigr).
\end{equation}
This is~\cite[eq.~(5.30)]{OAI78} with $\overline{\alpha(cn^3)}$ replaced by
$\overline{\alpha(cn^3)}^{\,2}$; in Section~\ref{sec:78probe} it is the completed row $B_m$, with $X=Z$. The table
lists every statement of~\cite[Sections~5.2--5.5]{OAI78} and its status for this sum.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{2.0cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{4.4cm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{8.7cm}}
\toprule
Section of~\cite{OAI78} & statement & CM status\\\midrule
5.2 & Lemma~5.2 & identical: concerns only $A\mapsto\chi_A(m)$\\
5.3 & Lemma~5.3, (5.24), Gaussian remark & identical: uses
 only~(R5), and the CM kernel has the same seminorm orders\\
5.3 & Lemma~5.4 & identical; for $K=V^\sharp$ the constant $C_A\ll\mathfrak M_{A,\lceil4A\rceil+2}(V)$
 comes from~(R5)\\
5.4 & (5.26), Lemma~5.5 & identical: no reflection; the
 coefficients $\beta(n,b)$ are arbitrary\\
5.5 & (5.30), (5.31) & replaced by~\eqref{eq:s1-central}; its Mellin
 form is~(R6) with $\Psi=\nu\chi_\bullet(m)$, by the same zero-preserving identity
 $\chi_{cn^3}(m)=\chi_c(m)\chi_n(m)^3$\\
5.5 & heuristic for squarefree rows & identical, with $\alpha(\ell)^2$ in the dual coefficient\\
5.5 & Definition~5.6 & the specified term is now a term of~\eqref{eq:s1-reflection}\\
5.5 & Lemma~5.7 & statement identical; in its proof put
 $\delta(n,b)=d_\sigma(\ell)\alpha(\ell)^2\vartheta(\lambda^4\ell)/(27\cdot3^{k/6}|B_Fb|)$ and $\rho(R)=81C_R\chi_R(x_F)^3$,
 still bounded by one by~(R2),~(R3); the rest is verbatim\\
5.5 & retention and tail removal & identical: uses $|d_\sigma(\ell)|\N(\ell)^{-1/2}\le27\N(\lambda)^{4/3}$,
 $|B_p|\le\N(p)^{1/2}$,~(R4) and~(R5)\\
5.5 & (5.35) & identical\\
5.5 & Lemma~5.8 & statement and proof identical for $\mathcal C^{(2)}_V$
 (Lemma~\ref{lem:s1-unmarked})\\
5.5 & Gaussian test, (5.39) & identical\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{lemma}[CM form of {\cite[Lemma~5.8]{OAI78}}]\label{lem:s1-unmarked}
Let $\nu$ range over a fixed finite family as above. Fix bounded ranges for $M\ge0$ and $N\in\mathbb R$, a constant
$C_m\ge1$ and fixed dyadic support comparisons, and let $m_{\rm pow}$ be the maximal powerful ideal factor of $m$. For
every $\varepsilon>0$ there are $A\ge0$ and an integer $J\ge0$ such that, for every log-Schwartz test $V$, every
dyadic range $\N(m_{\rm pow})\asymp Z^O$ with $O\ge0$, and all sufficiently large $Z$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d137}
 \sum_{\substack{0<\N(m)\le C_mZ^M\\\N(m_{\rm pow})\asymp Z^O}}\bigl|\mathcal C^{(2)}_V(Z^N;m,\nu)\bigr|^2
 \ll Z^{O/2+\max\{M-O,\,2M-O-N\}+\varepsilon}\,\mathfrak M_{A,J}(V)^2 .
\end{equation}
The uniformity is that of~\cite[Lemma~5.8]{OAI78}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The proof of~\cite[Lemma~5.8]{OAI78} applies verbatim. It uses the
reflection only through~(R1)--(R6), via~\cite[Lemma~5.7]{OAI78} and the tail argument after it, and the
table above records the CM form of each.
\end{proof}

Summing over the $O(\log Z)$ dyads $O\ge0$, with $N=1$, gives the precise form of
Proposition~\ref{prop:cm-completed}: for every $\varepsilon>0$ and $M$ in a bounded range there are $A,J$ with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s1-cm-completed}
 \sum_{0<\N(m)\le Z^M}\bigl|\mathcal C^{(2)}_V(Z;m,\nu)\bigr|^2\ll Z^{\max\{M,\,2M-1\}+\varepsilon}\,
 \mathfrak M_{A,J}(V)^2 ,
\end{equation}
since $O/2+\max\{M-O,2M-O-1\}\le\max\{M,2M-1\}$ for $O\ge0$. With $Q=Z^M$ the right side is
$Z^\varepsilon(Q+Q^2/Z)\mathfrak M_{A,J}(V)^2$. The test of
the probe is the Gaussian, and the finite Fourier sum over $\theta\in\widehat T$ costs a fixed factor, as
in~\cite[Section~6.1]{OAI78}.

\subsection{Marks}\label{sec:s1-marks}

\begin{proposition}[CM form of {\cite[Cor.~14.1]{OAI78}}]\label{prop:s1-marked}
Fix an instance of Proposition~\ref{prop:s1-reflection}, with local set $\mathcal P_0$, exponents $j_p$, function
$\phi$ and test $V$, and let $\mathcal Q$ be a finite set of good primes disjoint from $\mathcal P_0$. Let
$T_{V,\mathcal Q}(X;\Psi)$ be $T_V(X;\Psi)$ with the factor $\prod_{q\in\mathcal Q}\ind_{q\mid nb^3}$ inserted. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d138}
 T_{V,\mathcal Q}(X;\Psi)=\sum_{\mathcal B}C_{\mathcal Q}\sum_{0\ne\ell\in\lambda^{-4}\OO}
 \frac{d_\sigma(\ell)\alpha(\ell)^2\vartheta(\lambda^4\ell)}{\sqrt{\N(\ell)}}\prod_{p\in\mathcal A_0}B_p(\lambda^4\ell)
 \prod_{q\in\mathcal A_{\mathcal Q}}\frac{\chi_q(\lambda^4\ell)^{-2}}{\sqrt{\N(q)}}\;
 V^\sharp\Bigl(\frac{\N(\ell)X}{\N(c)^2}\Bigr),
\end{equation}
where a branch $\mathcal B$ specifies $h_0$, the base active and inactive sets $\mathcal A_0,\mathcal I_0$ and a
partition $\mathcal Q=\mathcal A_{\mathcal Q}\sqcup\mathcal I_{\mathcal Q}$; $c=c_F(h_0)\prod_{p\in\mathcal
A_0\cup\mathcal A_{\mathcal Q}}p$; $d_\sigma,\vartheta,B_p$ are those of Proposition~\ref{prop:s1-reflection} for this active
radical; and, with $\sigma_p,\epsilon_p,\varpi_{p,j}$ computed from this $c$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d139}
 C_{\mathcal Q}=-\frac{\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}}{81}\,\widehat\phi(h_0)\,\overline{\kappa_F}
 \prod_{p\in\mathcal I_0}\bigl(1-\N(p)^{-1}\bigr)\prod_{q\in\mathcal I_{\mathcal Q}}\N(q)^{-1}
 \prod_{p\in\mathcal A_0}\chi_p(\sigma_p)^{-2}\varpi_{p,j_p}
 \prod_{q\in\mathcal A_{\mathcal Q}}\chi_q(\sigma_q)^{-2}\bigl(-\varpi_{q,0}\bigr).
\end{equation}
So $C_{\mathcal Q}=-i\,\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,2}\zeta_{\mathcal Q}$ with $\zeta_{\mathcal Q}$
of~\cite[eq.~(14.4)]{OAI78}, and $|C_{\mathcal Q}|\le\frac1{81}$. Every dual series converges
absolutely. A marked prime that belongs to $\mathcal P_0$ makes the primal term vanish identically.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The proof of~\cite[Cor.~14.1]{OAI78} expands $\prod_q(1-\chi_q(A)^0)$ and applies the
reflection to each of the finitely many resulting completed sums. It then combines branches using only the branch
compatibility. Both hold for Proposition~\ref{prop:s1-reflection}, by~(c) and~(d), so the proof applies verbatim. The
inactive coefficient is $1-(1-\N(q)^{-1})=\N(q)^{-1}$. The active contribution comes from the subtracted zero-mask
transform, with local factor $\N(q)^{-1/2}\chi_q(\lambda^4\ell)^{-2}$ and scalar
$-\varpi_{q,0}=\mathfrak g^+_{q,2}\chi_q(\epsilon_q)^{-2}$.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Separation of the phases.} The discussion after the corollary in~\cite[Section~14.2]{OAI78} carries over.
The pair identity~\cite[eq.~(14.5)]{OAI78},
$\chi_p(q)^{2j_p+2}\chi_q(p)^{2j_q+2}=(q/p)_3^{j_p+j_q+2}$, is local and unchanged. A residual row prime ($j_p=1$) and
an active mark ($j_q=0$) therefore have pair factor $(q/p)_3^3=1$. Write $R$ for the product of the residual row
primes, $P$ for the product of the active marked primes and $F_{\rm act}$ for the frozen active base product, so that
$c=c_FF_{\rm act}RP$. The angular factor of $C_{\mathcal Q}$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d140}
 \overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,4}=\overline{\alpha(c_FF_{\rm act})}^{\,4}\cdot\overline{\alpha(R)}^{\,4}\cdot
 \overline{\alpha(P)}^{\,4},
\end{equation}
a fixed factor times a row factor times a tuple factor. On the dual side, after the unit, the
$\lambda$-valuation and the extracted frozen factors are fixed, $\ell=u\lambda^{h_\lambda}N_Fn(B_Fb)^3$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d141}
 \alpha(\ell)^2=\alpha\bigl(u\lambda^{h_\lambda}N_FB_F^3\bigr)^2\,\alpha(n)^2\,\alpha(b)^6,
\end{equation}
a function of the dual index alone. So, after the common smooth separation, the tuple coefficient is a bounded
function of the tuple independent of $R,n,b$, and the dual coefficient is independent of $R$ and $P$, exactly as stated
in~\cite[Section~14.2]{OAI78}.

\subsection{Reflected energy and the compensated completed row}\label{sec:s1-energy}

\paragraph{The quadratic--cubic norm.} \cite[Lemma~14.2]{OAI78} is a statement about
arbitrary bounded coefficients $a(P)$ and $\beta(n,b)$, proved with the quadratic and cubic large sieves. It involves
neither the reflection nor the weight, and it is used without change.

\paragraph{The marked completion.} Take the data of~\cite[Section~14.1]{OAI78}: disjoint slot lists $\mathcal P_i$ of
good primes with bounded coefficients $a_i(p)$, a fixed puncture $\rho$, a squarefree ideal $f$ outside $S$, a
multiplicative finite-ray character $\nu$ with defining modulus supported on $S$, $\Psi_k(n)=\nu(n)\rho(n)\chi_n(k)
\chi_n(f)^4$, the mark $\mathfrak d$ of~\cite[eq.~(14.1)]{OAI78}, and $V_*(y)=y^{1/2}W(y)$ with $W$ smooth and
annular. The CM marked completion is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s1-marked-completion}
 \mathcal T^{(2)}_{\mathfrak d}(X;k)=\sum_{\substack{n\ {\rm sf}\\b}}
 \frac{\overline{\alpha(n)}^{\,2}\gamma_2(n)\Psi_k(n)\,\overline{\alpha(b)}^{\,6}\Psi_k(b)^3}{\sqrt{\N(n)}\,\N(b)}
 \,\mathfrak d(nb^3)\,V_*\Bigl(\frac{\N(n)\N(b)^3}X\Bigr),
\end{equation}
which is~\cite[eq.~(14.2)]{OAI78} with $\overline\alpha,\overline\alpha^{\,3}$ replaced by
$\overline\alpha^{\,2},\overline\alpha^{\,6}$. Its reflection is Proposition~\ref{prop:s1-marked}, applied after the
row-sector reduction of~\cite[Section~14.1]{OAI78}, which does not involve the weight.

\begin{lemma}[CM form of {\cite[Lemma~14.3]{OAI78}}]\label{lem:s1-reflected-energy}
Define the reflected block data as in~\cite[Section~14.4, \emph{Reflected block data}]{OAI78}, with~\cite[eq.~(14.3)]{OAI78} replaced by
the expansion of Proposition~\ref{prop:s1-marked} for~\eqref{eq:s1-marked-completion}. Then every assertion
of~\cite[Lemma~14.3]{OAI78} holds verbatim: the retention rule~\cite[eq.~(14.11)]{OAI78} with the
same $T_d$ and the same negligible tail, the coefficient exponent~\cite[eq.~(14.12)]{OAI78}, and, with
$u=\min\{v,z_a,(v+z_a)/3\}$ and $E_{\rm ref}$ of~\cite[eqs.~(14.13) and (14.14)]{OAI78},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d142}
 \sum_{k_{\rm res}\ {\rm sf}}|\mathcal U_{v,\ell_b,e_\lambda}(k_{\rm res})|^2\ll Z^{E_{\rm ref}-O/2+\varepsilon}
\end{equation}
for fixed $k_{\rm pow},k_{\rm sup}$, and $O(Z^{E_{\rm ref}+\varepsilon})$ after summing them, with the same
uniformity. Consequently~\cite[eqs.~(14.17) and~(14.18)]{OAI78} hold for
$\mathcal T^{(2)}_1$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d143}
 \sideset{}{'}\sum_{0<\N(k)\ll Z^M}\bigl|\mathcal T^{(2)}_1(Z^{N_*};k)\bigr|^2\ll
 Z^{O/2+\max\{M-O,\,2M-O+Q_\rho+V_f-N_*\}+\varepsilon},
\end{equation}
where $Q_\rho=\log_Z\N(\mathfrak r_\rho)$ and $V_f=\log_Z\N(f)$ are the lengths called $Q$ and $V$
in~\cite{OAI78}, and the prime restricts the powerful part of $k$ to its $O$-dyad.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
We go through the proof of~\cite[Lemma~14.3]{OAI78} and list where the reflection
enters.
\begin{itemize}
\item \cite[proof of Lemma~14.3]{OAI78}: inactive slots are removed by the triangle inequality, using the inactive coefficient
 $\N(p)^{-1}$ and the fact that an inactive prime is absent from the conductor. This is (R1) for
 Proposition~\ref{prop:s1-marked}.
\item \cite[proof of Lemma~14.3]{OAI78}: the frozen-base extraction~\cite[eq.~(5.32)]{OAI78}, made simultaneous over the
 tuples by the separation of phases. Its common coefficient comes from $|d_\sigma(\ell)|\le27\cdot3^{k/6}|b|$, the
 normalization $\N(\ell)^{-1/2}$ and the local factors; the CM dual coefficient differs by the unimodular
 $\alpha(\ell)^2$, which goes into $\beta(n,b)$ (R3), and the scalar is separated as in Section~\ref{sec:s1-marks}
 (R2).
\item \cite[proof of Lemma~14.3]{OAI78}: the kernel argument~\cite[eq.~(14.15)]{OAI78} and the extraction of
 $m_{\rm ker}=Z^{-(D_{\rm ker})_+/4}$ by~\cite[Lemma~5.3]{OAI78}. These use (R4) and (R5).
\item \cite[proof of Lemma~14.3]{OAI78}: the tail, by~\cite[Lemma~5.4]{OAI78}, uses $|d_\sigma(\ell)|\N(\ell)^{-1/2}\le
 27\N(\lambda)^{4/3}$, $|B_p|,|\chi_q^{-2}|\le\N(p)^{1/2}$, (R4) and (R5).
\item \cite[proof of Lemma~14.3]{OAI78}: Fourier separation and the verification of the two independence hypotheses. In the CM case the
 tuple scalar acquires $\overline{\alpha(P)}^{\,4}$, the row scalar acquires $\overline{\alpha(R)}^{\,4}$, and the dual
 coefficient acquires $\alpha(\ell)^2$, as shown in Section~\ref{sec:s1-marks}. The independence hypotheses
 of~\cite[Lemma~14.2]{OAI78} are preserved.
\item \cite[proof of Lemma~14.3]{OAI78}: the application of~\cite[Lemma~14.2]{OAI78} and the count of the frozen row parts. No
 reflection enters.
\end{itemize}
None of these changes alters a norm power, so every exponent is the same. The deductions in \cite[Section~14.4, \emph{Reflected block data}]{OAI78} use only the
lemma and norm inequalities for the rows.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{The compensated completed row.} For the CM target $\psi=\overline\alpha\eta$, the completed row of the
compensated probe (\cite[Section~15.1]{OAI78}, with the weight~\eqref{eq:cm-weight}) is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d144}
 B^{J,(2)}_{m,\varsigma}(Z)=\sum_{\substack{p_i\in\mathcal P_i(Z)\\i\notin J}}\prod_{i\notin J}a(p_i)\,
 W_i\Bigl(\frac{\N(p_i)}{P_i}\Bigr)\sum_{\theta\in\widehat T}a_\theta\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{(4)}
 \bigl(Z\N(p_{J^c})\bigr)^t\,\Phi(t)\,\mathcal T^{(2)}_{p_{J^c}}\bigl(t+\tfrac12,\nu_\varsigma\theta\chi_\bullet(m)\bigr)\,dt,
\end{equation}
where $\Phi(t)=e^{t^2}$, $\varsigma\in T$ is a ray class (called $\sigma$ in~\cite{OAI78}),
$\nu_\varsigma=\eta\,\Xi^{-1}\mathcal R(\cdot,s_\varsigma)$ is the finite-order character
of~\cite[Section~6.1]{OAI78} built from the finite part $\eta$, $a(p)=\overline{\psi(p)}=\alpha(p)\overline{\eta(p)}$
is the slot normalization of Section~\ref{sec:78rows} (\cite{OAI78} has $\overline{\eta(p)}$), and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d145}
 \mathcal T^{(2)}_D(s_0,\nu')=\sum_{\substack{c\ {\rm sf}\\n}}\gamma_2(c)\,\overline{\alpha(cn^3)}^{\,2}\,
 \nu'(cn^3)\,\ind_{D\mid cn^3}\,\N(c)^{-s_0}\N(n)^{-3s_0+1/2}.
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[CM form of {\cite[Lemma~15.1]{OAI78}}]\label{lem:s1-probe-row-norm}
With $d_J=\sum_{i\in J}\ell_i$ (called $d$ in~\cite[eq.~(15.1)]{OAI78}), and $M'$ and $Q=\N(b_*)X'Y'\asymp Z^{M'}$ as there, for every $\varepsilon>0$, every
fixed rescaled subset $J$ and every ray class $\varsigma\in T$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d146}
 \sum_{0<\N(m)\ll Q}\bigl|B^{J,(2)}_{m,\varsigma}(Z)\bigr|^2\ll Z^{M'+((d_J-1/6)/4)_++\varepsilon}=Z^{M'+\varepsilon},
\end{equation}
uniformly in the fixed rescaled tuple and with no loss depending on the mesh of the surviving slots. The same holds
for any slot coefficients $a(p)$ with $|a(p)|\le1$ that are independent of the row and of the other slots.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The proof of~\cite[Lemma~15.1]{OAI78} applies verbatim. It uses the Gaussian annular
decomposition~\cite[Lemma~4.6]{OAI78}, the smooth calculus~\cite[Lemma~4.5]{OAI78},
the row sectors~\cite[Lemma~5.2]{OAI78}, and the reflected energy, here
Lemma~\ref{lem:s1-reflected-energy}; the remaining steps are exponent bookkeeping. The target enters in two places
only. First, the completed factor is the CM marked completion~\eqref{eq:s1-marked-completion}, with an annular test
at the length $N_*$; this is covered by Lemma~\ref{lem:s1-reflected-energy}. Second, the slot coefficients are
required (\cite[proof of Lemma~15.1]{OAI78}) to be bounded, independent of the row and of the other slots, and of product form after
separation. This holds for $\alpha(p)\overline{\eta(p)}$ as for $\overline{\eta(p)}$. The small-argument exponent
$\frac14$ of the kernel, used at \cite[proof of Lemma~15.1]{OAI78}, is that of Proposition~\ref{prop:s1-reflection}(e).
\end{proof}

With Lemma~\ref{lem:s1-probe-row-norm} in place of~\cite[Lemma~15.1]{OAI78}, the compensated low
estimate $|I_{\rm mod}(Z)|\ll Z^{3/16+\varepsilon}$ follows as in~\cite[Prop.~15.3]{OAI78}, given the additive Gram
bound, which does not involve the target.

\paragraph{Numerical checks.} The new identities were tested.
\begin{itemize}
\item End to end, with an active prime: for the twist $\Psi_0=\overline{(\lambda/\cdot)_3}$ of
 Appendix~\ref{app:reflection}, $L=18$, a local prime $p$ of norm $7$ with $j_p=0,\dots,5$ (both primes of norm $7$),
 and two log-Schwartz tests without compact support, the log-Gaussian and $e^{-y-1/y}$ (with
 $\widehat V(t)=2K_t(2)$), $T_V(X;\Psi)$ computed from its definition equals both the ungrouped sum over the $252$
 translates and the grouped formula~\eqref{eq:s1-reflection}. The relative error is at most $1.7\cdot10^{-12}$ and
 $5.2\cdot10^{-8}$ for the two tests; the cusps cover $\sigma\in\{0,+,-\}$ and $\N(c)\in\{1,4,7,28\}$. The
 combination $\overline{\kappa_F}\vartheta(\lambda^4\ell)$ extracted from each active translate is the same for all
 $h_p\ne0$ to $2.4\cdot10^{-12}$; this checks the multiplier and additive-phase bookkeeping. The marked formula of
 Proposition~\ref{prop:s1-marked} with $\mathcal Q=\{p\}$ holds to $8\cdot10^{-13}$.
\item The local identities: the Fourier coefficients $C_{p,j}$, the local sums giving $\varpi_{p,j}B_p$ for every unit
 $c/p$, the cross-prime identity and the pair identity, at $15$ good primes (split and inert) and all $j$, to
 $10^{-13}$.
\item The constant $-\frac1{81}((2\pi)^4/27)^{-t}$, the
 Stirling exponent $4\Re t$ of $R_2$, the line independence of $V^\sharp$ down to $\Re t=-1$, the small-$x$ expansion
 of $V^\sharp$ from the poles at $-\frac43,-\frac53$, and the
 bound~(e) for both tests on $10^{-8}\le x\le10^6$.
\end{itemize}

\section{Hecke \texorpdfstring{$L$}{L}-functions of infinity type \texorpdfstring{$\bar z/|z|$}{zbar/|z|}}\label{app:s2-hecke}

This appendix proves Lemma~\ref{lem:cm-growth}. It also proves the CM form
of~\cite[Lemma~4.9]{OAI78}, the logarithmic control. Section~\ref{sec:s2-uses} lists every
place where~\cite{OAI78} uses its growth lemmas, and the CM statement that replaces each. The only change is the gamma factor:
$\Gamma(s+\frac12)$ in place of $\Gamma(s)$. Every exponent of the conductor and of the height used
in~\cite{OAI78} stays the same, and no constant depends on the character.

We write $\zeta_K(\sigma)=\zeta(\sigma)L(\sigma,\chi_{-3})$ for the Dedekind zeta function of
$K$~\cite[Theorem~3]{QF}, $e(z)=\exp(4\pi i\operatorname{Im}z/\sqrt3)$ as in Section~\ref{sec:poisson}, and
$\alpha(a)=a/|a|$ for every $a\ne0$. Since $|\psi(\mathfrak p)|\le1$ for every character below, we have
$|L(s,\psi)|\le\zeta_K(\sigma)$ and $|L(s,\psi)^{-1}|\le\zeta_K(\sigma)$ for $\sigma=\Re s>1$.

\subsection{The characters}\label{sec:s2-chars}
Let $\mathfrak m$ be a nonzero ideal. Following Hecke~\cite{Hec20} (see~\cite[Ch.~VII, \S6]{Neukirch}), a \emph{character of modulus $\mathfrak m$ and infinity type $\bar z/|z|$}
is a homomorphism $\psi$ from the group of fractional ideals prime to $\mathfrak m$ to the unit circle such
that $\psi((a))=\overline{\alpha(a)}$ whenever $a=b/c$ with $b,c\in\OO$ prime to $\mathfrak m$ and
$b\equiv c\pmod{\mathfrak m}$. Its conductor is the least modulus, for divisibility, from which it is induced,
and it is primitive if its modulus is its conductor. Infinity type $z/|z|$ is defined in the same way with
$\alpha(a)$ in place of $\overline{\alpha(a)}$; $\psi$ has infinity type $\bar z/|z|$ exactly when
$\overline\psi$ has infinity type $z/|z|$. We write $\overline{\mathcal F}_{\rm CM}=\{\overline\psi:
\psi\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}\}$.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:s2-pres}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] Let $\psi$ have modulus $\mathfrak m$ and infinity type $\bar z/|z|$. For $a\in\OO$ prime to
 $\mathfrak m$ put $\phi(a)=\psi((a))\alpha(a)$, and put $\phi(a)=0$ for the other $a\in\OO$. Then $\phi$ is a
 character of $(\OO/\mathfrak m)^\times$, extended by zero, with $\phi(\zeta)=\zeta$ for the six units $\zeta$,
 and
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:s2-pres}
  \psi((a))=\phi(a)\,\overline{\alpha(a)}\qquad(a\in\OO\text{ prime to }\mathfrak m).
 \end{equation}
 Conversely, every character $\phi$ of $(\OO/\mathfrak m)^\times$ with $\phi(\zeta)=\zeta$ for all units
 defines by~\eqref{eq:s2-pres} a character of modulus $\mathfrak m$ and infinity type $\bar z/|z|$.
\item[(ii)] $\psi$ is primitive modulo $\mathfrak m$ if and only if $\phi$ is primitive modulo $\mathfrak m$ in
 the sense of~\cite[Section~4.2]{QF}. The conductor of $\psi$ is never $(1)$.
\item[(iii)] A character has infinity type $\bar z/|z|$ if and only if it agrees on primary elements with
 $\overline\alpha\eta$ for a finite-order Hecke character $\eta$. Thus $\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$ (Section~\ref{sec:78})
 is the set of primitive characters of infinity type $\bar z/|z|$. The character $\overline\alpha$, that is
 $(n)\mapsto\overline{\alpha(n)}$ for primary $n$, has $\phi(a)=$ the unit congruent to $a$ modulo $3$, and
 conductor $(3)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(i) Let $a,b\in\OO$ be prime to $\mathfrak m$ with $a\equiv b\pmod{\mathfrak m}$. Then
$\psi((a))/\psi((b))=\psi((a/b))=\overline{\alpha(a)}/\overline{\alpha(b)}$, that is, $\phi(a)=\phi(b)$. So
$\phi$ is a function on $(\OO/\mathfrak m)^\times$. It is multiplicative because $\psi$ and $\alpha$ are, and
$\phi(\zeta)=\psi((1))\alpha(\zeta)=\zeta$. Conversely, the right side of~\eqref{eq:s2-pres} does not change
when $a$ is replaced by $\zeta a$, since $\phi(\zeta a)\overline{\alpha(\zeta a)}=\zeta\bar\zeta\,\phi(a)\overline{\alpha(a)}$.
Every ideal of $\OO$ is principal, so~\eqref{eq:s2-pres} defines a character of the integral ideals prime to
$\mathfrak m$, and hence of the fractional ones. If $b\equiv c\pmod{\mathfrak m}$, then $\phi(b)=\phi(c)$ and
$\psi((b/c))=\overline{\alpha(b)}/\overline{\alpha(c)}=\overline{\alpha(b/c)}$.

(ii) For a divisor $\mathfrak m'$ of $\mathfrak m$, $\psi$ is induced from modulus $\mathfrak m'$ exactly when
$\psi((a))=\overline{\alpha(a)}$ for all $a\in\OO$ prime to $\mathfrak m$ with $a\equiv1\pmod{\mathfrak m'}$, that
is, when $\phi$ is trivial on these classes. This is the definition of~\cite[Section~4.2]{QF}. If the
conductor were $(1)$, $\phi$ would be a character of the trivial group, but $\phi(-1)=-1$.

(iii) Every ideal $\mathfrak a$ prime to $3$ has a unique primary generator $n_{\mathfrak a}$, and products of
primary elements are primary~\cite[Section~2.7]{QF}. If $a=b/c$ with $b,c\in\OO$ prime to $3\mathfrak m$ and
$b\equiv c\pmod{3\mathfrak m}$, write $b=\zeta_bn_{(b)}$ and $c=\zeta_cn_{(c)}$ with units $\zeta_b,\zeta_c$. Since
the six units are distinct modulo $3$, $b\equiv c\pmod3$ forces $\zeta_b=\zeta_c$, and then
$n_{(b)}\equiv n_{(c)}\pmod{\mathfrak m}$.

Let $\psi$ have modulus $\mathfrak m$ and infinity type $\bar z/|z|$, and put $\eta(\mathfrak a)=\psi(\mathfrak a)\alpha(n_{\mathfrak a})$
for $\mathfrak a$ prime to $3\mathfrak m$. By~\eqref{eq:s2-pres}, $\eta(\mathfrak a)=\phi(n_{\mathfrak a})$. So
$\eta$ is multiplicative, takes values in roots of unity, and is trivial on $(b/c)$ when
$b\equiv c\pmod{3\mathfrak m}$, by the previous paragraph. Hence $\eta$ is a finite-order ray class character
modulo $3\mathfrak m$, and $\psi((n))=\overline{\alpha(n)}\eta((n))$ for primary $n$. Conversely, let $\eta$ be a
ray class character modulo $\mathfrak m_0$, and put $\psi(\mathfrak a)=\overline{\alpha(n_{\mathfrak a})}\eta(\mathfrak a)$
for $\mathfrak a$ prime to $3\mathfrak m_0$. If $b\equiv c\pmod{3\mathfrak m_0}$, then $\zeta_b=\zeta_c$, so
$\overline{\alpha(n_{(b)})}/\overline{\alpha(n_{(c)})}=\overline{\alpha(b/c)}$, and $\eta((b/c))=1$. So $\psi$ has
modulus $3\mathfrak m_0$ and infinity type $\bar z/|z|$.

For $\overline\alpha$: if $\zeta\equiv a\pmod3$ is a unit, then $n_{(a)}=\zeta^{-1}a$ and
$\overline{\alpha(n_{(a)})}=\zeta\,\overline{\alpha(a)}$, so $\phi(a)=\zeta$, a character modulo $3$. It is not
trivial on the classes $a\equiv1\pmod\lambda$, because $\omega\equiv1\pmod\lambda$ and $\phi(\omega)=\omega$.
The only proper divisors of $(3)=(\lambda)^2$ are $(\lambda)$ and $(1)$, so the conductor is $(3)$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Theta series and the functional equation}\label{sec:s2-fe}
We follow Hecke's method~\cite{Hec20} (see~\cite[Ch.~VII, \S\S7--8]{Neukirch}). For $\psi\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$ with conductor $\mathfrak f=(f)$, $Q=\N\mathfrak f=|f|^2$, and $\phi$ as in
Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-pres}, put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d147}
 \Theta_\phi(v)=\sum_{a\in\OO}\phi(a)\,\bar a\,e^{-2\pi v\N(a)/\sqrt3}\quad(v>0),\qquad
 \gamma(\phi)=\frac1{\sqrt Q}\sum_{x\bmod f}\phi(x)\,e\Bigl(\frac xf\Bigr).
\end{equation}
The term $a=0$ vanishes. Since $\phi$ is primitive modulo $f$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-pres}(ii)),
\cite[Lemma~17]{QF} gives $|\gamma(\phi)|=1$ and, for every $h\in\OO$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s2-gauss}
 \sum_{x\bmod f}\phi(x)\,e\Bigl(\frac{hx}f\Bigr)=\overline{\phi(h)}\,\sqrt Q\,\gamma(\phi).
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:s2-fe}
Let $\psi\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$, with $f$, $Q$ and $\phi$ as above.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] For $v>0$,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:d148}
  \Theta_\phi(v)=-\frac{\gamma(\phi)}{f\sqrt Q}\,v^{-2}\,\overline{\Theta_\phi\bigl(1/(Qv)\bigr)} .
 \end{equation}
\item[(ii)] For $\Re s>1$,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:d149}
  \int_0^\infty\Theta_\phi(v)\,v^{s+1/2}\,\frac{dv}v
  =6\Bigl(\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt3}\Bigr)^{-s-1/2}\Gamma(s+\tfrac12)\,L(s,\psi),
 \end{equation}
 and the same holds with $\overline{\Theta_\phi}$ and $\overline\psi$ in place of $\Theta_\phi$ and $\psi$.
\item[(iii)] The function $\Lambda(s,\psi)=(3Q)^{s/2}(2\pi)^{-s}\Gamma(s+\frac12)L(s,\psi)$ extends to an entire
 function that is bounded on every vertical strip of finite width, and
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:d150}
  \Lambda(s,\psi)=W(\psi)\,\Lambda(1-s,\overline\psi),\qquad
  W(\psi)=-\frac{\gamma(\phi)\sqrt Q}{f},\qquad |W(\psi)|=1 .
 \end{equation}
 The same holds for $\overline\psi$, with $W(\overline\psi)=\overline{W(\psi)}$.
\item[(iv)] $L(s,\psi)$ and $L(s,\overline\psi)$ are entire and have no zeros in $\Re s>1$. For each fixed $\psi$
 and each bounded interval $J$, $|L(\sigma+it,\psi)|\ll_{\psi,J}e^{2|t|}$ for $\sigma\in J$, and likewise for
 $\overline\psi$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(i) Fix $v>0$ and put $G(w)=\bar w\,e^{-2\pi v|w|^2/\sqrt3}$ and
$\widehat G(\eta)=\int_{\mathbb C}G(w)e(-w\eta)\,dx\,dy$. We first compute $\widehat G$. By~\cite[Example~20]{QF},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d151}
 \int_{\mathbb C}e^{-2\pi v|w|^2/\sqrt3}\,e(-w\eta)\,dx\,dy=\frac{\sqrt3}{2v}\,e^{-2\pi|\eta|^2/(\sqrt3v)} .
\end{equation}
Since $\operatorname{Im}z=(z-\bar z)/(2i)$, we have $e(-w\eta)=\exp\bigl(-\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt3}(w\eta-\bar w\bar\eta)\bigr)$, so
$\partial_{\bar\eta}e(-w\eta)=\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt3}\,\bar w\,e(-w\eta)$, where $\partial_{\bar\eta}$ is the Wirtinger
derivative. Differentiating under the integral sign, which the Gaussian decay permits,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d152}
 \widehat G(\eta)=\frac{\sqrt3}{2\pi}\,\partial_{\bar\eta}\Bigl(\frac{\sqrt3}{2v}e^{-2\pi\eta\bar\eta/(\sqrt3v)}\Bigr)
 =-\frac{\sqrt3}{2v^2}\,\eta\,e^{-2\pi|\eta|^2/(\sqrt3v)} .
\end{equation}
Now split $a=t+fz$, with $t$ running over a set of representatives modulo $f$ and $z\in\OO$, so that
$\Theta_\phi(v)=\sum_t\phi(t)\sum_{z\in\OO}G(t+fz)$. Poisson summation over $\OO$~\cite[Theorem~19]{QF} gives
$\sum_zG(t+fz)=\frac2{\sqrt3}\sum_{y\in\OO}\widehat F_t(y)$ with $F_t(z)=G(t+fz)$. The substitution $w=t+fz$ has
Jacobian $|f|^2=Q$, and $e(-zy)=e(ty/f)\,e(-wy/f)$, so
$\widehat F_t(y)=Q^{-1}e(ty/f)\,\widehat G(y/f)$. Summing over $t$ and using~\eqref{eq:s2-gauss} with $h=y$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d153}
\begin{split}
 \Theta_\phi(v)&=\frac2{\sqrt3\,Q}\sum_{y\in\OO}\overline{\phi(y)}\sqrt Q\,\gamma(\phi)
 \Bigl(-\frac{\sqrt3}{2v^2}\Bigr)\frac yf\,e^{-2\pi\N(y)/(\sqrt3Qv)}\\
 &=-\frac{\gamma(\phi)}{f\sqrt Q}\,v^{-2}\sum_{y\in\OO}\overline{\phi(y)}\,y\,e^{-2\pi\N(y)/(\sqrt3Qv)} .
\end{split}
\end{equation}
The last sum is $\overline{\Theta_\phi(1/(Qv))}$.

(ii) For $\sigma>1$,
$\sum_{a\ne0}|a|\int_0^\infty e^{-2\pi v\N(a)/\sqrt3}v^{\sigma+1/2}\,dv/v
=6\,(2\pi/\sqrt3)^{-\sigma-1/2}\Gamma(\sigma+\frac12)\zeta_K(\sigma)<\infty$, because every nonzero ideal has six
generators. So we may integrate termwise:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d154}
 \int_0^\infty\Theta_\phi(v)v^{s+1/2}\frac{dv}v
 =\Bigl(\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt3}\Bigr)^{-s-1/2}\Gamma(s+\tfrac12)\sum_{a\ne0}\phi(a)\,\bar a\,\N(a)^{-s-1/2}.
\end{equation}
Since $\bar a\,\N(a)^{-1/2}=\overline{\alpha(a)}$, the summand is $\psi((a))\N(a)^{-s}$ by~\eqref{eq:s2-pres}, and
it vanishes unless $a$ is prime to $\mathfrak f$. Each ideal prime to $\mathfrak f$ has six generators, so the sum
is $6L(s,\psi)$. For $\overline\psi$ use $\overline{\Theta_\phi(v)}=\sum_a\overline{\phi(a)}\,a\,e^{-2\pi v\N(a)/\sqrt3}$
and $\overline\psi((a))=\overline{\phi(a)}\alpha(a)$.

(iii) Let $I_\psi(s)$ denote the left side of~(ii), and $I_{\overline\psi}(s)$ the same integral with
$\overline{\Theta_\phi}$. Put $v_0=Q^{-1/2}$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d155}
 B(w)=\int_{v_0}^\infty\Theta_\phi(v)v^{w+1/2}\frac{dv}v,\qquad
 B^-(w)=\int_{v_0}^\infty\overline{\Theta_\phi(v)}\,v^{w+1/2}\frac{dv}v .
\end{equation}
Since $\N(a)\ge1$ for $a\ne0$, for $v\ge v_0$ we have
$|\Theta_\phi(v)|\le e^{-\pi v/\sqrt3}\sum_{a\ne0}|a|e^{-\pi v_0\N(a)/\sqrt3}$. So $B$ and $B^-$ are entire and
bounded on every vertical strip of finite width. For $\Re s>1$ split $I_\psi(s)$ at $v_0$, substitute
$v=1/(Qu)$ in the integral over $(0,v_0)$, and use~(i):
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d156}
 \int_0^{v_0}\Theta_\phi(v)v^{s+1/2}\frac{dv}v
 =-\frac{\gamma(\phi)}{f\sqrt Q}\int_0^{v_0}\overline{\Theta_\phi\bigl(1/(Qv)\bigr)}\,v^{s-3/2}\frac{dv}v
 =-\frac{\gamma(\phi)}{f\sqrt Q}\,Q^{3/2-s}B^-(1-s).
\end{equation}
With $W=-\gamma(\phi)\sqrt Q/f$ this gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s2-split}
 Q^{s/2}I_\psi(s)=Q^{s/2}B(s)+W\,Q^{(1-s)/2}B^-(1-s).
\end{equation}
Conjugating~(i) gives $\overline{\Theta_\phi(v)}=-\frac{\overline{\gamma(\phi)}}{\bar f\sqrt Q}\,v^{-2}\Theta_\phi(1/(Qv))$,
and the same computation gives
$Q^{s/2}I_{\overline\psi}(s)=Q^{s/2}B^-(s)+\overline W\,Q^{(1-s)/2}B(1-s)$. Here $|W|=|\gamma(\phi)|=1$. By~(ii),
$Q^{s/2}I_\psi(s)=\kappa\,\Lambda(s,\psi)$ and $Q^{s/2}I_{\overline\psi}(s)=\kappa\,\Lambda(s,\overline\psi)$, with
$\kappa=6\cdot3^{1/4}(2\pi)^{-1/2}$, because $(2\pi/\sqrt3)^{-s-1/2}=3^{1/4}(2\pi)^{-1/2}\,3^{s/2}(2\pi)^{-s}$. The
right sides of these two identities are entire and bounded on vertical strips of finite width, which gives the
continuation. Replacing $s$ by $1-s$ in the second identity and multiplying by $W$ gives the first one, since
$|W|^2=1$; that is, $\Lambda(s,\psi)=W\Lambda(1-s,\overline\psi)$. In the same way
$\Lambda(s,\overline\psi)=\overline W\Lambda(1-s,\psi)$.

(iv) $1/\Gamma$ and $(3Q)^{-s/2}(2\pi)^s$ are entire, so~(iii) shows that $L(s,\psi)$ is entire. The Euler
product converges absolutely in $\Re s>1$, so there are no zeros there. On $\sigma\in J$, Stirling's formula gives
$|1/\Gamma(s+\frac12)|\ll_J(1+|t|)^{c_J}e^{\pi|t|/2}\ll_Je^{2|t|}$, and $\Lambda$ is bounded there. The same
argument applies to $\overline\psi$.
\end{proof}

The root number $W(\psi)$ is the number $\epsilon(\psi)$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:cm-growth}. It does not depend on
the choice of the generator $f$: replacing $f$ by $\zeta f$ multiplies $\gamma(\phi)$ by $\phi(\zeta)=\zeta$
(substitute $x=\zeta x'$). For $\overline\alpha$ ($f=3$) the six units represent $(\OO/3)^\times$, and
$\sum_\zeta\zeta\,e(\zeta/3)=-3$, so $\gamma(\phi)=-1$ and $W(\overline\alpha)=1$. This agrees with the root
number $+1$ of $y^2=x^3+16$, the curve $E_{16}$ of conductor $27$: since $4\cdot16=2^6$, its character
$\psi_{16}^*$ of Section~\ref{sec:78} is $\overline\alpha$ on primary $n$ prime to $2$.

\subsection{The gamma quotient and the strip bound}\label{sec:s2-strip}
Put $G(\sigma,t)=|\Gamma(\frac32-\sigma-it)/\Gamma(\frac12+\sigma+it)|$. This is the gamma quotient in the
functional equation: by Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-fe}(iii), for $\psi\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}\cup\overline{\mathcal F}_{\rm CM}$
with conductor norm $Q$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s2-fe-unc}
 L(s,\psi)=W(\psi)\Bigl(\frac{3Q}{(2\pi)^2}\Bigr)^{1/2-s}\frac{\Gamma(\frac32-s)}{\Gamma(\frac12+s)}\,L(1-s,\overline\psi).
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:s2-gamma}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] For every compact $I\subset(-\frac12,\frac32)$, $G(\sigma,t)\asymp_I(3+|t|)^{1-2\sigma}$ for
 $\sigma\in I$ and $t\in\mathbb R$.
\item[(b)] For every compact $I\subset(-\infty,\frac32)$, $G(\sigma,t)\ll_I(3+|t|)^{1-2\sigma}$ for $\sigma\in I$.
 Equivalently, since $G(1-\sigma,-t)=|\Gamma(\frac12+\sigma+it)/\Gamma(\frac32-\sigma-it)|$, for every compact
 $I\subset(-\frac12,\infty)$,
 $|\Gamma(\frac12+\sigma+it)/\Gamma(\frac32-\sigma-it)|\ll_I(3+|t|)^{2\sigma-1}$ for $\sigma\in I$.
\end{enumerate}
The exponent $1-2\sigma$ is that of $|\Gamma(1-\sigma-it)/\Gamma(\sigma+it)|$, the quotient of~\cite{OAI78} for
finite-order characters.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Stirling's formula gives $|\Gamma(x+iy)|=\sqrt{2\pi}\,|y|^{x-1/2}e^{-\pi|y|/2}\bigl(1+O_K(|y|^{-1})\bigr)$ for $|y|\ge1$,
uniformly for $x$ in a compact set $K\subset\mathbb R$. For $|t|$ larger than a constant depending on $I$, it
gives $G(\sigma,t)=|t|^{(1-\sigma)-\sigma}(1+O_I(|t|^{-1}))$, since the factors $e^{-\pi|t|/2}$ cancel. In~(a)
the real parts $\frac32-\sigma$ and $\frac12+\sigma$ lie in a compact subset of $(0,2)$, so $G$ is continuous
and positive on $I\times[-T,T]$ for every $T$. In~(b) the real part $\frac32-\sigma$ is bounded below by a
positive constant, so $\Gamma(\frac32-\sigma-it)$ is continuous on $I\times[-T,T]$, and $1/\Gamma$ is entire.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:cm-growth}.} The first assertion is Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-fe}(iii), with
$\epsilon(\psi)=W(\psi)$. The last assertion follows from~\eqref{eq:s2-fe-unc} and Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-gamma}(a). For
the strip bound, let $\psi\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}\cup\overline{\mathcal F}_{\rm CM}$. On $\sigma=\frac{11}{10}$,
$|L(s,\psi)|\le\zeta_K(\frac{11}{10})$. On $\sigma=-\frac1{10}$,~\eqref{eq:s2-fe-unc} and
Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-gamma}(a) give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d157}
 |L(-\tfrac1{10}+it,\psi)|=(3Q)^{3/5}(2\pi)^{-6/5}\,G(-\tfrac1{10},t)\,|L(\tfrac{11}{10}-it,\overline\psi)|
 \ll Q^{3/5}(3+|t|)^{6/5}.
\end{equation}
This is the left-line bound of~\cite[proof of Lemma~4.8]{OAI78}, with the same exponents. The rest is the damped
maximum principle of~\cite[proof of Lemma~4.8]{OAI78}, which applies verbatim. Namely,
$H(s)=Q^{-3/5}L(s,\psi)(s+2)^{-2}$ is holomorphic on the closed strip $-\frac1{10}\le\sigma\le\frac{11}{10}$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-fe}(iv)). It is bounded on both boundary lines by an absolute constant $C_0$, because
$|s+2|^2\ge(\frac{19}{10})^2+t^2$. Inside the strip, $|H(s)|\ll_\psi e^{2|t|}$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-fe}(iv). For
$\delta>0$, $|e^{\delta(s-1/2)^2}|=e^{\delta((\sigma-1/2)^2-t^2)}$ is at most $e^{9\delta/25}$ in the strip and
at most $e^{\delta(9/25-T^2)}$ at height $\pm T$, which beats $e^{2T}$. The maximum principle on the rectangles
$|t|\le T$, with $T\to\infty$, gives $|H(s)e^{\delta(s-1/2)^2}|\le C_0e^{9\delta/25}$ in the strip. Letting
$\delta\to0$ gives $|H|\le C_0$. Hence $|L(s,\psi)|\le C_0Q^{3/5}|s+2|^2\le(\frac{31}{30})^2C_0\,Q^{3/5}(3+|t|)^2$.
\qed

Numerically, $\zeta_K(\frac{11}{10})\approx6.6298$, and sampling the left-boundary
gamma quotient gives a contribution about $0.446$ to $C_0$, compared with about
$0.690$ on the right boundary. These are numerical observations, not certified
supremum bounds; the proof uses only the existence of an absolute $C_0$ established above.

\paragraph{Zeros.} \cite{OAI78} never counts zeros. Its detector~\cite[Lemma~8.1]{OAI78} uses only
that the zeros of each $L(s,\psi^*)$ are discrete and lie in $\Re s\le1$~\cite[proof of Lemma~8.1]{OAI78}, which for CM
characters is Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-fe}(iv). If a count is wanted, Jensen's formula~\cite{Jen99}, \cite[\S3.61]{Tit39} on the disks
$|s-(2+it)|\le2.07$ and $2.1$, with $|L(2+it,\psi)|\ge\zeta_K(4)/\zeta_K(2)$ and the strip bound, shows that the
number of zeros with $0\le\Re\rho\le1$ and $|\operatorname{Im}\rho-t|\le\frac12$ is $\ll\log(Q(3+|t|))$. This is the
same bound as for finite order.

\subsection{Logarithmic control}\label{sec:s2-log}
\begin{lemma}\label{lem:s2-logcontrol}
Let $\psi\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}\cup\overline{\mathcal F}_{\rm CM}$ have conductor norm $Q$, and put
$\mathcal L(s)=L(s,\psi)$. Fix $a\in[\frac12,1]$ and $0<e<10^{-3}$, and suppose that $\mathcal L$ has no zero in
the open disk of radius $2-a-2e$ centred at $2+it$. Then, for every $\epsilon>0$, uniformly on the closed
concentric disk of radius $2-a-6e$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d158}
 |\mathcal L(s)|+|\mathcal L(s)^{-1}|\ll_{e,\epsilon}\{2Q(3+|t|)^2\}^\epsilon,
\end{equation}
and on the closed disk of radius $2-a-8e$,
$|\mathcal L'(s)/\mathcal L(s)|\ll_e\log\{2Q(3+|t|)^2\}$. The implied constants depend only on $e$ and $\epsilon$.
In particular, for every fixed $b\ge\beta_*$ and $v>0$ these bounds hold on $\Re s\ge b+v$, with the height of
$s$ in place of $t$ and constants depending only on $v$ and $\epsilon$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
This is~\cite[Lemma~4.9]{OAI78} without its principal clauses, and its
proof~\cite[proof of Lemma~4.9]{OAI78} applies word for word. That proof uses exactly the following four properties
of $\mathcal L$. Everything else in it is complex analysis that does not involve the character:
Borel--Carath\'eodory~\cite[\S5.5]{Tit39} between the radii $R_3$ and $R_4$, Hadamard's three circles~\cite[\S5.3]{Tit39} with inner radius $r_0=49/100$
and exponent $\theta=\log(R_6/r_0)/\log(R_4/r_0)<1$, uniformly in $a\in[\frac12,1]$, and Cauchy's estimate between
$R_6$ and $R_8$, where $R_j=2-a-je$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\mathcal L$ is holomorphic on the disks used; they lie in $\Re s\ge a+2e>\frac12$. This holds by
 Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-fe}(iv).
\item $\log|\mathcal L(s)|\ll\log\{2Q(3+|t|)^2\}$ on the disk of radius $R_3$. That disk lies in
 $a+3e\le\sigma\le\frac72$, at heights within $\frac32$ of $t$. Lemma~\ref{lem:cm-growth} gives
 $|\mathcal L(s)|\ll Q^{3/5}(3+|t|+\frac32)^2$ for $\sigma\le\frac{11}{10}$, and
 $|\mathcal L(s)|\le\zeta_K(\frac{11}{10})$ for $\sigma\ge\frac{11}{10}$. The constants are absolute, as
 in~\cite[proof of Lemma~4.9]{OAI78}.
\item The Euler logarithm
 $\log\mathcal L(s)=\sum_{\mathfrak p}\sum_{k\ge1}\psi(\mathfrak p)^k/(k\N\mathfrak p^{ks})$ converges absolutely
 for $\Re s>1$, and $|\log\mathcal L(s)|\le\log\zeta_K(\sigma)$ because $|\psi(\mathfrak p)|=1$. It is therefore
 at most $\log\zeta_K(2)$ at the centre and at most $\log\zeta_K(\frac{151}{100})$ on the disk of radius $r_0$.
\item For the global assertion with $\frac12\le b\le1$, take $a=b$ and $e<\min(10^{-3},v/8)$. The disk of
 radius $R_2$ about $2+it$ lies in $\Re s>b+2e>\beta_*$, so it contains no zero of $\mathcal L$, by the
 definition of $\beta_*$ in Section~\ref{sec:78}. For $\overline\psi$ note that $L(s,\overline\psi)=\overline{L(\bar s,\psi)}$.
 For $b>1$ the Euler product suffices.
\end{enumerate}
In particular the constants depend only on $e$, $\epsilon$, the absolute constant of Lemma~\ref{lem:cm-growth},
and $\zeta_K(\frac{151}{100})$, and not on $\psi$.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{The whole row family.} For a finite-order character,~\cite[Lemma~4.9]{OAI78}
applies as it stands. Its global assertion needs $b$ at least the supremum~\cite[eq.~(2.1)]{OAI78} over
finite-order characters. That supremum is at most $7/8$ by~\cite[Theorem~1.1]{OAI78}. Hence, for the label
sets $\mathcal X_u$ of Section~\ref{sec:78rows}, which contain finite-order and CM members, the global assertion
holds for every fixed $b\ge\max(\beta_*,\frac78)$. This is $b\ge\beta_*$ under the contradiction hypothesis
$\beta_*>\frac78$ of Section~\ref{sec:78final}, and under the hypothesis $\beta_*>\frac{11}{12}$ of statement~6 of
Section~\ref{sec:78status}. So it is the same range as in~\cite{OAI78}. The deleted Euler factors
of~\cite[Lemma~4.10]{OAI78} use only $|a_p|\le1$, so they are unchanged.

\subsection{Conductors of the row characters}\label{sec:s2-cond}
\begin{lemma}\label{lem:s2-rowcond}
Let $\psi_0$ be a primitive finite-order Hecke character with conductor $\mathfrak f_0$, and let $\psi^*$ be the
primitive character inducing $\overline\alpha\psi_0$ (on primary $n$ prime to $3\mathfrak f_0$). Then
$\psi^*\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$, and its conductor divides $\operatorname{lcm}((3),\mathfrak f_0)$. In particular
$Q_{\psi^*}\le9\,\N\mathfrak f_0$. Moreover $\psi^*$ and $\psi_0$ have the same conductor exponent at every prime
other than $\lambda$. The same holds for $\alpha\psi_0$, with $\overline{\mathcal F}_{\rm CM}$ in place of
$\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$.

Consequently, let $\theta\in\Theta_0$ and let $u$ be a sixth-power-free row that is not supported on $S$. Then
the CM members $n\mapsto\overline{\alpha(n)}\theta(n)\overline{\chi_n(u)}$ and $n\mapsto\alpha(n)\theta(n)\chi_n(u)$ of
$\mathcal X_u$ satisfy the conclusions of~\cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78}. That is, the primitive character $\psi^*$
inducing such a member satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d159}
 Q_{\psi^*}\ll_{\mathcal A}\N(u),\qquad
 L_{\rm orig}(s)=L(s,\psi^*)\prod_{p\in E_u}\bigl(1-\psi^*(p)\N(p)^{-s}\bigr),\qquad E_u\subset S\cup\{p:p\mid u\},
\end{equation}
where $L_{\rm orig}$ is the Dirichlet series of the zero-extended member. No CM member is principal.
(In fact $E_u\subset S$, since every good prime dividing $u$ divides the conductor; see
Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}.)
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-pres}(iii), $\overline\alpha$ has conductor $(3)$. Put $\mathfrak m=\operatorname{lcm}((3),\mathfrak f_0)$.
On ideals prime to $\mathfrak m$, the product $\overline\alpha\psi_0$ is a character of modulus $\mathfrak m$ and
infinity type $\bar z/|z|$: if $b\equiv c\pmod{\mathfrak m}$, then $\overline\alpha((b/c))=\overline{\alpha(b/c)}$
and $\psi_0((b/c))=1$. So $\psi^*\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$ and its conductor divides $\mathfrak m$, and
$\N\mathfrak m\le9\N\mathfrak f_0$. Conversely $\psi_0$ is induced by $\alpha\psi^*$, and $\alpha$ has conductor
$(3)$, so $\mathfrak f_0$ divides $\operatorname{lcm}((3),\mathfrak f(\psi^*))$. At a prime $\mathfrak p\ne(\lambda)$
the two divisibilities give
$v_{\mathfrak p}(\mathfrak f(\psi^*))\le v_{\mathfrak p}(\mathfrak f_0)\le v_{\mathfrak p}(\mathfrak f(\psi^*))$.
For $\alpha\psi_0$, conjugate: $\alpha\psi_0=\overline{\overline\alpha\,\overline{\psi_0}}$.

For the rows, let $\psi_0$ be the primitive character inducing $\theta\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)}$.
By Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(e) (which proves the bound that~\cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78} uses without stating it),
$\N\mathfrak f_0\ll_{\mathcal A}\N(u)$, and $\theta(n)\overline{\chi_n(u)}=\psi_0((n))$ for
primary $n$ prime to $S$ and $u$. At such $n$ the CM member equals $\overline{\alpha(n)}\psi_0((n))=\psi^*((n))$,
because $(\lambda)\in S$ and the primes of $\mathfrak f_0$ lie in $S\cup\{p:p\mid u\}$. So $L_{\rm orig}(s)$ is the
Euler product of $\psi^*$ over the primes outside $S\cup\{p:p\mid u\}$. This gives the factorization, with
$E_u$ the primes of $S\cup\{p:p\mid u\}$ that do not divide $\mathfrak f(\psi^*)$. A principal character has
trivial infinity type, so no CM member is principal. The second member is the conjugate of a first one, since
$\Theta_0$ is a group.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Reflection of a CM plain sum}\label{sec:s2-refl}
For a character $\psi$ and a smooth function $W$ with compact support in $(0,\infty)$, put
$T_\psi(X;W)=X^{-1/2}\sum_{\mathfrak a}\psi(\mathfrak a)W(\N\mathfrak a/X)$. The sum is over nonzero integral ideals,
with $\psi(\mathfrak a)=0$ unless $\mathfrak a$ is prime to the conductor. We use the Mellin convention
$\mathcal MW(s)=\int_0^\infty W(y)y^s\,dy/y$ of~\cite[Lemma~4.5]{OAI78}. The next lemma replaces
the reflection step~\cite[Section~18.1]{OAI78} of the plain fourth moment when the row character has infinite
order.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:s2-reflection}
Let $\psi\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}\cup\overline{\mathcal F}_{\rm CM}$ have conductor norm $Q$ and root number
$W(\psi)$, and put $C_\psi=3Q/(2\pi)^2$. For $X>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d160}
 T_\psi(X;W)=W(\psi)\,T_{\overline\psi}\bigl(C_\psi/X;W^\sharp\bigr),\qquad
 W^\sharp(y)=\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{(c)}\mathcal MW(1-u)\,\frac{\Gamma(u+\frac12)}{\Gamma(\frac32-u)}\,y^{-u}\,du ,
\end{equation}
for any $c>-\frac12$. Thus
$\mathcal MW^\sharp(u)=\mathcal MW(1-u)\Gamma(u+\frac12)/\Gamma(\frac32-u)$ on $\Re u>-\frac12$, in place of
$\mathcal MW(1-u)\Gamma(u)/\Gamma(1-u)$ in~\cite{OAI78}. Moreover:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] $W^\sharp$ is smooth on $(0,\infty)$. For all integers $j\ge0$ and all $A\ge0$,
 $(y\partial_y)^jW^\sharp(y)\ll\min(y^{1/2},y^{-A})$. The implied constant is at most a constant depending
 on $j$ and $A$ times finitely many integrals $\int_0^\infty y^{\sigma}|(y\partial_y)^kW(y)|\,dy/y$, with $\sigma$
 in a compact set depending only on $j$ and $A$.
\item[(b)] For $W_\omega(y)=W(y)y^{i\omega}$, the bounds in~(a) hold for $W_\omega^\sharp$ with an extra factor
 $(1+|\omega|)^{h}$, where $h$ depends only on $j$ and $A$.
\end{enumerate}
The new kernel is holomorphic on $\Re u>-\frac12$, so $W^\sharp(y)=\mathcal MW(\frac32)y^{1/2}+O(y^{3/2})$ has no
constant term at $0$. In~\cite{OAI78}, $W^\sharp(0)=\mathcal MW(1)$. On $\Re u=c$ both kernels are
$\ll_c(3+|t|)^{2c-1}$, by Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-gamma}(b) and its finite-order analogue. So the reflected lengths, the annular
decomposition, the seminorm bounds and the height polynomials of~\cite[Section~18.1]{OAI78} are unchanged.
The only difference is that the annuli below the main scale now carry coefficients $O(2^{-j})$ in place of
$O(2^{-j/2})$, which only helps.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Use the direct Poisson proof in Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-reflection}, which establishes
the reflection for both infinity types, and Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-sharp} for its profile.
Those proofs use the finite Gauss identity and the planar Fourier transform;
they do not use the present lemma. For infinity type $\bar z/|z|$, the root number
there is $-\gamma(\phi)\overline{\alpha(f)}=W(\psi)$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-fe};
the other type follows by conjugation. The conductor scale is
$C_\psi=3\N(f)/(4\pi^2)$ in both formulas.

Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-sharp}(a) gives the Mellin identity initially on
$-\frac12<\Re u<\frac14$. Its decay bounds in~(c) make the Mellin transform
holomorphic throughout $\Re u>-\frac12$, so the identity extends there.
The rapid vertical decay of $\mathcal MW(1-u)$ permits inversion and contour
shifts within this half-plane. The seminorm and norm-twist bounds in~(a)--(b)
are exactly those proved in Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-sharp}(c).

For clarity, the expansion at zero comes directly from
$W^\sharp(y)=\int W(x)J_1(2\sqrt{xy})\,dx$ and
$J_1(t)=t/2+O(t^3)$: compact support of $W$ gives
$W^\sharp(y)=\mathcal MW(\frac32)y^{1/2}+O(y^{3/2})$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Where these statements are used}\label{sec:s2-uses}
We searched~\cite{OAI78} for every use of its Lemma~4.8 (equation~(4.14) there),
Lemmas~4.9 and~4.10, and the primitive functional equation. We also
searched for every count of zeros. There is none. The uses are listed below, with the CM statement that replaces
each. The characters that occur are of three kinds: the target; the denominators $\psi\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)}$
and the other CM members of $\mathcal X_u$; and finite-order characters (numerators, slot characters, and the
finite-order members of $\mathcal X_u$). For the third kind,~\cite{OAI78} applies as it stands, with zero-free
input $b\ge\frac78$ from~\cite[Theorem~1.1]{OAI78}.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Buffered bins}~\cite[Lemma~8.1]{OAI78}.
 \begin{itemize}
 \item The maxima $M_j(u)$ exist (\cite[proof of Lemma~8.1]{OAI78}): Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-fe}(iv). No count of zeros is used.
 \item Disk control (\cite[proof of Lemma~8.1]{OAI78}): Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-logcontrol}, with $Q_{\psi^*}\ll_{\mathcal A}\N(u)$ from
  Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-rowcond} and $(3+|t|)^2\ll U^{1/50}$ as there.
 \item Reflected estimate (\cite[proof of Lemma~8.1]{OAI78}): \eqref{eq:s2-fe-unc} and Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-gamma}(a) on the line
  $\sigma=1-a-6e\in[-6e,\frac12-6e]$. The conductor and the gamma factor contribute
  $(3Q_{\psi^*})^{a-1/2+6e}(3+|t|)^{2a-1+12e}$, exactly as for finite order. The conjugate member lies in
  $\mathcal X_u$.
 \item Bin ceiling (\cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78}): $M_i(u)\le\max(\beta_*,\frac78)$, because the CM members have no zeros
  in $\Re s>\beta_*$ and the finite-order members none in $\Re s>\frac78$.
 \end{itemize}
\item \emph{Pointwise dyadic estimates}~\cite[Lemma~8.2 and its proof]{OAI78}. The plain witness of a
 CM member is shifted to $1-a-6e$: $L(s,\psi^*)$ is entire (Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-fe}(iv)), and the joins use
 Lemma~\ref{lem:cm-growth}.
\item \emph{Witness construction}~\cite[Prop.~8.3 and its proof]{OAI78}. On $\Re z=-\frac14$ the
 argument $\rho+z$ has real part in $[0.26,0.75]$, and Lemma~\ref{lem:cm-growth} gives the same bound
 $Q_{\psi^*}^{3/5}(3+|\gamma+v|)^2$ as~\cite[eq.~(4.14)]{OAI78}.
\item \emph{Contour moves of the high expansion}~\cite[Lemmas 10.3,
 10.5 and 10.6]{OAI78}, \cite[proof of Lemma~10.3]{OAI78}, \cite[proof of Lemma~10.5]{OAI78}, \cite[proof of Lemma~10.6]{OAI78}. These
 need the reciprocal of a CM denominator, or of the target, on $\Re s=\beta_*+20e$ or $\beta_*+e$. That is the
 global part of Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-logcontrol}, with Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-rowcond} and the deleted factors. The
 principal specialization (\cite[proof of Lemma~10.6]{OAI78}) is not needed, since no CM denominator is principal. The numerator
 bounds there, the exponent $3/5$ at \cite[proof of Lemma~10.6]{OAI78} included, concern finite-order numerators and are unchanged.
\item \emph{Compensated probe}, \cite[Section~16.2]{OAI78} and \cite[proof of Proposition~16.1]{OAI78}. These use the buffered estimates of item~1 and the
 functional equation of the finite-order numerator. They are unchanged.
\item \emph{Plain fourth moment}. The reflection \cite[Section~18.1]{OAI78} becomes Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-reflection} for rows
 $\alpha^e\tau\chi_\bullet(k)$. The conductor scale~\cite[eq.~(18.7)]{OAI78} keeps its proof, because by
 Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-rowcond} the CM conductor differs from that of $\tau\chi_\bullet(k)$ only at $\lambda\in S$.
 The prime estimate~\cite[eq.~(18.11)]{OAI78} concerns finite-order slot characters. It needs
 $s_\kappa\ge\frac78$, which allows $\kappa=\frac34$.
\item \emph{Prime bound in a bin}~\cite[Lemma~19.1 and its proof]{OAI78}. Here
 $\mathcal L'/\mathcal L$ is taken for finite-order slot characters, inside disks that the CM bins of item~1 make
 zero-free for all of $\mathcal X_u$. \cite[Lemma~4.9]{OAI78} applies as it stands.
\end{enumerate}
In every item the CM statement has the same conductor exponent and the same height exponent as the
finite-order one, and its constants do not depend on the character. So the uniformity in the row and in the
conductor required by~\cite{OAI78} holds as stated there.

\section{The zero detector for a CM target}\label{app:s3-detector}

This appendix writes out item~3 of Section~\ref{sec:78status}: the zero detector (a truncated-inverse detector; compare~\cite[Appendix~C]{MP24})
of~\cite[Section~8]{OAI78} for a target $\psi\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$, with the
label set $\mathcal X_u$ of Section~\ref{sec:78rows}. In~\cite{OAI78} the detector is used by the
sextic row count~\cite[Prop.~9.2]{OAI78}, by the contour moves of the high
side~\cite[Lemma~10.3]{OAI78}, by the dynamic local errors of the compensated
probe~\cite[Section~16.2]{OAI78}, and by the Part~II row counts~\cite[Section~19]{OAI78}.
We go through~\cite[Section~8]{OAI78} in order. Every step that involves a row character is
restated and proved below; every other step is identical and is cited by line. Apart
from~\cite{OAI78}, the inputs are Lemma~\ref{lem:cm-growth} and the logarithmic control of
Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-logcontrol} (item~2 of Section~\ref{sec:78status}, proved in Appendix~\ref{app:s2-hecke}).

\subsection{Setting and the label set}

\paragraph{Hecke characters.} As in~\cite[Ch.~VII, \S6]{Neukirch}, a Hecke character modulo an
integral ideal $\mathfrak m$ is a character $\varphi$ of the group of fractional ideals prime to
$\mathfrak m$ for which there are characters $\varphi_{\rm f}$ of $(\OO/\mathfrak m)^\times$ and
$\varphi_\infty$ of $\mathbb C^\times$ with $\varphi((a))=\varphi_{\rm f}(a)\varphi_\infty(a)$ for every
$a\in\OO$ prime to $\mathfrak m$. Here $\varphi_\infty$ is the infinity type. Such a $\varphi$ is
induced by a unique primitive character $\varphi^*$: its conductor $\mathfrak f_{\varphi^*}$ divides
$\mathfrak m$, it agrees with $\varphi$ on ideals prime to $\mathfrak m$, and it is zero on primes
dividing $\mathfrak f_{\varphi^*}$. Put $Q_{\varphi^*}=\N\mathfrak f_{\varphi^*}$. The exponent of
$\mathfrak f_{\varphi^*}$ at a prime $\mathfrak p$ is the least $c\ge0$ such that $\varphi_{\rm f}(a)=1$
whenever $a\equiv1\pmod{\mathfrak p^c}$ and $a\equiv1$ modulo the part of $\mathfrak m$ prime to
$\mathfrak p$. A finite-order character has trivial infinity type~\cite[proof of Lemma~4.8]{OAI78}; the
members of $\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$ have $\varphi_\infty(z)=\bar z/|z|$.

\paragraph{The target and the fixed data.} Fix $\psi\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$, of conductor
$\mathfrak f_\psi$. For primary $n$ prime to $\mathfrak f_\psi$ put $\eta(n)=\alpha(n)\psi((n))$. If
$x\equiv1\pmod{{\rm lcm}(\mathfrak f_\psi,3)}$, then $x$ is primary and $\psi((x))=\bar x/|x|$, so
$\eta(x)=1$. Thus $\eta$ is a ray class character modulo ${\rm lcm}(\mathfrak f_\psi,3)$, of finite
order, and $\psi(n)=\overline{\alpha(n)}\eta(n)$, as in Theorem~\ref{thm:78}. The arithmetic
datum $\mathcal A$ is that of~\cite[Section~4.1]{OAI78} for this $\eta$. It contains the finite
group
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d161}
 \Theta_0=\langle\eta,\widehat T\rangle
\end{equation}
of~\cite[Section~6.1]{OAI78} (written $\Theta$ there), and the finite set $S$ of~\cite[Section~6.1]{OAI78}.
It contains the primes above $6$, the defining-modulus supports of $\eta$ and of the members of
$\widehat T$, the primes dividing $\mathfrak f_\psi$, and every prime of norm at most $P_0$. The angular factor needs no new prime: on ideals prime to $3$,
$(a)\mapsto\alpha(a')$, with $a'$ the primary generator, is the Hecke character modulo $3$ with
$\alpha_\infty(z)=z/|z|$ and $\alpha_{\rm f}(a)=a'/a$. The datum depends on $\psi$, which~\cite[Section~4.1]{OAI78} allows. A prime is \emph{good} if it is not in $S$. As in~\cite[Section~4.1]{OAI78},
the letters $n,l,k$ denote good primary elements, $p$ a good primary prime, and $q_n=\N(n)$.

\paragraph{The zero ceiling.} Let $\beta_*^{(0)}$ be the supremum~\cite[eq.~(2.1)]{OAI78} over
primitive finite-order characters, let $\beta_*$ be the supremum of Section~\ref{sec:78} over
$\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$, and put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s3-beta}
 \beta^\sharp=\max\bigl(\beta_*,\beta_*^{(0)}\bigr).
\end{equation}
By~\cite[Theorem~1.1]{OAI78}, $\beta_*^{(0)}\le7/8$. Hence $\beta^\sharp=\beta_*$ whenever
$\beta_*\ge7/8$: under the contradiction hypothesis $\beta_*>7/8$ of Section~\ref{sec:78final}, and under
$\beta_*>11/12$ in the $11/12$ stage.

\paragraph{The label set.} Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d162}
 \Lambda=\bigl\{(\nu,\varsigma):\ \varsigma\in\{1,-1\},\ \nu\in\Theta_0\cup\alpha^{\varsigma}\Theta_0\bigr\},
\end{equation}
a set of $4|\Theta_0|$ labels, independent of the row. For a nonzero sixth-power-free element $u$
and $(\nu,\varsigma)\in\Lambda$ define, on good primary $n$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d163}
 \varphi_{u,\nu,\varsigma}(n)=\nu(n)\chi_n(u)^{\varsigma},
\end{equation}
with the zero convention of~\cite[Section~4.1]{OAI78}: $\chi_n(u)^{\pm1}=0$ when $(n,u)\ne1$. Let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d164}
 \mathcal X_u=\{\varphi_{u,\nu,\varsigma}:(\nu,\varsigma)\in\Lambda\}.
\end{equation}
A member is \emph{finite-order} if $\nu\in\Theta_0$, and \emph{CM} if $\nu\in\alpha^\varsigma\Theta_0$.
The labels $(\theta,\pm1)$, $(\overline\alpha\theta,-1)$ and $(\alpha\theta,1)$ give the three kinds of
character displayed in Section~\ref{sec:78rows}, so this is the set used there. Its finite-order
members form the set $\mathcal X_u$ of~\cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78} for the group $\Theta=\Theta_0$. In a
CM member the angular factor and the residue symbol carry the same exponent $\varsigma$; these are
the co-oriented characters of Section~\ref{sec:78rows}. For a member $\varphi$ and $\Re s>1$ put
$L_{\rm orig}(s,\varphi)=\sum_n\varphi(n)q_n^{-s}$, the series of~\cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78}.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:s3-labels}
Let $u$ be a nonzero sixth-power-free element, and let $\varphi=\varphi_{u,\nu,\varsigma}\in\mathcal X_u$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] $(\bar\nu,-\varsigma)\in\Lambda$ and $\overline{\varphi_{u,\nu,\varsigma}}=\varphi_{u,\bar\nu,-\varsigma}$.
 So $\mathcal X_u$ is closed under conjugation.
\item[(b)] $\mathcal X_u$ contains the numerator $\chi_\bullet(u)$ and its conjugate, the denominator
 $\psi\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)}=\varphi_{u,\overline\alpha\eta,-1}$ and its conjugate, and the slot
 characters $\theta\,\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)}$, $\theta\in\widehat T$.
\item[(c)] $\varphi$ is completely multiplicative on good primary elements and $|\varphi(n)|\le1$. For
 $\Re s>1$,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:d165}
 L_{\rm orig}(s,\varphi)=\prod_p\bigl(1-\varphi(p)q_p^{-s}\bigr)^{-1},\qquad
 \frac1{L_{\rm orig}(s,\varphi)}=\sum_n\mu(n)\varphi(n)q_n^{-s}.
 \end{equation}
\item[(d)] There is a unique primitive Hecke character $\varphi^*$ with $\varphi(n)=\varphi^*((n))$
 for every good primary $n$ prime to $u$. If $\varphi$ is finite-order, so is $\varphi^*$. If $\varphi$
 is CM, then $\varphi^*_\infty(z)=(z/|z|)^\varsigma$: for $\varsigma=-1$, $\varphi^*\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$, and
 for $\varsigma=1$, $\overline{\varphi^*}\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$. A CM $\varphi^*$ does not have finite
 order; in particular it is not principal, and $L(s,\varphi^*)$ is entire.
\item[(e)] Let $u'$ be the product of the good primes dividing $u$. There is an ideal
 $\mathfrak m_{\mathcal A}$, supported on $S$ and depending only on $\mathcal A$, with
 $\mathfrak f_{\varphi^*}\mid\mathfrak m_{\mathcal A}u'$. Hence
 $Q_{\varphi^*}\le\N(\mathfrak m_{\mathcal A})\N(u)$. If $\varphi=\varphi_{u,\alpha^\varsigma\theta,\varsigma}$
 is CM and $\varphi_0=\varphi_{u,\theta,\varsigma}$, then $\mathfrak f_{\varphi^*}$ and
 $\mathfrak f_{\varphi_0^*}$ have the same exponent at every prime not above $3$, and
 $\mathfrak f_{\varphi^*}\mid{\rm lcm}(\mathfrak f_{\varphi_0^*},3)$; so $Q_{\varphi^*}\le9\,Q_{\varphi_0^*}$.
\item[(f)] Every good prime $p\mid u$ divides $\mathfrak f_{\varphi^*}$, so $\varphi^*(p)=0$. Consequently
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:d166}
 L_{\rm orig}(s,\varphi)=L(s,\varphi^*)\prod_{\mathfrak p\in S}\bigl(1-\varphi^*(\mathfrak p)\N\mathfrak p^{-s}\bigr),
 \end{equation}
 which is the identity of~\cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78} with $E_u\subset S$. If $u$ is not supported on $S$,
 then $\varphi^*$ is not principal.
\item[(g)] If $L(\rho,\varphi^*)=0$ and $\Re\rho\ge1/2$, then $\Re\rho\le\min(1,\beta^\sharp)$; if
 $\varphi$ is CM, then $\Re\rho\le\min(1,\beta_*)$.
\item[(h)] Let $\varphi$ be CM and $Q=Q_{\varphi^*}$. Then
 $\Lambda(s,\varphi^*)=(3Q)^{s/2}(2\pi)^{-s}\Gamma(s+\frac12)L(s,\varphi^*)$ satisfies
 $\Lambda(s,\varphi^*)=\epsilon(\varphi^*)\Lambda(1-s,\overline{\varphi^*})$ with $|\epsilon(\varphi^*)|=1$, and
 $|L(\sigma+it,\varphi^*)|\ll Q^{3/5}(3+|t|)^2$ for $-1/10\le\sigma\le11/10$. For $51/100\le a\le1$,
 $0<e<10^{-3}$ and real $t$,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:s3-reflected}
 |L(1-a-6e+it,\varphi^*)|\le C_0\,(3Q)^{a-1/2+6e}(3+|t|)^{2a-1+12e}\,
 |L(a+6e-it,\overline{\varphi^*})|,
 \end{equation}
 with an absolute constant $C_0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) $\Theta_0$ is a finite group, so $\bar\theta=\theta^{-1}\in\Theta_0$, and
$\overline{\alpha^\varsigma\theta}=\alpha^{-\varsigma}\bar\theta$ because $|\alpha|=1$. If $(n,u)=1$, then
$\overline{\chi_n(u)^\varsigma}=\chi_n(u)^{-\varsigma}$ is a sixth root of unity; otherwise both sides are $0$.

(b) $\chi_\bullet(u)=\varphi_{u,1,1}$ and $\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)}=\varphi_{u,1,-1}$. On good primary $n$,
$\psi(n)=\overline{\alpha(n)}\eta(n)$ with $\eta\in\Theta_0$, so
$\psi\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)}=\varphi_{u,\overline\alpha\eta,-1}$; its conjugate is given by (a). Finally
$\widehat T\subset\Theta_0$.

(c) $\alpha(mn)=\alpha(m)\alpha(n)$ for all nonzero $m,n$, and products of primary elements are
primary~\cite[Section~4.1]{OAI78}. The members of $\Theta_0$ are multiplicative on good ideals. By
definition $\chi_{mn}(u)=\chi_m(u)\chi_n(u)$~\cite[Section~4.1]{OAI78}, and with the zero convention
$\chi_{mn}(u)^\varsigma=\chi_m(u)^\varsigma\chi_n(u)^\varsigma$, both sides vanishing exactly when
$(mn,u)\ne1$. The series converges absolutely since $|\varphi|\le1$; the two identities are its
Euler product and M\"obius inversion.

(d) Let $\theta\in\Theta_0$ and $\varphi_0=\varphi_{u,\theta,\varsigma}$. By~\cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78} and
\cite[Lemma~4.1]{OAI78}, $\varphi_0$ is induced by a primitive finite-order
character $\varphi_0^*$ whose conductor $\mathfrak f_0$ is supported on $S\cup\{p:p\mid u\}$. This is
the finite-order case. Put $\mathfrak m={\rm lcm}(\mathfrak f_0,3)$. For $a\in\OO$ prime to
$\mathfrak m$ write $a=\epsilon_aa'$ with $a'$ primary and $\epsilon_a$ a unit; $\epsilon_a$ depends only
on $a\bmod3$, and $\epsilon_{ab}=\epsilon_a\epsilon_b$. Since $\varphi_0^*$ is a ray class character
modulo $\mathfrak f_0$, $\varphi_0^*((a))=:\varphi_{0,\rm f}(a)$ depends only on $a\bmod\mathfrak f_0$.
On ideals prime to $\mathfrak m$ define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d167}
 \phi((a))=\alpha(a')^{\varsigma}\varphi_0^*((a))
 =\Bigl(\frac{a}{|a|}\Bigr)^{\varsigma}\epsilon_a^{-\varsigma}\varphi_{0,\rm f}(a).
\end{equation}
So $\phi$ is a Hecke character modulo $\mathfrak m$, with $\phi_\infty(z)=(z/|z|)^\varsigma$ and
$\phi_{\rm f}(a)=\epsilon_a^{-\varsigma}\varphi_{0,\rm f}(a)$. Let $\varphi^*$ be the primitive character
inducing it. The primes of $\mathfrak m$ lie in $S\cup\{p:p\mid u\}$, so a good primary $n$ prime to
$u$ is prime to $\mathfrak m$, and $\varphi^*((n))=\phi((n))=\alpha(n)^\varsigma\theta(n)\chi_n(u)^\varsigma=\varphi(n)$.
Uniqueness holds because a primitive Hecke character is determined by its values at all but finitely
many primes. For $\varsigma=-1$, $\varphi^*_\infty(z)=\bar z/|z|$, so $\varphi^*\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$; for
$\varsigma=1$, $\overline{\varphi^*}$ is primitive with the same conductor and infinity type
$\bar z/|z|$. Suppose $\varphi^{*N}=1$ for some $N\ge1$. For $x\in\OO$ with $x\equiv1\pmod{\mathfrak m}$
we have $\epsilon_x=1$ and $\varphi_{0,\rm f}(x)=1$, so $(x/|x|)^{\varsigma N}=\phi((x))^N=1$. Write
$\mathfrak m=(g)$ and choose $h\in\{g,g\omega\}$ not real. The points $1+jh$, $j\in\mathbb Z$, lie on a
line not through $0$, so $x/|x|$ takes infinitely many values on them, a contradiction. Finally
$L(s,\varphi^*)$ is entire by Lemma~\ref{lem:cm-growth}, applied to $\varphi^*$ or to
$\overline{\varphi^*}$, since $L(s,\varphi^*)=\overline{L(\bar s,\overline{\varphi^*})}$.

(e) First the finite-order members. Let $\kappa_u$ be the ray class character inducing
$n\mapsto\chi_n(u)$. By~\cite[Lemma~4.1]{OAI78} it is the Artin character of
$K(u^{1/6})/K$, so, by local class field theory~\cite[Ch.~V]{Neukirch}, its local component $\kappa_{u,\mathfrak p}$ at each prime is a character of
$K_{\mathfrak p}^\times$ trivial on $(K_{\mathfrak p}^\times)^6$.
\begin{itemize}
\item At a good $\mathfrak p\nmid u$ it is unramified.
\item At a prime $\mathfrak p$ of residue characteristic $\ell\ge5$, the group
 $1+\mathfrak p\OO_{\mathfrak p}$ is pro-$\ell$, hence consists of sixth powers. So the exponent of the
 conductor is at most $1$. This covers the good primes dividing $u$ and the primes of $S$ not above $6$.
\item At the prime $2$ (inert, $K_2$ unramified over $\mathbb Q_2$): Hensel's lemma applied to
 $(1+4z)^2=1+8(z+2z^2)$ and to $(1+2z)^3=1+2(3z+6z^2+4z^3)$ gives
 $1+8\OO_2\subset(1+4\OO_2)^2$ and $1+2\OO_2\subset(\OO_2^\times)^3$. Hence
 $1+8\OO_2\subset(K_2^\times)^6$, and the exponent is at most $3$.
\item At $\lambda$: for $k\ge2$ the map $x\mapsto(1+x)^3=1+3x+3x^2+x^3$ takes $1+\lambda^k\OO_\lambda$
 onto $1+\lambda^{k+2}\OO_\lambda$, by successive approximation, since $v_\lambda(3)=2$. Also
 $1+\lambda\OO_\lambda$ consists of squares. Hence $1+\lambda^4\OO_\lambda\subset(K_\lambda^\times)^6$, and the
 exponent is at most $4$.
\end{itemize}
So $\mathfrak f(\kappa_u)$ divides $M_Su'$, where $M_S=2^3\lambda^4\prod\mathfrak p$, the product over
the primes of $S$ not above $6$. Let $\mathfrak f_{\Theta_0}$ be the least common multiple of the
conductors of the members of $\Theta_0$, and $\mathfrak m_{\mathcal A}={\rm lcm}(M_S,\mathfrak f_{\Theta_0})$.
Then $\mathfrak f_{\varphi_0^*}=\mathfrak f(\theta\kappa_u^\varsigma)$ divides $\mathfrak m_{\mathcal A}u'$. This is
the conductor bound $\ll_{\mathcal A}q_u$ of~\cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78}, which is attributed there to
reciprocity; \cite[Lemma~4.1]{OAI78} states no bound on the exponents. For a CM member, $\mathfrak f_{\varphi^*}$
divides the modulus $\mathfrak m={\rm lcm}(\mathfrak f_0,3)$ of (d), and $3\mid M_S$, so
$\mathfrak f_{\varphi^*}\mid\mathfrak m_{\mathcal A}u'$ and $Q_{\varphi^*}\le\N(\mathfrak m)\le9Q_{\varphi_0^*}$.
At a prime $\mathfrak p$ not above $3$, the condition $a\equiv1$ modulo the part of $\mathfrak m$ prime to
$\mathfrak p$ forces $a\equiv1\pmod3$, hence $\epsilon_a=1$ and $\phi_{\rm f}(a)=\varphi_{0,\rm f}(a)$. So the
two conductors have the same exponent at $\mathfrak p$.

(f) Let $p\mid u$ be good, with $j=v_p(u)\in\{1,\dots,5\}$. By~\cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78} the local
character of $\chi_\bullet(u)$ at $p$ has order $6/\gcd(6,j)>1$ on units, and $\theta$ is unramified at
$p$; so $p\mid\mathfrak f_{\varphi_0^*}$, and by (e) also $p\mid\mathfrak f_{\varphi^*}$. Hence
$\varphi^*(p)=0=\varphi(p)$. At good primes $p\nmid u$, $\varphi(p)=\varphi^*(p)$ by (d), and the primes of
$S$ are absent from $L_{\rm orig}$. This gives the identity. If $u$ has a good prime factor, the
conductor is not $(1)$, so $\varphi^*$ is not principal.

(g) $|\varphi^*(\mathfrak p)|\le1$, so the Euler product converges absolutely in $\Re s>1$ and has no zeros
there. If $\varphi$ is finite-order, $\varphi^*$ is a primitive finite-order character, and $\Re\rho\le\beta_*^{(0)}$
by~\cite[eq.~(2.1)]{OAI78} (which includes the principal character). If $\varphi$ is CM with
$\varsigma=-1$, then $\varphi^*\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$ and $\Re\rho\le\beta_*$. If $\varsigma=1$, then
$L(\bar\rho,\overline{\varphi^*})=\overline{L(\rho,\varphi^*)}=0$ with $\overline{\varphi^*}\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$, so
again $\Re\rho\le\beta_*$.

(h) For $\varsigma=-1$ the functional equation and the strip bound are Lemma~\ref{lem:cm-growth}. For
$\varsigma=1$ apply that lemma to $\overline{\varphi^*}\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$: conjugating
$\Lambda(\bar s,\overline{\varphi^*})=\epsilon\,\Lambda(1-\bar s,\varphi^*)$ gives
$\Lambda(s,\varphi^*)=\bar\epsilon\,\Lambda(1-s,\overline{\varphi^*})$, and
$|L(\sigma+it,\varphi^*)|=|L(\sigma-it,\overline{\varphi^*})|$. The functional equation gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d168}
 L(s,\varphi^*)=\epsilon(\varphi^*)\,(3Q)^{1/2-s}(2\pi)^{2s-1}\,
 \frac{\Gamma(\frac32-s)}{\Gamma(\frac12+s)}\,L(1-s,\overline{\varphi^*}).
\end{equation}
At $s=1-a-6e+it$ we have $|(3Q)^{1/2-s}|=(3Q)^{a-1/2+6e}$ and
$|(2\pi)^{2s-1}|=(2\pi)^{1-2a-12e}<1$. The gamma quotient is $\Gamma(x_1-it)/\Gamma(x_2+it)$ with
$x_1=\frac12+a+6e\in[1.01,1.506]$ and $x_2=\frac32-a-6e\in[0.494,0.99]$. Stirling's formula, uniform
for $x$ in a compact subset of $(0,\infty)$, gives
$|\Gamma(x+iy)|\asymp(1+|y|)^{x-1/2}e^{-\pi|y|/2}$. So the quotient is
$\ll(3+|t|)^{x_1-x_2}=(3+|t|)^{2a-1+12e}$. This proves~\eqref{eq:s3-reflected}.
\end{proof}

For finite-order characters the quotient in the same computation is
$\Gamma(a+6e-it)/\Gamma(1-a-6e+it)$, which has the same exponent $2a-1+12e$. So~\eqref{eq:s3-reflected}
is the CM form of the bound $Q^{a-1/2+6e}(3+|t|)^C$ of~\cite[proof of Lemma~8.1]{OAI78}, with the same exponent
$C$ (one may take $C=2$, since $2a-1+12e<2$). Numerically the normalized quotient
$|\Gamma(x_1-it)/\Gamma(x_2+it)|(3+|t|)^{-(2a-1+12e)}$ lies in $[0.16,1]$ on the whole range.

\begin{lemma}[Logarithmic control for CM characters]\label{lem:s3-logcontrol}
Let $\varphi^*$ be a primitive Hecke character with $\varphi^*\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$ or
$\overline{\varphi^*}\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$, of conductor norm $Q$, and put $\mathcal L(s)=L(s,\varphi^*)$. Let
$a\in[1/2,1]$ and $0<e<10^{-3}$, and suppose $\mathcal L$ has no zero in the open disk of radius $2-a-2e$
about $2+it$. Then, on the closed disk of radius $2-a-6e$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d169}
 |\mathcal L(s)|+|\mathcal L(s)^{-1}|\ll_{e,\epsilon}\{2Q(3+|t|)^2\}^\epsilon,
\end{equation}
and on the disk of radius $2-a-8e$, $|\mathcal L'(s)/\mathcal L(s)|\ll_e\log\{2Q(3+|t|)^2\}$. For every
fixed $b\ge\beta_*$ and $v>0$ these bounds hold on $\Re s\ge b+v$, with the height of $s$ in place of $t$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
This is Lemma~\ref{lem:s2-logcontrol}, applied to $\varphi^*$ or to its conjugate. The inputs it needs are that
$L(s,\varphi^*)$ is entire (Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(d)), the strip bound (Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(h)), the
bound $|\varphi^*(\mathfrak p)|\le1$, and, for the last assertion, the absence of zeros in $\Re s>\beta_*$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(g)).
\end{proof}



\subsection{The detector}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:s3-detector}
Assume $\beta^\sharp>51/100$. Fix the datum $\mathcal A$ above and the parameters
$d_{\min},d_{\max},d,U=Z^d,\tau,T_1=Z^\tau,e,I$ of~\cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78}. Remove the finitely many
sixth-power-free rows supported on $S$, as in~\cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78}. In~\cite[Section~8]{OAI78}
replace the set $\mathcal X_u$ by the set $\mathcal X_u$ above, the characters $\psi^*$ by $\varphi^*$, the
label set $\Theta\times\{-1,1\}$ by $\Lambda$, and $\beta_*$ by $\beta^\sharp$. Then, for every retained row
$u$ with $\N(u)\asymp U$:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] \cite[Lemma~8.1]{OAI78} holds, with constants depending only on $\mathcal A$, $e$
 and $\epsilon_1$;
\item[(ii)] every bin satisfies $a\le\beta^\sharp$ and $\delta=2a-1\le2\beta^\sharp-1$;
\item[(iii)] \cite[Lemma~8.2]{OAI78} holds for every $\varphi\in\mathcal X_u$;
\item[(iv)] \cite[Prop.~8.3]{OAI78} holds, the two witnesses using one presentation
 $\varphi_{u,\nu,\varsigma}$ whose label $(\nu,\varsigma)$ lies in $\Lambda$. For a CM label
 $\nu=\alpha^\varsigma\theta$ they are
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:d200}\begin{aligned}
 M_r&=U^{-r/2}\sum_n\mu(n)\alpha(n)^\varsigma\theta(n)\chi_n(u)^\varsigma W_M\Bigl(\frac{q_n}{U^r}\Bigr),\\
 S_m&=U^{-m/2}\sum_l\alpha(l)^\varsigma\theta(l)\chi_l(u)^\varsigma W_S\Bigl(\frac{q_l}{U^m}\Bigr),
 \end{aligned}\end{equation}
 with the profiles $W_M,W_S$ of~\cite[proof of Proposition~8.3]{OAI78}.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We list every place in~\cite[Section~8]{OAI78} where a property of a row character is used.
The other steps concern the bin parameters, the Mellin and Fourier calculus of the profiles
(\cite[Lemma~4.5, eqs.~(4.11) and~(4.10)]{OAI78}),
or the dyadic partitions, and hold verbatim.

\emph{Setting, \cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78}.} The group $\Theta$ of \cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78} is $\Theta_0$; its conductor primes lie in
$S$ by the choice of $S$. Closure under conjugation and the list of members (\cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78}) are
Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(a),(b); \cite{OAI78} used the group property of $\Theta$, which $\Lambda$ replaces.
The conductor bound and the deletion identity (\cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78}) are Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(e),(f), now with
$E_u\subset S$ and a deletion radical of norm at most $\prod_{\mathfrak p\in S}\N\mathfrak p$. The
ramification argument (\cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78}) applies verbatim to the finite-order members; the CM members are
never principal (Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(d)). The rows supported on $S$ are still removed, because a
finite-order member of such a row can be principal. \cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78} involve no character.

\emph{(i), proof at \cite[proof of Lemma~8.1]{OAI78}.}
\begin{itemize}
\item \cite[proof of Lemma~8.1]{OAI78}, existence of the maxima $M_j(u)$: $\mathcal X_u$ is finite; for a retained row every
 $\varphi^*$ is nonprincipal (Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(d),(f)), so $L(s,\varphi^*)$ is entire (for finite-order
 members by~\cite[proof of Lemma~4.8]{OAI78}) and its zeros are discrete; there are none in $\Re s>1$ by
 Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(g).
\item \cite[proof of Lemma~8.1]{OAI78}: the pigeonhole choice of $(i,a)$ and the zero-free disks involve no character.
\item \cite[proof of Lemma~8.1]{OAI78}: for finite-order members~\cite[Lemma~4.9]{OAI78}, for CM members
 Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-logcontrol}; in both, $Q_{\varphi^*}\ll_{\mathcal A}U$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(e).
 The deleted factors are bounded by~\cite[Lemma~4.10]{OAI78}, which uses only
 $|\varphi^*(\mathfrak p)|\le1$; by Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(f) the deletion is over the fixed set $S$.
\item \cite[proof of Lemma~8.1]{OAI78}, the reflected line: the functional equation recalled in~\cite[proof of Lemma~4.8]{OAI78} is
 replaced, for CM members, by~\eqref{eq:s3-reflected}. The conjugate presentation lies in $\mathcal X_u$
 (Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(a)), so the reflected primitive value $L(a+6e-it,\overline{\varphi^*})$ is
 $\ll U^{\epsilon_1/3}$ by the disk bound just proved. The deleted product at $\Re s=1-a-6e\ge-6e$ is
 $O_{\mathcal A}(1)$. With $(3Q)^{a-1/2+6e}\ll_{\mathcal A}U^{a-1/2+6e}$ and
 $(3+|t|)^{2a-1+12e}\le(3+(3i+2)T_1)^2$ this gives the stated
 $U^{a-1/2+12e+\epsilon_1}(3+T_1)^C$.
\end{itemize}

\emph{(ii), replacing \cite[Section~8.1]{OAI78}.} The sentence ``Every primitive character inducing a presentation in
$\mathcal X_u$ is a finite-order Hecke character'' is false for CM targets. Instead,
Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(g) gives $M_i(u)\le\beta^\sharp$ for every retained row, hence
