$a\le M_i(u)\le\beta^\sharp$.

\emph{(iii), proof at \cite[proof of Lemma~8.2]{OAI78}.} The Mellin identity of \cite[proof of Lemma~8.2]{OAI78} is the second identity of
Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(c). The shifted central portion and its joins lie in the rectangle of (i). On
$\Re(s+\sigma)=2$ the reciprocal Euler product is bounded by $\zeta_K(2)$, since $|\varphi|\le1$. For
$S_m$, the continuation to $\Re(s+\sigma)=1-a-6e$ uses that $L_{\rm orig}(s,\varphi)$ is entire
(Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(d),(f)); its joins use the strip bound of Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(h) (for
finite-order members~\cite[eq.~(4.14)]{OAI78}) and the bounded deleted product over $S$;
the reflected estimate is (i).

\emph{(iv), proof at \cite[proof of Proposition~8.3]{OAI78}.} \cite[proof of Proposition~8.3]{OAI78}: $L_{\rm orig}(s,\varphi)$ is entire. \cite[proof of Proposition~8.3]{OAI78}: on
$\Re(\rho+z)=\sigma-1/4\in[26/100,3/4]$ the strip bound of Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(h) gives
$|L(\rho+z,\varphi^*)|\ll Q^{3/5}(3+|\gamma+v|)^2$ with $Q^{3/5}\ll_{\mathcal A}U^{3/5}\le U^2$, and the
deleted product over $S$ is bounded; the exponent $-3+t(5/4-\sigma)\le-189/100$ is unchanged.
\cite[proof of Proposition~8.3]{OAI78}: the double sum for $J$ and the vanishing of the coefficients with $1<q_n\le D_*$ use
$\varphi(l)\varphi(k)=\varphi(lk)$, Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(c). \cite[proof of Proposition~8.3]{OAI78} concern profiles and dyadic
pairs only. The label is selected in $\Lambda$, a fixed set of $4|\Theta_0|$ elements (\cite[Proposition~8.3]{OAI78}).
\end{proof}

No step uses the order of a member, or the group structure of $\Theta_0$ beyond
Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(a). The gamma factor of a CM $L$-function enters only through
Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(h) and Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-logcontrol}, that is, through fixed polynomial orders in
the height, as stated in Section~\ref{sec:78final}.

\subsection{Use of the detector}

\paragraph{Labels.} The high side uses only the bounds of (i) for the members listed in
Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(b). Every row count that uses the witnesses subdivides the rows of one bin by
the label and by the dyadic pair, and applies a mean-square estimate to each subdivision
(\cite[proof of Proposition~9.2]{OAI78} for the sextic count, \cite[proof of Proposition~19.2]{OAI78} for Part~II). Only the
finiteness of $\Lambda$ is used. Each row lies in one subdivision, each subdivision is a set of rows,
and the estimates are sums of nonnegative terms over rows. No property of the map
$u\mapsto(\nu,\varsigma)$, such as bounded fibres, is needed.

\paragraph{The sextic row count.} The proof of~\cite[Prop.~9.2]{OAI78} uses the
presentation only through the coefficients
$c_n=D^{-1/2}\mu(n)\nu(n)\chi_n(v)^\varsigma W(q_n/D)$ of~\cite[proof of Proposition~9.2]{OAI78}, which must be
independent of the residual row and bounded by $D^{-1/2}|W(q_n/D)|$, and through the identity
$\chi_n(u)^\varsigma=\chi_n(v)^\varsigma\chi_n(k)^\varsigma$ of~\cite[eq.~(9.20)]{OAI78}, which
does not involve $\nu$. Both hold for $\nu=\alpha^\varsigma\theta$, and the sextic large
sieve~\cite[Lemma~9.1]{OAI78} allows any coefficients fixed before the row sum. So the
row envelope $R(\delta)$ of that proposition holds for a CM target, with the bin ceiling (ii).

\paragraph{Orientation.} A CM witness has coefficients $\alpha(n)^\varsigma\theta(n)\chi_n(u)^\varsigma$: it is
co-oriented. Conjugating a whole witness, which~\cite[Section~4.1]{OAI78} allows, replaces
$(\theta,\varsigma)$ by $(\bar\theta,-\varsigma)$. So items~4 and~5 of Section~\ref{sec:78status} are needed
only for co-oriented coefficients, and both signs of $\varsigma$ are covered; Section~\ref{sec:78rows} uses
$\varsigma=-1$ for the inverse moment and $e=+1$ for the plain moment. The anti-oriented twists
$\alpha^{-\varsigma}\theta\chi_\bullet(u)^\varsigma$ are not in $\mathcal X_u$. None is needed: by
Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(b), $\mathcal X_u$ already contains every $L$-function that the high side bounds
through the bins, namely the numerator, the denominator, their conjugates and the slot characters.

\paragraph{Status.} This appendix proves item~3 of Section~\ref{sec:78status}. From Lemma~\ref{lem:cm-growth},
proved in Appendix~\ref{app:s2-hecke}, the detector uses the functional equation, the absence of poles, and the
uniform strip bound. Under the contradiction hypothesis $\beta_*>7/8$ one has $\beta^\sharp=\beta_*$, so
Proposition~\ref{prop:s3-detector}(ii) is the bin ceiling of Lemma~\ref{lem:r3-ceiling}. The mean-value estimates that consume the CM witnesses in Part~II are items~4
and~5; the sextic count was treated above.

We checked numerically:
\begin{itemize}
\item the conductors of $180$ pairs of members (a finite-order member $\varphi_0$ and its CM partner
 $\varphi$, both $\varsigma$), for $15$ rows with valuations
 $1$--$5$, units and factors at $2$ and $\lambda$: exponent $1$ at every good prime of $u$, at most $3$
 at $2$, at most $4$ at $\lambda$, and $Q_{\varphi^*}\le9Q_{\varphi_0^*}$, with equality of exponents away from
 $\lambda$. In $7$ cases the CM member is unramified at $\lambda$;
\item the infinity type $(z/|z|)^\varsigma$ of every CM member;
\item the functional equation of Lemma~\ref{lem:s3-labels}(h), through PARI's~\cite{PARI} \texttt{lfuncheckfeq}, for
 $8$ CM members with conductor norms from $28$ to $117936$ (agreement to $2^{-49}$ or better), and the deletion
 identity of (f);
\item closure under conjugation, membership, complete multiplicativity and the truncated-inverse
 coefficients of (iv), on a model group $\Theta_0$ of order $216$;
\item the gamma quotient in~\eqref{eq:s3-reflected} on a grid with $|t|\le10^{12}$.
\end{itemize}

\section{The inverse moment for co-oriented coefficients}\label{sec:s4-inverse}

This appendix writes out item~4 of Section~\ref{sec:78status}: the CM form~\eqref{eq:cm-inverse} of
\cite[Lemma~17.1]{OAI78}, together with the CM form of the canonical estimate
\cite[Lemma~17.2]{OAI78} through which it is proved. In~\cite{OAI78} the proof
occupies \cite[Section~17]{OAI78}, and the amplification on sixth-power-free rows \cite[Section~17.7]{OAI78}. We follow it step
by step. The angular factor enters only as a completely multiplicative unimodular factor on the column
variables (Lemma~\ref{lem:s4-transfer}). We display every formula in which it appears and cite the
others by number. The terminal step needs the CM form of the reflected energy
\cite[Lemma~14.3]{OAI78}. This is Lemma~\ref{lem:s1-reflected-energy} of
Appendix~\ref{app:s1-general}; we record the form in which it is used as Statement~(R) below.

\subsection{Notation and statements}\label{sec:s4-statements}
We use the conventions of~\cite[Section~4]{OAI78}, which are those of
Section~\ref{subsec:b2-cm} (``Normalizations''). In particular $S$ is a fixed finite set of primes containing
those above $6$ and the support of the defining modulus of every fixed finite-order character; ideal variables
are represented by their primary generators and lie outside $S$; every power of a residue symbol is zero on
nonunits, so that $\chi_c(a)^0=\ind_{(a,c)=1}$; $\gamma_j$ is the normalized Gauss sum of~\eqref{eq:gauss}; and
$G(c)=\overline{\chi_c(4)}\Gamma(c)$ is the finite-ray function of~\cite[Lemma~4.4]{OAI78}, equal to $\overline{\chi_c(4)}\gamma_3(c)$ for squarefree $c$. As there, $\mu$ is the M\"obius
function and ``sf'' means squarefree. Put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d170}
 \varphi(n)=\overline{\alpha(n)} .
\end{equation}
The product of primary generators is the primary generator of the product, so $\varphi$ is completely
multiplicative on primary elements, and $|\varphi|=1$.

Fix a finite-order ray character $\nu$ whose zero-extended defining modulus has prime support in $S$, and a
sign $\epsilon\in\{1,-1\}$. Put $\psi_u(n)=\nu(n)\chi_n(u)^{\epsilon}$, with all zero extensions. For a smooth
annular $W$ (supported in a fixed compact subinterval of $(0,\infty)$) put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s4-M}
 M_u(Z^r;W)=Z^{-r/2}\sum_n\mu(n)\,\alpha(n)^{\epsilon}\psi_u(n)\,W\Bigl(\frac{\N(n)}{Z^r}\Bigr).
\end{equation}
The angular factor $\alpha^\epsilon$ is paired with $\chi_\bullet(u)^\epsilon$: the sum is co-oriented in the
sense of Section~\ref{sec:78rows}. For a finite set $I$ of slots, disjoint sets $\mathcal P_i$ of primes outside
$S$ with $\N(p)\asymp Z^{z_i}$ for $p\in\mathcal P_i$, coefficients $a_i(p)$ independent of $u$ with
$|a_i(p)|\le1$, and smooth annular $W_i$, put, exactly as in~\cite[Section~17.1]{OAI78},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d171}
 Q_u=\prod_{i\in I}Z^{-z_i/2}\sum_{p\in\mathcal P_i}a_i(p)\psi_u(p)W_i\Bigl(\frac{\N(p)}{Z^{z_i}}\Bigr),
 \qquad z=\sum_{i\in I}z_i .
\end{equation}
The slots carry no angular factor; a factor $\alpha(p)^\epsilon$ could be absorbed into $a_i(p)$.

\begin{proposition}[CM marked inverse moment]\label{prop:s4-marked}
Fix bounded ranges for the nonnegative parameters $m,r,z_i$, a bound for
$|I|$, and positive constants $c_1,c_2$, and suppose
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d172}
 r+2z\le m-c_1,\qquad 2r+8z\le3m-c_2 .
\end{equation}
Then, for every $\varepsilon>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s4-marked}
 \sum_{\substack{u\in\OO\\0<\N(u)\ll Z^m}}|M_u(Z^r;W)Q_u|^2\ll Z^{m+\varepsilon}.
\end{equation}
The uniformity is that of~\cite[Lemma~17.1]{OAI78}: the implied constant depends on the
fixed arithmetic data, $c_1,c_2$, the parameter ranges and finitely many smooth seminorms of the tests, and is
uniform in the prime supports and their bounded coefficients; the estimate holds for both signs $\epsilon$,
and for every subcollection of the slots; norm twists of the tests cost a fixed power of their heights; there is
no lower bound or mesh condition on the slot lengths. A fixed finite sum of whole products $M_uQ_u$ is
allowed, provided each summand uses one $\nu$ and one $\epsilon$ in its inverse polynomial and all its slots
(\cite[Section~17.1]{OAI78}).
\end{proposition}

This is~\eqref{eq:cm-inverse}. It is the form in which~\cite{OAI78} uses its lemma for a witness row
(\cite[Section~19.2]{OAI78}): a CM witness of Section~\ref{sec:78rows} is co-oriented, and after conjugation, if
necessary, it has $\epsilon=-1$, $\nu=\theta\in\Theta_0$, and slot coefficients
$a_i(p)=\overline{\nu(p)}\ind_{p\in 1_T}$ times a profile, which are bounded and independent of $u$.

\paragraph{The canonical family.} Let $f$ range over squarefree ideals outside $S$ with $\N(f)\asymp Z^V$, with a
divisor-bounded weight $w(f)$ depending on $f$ alone; let $\rho(n)=\ind_{(n,\mathfrak r_\rho)=1}$ be a fixed
puncture; and let $\mathfrak d$ be a mark of the product form~\cite[eq.~(14.1)]{OAI78}, with
disjoint fixed prime lists of total nominal length at most $z_0$ and bounded coefficients independent of
$k,f$. For squarefree $n$ outside $S$ put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s4-a}
 a(n)=\overline{\alpha(n)}^{\,2}\gamma_2(n)\nu(n)\rho(n)=\varphi(n)\cdot\overline{\alpha(n)}\gamma_2(n)\nu(n)\rho(n),
\end{equation}
which is $\varphi$ times the coefficient of~\cite[eq.~(17.3)]{OAI78}, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s4-E}
 \mathcal E=Z^{-V}\sum_fw(f)\sum_{\substack{k\in\OO\\0<\N(k)\ll Z^M}}
 \Bigl|Z^{-N/2}\ssum_n a(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4\mathfrak d(n)W\Bigl(\frac{\N(n)}{Z^N}\Bigr)\Bigr|^2.
\end{equation}
Without the puncture, the mark and the weight $w$, this is the average~\eqref{eq:energy} of Part~I, with
$a_\xi=\overline\alpha^{\,2}\gamma_2\xi$.

\begin{lemma}[CM canonical estimate]\label{lem:s4-canonical}
Under the hypotheses of~\cite[Lemma~17.2]{OAI78}, with the coefficient~\eqref{eq:s4-a} in place of $\overline\alpha\gamma_2\nu\rho$ (and no other
residual coefficient, as required there), in particular the inequalities~\cite[eq.~(17.2)]{OAI78} with $F_0=N+V$, one has
$\mathcal E\ll Z^{F_0+\varepsilon}$ for every $\varepsilon>0$, with the uniformity stated there (\cite[Lemma~17.2]{OAI78}).
\end{lemma}

\paragraph{The completed sums and Statement~(R).} Put $\Psi_k(n)=\nu(n)\rho(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4$, as
in~\cite[Section~14.1]{OAI78}; it is completely multiplicative on primary ideals outside $S$. Put $V_*(y)=y^{1/2}W(y)$.
The CM form of the completed marked sum~\cite[eq.~(14.2)]{OAI78} is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s4-T}
 \mathcal T_{\mathfrak d}(X;k)=\sum_{\substack{n\ {\rm sf}\\b}}
 \frac{\overline{\alpha(n)}^{\,2}\gamma_2(n)\Psi_k(n)\,\overline{\alpha(b)}^{\,6}\Psi_k(b)^3}{\sqrt{\N(n)}\,\N(b)}\,
 \mathfrak d(nb^3)\,V_*\Bigl(\frac{\N(n)\N(b)^3}X\Bigr),
\end{equation}
over primary $n,b$ outside $S$, which may share primes. Its angular factor $\overline{\alpha(nb^3)}^{\,2}$ is that
of the weight~\eqref{eq:cm-weight} and of~\eqref{eq:T}; this is the completed sum of
$\partial_{\bar z}^{\,2}\theta$ (Section~\ref{sec:theta}, Appendix~\ref{app:reflection}).

\smallskip\noindent\emph{Statement~{\rm(R)}.} \cite[Lemma~14.3 and its proof]{OAI78}, with its block
data (\cite[Section~14.4, \emph{Reflected block data}]{OAI78}) and the norm inequalities~\cite[eqs.~(5.35) and~(14.17)]{OAI78}, holds for~\eqref{eq:s4-T} in place of~\cite[eq.~(14.2)]{OAI78}: with the same
retention rule~\cite[eq.~(14.11)]{OAI78}, the same exponent $E_{\rm ref}$ of~\cite[eq.~(14.14)]{OAI78},
the same constants $C_{\rm res},C_{\rm width},C_{\rm ker}$, and the same uniformity.

\smallskip
Statement~(R) is Lemma~\ref{lem:s1-reflected-energy}: the sum~\eqref{eq:s4-T} is~\eqref{eq:s1-marked-completion},
and that lemma is proved in Appendix~\ref{app:s1-general} from the CM transformation formula at full generality
(Propositions~\ref{prop:s1-reflection} and~\ref{prop:s1-marked}: general $S$, marks and punctures, log-Schwartz
tests), with the same retention rule, exponent, constants and uniformity. In that formula the dual coefficient is
$d_\sigma(\ell)\alpha(\ell)^2$, with the support and the bound $|d_\sigma(u\lambda^mnb^3)|\le27\cdot3^{m/6}|b|$
of~\cite[eq.~(5.2)]{OAI78}; the scalar differs from that of~\cite{OAI78} by the
unimodular factor $-i\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,2}$, so their moduli are equal and at most $1/81$; the local
factors $B_{\pi,j}$ are those of~\cite{OAI78}; and the weight obeys the same bounds as in~\cite{OAI78}.

\subsection{The angular factor as a column twist}\label{sec:s4-twist}

\begin{lemma}[Switching identities]\label{lem:s4-switch}
Let $u$ be squarefree, primary and outside $S$. Then
\begin{align}
 \overline{\alpha(u)}^{\,2}\gamma_2(u)\,\gamma_1(u)&=\mu(u)\overline{\alpha(u)}\,G(u),\label{eq:s4-S1}\\
 \overline{\overline{\alpha(u)}^{\,2}\gamma_2(u)}\,\gamma_{-1}(u)
   &=\overline{\mu(u)\overline{\alpha(u)}\,\chi_u(-1)G(u)},\label{eq:s4-S2}\\
 \mu(u)\overline{\alpha(u)}\,\gamma_{-1}(u)&=\overline{\alpha(u)}^{\,2}\gamma_2(u)\,\chi_u(-1)\overline{G(u)},
   \label{eq:s4-S3}\\
 \overline{\mu(u)\overline{\alpha(u)}}\,\gamma_1(u)&=\overline{\overline{\alpha(u)}^{\,2}\gamma_2(u)}\,G(u),
   \label{eq:s4-S4}\\
 \mu(u)\alpha(u)\,\gamma_{-1}(u)&=\gamma_2(u)\,\chi_u(-1)\overline{G(u)}.\label{eq:s4-S5}
\end{align}
Moreover, for every multiplicative $\nu'$ of modulus one outside $S$, the coefficient
$a'=\overline\alpha^{\,2}\gamma_2\nu'$ satisfies $|a'(u)|=1$ and $a'(vw)=a'(v)a'(w)\chi_w(v)^4$ for coprime
squarefree primary $v,w$ outside $S$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By~\cite[eq.~(4.7)]{OAI78}, which is~\eqref{eq:b2-g1g2}, $\gamma_1\gamma_2=\mu\alpha G$. By
\cite[Lemma~4.2]{OAI78} and the Chinese remainder theorem (\cite[proof of Lemma~4.4]{OAI78}),
$|\gamma_j(u)|=1$ for $6\nmid j$; also $|\alpha|=|G|=1$ and $\mu(u)^2=1$. Hence
$\gamma_1=\mu\alpha G\,\overline{\gamma_2}$. The substitution $v\mapsto-v$ in the Gauss sum gives
$\overline{\gamma_1(u)}=\chi_u(-1)\gamma_{-1}(u)$, with $\chi_u(-1)=\pm1$. Now~\eqref{eq:s4-S1} is $\gamma_1\gamma_2=\mu\alpha G$
multiplied by $\overline\alpha^{\,2}$. For~\eqref{eq:s4-S3},
$\mu\overline\alpha\gamma_{-1}=\mu\overline\alpha\chi(-1)\overline{\gamma_1}
=\mu^2\overline\alpha^{\,2}\gamma_2\chi(-1)\overline G$. The identities~\eqref{eq:s4-S2} and~\eqref{eq:s4-S4} are
the complex conjugates of~\eqref{eq:s4-S1} and~\eqref{eq:s4-S3}, rewritten with
$\overline{\gamma_{\pm1}}=\chi(-1)\gamma_{\mp1}$: for instance
$\overline{\overline\alpha^{\,2}\gamma_2}\gamma_{-1}=\chi(-1)\overline{\overline\alpha^{\,2}\gamma_2\gamma_1}
=\chi(-1)\overline{\mu\overline\alpha G}$. Finally~\eqref{eq:s4-S5} is~\eqref{eq:gauss} multiplied by $\alpha$.
The last assertion is~\eqref{eq:b2-twisted} with $\xi$ replaced by $\nu'$: it follows from
$\gamma_2(vw)=\gamma_2(v)\gamma_2(w)\chi_w(v)^4$~\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The first Poisson transformation}]{OAI78} and the complete multiplicativity of
$\overline\alpha^{\,2}\nu'$.
\end{proof}

Identities~\eqref{eq:s4-S1}--\eqref{eq:s4-S4} are~\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The first Poisson transformation}, \emph{The second Poisson transformation}]{OAI78} multiplied by
$\varphi(u)$ on the first side of a square and by $\overline{\varphi(u)}$ on the second. The first two switch the
cubic class $\overline\alpha^{\,2}\gamma_2$ to the M\"obius class $\mu\overline\alpha$; the last two switch back.
They are~\eqref{eq:signal-cm} and its companion in Section~\ref{sec:78moments}, multiplied by $\overline{\alpha(u)}$;
in paired form they are~\eqref{eq:b2-reverse} and~\eqref{eq:b2-forward}. By~\eqref{eq:s4-S5} the
anti-oriented coefficient $\mu\alpha$ loses its angular factor.

\begin{lemma}[Column twist]\label{lem:s4-transfer}
Let $\sum_{\tau}L(\tau)=\sum_{\sigma}R(\sigma)$ be an identity of absolutely convergent sums. Suppose that
there are a map $\lambda$ from both index sets to a set $\Lambda$ and a map $\Lambda\to\OO^2$,
$\lambda\mapsto(n_1(\lambda),n_2(\lambda))$, into pairs of primary elements outside $S$, such that the
identity holds separately on each fibre of $\lambda$. Then for every bounded function $\varphi$ on primary elements,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d173}
 \sum_\tau\varphi(n_1)\overline{\varphi(n_2)}\,L(\tau)=\sum_\sigma\varphi(n_1)\overline{\varphi(n_2)}\,R(\sigma),
\end{equation}
where $n_a=n_a(\lambda(\tau))$, respectively $n_a(\lambda(\sigma))$. If $\varphi$ is completely multiplicative
and $n_a(\lambda(\sigma))=e_a(\sigma)x_a(\sigma)$, then the twist on the right is
$\varphi(e_1)\overline{\varphi(e_2)}\cdot\varphi(x_1)\overline{\varphi(x_2)}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The twist is constant on each fibre and bounded. Multiply the fibrewise identities
by it and sum over $\Lambda$; the weighted sums remain absolutely convergent, so
regrouping is permitted. The same conclusion holds for an unbounded $\varphi$ if
absolute convergence of both weighted sums is assumed separately.
\end{proof}

We apply this lemma with $\varphi=\overline\alpha$, $\lambda$ the column data of the squared polynomial on the left,
and $(n_1,n_2)$ its two column indices (the index of the conjugated copy second). The identities
of~\cite{OAI78} to which we apply it have the fibrewise property. In each of the three Poisson transformations,
\cite{OAI78} expands the square, fixes the column data, and applies~\cite[Lemma~17.5]{OAI78} to the row sum, whose coefficient is constant on that fibre. The later exact steps rewrite
the terms of one fibre. These are the factorizations by greatest common divisors; the M\"obius expansion of a
coprimality condition together with its formal extension, which for fixed $(u_1,u_2)$ is
$\sum_{t\mid(u_1,u_2)}\mu(t)=\ind_{(u_1,u_2)=1}$; the slot-priority identity~\cite[eq.~(17.16)]{OAI78};
the extraction identities; and Fourier inversion of a joint profile evaluated at the norms of the indices. All
of them are pointwise in the column data. Every other step of the three transformations is an inequality. These
use the coefficients only through their absolute values: the triangle inequality, the weighted Cauchy
inequality~\cite[eq.~(D)]{OAI78}, the discarded raw tails and the principal terms, which are
bounded by lattice counts with absolute coefficients. The remaining inequalities are positive row norms of the new
polynomials. A unimodular twist therefore changes nothing in them, once it has been placed in the new polynomials
and in outer weights of the same absolute value.

\subsection{Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:s4-canonical}}\label{sec:s4-canonical-proof}
We follow the proof of~\cite[Lemma~17.2]{OAI78}. The following parts do not involve
the column coefficients and apply verbatim:
\begin{itemize}
\item the induction on the depth, with the margins $c_h$ and the parameters $d,D,\eta,\tau,\pi$ (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2]{OAI78});
\item the propagation of seminorm and height orders, \cite[Lemma~17.3,
 Cor.~17.4]{OAI78} and the normalization rules (\cite[Section~17.3]{OAI78}), which concern profiles
 and coefficient measures;
\item the localization conventions, the joint Fourier calculus and the raw tail estimate
 (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The two-transform reduction}]{OAI78}), which concern norms, profiles and lattice counts;
\item \cite[Lemma~17.5]{OAI78}, which is applied only to row sums with finite-order characters
 $\chi_\bullet(k)$; the factor $\alpha$ never enters a row character.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Step 1: the short completion (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The short completion}]{OAI78}).} The CM form of~\cite[eq.~(17.10)]{OAI78} is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s4-inversion}
\begin{split}
 &Z^{-N/2}\ssum_n a(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4\mathfrak d(n)W\Bigl(\frac{\N(n)}{Z^N}\Bigr)\\
 &\qquad=\sum_h\frac{\mu(h)\overline{\alpha(h)}^{\,6}\Psi_k(h)^3}{\N(h)}\,
 \mathcal T_{\mathfrak d_h}\Bigl(\frac{Z^N}{\N(h)^3};k\Bigr),\qquad \mathfrak d_h(A)=\mathfrak d(h^3A),
\end{split}
\end{equation}
with $h$ squarefree. Indeed, $\overline{\alpha(n)}^{\,2}\gamma_2(n)\Psi_k(n)=a(n)\chi_n(k)\chi_n(f)^4$ since $\rho^2=\rho$.
Expand the right side and put $c=hb$. Complete multiplicativity of $\overline\alpha^{\,6}$ and of $\Psi_k$, including
its zeros, gives $\overline{\alpha(h)}^{\,6}\Psi_k(h)^3\cdot\overline{\alpha(b)}^{\,6}\Psi_k(b)^3=\overline{\alpha(c)}^{\,6}\Psi_k(c)^3$.
Also $\N(h)^{-1}\N(n)^{-1/2}\N(b)^{-1}V_*(\N(n)\N(c)^3/Z^N)=Z^{-N/2}\N(c)^{1/2}W(\N(n)\N(c)^3/Z^N)$, and
$\mathfrak d_h(nb^3)=\mathfrak d(nc^3)$ does not depend on $h$. So the coefficient of the term $(n,c)$ contains
$\sum_{h\mid c}\mu(h)=\ind_{c=1}$, and only the left side remains. The sums are finite because $W$ has compact
support. In~\cite{OAI78} the exponent is $3$ in place of $6$. When $V<d$ and the $h$-dyad has centre $\ell_1<d$,
the argument of \cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The short completion}]{OAI78} applies with $\mathcal T_{\mathfrak d_h}$ of~\eqref{eq:s4-T}:
\begin{itemize}
\item the slots assigned to $h$, with surviving coefficients $a_i(p)\ind_{p\nmid h}$;
\item the triangle inequality with $\sum_{\N(h)\asymp Z^{\ell_1}}\N(h)^{-1}\ll1$, where the row
 multiplier $\overline{\alpha(h)}^{\,6}\Psi_k(h)^3$ has modulus at most one, hence is a contraction;
\item Lemma~\ref{lem:s1-reflected-energy} (Statement~(R)) in place of~\cite[Lemma~14.3]{OAI78};
\item the exponent bookkeeping~\cite[eq.~(17.11), (17.12)--(17.14)]{OAI78}, which uses only
 the exponents provided by~(R).
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Step 2: marks and the block (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2]{OAI78}).} The slot-priority identities~\cite[eq.~(17.15),
eq.~(17.16)]{OAI78} concern indicators only. When the completion is reopened (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The remaining terms and their marks}]{OAI78}), the
cube coefficient of~\eqref{eq:s4-T} is $\overline{\alpha(b)}^{\,6}\Psi_k(b)^3$ instead of
$\overline{\alpha(b)}^{\,3}\Psi_k(b)^3$. The coefficient of the block~\cite[eq.~(A)]{OAI78} is therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s4-beta}
 \beta^{\rm CM}(b,f)=\overline{\alpha(b)}^{\,3}\beta(b,f),
\end{equation}
where $\beta$ is that of~\cite{OAI78}. It has the same absolute value, is independent of $k$ and $n$, and contains
the mask $\ind_{(b,f)=1}$. The CM block is~\cite[eq.~(A)]{OAI78} with $a$ from~\eqref{eq:s4-a} and
$\beta^{\rm CM}$; for $V\ge d$ it has $\ell=0$, $b=1$, $r=N$. The reduction
statement~\cite[eq.~(17.17)]{OAI78} is to be proved for this block, with child energies
of the form~\eqref{eq:s4-E}.

\paragraph{Step 3: the first Poisson transformation (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2]{OAI78}).} Relative to~\cite{OAI78}, the pair of
columns $n_1,n_2$ of the expanded block carries the extra factor $\varphi(n_1)\overline{\varphi(n_2)}$; the cube
factors are already in $\beta^{\rm CM}$. Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:s4-transfer} to the exact steps from the expansion
(\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The first Poisson transformation}]{OAI78}) to~\cite[eq.~(17.31)]{OAI78}. On the factorized expression
$n_a=\mathfrak A_aCt'x_a$ (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The first Poisson transformation}; proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{Retaining the fourth-power label from the cubes}]{OAI78}), with $C$ and $t'$ common to both sides, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d174}
 \varphi(n_1)\overline{\varphi(n_2)}=\varphi(\mathfrak A_1)\overline{\varphi(\mathfrak A_2)}\cdot\varphi(x_1)\overline{\varphi(x_2)} .
\end{equation}
Consequently:
\begin{itemize}
\item the column coefficient~\cite[eq.~(C)]{OAI78} acquires the factor $\overline{\alpha(u_i)}$ on side
 $i$. This agrees with~\eqref{eq:s4-S1} and~\eqref{eq:s4-S2}, which give at $u_1$ the factor
 $\mu(u_1)\overline{\alpha(u_1)}G(u_1)$ and, inside the conjugated second polynomial, the factor
 $\mu(u_2)\overline{\alpha(u_2)}\chi_{u_2}(-1)G(u_2)$, in place of $\mu(u_1)G(u_1)$ and
 $\mu(u_2)\chi_{u_2}(-1)G(u_2)$ (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The first Poisson transformation}]{OAI78});
\item the factors $B_{1,a}(t';y)$ (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{Separating the first transformed product}]{OAI78}) are unchanged, because $|\varphi(t')|^2=1$;
\item the polynomials~\cite[eq.~(17.30)]{OAI78} become
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:s4-Pa}
 \begin{split}
 P_a(y)={}&\ssum_x\mu(x)\overline{\alpha(x)}\nu_a(x)\rho(x)\overline{\chi_x(y)}\chi_x(CJ)^4\chi_x(d_k)
 \ind_{(x,\operatorname{rad}q_0t')=1}\\
 &\qquad\times\mathfrak d_{I_a}(x)\,\omega_{x,a}\Bigl(\frac{\N(x)}{Z^{s_a}}\Bigr)
 \Bigl(\frac{\N(x)}{Z^{s_a}}\Bigr)^{\varepsilon_ait_{x_a}};
 \end{split}
 \end{equation}
\item the outer product $e_\sigma$ (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{Separating the first transformed product}]{OAI78}) is multiplied by $\varphi(\mathfrak A_1)\overline{\varphi(\mathfrak A_2)}$
 and contains $\beta^{\rm CM}$, so the outer weight $w_{1,\sigma}$ (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{Separating the first transformed product}]{OAI78}) changes only by a factor of modulus one.
\end{itemize}
Then~\cite[eq.~(17.31)]{OAI78} holds with~\eqref{eq:s4-Pa}. The principal case $m_1=1$
(\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The first Poisson transformation}]{OAI78}) is bounded by an absolute count, and so are the discarded raw complement and the restored principal
frequencies; these bounds are unchanged. The first positive majorant (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The first positive majorant}]{OAI78}) applies the weighted Cauchy
inequality and then bounds the outer factors absolutely (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The first positive majorant}]{OAI78}), so it holds verbatim. The polynomial
$P_i(y)$ of~\eqref{eq:s4-Pa} again depends on $b_a,\mathfrak A_a,f$ only through $J,q_0$, the length $A_i$ and the
sector, because $\varphi(x)$ depends on $x$ alone. The output~\cite[eq.~(17.35)]{OAI78} therefore holds with the majorant~\cite[eq.~(17.33)]{OAI78} formed from~\eqref{eq:s4-Pa}.

\paragraph{Step 4: the second Poisson transformation (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The second Poisson transformation}]{OAI78}).} Now $P_i(y)=\sum_xc_i(x)\overline{\chi_x(y)}$
with $c_i=\varphi\,c_i^{\rm OAI}$, where $c_i^{\rm OAI}$ is the coefficient of \cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The second Poisson transformation}]{OAI78}. Apply
Lemma~\ref{lem:s4-transfer} with $\lambda=(x_1,x_2)$. Here $x_a=g'z_a=g'v'n_a$ (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The second Poisson transformation}]{OAI78}), so
$\varphi(x_1)\overline{\varphi(x_2)}=\varphi(n_1)\overline{\varphi(n_2)}$ and the outer twist is $1$. By~\eqref{eq:s4-S3}
and~\eqref{eq:s4-S4}, the signal calculation of \cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The second Poisson transformation}]{OAI78} holds with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s4-a0}
 a_0(z)=\overline{\alpha(z)}^{\,2}\gamma_2(z)
\end{equation}
in place of $a_0=\overline\alpha\gamma_2$ (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The second Poisson transformation}]{OAI78}), and with the same ray factor $G([z_2][z_1]^{-1})$. Hence:
\begin{itemize}
\item \cite[eq.~(17.41)]{OAI78} holds with $L_{o,d_2,k''}$ formed
 from~\eqref{eq:s4-a0}. The factor $|B_o(g')|^2$ is unchanged, since the common factor $\varphi(g')$ of the two sides
 has modulus one;
\item the extraction~\cite[eq.~(17.42)]{OAI78} holds for~\eqref{eq:s4-a0} by the twisted
 multiplicativity in Lemma~\ref{lem:s4-switch}. Its $D_o(v';d_2,k'')$ acquires $\varphi(v')$, and $|D_o|^2$ is unchanged;
\item the moving characters~\cite[eq.~(17.43)]{OAI78} and the expansion of $G$ over the ray group
 (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The second Poisson transformation}]{OAI78}) are unchanged.
\end{itemize}
The principal case $x_1=x_2$ (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The second Poisson transformation}]{OAI78}) and the discarded raw complement are bounded by absolute counts,
which are unchanged.
The new canonical data and their admissibility (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{The new canonical data and their admissibility}]{OAI78}) involve no coefficient: the child data, lengths
and clipping, the source triples, the transition identities, the new puncture, the child
margins~\cite[eq.~(17.51)]{OAI78} and the row decrease~\cite[eq.~(17.52)]{OAI78}. In the
common density (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{A common density for the second transformed sum}]{OAI78}) the child polynomial~\cite[eq.~(17.55)]{OAI78} becomes
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s4-child}
 Q_{a,\gamma,\boldsymbol\zeta}(k_{\rm new},f_{\rm new})=\ssum_n\overline{\alpha(n)}^{\,2}\gamma_2(n)\nu_a'(n)\rho'(n)
 \chi_n(k_{\rm new})\chi_n(f_{\rm new})^4\mathfrak d_a'(n)W_a'\Bigl(\frac{\N(n)}{Z^{N_{\rm ch}}}\Bigr),
\end{equation}
of the form~\eqref{eq:s4-E}, while the outer weight~\cite[eq.~(17.56)]{OAI78} is
literally unchanged, since it contains only $|B_o|^2$ and $|D_o|^2$. Thus~\cite[eq.~(17.57)]{OAI78} holds with~\eqref{eq:s4-child}. The second Cauchy inequality, the fibre and source counts, the positive
children and the energy exponent (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2]{OAI78}) use only the absolute weights and the positive child sums. The
latter are CM canonical energies~\eqref{eq:s4-E}, with label weight $D_{K_{\rm slot}}(f)$, puncture
$\rho_\gamma$ and mark $\mathfrak d_a'$; by~\cite[eqs.~(17.51) and~(17.52)]{OAI78}
they satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:s4-canonical} with shorter rows.

\paragraph{Step 5: order of choices and termination (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{Order of choices and termination}]{OAI78}).} The parameter choices
\cite[eq.~(17.66)]{OAI78}, the window family $\mathcal I_D$, the crude exponent $B_{\rm crude}$ and
the Fourier orders are unchanged. The exponent $B_{\rm ref}$ (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{Order of choices and termination}]{OAI78}) uses~\cite[eq.~(5.2)]{OAI78} for
the dual coefficients. The CM dual coefficients $d_\sigma(\ell)\alpha(\ell)^2$ satisfy the same bound
(Proposition~\ref{prop:s1-reflection} and the coefficient bounds after Proposition~\ref{prop:b3-reflection}), and
their local factors are the same,
so $B_{\rm ref}$ is unchanged. The thresholds for $C_{\rm res},C_{\rm width},C_{\rm ker}$ are those of~(R). The
backward induction of \cite[proof of Lemma~17.2, \emph{Order of choices and termination}]{OAI78} then gives $\mathcal E\ll Z^{F_0+\varepsilon}$. \qed

The family~\eqref{eq:s4-E} is closed under the recursion for the following reason. In~\cite{OAI78} the classes
alternate between $\overline\alpha\gamma_2\nu$ (cubic) and $\mu\nu$ (M\"obius). The twist multiplies every column
by $\varphi=\overline\alpha$, so the CM classes alternate between $\overline\alpha^{\,2}\gamma_2\nu$ and
$\mu\overline\alpha\nu$, and the child is again of the form~\eqref{eq:s4-a}.

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:s4-marked}}\label{sec:s4-marked-proof}
We follow the proof of~\cite[Lemma~17.1]{OAI78}.

\paragraph{Overlap with the slots (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.1]{OAI78}).} Let $P$ be the product of the slot primes, $j=(n,P)$,
$n=jn_0$, $P=jP_0$ and $c=n_0P_0$. Then
$\alpha(n)^{\epsilon}=\alpha(j)^{\epsilon}\alpha(c)^{\epsilon}\overline{\alpha(P_0)^{\epsilon}}$. Together with
$\mu(n)=\mu(j)\mu(c)\mu(P_0)$ and $\psi_u(n)\psi_u(P)=\psi_u(j)^2\psi_u(c)$ (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.1]{OAI78}), this gives, for a fixed
assigned tuple with surviving subset $I_0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s4-overlap}
\begin{split}
 &\mu(n)\alpha(n)^{\epsilon}\psi_u(n)\prod_{i\in I}a_i(p_i)\psi_u(p_i)\\
 &\quad=\Bigl[\mu(j)\alpha(j)^{\epsilon}\psi_u(j)^2\prod_{i\notin I_0}a_i(p_i)\Bigr]\cdot
 \mu(c)\alpha(c)^{\epsilon}\psi_u(c)\cdot\prod_{i\in I_0}\mu(p_i)\overline{\alpha(p_i)^{\epsilon}}\,a_i(p_i).
\end{split}
\end{equation}
So~\cite[eq.~(17.67)]{OAI78} holds with three changes:
\begin{itemize}
\item the assigned scalar is multiplied by $\alpha(j)^{\epsilon}$, which has modulus one and does not depend on $u$;
\item the residual coefficient is $\mu(c)\alpha(c)^{\epsilon}\psi_u(c)$;
\item the surviving slot coefficients are $\mu(p)\overline{\alpha(p)^{\epsilon}}a_i(p)$, which are bounded by one,
 independent of $u$, and of product form.
\end{itemize}
The Fourier separation of the overlap profile (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.1]{OAI78}) then gives marks
$\mathfrak d_{\boldsymbol\upsilon}$ with these individual coefficients times $W_i(y_i)y_i^{i\upsilon_i}$.

\paragraph{Orientation (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.1]{OAI78}).} For $\epsilon=-1$ the residual polynomial is unchanged; for $\epsilon=+1$
conjugate it, with its character, mark and test. Since
$\overline{\mu(c)\alpha(c)\nu(c)\chi_c(u)}=\mu(c)\overline{\alpha(c)}\,\overline{\nu(c)}\,\overline{\chi_c(u)}$, in
both cases it suffices to bound
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s4-R0}
 \mathcal R_0(u)=Z^{-D'/2}\ssum_c\mu(c)\overline{\alpha(c)}\nu_0(c)\ind_{(c,j)=1}\overline{\chi_c(u)}\,
 \mathfrak d_0(c)\,W_0\Bigl(\frac{\N(c)}{Z^{D'}}\Bigr),
\end{equation}
which is~\cite[eq.~(17.68)]{OAI78} times $\varphi(c)$. Co-orientation is what makes
the twist $\varphi=\overline\alpha$ for both signs; an anti-oriented sum would give the twist $\alpha$.

\paragraph{The Poisson transformation (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.1]{OAI78}).} Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:s4-transfer} with $\lambda=(c_1,c_2)$.
Here $c_a=Cz_a=Csn_a$ (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.1]{OAI78}), so the outer twist is $1$ and the columns $n_a$ carry
$\varphi(n_a)$. By~\eqref{eq:s4-S3} and~\eqref{eq:s4-S4}, the signal step (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.1]{OAI78}) gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s4-Astar}
 A_*(z)=\overline{\alpha(z)}^{\,2}\gamma_2(z)\nu_*(z),\qquad\nu_*=\nu_0\overline\vartheta,
\end{equation}
in place of $\overline\alpha\gamma_2\nu_*$. Then:
\begin{itemize}
\item \cite[eq.~(17.71)]{OAI78} holds with~\eqref{eq:s4-Astar}, the common factor being
 $|\mu(C)\overline{\alpha(C)}\nu_0(C)|^2=1$ (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.1]{OAI78});
\item the extraction~\cite[eq.~(17.72)]{OAI78} holds for~\eqref{eq:s4-Astar} by the
 twisted multiplicativity in Lemma~\ref{lem:s4-switch}, and $|A_*(s)|=1$, so the outer
 arithmetic weight~\cite[eq.~(17.73)]{OAI78} is unchanged;
\item the child polynomial~\cite[eq.~(17.77)]{OAI78} has the coefficient~\eqref{eq:s4-Astar},
 so it is of the form~\eqref{eq:s4-E}, and the outer weight~\cite[eq.~(17.78)]{OAI78} and the
 separated identity~\cite[eq.~(17.79)]{OAI78} hold unchanged.
\end{itemize}
The rest (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.1]{OAI78}) involves no coefficient beyond absolute weights. This covers the weighted Cauchy
inequality, the fibre bound~\cite[eq.~(17.80)]{OAI78}, the quotient measure, the puncture
bound~\cite[eq.~(17.84)]{OAI78}, the margins~\cite[eq.~(17.85)]{OAI78}, the
energy~\cite[eq.~(17.86)]{OAI78}, and the raw-tail orders. It applies with
Lemma~\ref{lem:s4-canonical} in place of~\cite[Lemma~17.2]{OAI78}. The principal columns have the
direct bound $O(Z^{m+\varepsilon})$ (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.1]{OAI78}), with absolute coefficients. This proves~\eqref{eq:s4-marked};
the remaining clauses follow as in \cite[proof of Lemma~17.1]{OAI78}. \qed

\subsection{Amplification on sixth-power-free rows}\label{sec:s4-amplification}
\cite[Lemma~17.6]{OAI78} holds for the polynomials~\eqref{eq:s4-M} without slots.
Its input~\cite[eq.~(17.87)]{OAI78} is Proposition~\ref{prop:s4-marked} with $Z=H$,
$m=1$, $r=\log_HD$ and no slots (\cite[Section~17.7]{OAI78}). In the proof (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.6]{OAI78}) only the column
identity~\cite[eq.~(17.90)]{OAI78} changes. It becomes
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s4-sixth}
 M_u(D;W)=\sum_{d\mid\operatorname{rad}a}\mu(d)\alpha(d)^{\epsilon}\psi_u(d)\N(d)^{-1/2}M_{ua^6}(D/\N(d);W),
\end{equation}
because $n=dm$ with $d\mid\operatorname{rad}a$, $(m,a)=1$, gives $\alpha(n)^\epsilon=\alpha(d)^\epsilon\alpha(m)^\epsilon$,
and $\psi_{ua^6}(m)=\psi_u(m)\ind_{(m,a)=1}$ (\cite[proof of Lemma~17.6]{OAI78}). The coefficient mass is still at most
$d_{\OO}(a)$, and the rest is verbatim.

\subsection{Orientation, and what this appendix proves}\label{sec:s4-summary}
The descent itself would also run for the anti-oriented sum $\sum\mu\alpha\nu\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)}$, with twist
$\alpha$ in Lemma~\ref{lem:s4-transfer}. Its canonical class would be $\gamma_2\nu$, with no angular factor,
by~\eqref{eq:s4-S5}. That is the completed sum of $\theta$ itself, and Statement~(R) would have to be replaced by a
reflected energy for $\theta$, whose expansion at the identity cusp has a constant term (Section~\ref{sec:78probe}).
This case is not covered here, and it is not needed: every CM witness is co-oriented (Section~\ref{sec:78rows}),
and conjugation preserves co-orientation.

In summary:
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Proved here}: Lemmas~\ref{lem:s4-switch} and~\ref{lem:s4-transfer}, and every display of~\cite{OAI78}
 that changes. These are the definitions~\eqref{eq:s4-M}, \eqref{eq:s4-a}, \eqref{eq:s4-E}, \eqref{eq:s4-T}
 and the identities \eqref{eq:s4-inversion}, \eqref{eq:s4-beta}, \eqref{eq:s4-Pa}, \eqref{eq:s4-a0},
 \eqref{eq:s4-child}, \eqref{eq:s4-overlap}, \eqref{eq:s4-R0}, \eqref{eq:s4-Astar} and~\eqref{eq:s4-sixth}.
 Hence Lemma~\ref{lem:s4-canonical}, Proposition~\ref{prop:s4-marked} (that is,~\eqref{eq:cm-inverse}) and the CM
 form of~\cite[Lemma~17.6]{OAI78} hold.
\item \emph{Cited verbatim}: all other steps of~\cite[Section~17]{OAI78}, at the lines given above.
\item \emph{From Appendix~\ref{app:s1-general}}: Statement~(R), the CM reflected energy
 (Lemma~\ref{lem:s1-reflected-energy}).
\end{itemize}
We checked numerically:
\begin{itemize}
\item \eqref{eq:s4-S1}--\eqref{eq:s4-S5} at all $366$ squarefree $u$ with $\N(u)\le1300$, $157$ of them composite;
\item the twisted multiplicativity for $\nu'=\chi_\bullet(\lambda)$;
\item the ray-quotient forms of the two switches;
\item \eqref{eq:s4-inversion} with a character, a puncture and a two-slot mark;
\item \eqref{eq:s4-overlap} for both signs, including a row sharing a slot prime;
\item \eqref{eq:s4-sixth} for both signs.
\end{itemize}
The errors are at most $3.3\cdot10^{-15}$.

\section{The plain fourth moment with an angular factor}\label{app:s5-plain}

This appendix writes out item~5 of Section~\ref{sec:78status}: the CM form of the fourth moment
with short prime factors~\cite[Lemma~18.1]{OAI78}, stated in paragraph~4 of
Section~\ref{sec:78moments}. Steps that are identical to~\cite{OAI78} are cited by number, with the
reason they carry over. Every step that changes is proved here. There are four such
steps:
\begin{itemize}
\item the reflection of a plain sum, now with a Bessel kernel of order one
 (Lemmas~\ref{lem:s5-rowchar}--\ref{lem:s5-sharp});
\item the vanishing of the lattice main terms (Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-lattice});
\item the bookkeeping of the angular factor through the transforms (Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-coefficients});
\item the zero-free input for the prime factors, which becomes a theorem (Step~5 of the proof).
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Setting and statement}\label{sec:s5-setting}
We use the notation of~\cite[\S\S 18 and 13]{OAI78}. In particular:
\begin{itemize}
\item $Z>1$ is the base, and $\mathcal S$ is the fixed finite set of primes; it contains the primes above $6$,
 so $\lambda\in\mathcal S$. Column sums run over ideals outside $\mathcal S$, through their primary
 generators;~\cite{OAI78} writes $q_l$ for $\N(l)$.
\item The rows are $k\in\OO$ with $0<\N(k)\ll Z^m$, and $\psi_k(n)=\tau(n)\chi_n(k)$ is the finite-order row
 character, with $\tau$ fixed in the row sum. The moving support has norm at most $Z^q$, and $M=m+q$.
\item Masks are as in \cite[Section~18]{OAI78} and \cite[Section~13.1]{OAI78}.
\item $\Theta$ is the fixed finite group of finite-order ray characters with conductor primes in $\mathcal S$
 (\cite[Section~18]{OAI78}). The row sets $\mathcal R_0$ and $\mathcal R_z$ (\cite[Section~18]{OAI78}) are defined through
 $\psi_k$.
\item $A=n_1+n_2+z$.
\end{itemize}
The prime slots $Q_{\psi_k,i}$ are those of~\cite[eq.~(13.1)]{OAI78}. They carry
$\psi_k$ and never the angular factor.

For an ideal $l$ outside $\mathcal S$ put $\alpha(l)=\alpha(l_0)$, where $l_0$ is its primary
generator. Products of primary elements are primary (\cite[Section~4.1]{OAI78}), so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s5-alpha-mult}
 \alpha(ll')=\alpha(l)\alpha(l'),\qquad|\alpha(l)|=1\qquad(l,l'\text{ outside }\mathcal S).
\end{equation}
For $e\in\{1,-1\}$ the \emph{CM plain polynomial} of log-length $n$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s5-plain-def}
 S^{(e)}_{\psi_k}(n;W)=Z^{-n/2}\sum_l\alpha(l)^e\psi_k(l)\,W\Bigl(\frac{\N(l)}{Z^n}\Bigr),
\end{equation}
the analogue of~\cite[eq.~(4.1)]{OAI78}, with every mask of $\psi_k$ retained.

\begin{proposition}[Fourth moment with an angular factor]\label{prop:s5-plain}
Let $3/4\le\kappa\le1$ and $e\in\{1,-1\}$. Let $n_1,n_2$, the slot product $Q=\prod_iQ_{\psi_k,i}$,
its coefficients $\nu_i$, the masks and the profiles satisfy the hypotheses of~\cite[Lemma~18.1]{OAI78}. For every $\varepsilon>0$ there is a slot mesh $\eta>0$ such that, if $z_i\le\eta$
for every $i$, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s5-plain-bound}
 \sum_{k\in\mathcal R_z}\Bigl|S^{(e)}_{\psi_k}(n_1;W_1)\,S^{(e)}_{\psi_k}(n_2;W_2)\,Q\Bigr|^2\ll Z^{M+\varepsilon}
\end{equation}
in either of the following cases:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $z=0$, with no restriction on the lengths $n_1,n_2\ge0$ and no lower bound on the conductor;
\item $z>0$ and $n_1+n_2+6\kappa z\le M$.
\end{enumerate}
No zero-free hypothesis is assumed. The remaining clauses of~\cite[Lemma~18.1]{OAI78} hold verbatim, uniformly in $\kappa\in[3/4,1]$ and $e$. These clauses cover zero-length
slots, bounded length sets, a mesh depending only on those sets and $\varepsilon$, finitely many seminorms
and a fixed polynomial in the separated heights, and uniformity over moving moduli, admissible masks and
frozen outer labels.
\end{proposition}

For $\kappa<1$,~\cite[Lemma~18.1]{OAI78} assumes $\beta_*\le(1+\kappa)/2$. Here $\beta_*$ is the
supremum~\cite[eq.~(2.1), Section~2]{OAI78} over \emph{finite-order} characters, not the CM
quantity of Section~\ref{sec:78}. OpenAI's theorem~\cite[Theorem~1.1]{OAI78} gives
$\beta_*\le7/8\le(1+\kappa)/2$ for every $\kappa\ge3/4$. That is why the hypothesis disappears, and why
$\kappa=3/4$ is admissible (Step~5 below).

\subsection{The primitive CM row character}\label{sec:s5-rowchar}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:s5-rowchar}
Fix a row $k$ and $e\in\{1,-1\}$. Write:
\begin{itemize}
\item $\psi_k^0$ for the natural character of~\cite{OAI78} (\cite[Section~18.1]{OAI78});
\item $\psi_k^*$ for the primitive finite-order character inducing $\psi_k^0$, and $\mathfrak g_k$ for its
 conductor;
\item $R_{0,k}$ for the redundant natural radical (\cite[Section~18.1]{OAI78}): the product of the primes not dividing
 $\mathfrak g_k$ at which $\psi_k^0$, extended by zero on $\mathcal S$, vanishes.
\end{itemize}
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] There is a unique primitive Hecke character $\Xi_k$ of $K$, of infinity type $(a/|a|)^e$, with
 $\Xi_k(l)=\alpha(l)^e\psi_k^*(l)$ for every ideal $l$ prime to $3\mathfrak g_k$. Explicitly,
 $\Xi_k((a))=\varphi_k(a)(a/|a|)^e$ for $a$ prime to its conductor $\mathfrak f_k$. Here $\varphi_k$ is a
 primitive character of $(\OO/\mathfrak f_k)^\times$ with $\varphi_k(u)=u^{-e}$ for the six units $u$.
\item[(b)] $v_p(\mathfrak f_k)=v_p(\mathfrak g_k)$ for every prime $p\ne\lambda$. Moreover
 $v_\lambda(\mathfrak f_k)\le\max(2,v_\lambda(\mathfrak g_k))$ and
 $v_\lambda(\mathfrak g_k)\le\max(2,v_\lambda(\mathfrak f_k))$. In particular
 $1/9\le\N(\mathfrak f_k)/\N(\mathfrak g_k)\le9$.
\item[(c)] Let $\mathfrak r_k$ be the product of the primes not dividing $\mathfrak f_k$ that lie in
 $\mathcal S$ or at which $\psi_k^0$ vanishes. Write $[\alpha^e\psi_k^0](l)$ for $\alpha(l)^e\psi_k^0(l)$
 when $l$ is outside $\mathcal S$, and for $0$ otherwise. Then, for every nonzero ideal $l$,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:s5-natural}
 [\alpha^e\psi_k^0](l)=\Xi_k(l)\,\ind_{(l,\mathfrak r_k)=1}.
 \end{equation}
 The ideal $\mathfrak r_k$ is squarefree, prime to $\mathfrak f_k$, and differs from $R_{0,k}$ at most
 at~$\lambda$.
\item[(d)] Put $C_k^{\rm CM}=3\N(\mathfrak f_k)/(4\pi^2)$, and let $C_k=3\N(\mathfrak g_k)/(4\pi^2)$ be the
 conductor scale of~\cite{OAI78} (\cite[Section~18.1]{OAI78}). Then $C_k^{\rm CM}\N(\mathfrak r_k)\le27\,C_k\N(R_{0,k})$.
 Hence~\cite[eq.~(18.7)]{OAI78} holds for the CM data, with $C_{\mathcal A}$ replaced by
 $27C_{\mathcal A}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) Reduction modulo $3$ maps the six units bijectively onto $(\OO/3)^\times$ (\cite[Section~4.1]{OAI78}). So each $a$ prime
to $3$ is uniquely $a=u(a)a_0$, with $u(a)$ a unit and $a_0$ primary. Since $u(ab)=u(a)u(b)$, the function
$\varphi_\alpha(a)=u(a)^{-1}$ is a character of $(\OO/3)^\times$ with $\varphi_\alpha(u)=u^{-1}$. Hence
$\varphi_\alpha(a)\,a/|a|=a_0/|a_0|$ depends only on the ideal $(a)$, and on an ideal outside $\mathcal S$
it is the $\alpha(l)$ of~\eqref{eq:s5-plain-def}. So $\alpha$ is a Hecke character modulo $3$ of infinity
type $a/|a|$.

Since $K$ has class number one, $\psi_k^*((a))=\varphi_0(a)$, where $\varphi_0$ is a primitive character of
$(\OO/\mathfrak g_k)^\times$ that is trivial on units. Thus, for $a$ prime to $3\mathfrak g_k$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d175}
 \alpha^e\psi_k^*((a))=\varphi_\alpha(a)^e\varphi_0(a)\,(a/|a|)^e .
\end{equation}
Let $\varphi_k$ be the primitive character inducing $\varphi_\alpha^e\varphi_0$, and let $\mathfrak f_k$ be
its conductor. On units $\varphi_k(u)=\varphi_\alpha(u)^e\varphi_0(u)=u^{-e}$, so
$\Xi_k((a))=\varphi_k(a)(a/|a|)^e$ is well defined on ideals prime to $\mathfrak f_k$. It agrees with
$\alpha^e\psi_k^*$ on ideals prime to $3\mathfrak g_k$. It is primitive because $\varphi_k$ is: a
character modulo a proper divisor of $\mathfrak f_k$ would induce~$\varphi_k$. Two primitive characters
that agree outside a finite set of primes coincide, which gives uniqueness.

(b) By the Chinese remainder theorem, a character of $(\OO/m)^\times$ is a product of components on the
groups $(\OO/p^{v_p(m)})^\times$. Its conductor exponent at $p$ is that of the $p$-component. Since
$3=-\lambda^2$, the character $\varphi_\alpha$ lives on $(\OO/\lambda^2)^\times$. So the
$p$-components of $\varphi_\alpha^e\varphi_0$ and $\varphi_0$ agree for $p\ne\lambda$. At $\lambda$, a
product of characters trivial on $1+\lambda^a\OO$ and on $1+\lambda^b\OO$ is trivial on
$1+\lambda^{\max(a,b)}\OO$. This gives the first inequality; the second follows from
$\varphi_0=\varphi_\alpha^{-e}(\varphi_\alpha^e\varphi_0)$. If $v_\lambda(\mathfrak g_k)\ge3$, the two
inequalities force equality. Otherwise both exponents are at most $2$, and $\N(\lambda)=3$.

(c) Let $l$ be a nonzero ideal. Suppose first that $l$ is prime to $\mathfrak f_k\mathfrak r_k$, and let
$p\mid l$. Then $p\notin\mathcal S$ and $\psi_k^0(p)\ne0$, so $p\nmid\mathfrak g_k$, because
$\psi_k^0$ vanishes at the conductor primes of $\psi_k^*$. Hence $\psi_k^0(p)=\psi_k^*(p)$, and
$\alpha(p)^e\psi_k^*(p)=\Xi_k(p)$ by~(a). Multiplicativity then gives~\eqref{eq:s5-natural}.

If $p\mid l$ and $p\mid\mathfrak f_k$, both sides vanish. Indeed $\Xi_k(p)=0$, and $p\mid3\mathfrak g_k$,
so either $p\in\mathcal S$ or $\psi_k^0(p)=\psi_k^*(p)=0$. If $p\mid l$ and $p\mid\mathfrak r_k$, both sides
vanish by definition.

The ideals $\mathfrak r_k$ and $R_{0,k}$ are built from the same zero set, since $\alpha$ never vanishes.
By~(b), $\mathfrak f_k$ and $\mathfrak g_k$ have the same prime divisors other than $\lambda$.

(d) Away from $\lambda$, $\mathfrak f_k\mathfrak r_k$ and $\mathfrak g_kR_{0,k}$ coincide by~(b) and~(c).
At $\lambda$, write $v=v_\lambda(\mathfrak g_k)$. The $\lambda$-part of $\N(\mathfrak f_k\mathfrak r_k)$ is
at most $3^{\max(2,v)+1}\le27\cdot3^v$. So $\N(\mathfrak f_k)\N(\mathfrak r_k)\le27\N(\mathfrak g_k)\N(R_{0,k})$.
The last claim is~\cite[eq.~(18.7), Section~18.1]{OAI78}.
\end{proof}

The $\lambda$-part of the conductor can move in both directions: for $\psi=\chi_\pi^5$ with $\N(\pi)=7$
and $e=1$, the finite part has conductor norm $63$ but $\Xi$ has conductor norm $7$. So $\lambda$ can pass
from the conductor into $\mathfrak r_k$, and the reverse also occurs. Only the bound in~(d) is used.

\subsection{Reflection of a plain sum with an angular factor}\label{sec:s5-reflection}

\begin{lemma}[CM reflection]\label{lem:s5-reflection}
Let $\Xi$ be a primitive Hecke character of $K$ with conductor $\mathfrak f=(f)$ and infinity type
$(a/|a|)^e$, $e\in\{1,-1\}$. Thus $\Xi((a))=\varphi(a)(a/|a|)^e$ for $a$ prime to $\mathfrak f$, with
$\varphi$ primitive modulo $f$ and $\varphi(u)=u^{-e}$ on units; in particular $\mathfrak f\ne(1)$, since
$\varphi(-1)=-1$. Put $C_\Xi=3\N(\mathfrak f)/(4\pi^2)$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s5-root}
 \gamma(\varphi)=\frac1{\sqrt{\N(f)}}\sum_{x\bmod f}\varphi(x)\,e\Bigl(\frac xf\Bigr),\qquad
 \epsilon(\Xi)=e\,\gamma(\varphi)\,\Bigl(\frac f{|f|}\Bigr)^e .
\end{equation}
Let $W$ be smooth on $(0,\infty)$, such that $g(z)=(z/|z|)^eW(|z|^2)$, with $g(0)=0$, is a Schwartz
function on $\mathbb C$. This holds, for example, for annular $W$. Define the Hankel transform~\cite[\S10.22(v)]{DLMF}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s5-sharp-def}
 W^\sharp(y)=\int_0^\infty W(x)\,J_1\bigl(2\sqrt{xy}\bigr)\,dx .
\end{equation}
Then $|\epsilon(\Xi)|=1$, and for every $X>0$
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s5-reflection}
 X^{-1/2}\sum_{\mathfrak a}\Xi(\mathfrak a)\,W\Bigl(\frac{\N\mathfrak a}X\Bigr)
 =\epsilon(\Xi)\,Y^{-1/2}\sum_{\mathfrak b}\overline{\Xi(\mathfrak b)}\,W^\sharp\Bigl(\frac{\N\mathfrak b}Y\Bigr),
 \qquad Y=\frac{C_\Xi}X .
\end{equation}
Both sums run over nonzero ideals and converge absolutely.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For $a\in\OO$ put $c(a)=\varphi(a)(a/|a|)^e$, which is $0$ unless $(a,f)=1$. Then $c(ua)=c(a)$ for units
$u$, and every ideal prime to $\mathfrak f$ has six generators. So
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d176}
 \sum_{\mathfrak a}\Xi(\mathfrak a)W(\N\mathfrak a/X)=\frac16\sum_{a\in\OO}\varphi(a)\,g_X(a),\qquad
 g_X(z)=(z/|z|)^eW(|z|^2/X).
\end{equation}
Write $\widehat h(w)=\int_{\mathbb C}h(z)e(-zw)\,d^2z$. Write $a=t+fy$ with $t\bmod f$ and $y\in\OO$. Poisson
summation over $\OO$~\cite[Theorem~19]{QF}, applied to $y\mapsto g_X(t+fy)$, and the substitution
$z=t+fy$ (as in~\cite[(4.20)]{QF}) give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d177}
 \sum_{y\in\OO}g_X(t+fy)=\frac2{\sqrt3}\,\frac1{\N(f)}\sum_{w\in\OO}e\Bigl(\frac{tw}f\Bigr)\widehat{g_X}\Bigl(\frac wf\Bigr).
\end{equation}
By~\cite[Lemma~17]{QF}, $\sum_{t\bmod f}\varphi(t)e(tw/f)=\overline{\varphi(w)}\sqrt{\N(f)}\,\gamma(\varphi)$ for
every $w\in\OO$; both sides vanish when $(w,f)\ne1$. The same lemma gives $|\gamma(\varphi)|=1$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s5-step1}
 \sum_{a\in\OO}\varphi(a)g_X(a)=\frac2{\sqrt3}\,\frac{\gamma(\varphi)}{\sqrt{\N(f)}}
 \sum_{w\in\OO}\overline{\varphi(w)}\,\widehat{g_X}(w/f).
\end{equation}

We now compute $\widehat{g_X}$. Write $z=re^{i\theta}$ and $w=\rho e^{i\beta}$. Then
$\operatorname{Im}(zw)=r\rho\sin(\theta+\beta)$. The Jacobi--Anger expansion~\cite[(10.12.1)]{DLMF}
$e^{-ix\sin\phi}=\sum_nJ_n(x)e^{-in\phi}$ and $J_{-1}=-J_1$ give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d178}
 \int_0^{2\pi}e^{ie\theta}\exp\Bigl(-\frac{4\pi i r\rho}{\sqrt3}\sin(\theta+\beta)\Bigr)d\theta
 =2\pi J_e\Bigl(\frac{4\pi r\rho}{\sqrt3}\Bigr)e^{-ie\beta}
 =2\pi e\,J_1\Bigl(\frac{4\pi r\rho}{\sqrt3}\Bigr)\Bigl(\frac{\bar w}{|w|}\Bigr)^e .
\end{equation}
Integrating against $W(r^2/X)\,r\,dr$ and substituting $x=r^2/X$ gives, for $w\ne0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s5-hankel}
 \widehat{g_X}(w)=\pi eX\Bigl(\frac{\bar w}{|w|}\Bigr)^e\int_0^\infty W(x)\,
 J_1\Bigl(4\pi|w|\sqrt{Xx/3}\Bigr)dx .
\end{equation}
At $w=0$ we get $\widehat{g_X}(0)=\int g_X=0$, because $\int_0^{2\pi}e^{ie\theta}d\theta=0$.

Insert~\eqref{eq:s5-hankel} at $w/f$ into~\eqref{eq:s5-step1}. Three identities simplify the result:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d179}
 \Bigl(\frac{\overline{w/f}}{|w/f|}\Bigr)^e=\Bigl(\frac{\bar w}{|w|}\Bigr)^e\Bigl(\frac f{|f|}\Bigr)^e,\qquad
 \overline{\varphi(w)}\Bigl(\frac{\bar w}{|w|}\Bigr)^e=\overline{c(w)},\qquad
 4\pi\Bigl|\frac wf\Bigr|\sqrt{\frac{Xx}3}=2\sqrt{\frac{x\N(w)}Y}.
\end{equation}
The last uses $Y=C_\Xi/X$. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d180}
 \sum_{a}\varphi(a)g_X(a)=\frac{2\pi eX}{\sqrt3}\,\frac{\gamma(\varphi)}{\sqrt{\N(f)}}\Bigl(\frac f{|f|}\Bigr)^e
 \sum_{w\ne0}\overline{c(w)}\,W^\sharp\Bigl(\frac{\N(w)}Y\Bigr).
\end{equation}
Group $w$ into ideals (six generators each, and $\overline c$ is invariant under units) and divide by $6$.
Multiplying by $X^{-1/2}$ and using $X^{1/2}Y^{1/2}=C_\Xi^{1/2}=\sqrt{3\N(f)}/(2\pi)$ gives the
coefficient $e\gamma(\varphi)(f/|f|)^e=\epsilon(\Xi)$. Absolute convergence holds on the left because $g$
is Schwartz. On the right it holds because $\widehat{g_X}$ is Schwartz, and the $w$-sum is the Poisson dual
sum.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Remark (relation to Hecke's functional equation).} Take Mellin transforms in $X$
of~\eqref{eq:s5-reflection} and use Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-sharp}(a). This gives
$C_\Xi^{s/2}\Gamma(s+\frac12)L(s,\Xi)=\epsilon(\Xi)\,C_\Xi^{(1-s)/2}\Gamma(\frac32-s)L(1-s,\overline\Xi)$.
This is the functional equation of Lemma~\ref{lem:cm-growth}, with the explicit root
number~\eqref{eq:s5-root}. \cite{OAI78} goes the other way: it derives its reflection (\cite[Section~18.1]{OAI78})
from the functional equation by Mellin inversion. For a CM character~\eqref{eq:s5-reflection} is proved
directly, so this step uses no analytic continuation. Take $\Xi=\alpha$, of conductor $(3)$. Then
$L(s,\alpha)=L(E_{16},s+\frac12)$, where $E_{16}\colon y^2=x^3+16$ has conductor $27=3\N(3)$. We found
$\epsilon(\alpha)=1$ numerically, which is the root number of that curve.

\begin{lemma}[The dual profile]\label{lem:s5-sharp}
Let $W$ be annular. Use the Mellin convention $\mathcal MW(s)=\int_0^\infty W(y)y^{s}\,dy/y$ of~\cite{OAI78}
(\cite[Lemma~4.5]{OAI78}), and put $G(s)=\Gamma(s+\frac12)/\Gamma(\frac32-s)$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] $W^\sharp$ is smooth on $(0,\infty)$, and $\mathcal MW^\sharp(s)=\mathcal MW(1-s)\,G(s)$ for
 $-\frac12<\Re s<\frac14$. In the notation of~\cite[Lemma~4.7]{OAI78},
 $W^\sharp=K_{W_1}$ with $W_1(y)=yW(y)$ and $m=G$.
\item[(b)] $G$ is holomorphic on $\Re s>-\frac12$. Its poles are at $-\frac12-n$ and its zeros at
 $\frac32+n$ ($n\ge0$). Uniformly for $\sigma$ in compact subsets of $(-\frac12,\infty)$,
 $|G(\sigma+it)|=|t|^{2\sigma-1}(1+O(|t|^{-1}))$ as $|t|\to\infty$, the same exponent as for
 $\Gamma(s)/\Gamma(1-s)$.
\item[(c)] For integers $j\ge0$ and every $A\ge0$,
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:d181}
 |(y\partial_y)^jW^\sharp(y)|\ll_{j,A}\min\bigl(y^{1/2},y^{-A}\bigr),
 \end{equation}
 with constants given by finitely many seminorms of $W$. Also
 $W^\sharp(y)=\mathcal MW(\tfrac32)\,y^{1/2}+O(y^{3/2})$ as $y\to0$. For $W_\omega(y)=W(y)y^{i\omega}$ the
 same bounds hold with an extra factor $(1+|\omega|)^{h}$, where $h=h(j,A)$ is fixed.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) Smoothness follows by differentiating under the integral, since $W$ has compact support in
$(0,\infty)$. For $-\frac12<\sigma<\frac14$ the double integral of $|W(x)J_1(2\sqrt{xy})|y^{\sigma-1}$
converges, by $|J_1(t)|\le t/2$ and $|J_1(t)|\ll t^{-1/2}$~\cite[(10.14.4), (10.7.8)]{DLMF}. The classical Mellin transform
$\int_0^\infty t^{\mu-1}J_1(t)\,dt=2^{\mu-1}\Gamma(\frac{1+\mu}2)/\Gamma(\frac{3-\mu}2)$, valid for
$-1<\Re\mu<\frac32$ (\cite[(10.22.43)]{DLMF}, with $\mu-1$ in place of $\mu$), with $t=2\sqrt{xy}$, gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d182}
 \int_0^\infty J_1(2\sqrt{xy})\,y^{s-1}\,dy=x^{-s}G(s)\qquad(-\tfrac12<\Re s<\tfrac34).
\end{equation}
Fubini then gives $\mathcal MW^\sharp(s)=\mathcal MW(1-s)G(s)$. Since $\mathcal MW_1(-s)=\mathcal MW(1-s)$,
Mellin inversion on a line in $(-\frac12,\frac14)$ identifies $W^\sharp$ with $K_{W_1}$. The inversion is
legitimate because $W^\sharp$ is continuous and $\mathcal MW(1-s)G(s)$ is integrable on the line,
by~\cite[eq.~(4.11)]{OAI78}.

(b) $\Gamma(s+\frac12)$ has poles at $-\frac12-n$, and $1/\Gamma(\frac32-s)$ is entire with zeros at
$\frac32+n$. Stirling's formula $|\Gamma(\sigma+it)|=\sqrt{2\pi}|t|^{\sigma-1/2}e^{-\pi|t|/2}(1+O(|t|^{-1}))$
gives the exponent $\sigma-(1-\sigma)=2\sigma-1$.

(c) Large $y$: $G$ is holomorphic on the closed strip $-\frac14\le\Re s\le A$, where
$|G(s)|\ll(1+|\Im s|)^{2A+1}$. So~\cite[Lemma~4.7]{OAI78} applies on the available line
$\Re s=A$ and gives $|(y\partial_y)^jW^\sharp(y)|\ll y^{-A}$, with the stated seminorm and twist
dependence. This is the argument of \cite[Section~18.1]{OAI78} with $G$ in place of $\Gamma(s)/\Gamma(1-s)$.

Small $y$: $y\partial_y[J_1(2\sqrt{xy})]=x\partial_x[J_1(2\sqrt{xy})]$. Integrating by parts $j$ times,
with no boundary terms, gives $(y\partial_y)^jW^\sharp(y)=\int_0^\infty W_j(x)J_1(2\sqrt{xy})\,dx$, where
$W_j=(-\partial_xx)^jW$ is annular. Then $|J_1(t)|\le t/2$ gives
$|(y\partial_y)^jW^\sharp(y)|\le y^{1/2}\int|W_j(x)|x^{1/2}dx$. The expansion $J_1(t)=t/2+O(t^3)$ gives the
asymptotic formula. Finally, $W_\omega$ is annular, with $j$-th seminorm $\ll(1+|\omega|)^j$.
\end{proof}

So the CM dual profile is better than that of~\cite{OAI78} at the origin: it vanishes like $y^{1/2}$,
while $\Gamma(s)/\Gamma(1-s)$ leaves a constant term. It is the same at infinity and on vertical lines.
In particular, the annular coefficients below the main scale in \cite[Section~18.1]{OAI78} are $O(2^{-j})$ instead
of $O(2^{-j/2})$.

\subsection{Lattice sums with an angular factor}\label{sec:s5-lattice}

\begin{lemma}[CM form of {\cite[Lemma~18.3]{OAI78}}]
\label{lem:s5-lattice}
Let:
\begin{itemize}
\item $\vartheta$ range over a fixed finite set of finite-order ray characters with conductor primes
 in $\mathcal S$;
\item $\mathfrak r_*$ be squarefree, with $\N(\mathfrak r_*)\le Z^{B_*}$;
\item $W_1,W_2$ be smooth profiles on fixed annuli, and $e\in\{1,-1\}$.
\end{itemize}
For $X>0$ and $t\in\mathbb R$ put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d183}
 \mathcal L_i(X,t)=\sum_{l\ \text{outside}\ \mathcal S}\alpha(l)^e\vartheta(l)\,\ind_{(l,\mathfrak r_*)=1}\,
 \N(l)^{it}\,W_i\Bigl(\frac{\N(l)}X\Bigr).
\end{equation}
Then, with $J$ fixed and finitely many seminorms, uniformly in $X>0$, in $\mathfrak r_*$ and in the
finite set,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s5-lattice}
 |\mathcal L_i(X,t)|\ll Z^{\varepsilon_1}(1+|t|)^J,\qquad|\mathcal L_i(X,t)|\ll X .
\end{equation}
Consequently:
\begin{itemize}
\item \cite[eq.~(18.45)]{OAI78} holds with $c_{\vartheta,\mathfrak r_*}=0$;
\item \cite[eq.~(18.46)]{OAI78} holds;
\item \cite[eq.~(18.47)]{OAI78} holds for the coefficient
 $\alpha(l_1)^e\alpha(l_2)^e\vartheta(l_1)\vartheta(l_2)\ind_{(l_1l_2,\mathfrak r_*)=1}\N(l_1l_2)^{it}D_{\mathbf b}(l_1,l_2)$,
 together with its fallback $O(T^{1/2})$ when there is no lower length.
\end{itemize}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
First let $\mathfrak r_*=(1)$, and call the sum $\mathcal L_i^0$. The primary generators of ideals outside
$\mathcal S$ are the $z\equiv1\pmod3$ that are prime to the primes of $\mathcal S$. The value $\vartheta(l)$
depends only on $z$ modulo a fixed modulus. So there are a fixed nonzero $N_0\in\OO$ and a function $w$ on
$\OO/N_0$ with $|w|\le1$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d184}
 \mathcal L_i^0(X,t)=\sum_{z\in\OO}w(z)\,g_{X,t}(z),\qquad
 g_{X,t}(z)=X^{it}h_t(z/\sqrt X),\qquad h_t(z)=(z/|z|)^e|z|^{2it}W_i(|z|^2).
\end{equation}
Poisson summation in each class $t_0+N_0\OO$, exactly as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-reflection},
gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d185}
 \mathcal L_i^0(X,t)=\frac2{\sqrt3}\,\frac1{\N(N_0)}\sum_{w\in\OO}\widehat w(w)\,X^{1+it}\,
 \widehat{h_t}\bigl(\sqrt X\,w/N_0\bigr),
\end{equation}
where $\widehat w(w)=\sum_{t_0\bmod N_0}w(t_0)e(t_0w/N_0)$, so $|\widehat w|\le\N(N_0)$.

The zero frequency is
$X^{1+it}\widehat{h_t}(0)=X^{1+it}\int_0^\infty r^{2it}W_i(r^2)\,r\,dr\int_0^{2\pi}e^{ie\theta}\,d\theta=0$.
This is the term $c_\vartheta X^{1+it}I_i(t)$ of~\cite{OAI78}. With the angular factor it vanishes
identically.

For the other frequencies, note that $h_t$ is smooth and supported on a fixed annulus, with
$\partial^\beta h_t\ll(1+|t|)^{|\beta|}$. So $|\widehat{h_t}(\xi)|\ll(1+|t|)^{J_0}(1+|\xi|)^{-J_0}$ for any
fixed $J_0\ge3$. For $X\ge1$ this gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d186}
 |\mathcal L_i^0|\ll(1+|t|)^{J_0}X\sum_{w\ne0}(1+\sqrt X|w|/|N_0|)^{-J_0}\ll(1+|t|)^{J_0}.
\end{equation}
For $0<X<1$, absolute counting on the fixed annulus gives $O(1)$.

For a general mask, remove from $\mathfrak r_*$ its primes in $\mathcal S$. Then~\eqref{eq:s5-alpha-mult}
and the multiplicativity of $\vartheta$ give
$\mathcal L_i(X,t)=\sum_{d\mid\mathfrak r_*}\mu(d)\alpha(d)^e\vartheta(d)\N(d)^{it}\mathcal L_i^0(X/\N(d),t)$.
The number of divisors is $\ll Z^{\varepsilon_1}$, which proves the first bound
in~\eqref{eq:s5-lattice}. The second is \cite[proof of Lemma~18.3]{OAI78} verbatim, since $|\alpha|=1$.

As in \cite[proof of Lemma~18.3]{OAI78}, the masked double sum in~\cite[eq.~(18.47)]{OAI78} is exactly
$\mathcal L_1(U_1,t)\mathcal L_2(U_2,t)-\mathcal L_1(V_1,t)\mathcal L_2(V_2,t)$, where the angular factor
is already split as $\alpha(l_1)^e\alpha(l_2)^e$. By~\eqref{eq:s5-lattice} its modulus is
$\ll Z^{2\varepsilon_1}(1+|t|)^{2J}$. If all four scales are at least $Z^r$, then $T\ge Z^{2r}$, so
$T^{-1/2}\le T^{1/2}Z^{-2r}\le T^{1/2}Z^{-r}$. This proves~\cite[eq.~(18.47)]{OAI78}. The fallback
follows from the second bound in~\eqref{eq:s5-lattice}.
\end{proof}

In~\cite{OAI78} the zero frequency is a volume term, and the equal-product centering exists to cancel it
(\cite[Section~18.3]{OAI78}). With the angular factor it is absent. So the centered estimate below holds with room to
spare. We keep~\cite{OAI78}'s argument unchanged rather than restructure the induction.

\subsection{The coefficient class}\label{sec:s5-coefficients}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:s5-coefficients}
Replace the coefficient~\cite[eq.~(18.18), Section~18.3]{OAI78} by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:s5-coeff}
 \Bigl(\prod_{i\in\mathcal I'}\nu_i(p_i)W_i^{\rm slot}(\N(p_i)/P_i)\Bigr)D_{\mathbf b}(l_1,l_2)\,
 \alpha(l_1l_2)^e\,\tau_1(u)\N(u)^{it}\ind_{(u,\mathfrak R)=1}\ind_{s\mid u},\qquad u=\Pi l_1l_2,
\end{equation}
with the angular factor on the two plain variables only. Every operation of~\cite{OAI78} in
\cite[Sections~18.1--18.7]{OAI78} takes~\eqref{eq:s5-coeff} to a coefficient of the same form. The angular factor of each
extracted plain prime power becomes a frozen scalar of modulus one, the same in both rectangles.
In particular~\cite[Lemma~18.2]{OAI78} holds with
$\alpha(l_1l_2)^e$ included. In its exceptional conclusion the plain coefficient is
$\alpha(l_1)^e\alpha(l_2)^e\vartheta(l_1)\vartheta(l_2)\ind_{(l_1l_2,\mathfrak R_*)=1}\N(l_1l_2)^{it}D_{\mathbf b}(l_1,l_2)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Each operation uses only~\eqref{eq:s5-alpha-mult}.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] \emph{Mask deletion}~\cite[eq.~(18.4)]{OAI78}. Writing $n=dl$ in a plain variable gives
 $\alpha(n)^e=\alpha(d)^e\alpha(l)^e$. The row-dependent scalar $\mu(d)\alpha(d)^e\psi_k^0(d)$ has modulus
 at most one, which is all \cite[Section~18.1]{OAI78} use. The slot identity~\cite[eq.~(18.5)]{OAI78} is
 unchanged.
\item[(ii)] \emph{Reflection.} By~\eqref{eq:s5-natural} and Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-reflection}, a CM plain sum
 is $\epsilon(\Xi_k)$ times a combination of conjugates of CM plain sums with the same coefficient
 $\alpha^e\psi_k^0$ and profile $\overline{W^\sharp}$. The conjugation is removed inside the absolute value
 as in \cite[Section~18.1]{OAI78} and \cite[Section~4.1]{OAI78}.
\item[(iii)] \emph{Complete common-support extraction} at the first and second transforms
 (\cite[Sections~18.4 and~18.6]{OAI78}). A prime power $p^{i_j}$ allocated to plain variable $j$ gives the scalar
 $\alpha(p)^{ei_j}$. The allocation is made once for the coefficient, so the same scalar multiplies both
 rectangles.
\item[(iv)] \emph{The amplifier} (\cite[Section~18.5]{OAI78}). The powers $p^{i_1},p^{i_2}$ go only to the plain
 variables. The scalar $\alpha(p)^{e(i_1+i_2)}$ is absorbed in $c_{p,i}(h)$, whose modulus is unchanged.
\item[(v)] \emph{The $\mathfrak t$-allocation} (\cite[Section~18.6]{OAI78}). Writing $n_i=pl_i$ gives $\alpha(p)^e$, a
 scalar depending only on $\mathfrak t$ and the allocation. It is absorbed in $c_{\mathfrak t,\mathbf J}(h')$
 (\cite[Section~18.7]{OAI78}).
\item[(vi)] \emph{The second side} (\cite[Section~18.6]{OAI78}). It enters as $\overline{B(v)}$, the complex conjugate of
 a convolution with coefficient $\alpha^e$ times a finite-order character. After conjugating the whole
 child, which is allowed inside an absolute value or a Cauchy--Schwarz norm, both children carry
 $\alpha^e$ with the same $e$. Their finite-order characters are those of~\cite{OAI78}.
\item[(vii)] \emph{Fourier separations} (\cite[Sections~18.4 and~18.6]{OAI78}). The kernels and inverse roots
 depend on whole-product norms, and the fixed-ray phases are finite-ray characters of whole columns. They
 produce powers $\N(u)^{it}$ and finite-ray characters, and never act on $\alpha$.
\end{enumerate}
For the exceptional conclusion, the proof in \cite[proof of Lemma~18.2]{OAI78} factors the character, mask and norm power on
$\Pi l_1l_2$. The angular factor $\alpha(l_1)^e\alpha(l_2)^e$ already sits on the plain variables.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:s5-plain}}\label{sec:s5-proof}

We follow~\cite[Section~18]{OAI78} in order. \emph{Verbatim} means that the cited text applies word for
word when the plain coefficient $\psi_k(l)$ is read as $\alpha(l)^e\psi_k(l)$; the reason is given in each
case.

\emph{Step 0: inputs} (\cite[Section~18]{OAI78}). The finite Gauss identities of~\cite[\S 13.3]{OAI78}
concern the column Gauss sums $G(u,h)$ and correlations $F(u,v;j)$ only.
\cite[Lemma~4.5]{OAI78} concerns profiles. \cite[Lemma~4.9]{OAI78}
is used only in Step~5, for finite-order characters.

\emph{Step 1} (\cite[Section~18]{OAI78}): zero-length slots, the induction on $M$ by bands, and the threshold
$A\le5M/6$. Verbatim.

\emph{Step 2: mask deletion} (\cite[Section~18.1]{OAI78}). This is Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-coefficients}(i);
\cite[eq.~(18.6)]{OAI78} is verbatim. The finite-order part of the inducing character is unchanged,
so the row family $\mathcal R_z$ is preserved.

\emph{Step 3: reflection} (\cite[Section~18.1]{OAI78}). This step changes. For a natural character,
\eqref{eq:s5-natural} gives, by M\"obius inversion on $(l,\mathfrak r_k)=1$ and multiplicativity of $\Xi_k$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d187}
 T_k(X;W)=\sum_{d_0\mid\mathfrak r_k}\frac{\mu(d_0)\Xi_k(d_0)}{\sqrt{\N(d_0)}}\,T_{\Xi_k}\bigl(X/\N(d_0);W\bigr).
\end{equation}
Here $T_{\Xi_k}$ is the normalized sum of $\Xi_k$ over all nonzero ideals. Reflect each term
by~\eqref{eq:s5-reflection}. Then restore the deleted factors: every ideal is uniquely $h_0\mathfrak b'$
with $\operatorname{rad}(h_0)\mid\mathfrak r_k$ and $(\mathfrak b',\mathfrak r_k)=1$, and~\eqref{eq:s5-natural}
conjugated gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d188}
 T_{\overline{\Xi_k}}(Y;W^\sharp)=\sum_{\operatorname{rad}(h_0)\mid\mathfrak r_k}
 \frac{\overline{\Xi_k(h_0)}}{\sqrt{\N(h_0)}}\,T_{\overline{\alpha^e\psi_k^0}}\bigl(Y/\N(h_0);W^\sharp\bigr).
\end{equation}
The result is the display \cite[Section~18.1]{OAI78}, with these substitutions:
\begin{itemize}
\item $\psi_k^*\to\Xi_k$, $R_{0,k}\to\mathfrak r_k$, $C_k\to C_k^{\rm CM}$, $\varepsilon_k\to\epsilon(\Xi_k)$;
\item $W^\sharp$ given by~\eqref{eq:s5-sharp-def}.
\end{itemize}
The coefficient mass is bounded as in \cite[Section~18.1]{OAI78}. The scales~\cite[eq.~(18.8)]{OAI78} satisfy
$Y\le27C_{\mathcal A}Z^{M-\log_ZX}$, by Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-rowchar}(d). The profile bounds of \cite[Section~18.1]{OAI78}
are replaced by Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-sharp}, which gives the same finite-seminorm and polynomial-height
bounds.

\emph{Step 4: annuli and reflected lengths} (\cite[Section~18.1]{OAI78}). Verbatim, with $C_{\rm ref}$ enlarged by the
factor $27$. Below the main scale the annular coefficients are $O(2^{-j})$, by
Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-sharp}(c), which only improves \cite[Section~18.1]{OAI78}. Equations
\cite[(18.9)]{OAI78} and~\cite[(18.10)]{OAI78} are unchanged.

\emph{Step 5: prime estimates} (\cite[Section~18.2]{OAI78}). The slots carry $\psi_k^0\nu_i$, which has finite order and
no angular factor. Expand $\nu_i$ in the $\vartheta_{ij}\in\Theta$. Each slot character $\psi_k^*\vartheta_{ij}$
is nonprincipal for $k\in\mathcal R_z$ (\cite[Section~18.2]{OAI78}, verbatim, because $\mathcal R_z$ is defined
through $\psi_k$). For~\cite[eq.~(18.11)]{OAI78} at $s_\kappa+e'$, with $e'>0$ the small displacement
of~\cite{OAI78} (written $e$ there), apply the global part
of~\cite[Lemma~4.9]{OAI78} with $b=s_\kappa=(1+\kappa)/2$. This is
allowed because OpenAI's theorem~\cite[Theorem~1.1]{OAI78} gives $\beta_*\le7/8\le s_\kappa$ for
$\kappa\ge3/4$. Hence~\cite[eq.~(18.12)]{OAI78} holds for every $\kappa\in[3/4,1]$ without hypothesis.
The rest is verbatim.

\emph{Step 6: induction order, terminal widths and comparisons} (\cite[Section~18.2]{OAI78}). Verbatim, with Step~3
for every reflection. The comparison has the same coefficient $\alpha^e\psi_k$ and the same profiles. The
$\kappa$-dependent inequalities are those of~\cite{OAI78} and hold on all of $[3/4,1]$, endpoint
included. They are \cite[eq.~(18.13)]{OAI78}, the bound $6\kappa-1\ge7/2$ at \cite[Section~18.2]{OAI78},
\cite[eq.~(18.15)]{OAI78} and~\cite[eq.~(18.16)]{OAI78}.

\emph{Step 7: the centered difference} (\cite[Section~18.2]{OAI78}). Now
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d189}
 \Delta_k=\frac Q{\sqrt{X_1X_2}}\sum_{l_1,l_2}\alpha(l_1l_2)^e\psi_k(l_1l_2)\{\cdots\},
\end{equation}
with the bracket of \cite[Section~18.2]{OAI78}. The enlargement to the smooth row ball uses only nonnegativity.
Verbatim.

\emph{Step 8: the centered coefficient} (\cite[Section~18.3]{OAI78}). \cite[eq.~(18.17)]{OAI78} is unchanged. The
coefficient form is~\eqref{eq:s5-coeff}. The main term of \cite[Section~18.3]{OAI78} has
$c_{\vartheta,\mathfrak R_*}=0$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-lattice}). The support convention concerns norms only.

\emph{Step 9: the first transform} (\cite[Section~18.4]{OAI78}). Poisson summation acts on the row $k$, on which $\alpha$
does not depend.~\cite[eq.~(18.21)]{OAI78} holds with two changes:
\begin{itemize}
\item $A_C(a)$ and $A_D(b)$ include the residual angular factors (Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-coefficients}(iii));
\item the frozen $\alpha$-scalars of the allocation are part of the scalar of bounded modulus in the frozen
 labels (\cite[Section~18.4]{OAI78}).
\end{itemize}
The zero frequency (\cite[Section~18.4]{OAI78}) uses only that the coefficients have modulus at most one.
\cite[eqs.~(18.23), (18.24)]{OAI78} are verbatim.

\emph{Step 10: the Gauss-row enlargement} (\cite[Section~18.5]{OAI78}). Verbatim, by
Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-coefficients}(iv).

\emph{Step 11: the second transform and the children} (\cite[Section~18.6]{OAI78}). Verbatim, by
Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-coefficients}(iii),(v)--(vii). A child is classified by its finite-order character
$\tau_1\rho\chi_\bullet(G_cV_{\rm id}h')$ (\cite[Section~18.6]{OAI78}). For a child outside $\Theta$, the induction
hypothesis is Proposition~\ref{prop:s5-plain} at the smaller width $M'$, with the same $e$, for rows in
$\mathcal R_{z'}$ (or $\mathcal R_0$ if $z'=0$); both sets contain every such child row.

\emph{Step 12: exceptional rows} (\cite[Section~18.7]{OAI78}). The count~\cite[eq.~(18.41)]{OAI78}
involves only the finite-order child character, so it is verbatim. The volume bounds in \cite[Section~18.7]{OAI78} use
only $|\alpha|=1$. For the centered exceptional terms, Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-coefficients} supplies the
hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-lattice}. Then \cite[Section~18.7]{OAI78} give~\cite[eq.~(18.48)]{OAI78} verbatim,
and~\cite[eq.~(18.50)]{OAI78} follows.

\emph{Step 13: completion of the induction} (\cite[Section~18.8]{OAI78}). Verbatim. The ``fixed gamma shape'' of
reflection (\cite[Section~18.8]{OAI78}) is now $G(s)=\Gamma(s+\frac12)/\Gamma(\frac32-s)$, which has the same vertical
growth (Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-sharp}(b)). This proves Proposition~\ref{prop:s5-plain}.\qed

\paragraph{Remarks.}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Two strengthenings, not used.} In case~(1), the proof uses $k\in\mathcal R_0$ only through the
 reflections of Steps~3 and~6. These are valid for every row in the CM case, because $\Xi_k$ always
 exists and is never principal. So in the CM case, (1) holds with $\mathcal R_0$ replaced by all rows
 $0<\N(k)\ll Z^m$. Also, by Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-lattice} each exceptional plain sum is
 $O(Z^{\varepsilon_1}(1+|t|)^J)$, far below the volume bound, so the centering is not needed. We have not
 used either simplification.
\item \emph{Application.} The witnesses $S_m$ of Section~\ref{sec:78rows} have coefficient
 $\overline\alpha\theta\overline{\chi_\bullet(u)}$. Conjugating the whole product, as
 in~\cite[Section~19.2]{OAI78} gives~\eqref{eq:s5-plain-def} with $e=1$, $\tau=\overline\theta$ and $k=u$.
 The slot coefficients relative to $\psi_u=\overline\theta\chi_\bullet(u)$ are fixed combinations of members
 of $\Theta$. Every retained row ramifies at a good prime, so it lies in $\mathcal R_z$. This is the
 instance of Proposition~\ref{prop:s5-plain} used in Section~\ref{sec:78moments}.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Numerical checks.} We checked the new identities numerically: six scripts and $106$ checks, all of
which pass.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{The Bessel--Mellin formulas.} $\int_0^\infty J_1(2\sqrt y)y^{s-1}dy=G(s)$ agrees to
 $10^{-30}$, and Mellin inversion reproduces $W^\sharp$ for a $C_c^\infty$ bump on three lines to
 $4\cdot10^{-9}$.
\item \emph{The behaviour of $W^\sharp$.} At $0$ it behaves like $y^{1/2}$, and the annular coefficients
 are $O(2^{-j})$.
\item \emph{Stirling exponents.} These were checked for both quotients.
\item \emph{The planar transform.} Formula~\eqref{eq:s5-hankel} and $\widehat g(0)=0$ hold to $10^{-13}$.

\item \emph{The reflection formula.} \eqref{eq:s5-reflection} was checked for $12$ primitive characters:
 $\alpha^{\pm1}$, and twists by sextic residue characters modulo primes of norm $7$, $13$ and $19$ (one a
 product of two) and by the cubic character modulo $2$. Both signs of $e$ and three profiles were used,
 and the error was at most $4\cdot10^{-11}$.
\item \emph{The reflection on actual rows.} Rows with redundant zeros were checked, with deleted and
 restored factors and the bound of Lemma~\ref{lem:s5-rowchar}(d); the error was at most $2\cdot10^{-15}$.
\item \emph{The lattice sums.} $\mathcal L_i^0(X,t)$ stays bounded and tends to $0$ for $X$ up to
 $3\cdot10^5$, while the sum without the angular factor grows like $\frac{\pi}{6\sqrt3}X|I(t)|$. The
 inclusion--exclusion identity holds to $2\cdot10^{-13}$.
\end{itemize}

\section{The \texorpdfstring{$11/12$}{11/12} stage for the family \texorpdfstring{$\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$}{F\_CM}}\label{app:s6}

This appendix proves item~6 of Section~\ref{sec:78status}. The family $\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$ and the
number $\beta_*$ are those of Section~\ref{sec:78}.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:s6-1112}
For every $\psi\in\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$, $L(s,\psi)\ne0$ for $\Re s>11/12$. Equivalently, $\beta_*\le11/12$.
\end{theorem}

The two assertions are equivalent. The number $\beta_*$ is the supremum of $1/2$ and of the real parts of
the zeros $\rho$, with $1/2\le\Re\rho\le1$, of the members of $\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$. No $L(s,\psi)$ vanishes
in $\Re s>1$, where its Euler product converges absolutely.

\subsection{What~\texorpdfstring{\cite{OAI78}}{[OAI78]} proves at this stage, and what Part~II uses}
\label{subsec:s6-what}
The $11/12$ stage of~\cite{OAI78} is its Theorem~3.1: every finite-order
Hecke $L$-function over $K$, and every Dirichlet $L$-function, has no zero in $\Re s>11/12$. The proof
(\cite[Sections~10--11]{OAI78}, with the probe, the Poisson representation and the detector of \cite[Sections~6--9]{OAI78}) is by
contradiction. Suppose that $\Delta_1=\beta_*-11/12>0$, where now $\beta_*$ is the supremum for the
finite-order family. For each target $\eta$ the balanced probe
$J_{\mathrm I,\eta}(Z)=I_\eta(Z^{1/2},Z^{1/2},Z)/c_S$ satisfies two estimates:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:d190}
 |J_{\mathrm I,\eta}(Z)|\ll_\eta Z^{1/4+\omega},\qquad
 |J_{\mathrm I,\eta}(Z)-f_{\mathrm I,\eta}(Z)|\ll_\eta Z^{C_{\mathrm I}(\beta_*)-1/4800},
\end{equation}
with $C_{\mathrm I}(s)=s-2/3$ and $\omega=\Delta_1/2$ (\cite[Props.~6.3 and
11.2]{OAI78}). The high estimate uses five inputs:
\begin{itemize}
\item the Poisson representation, \cite[Lemma~7.1]{OAI78};
\item the detector, \cite[Section~8]{OAI78};
\item the sextic large sieve and the row count, \cite[Lemma~9.1 and
 Prop.~9.2]{OAI78};
\item the analytic lemmas of \cite[Section~10]{OAI78};
\item the late choice of the analysis height, \cite[Lemma~11.1]{OAI78}.
\end{itemize}
The continuation criterion \cite[Prop.~2.1]{OAI78} then contradicts
$\Delta_1>0$. A quadratic transfer, \cite[Prop.~11.3]{OAI78}, passes to
Dirichlet $L$-functions.

Part~II of~\cite{OAI78} uses this stage in two ways.
\begin{itemize}
\item[(U1)] \emph{The bound $\beta_*\le11/12$} (\cite[Section~12]{OAI78}). It enters only through
 $0<\Delta=\beta_*-7/8\le1/24$, $\kappa=2\beta_*-1\in(3/4,5/6]$ and the bin ceiling $\delta\le\kappa$
 (\cite[eqs.~(12.1) and~(12.2)]{OAI78}; used again at \cite[Section~19.2]{OAI78} and \cite[proof of Proposition~20.3]{OAI78}). In
 Section~\ref{sec:78final} this is the range $0\le\delta\le5/6$ of the endpoint certificate.
\item[(U2)] \emph{The shared lemmas.} The $7/8$ conclusion of~\cite{OAI78} (its Section~20) applies the following results of its Part~I again, with $\sigma_0=7/8$:
 Definition~10.1; eqs.~(10.1) and~(10.2); and
 Lemmas~10.2, 10.3 (inside the proof of the next one),
 10.4, 10.5, 10.6 and
 11.1.
\end{itemize}
The sextic large sieve and the sextic row count are used only in Part~I of~\cite{OAI78}.

For $\mathcal F_{\rm CM}$, (U1) is Theorem~\ref{thm:s6-1112}. Its conclusion is not uniform in anything: it
is the conjunction of one statement for each member. In~\cite{OAI78} the margins $\omega$ and $\sigma$ of the
continuation criterion must be independent of the target, because the criterion argues by contradiction
about the supremum $\beta_*$. That requirement belongs to the proof, not to the conclusion. So we may prove
Theorem~\ref{thm:s6-1112} one character at a time. We do this in Section~\ref{subsec:s6-twist}, by running
Part~I of these notes with a fixed finite-order twist. This is how~\cite{OAI1112} proves the same theorem for
finite-order characters. Its Theorem~1.1 is deduced from the mean-square
estimate~\cite[Proposition~3.1]{OAI1112}, one character at a time, and that estimate carries a fixed
finite-order twist~$\nu$. Section~\ref{subsec:s6-shared} supplies (U2), and with it the CM form of the
argument of~\cite{OAI78}.
